JUDGMENT CALLS MADE DURING THE BOOTSTRAP OF shackles_harness
Commit audited: 6cbfc97

The definition used throughout is a reasonable person's definition of judgment call.

How this audit was made: the bootstrap trail was cut into nine slices - the six plans, the two merges and the final plan; the runner's code, prompt templates, docs and implementation commits; reviews 1 and 2 and the commits that fixed them; reviews 3 to 5 and fix reports 3 to 5; tests A and B; test C; test D's two rounds; tests E and F; and the two coordinating chat sessions - and one auditor read each slice in full and wrote one entry per judgment call in a fixed shape. The nine part files held 569 entries between them. Because the slices overlap by design (the code slice covers decisions the plans made and decisions embodied in fix commits, the coordination slice covers the final plan's amendments, one slice reports both of test D's rounds, and a decision made in a test is often also judged in a review), entries describing the same decision against the same open question have been merged here, and the ids they came from are listed on each merged entry's last line. Seventy-four merges folded 163 of the original entries into one another, leaving 480 entries, renumbered JC-1 to JC-480 in the order the bootstrap ran.


================================================================================
PLANNING: THE SIX PLANS, THE TWO MERGES, AND THE FINAL PLAN
================================================================================

## JC-1: Deciding which parts of the owner's files the runner is allowed to depend on (from JC-PLAN-1)
Who and where: all six planners, in their "what the spec fixes" sections - plan-1 section 2, plan-2 section 2.1 and rule R2, plan-3 section 2, plan-4 section 2, plan-5 section 2, plan-6 section 2.
Context: The owner's files mix two kinds of content: data the runner obviously needs (numbers, file names, the gate list) and prose written for agents to read. Before anything could be designed, each planner had to draw a line: which parts of those files may the runner's code look at, and which parts must it treat as text it only copies into a prompt.
Rationale: plan-6 states it as a principle: "The spec owns judgment; the runner owns mechanics. Every machine-parsed word (statuses, verdicts, finding fields, the `STEP:`/`ARTIFACT:` lines) is emitted by runner code into the `{{ plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS }}` block and parsed by runner code. The runner never greps a locked prose file for a phrase." plan-2's rule R2 draws the same line and lists the only conventions allowed: "placeholder grammar `{{ ns.key }}`; prose file naming `<STEP>-OVERVIEW.txt`, `<STEP>-GATE.txt`, `COMMON-*.txt`; YAML keys; `roundPaths` values; the `N` run in `roundPaths.folder`."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's header states the requirement without saying how to meet it: "They belong to the owner and change often. Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them." Nothing in the owner's files says which parts are structure and which are wording.
Other context: This is the decision every other decision in the planning slice rests on. Both merges carry it forward as decision 1, and the final plan restates it in amendment 10 ("the runner never matches a prose phrase"). It produced a concrete artefact in the build: both merges specify a "wording-adjacent rules table" in docs/PROCESS.md listing every place a runner rule still mirrors a prose sentence, so that a reviewer can find them after an owner edit (JC-129).
Sources: plan-1 section 2; plan-2 sections 2.1, 4.2 (R2), 4.16; plan-3 sections 2, 4.5; plan-4 section 2; plan-5 section 2; plan-6 section 2; merged-A decision 1 area and section 2.1; merged-B decision 1; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-2: Placing the steps the owner's gate list does not mention (from JC-PLAN-2)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 2, plan-2 section 4.4, plan-3 section 4.4, plan-4 section 4.3, plan-5 interpretation I3, plan-6 section 4.
Context: project.yaml lists the gates in "intended run order", which fixes the order of the gated producers. It does not list CLEANUP at all, and marks LANDING and the end of the round only in comments. Somebody had to decide the exact running order of the ungated steps.
Rationale: plan-3 records the derivation: "Checkpoints the prose asks for: after the plan (approval), after PLAN-TO-SPEC ... after CLEANUP ... Landing follows CLEANUP; POSTMORTEM and its gate come last". plan-5's I3 writes the full chain out and adds "The table lives in `harness/src/runner.yaml` ... not in code and not in a spec file."
Spec conflict: project.yaml's gate block carries only comments: "# Standard landing happens here, after TESTS-TO-SUITE." and "# Round ends here, all agents stop." CLEANUP appears only as a prose file, CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt, whose text is "Clean up the round, so that it can be landed without issue." The order CLEANUP then LANDING is an inference from that sentence plus the comment.
Other context: All six arrived at the same chain, and the summary of the sibling project supplied to the planners had the same step list, which is why no merge reopened it. The choice is load-bearing: the "checkpoint before landing" that project.yaml names at TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE was implemented after CLEANUP, one step later than the comment's position.
Sources: plan-1 section 2; plan-2 section 4.4 table; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.3 table; plan-5 I3; plan-6 section 4 table; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4.

## JC-3: Whether the step table is runner data or runner code (from JC-PLAN-3)
Who and where: split among the planners - data files in plan-1 (src/runner.yaml), plan-5 (src/runner.yaml) and plan-6 (src/process.yaml); code in plan-2 (pipeline.py), plan-3 (pipeline.py) and plan-4 (pipeline.KNOWN).
Context: The list of steps, their kinds, their artifacts and their declared paths has to live somewhere. A YAML file lets the owner reorder or add a step without touching code; a Python table keeps one source of truth but makes any change a code change.
Rationale: plan-1 argues for data: "The step table is data so the owner can reorder or add a step without touching code; each `kind` must have a handler in code (loader error otherwise)." merged-B argues for code and names the cost of the alternative: "a yaml table is a second source of truth whose only editor can also edit code; the cross-check (gate keys and order, prose file names, artifact keys) is what makes a structural spec change loud."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files name the gates and the round file names but never say where the pipeline itself is described; nothing in them constrains the choice.
Other context: The two merges split the same way - merged-A decision 6 chose the data table ("a reorder needs a code change" was its objection to code), merged-B decision 3 chose code. The final plan adopted merged-B, so the built runner has the table in code cross-checked against the owner's data; SPEC.md later records the same thing: "The step table is code, lint-checked against the spec's data; unknown tokens and keys warn, never crash."
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.4; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.3; plan-5 section 5.2; plan-6 section 4; merged-A decision 6; merged-B decision 3; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-4: What a missing or unexpected prose file means
Who and where: all six planners, most explicitly plan-2 section 4.4, plan-3 section 4.4 and plan-1 section 5.2; implemented by the implementer in `pipeline.lint` and `prompts.render_prompt`, commit ef1296d.
Context: Because the pipeline is discovered partly from file names, the planners had to say what happens if the owner deletes a step's prose file, or adds a prose file for a step the runner does not know. The prose files are the owner's and may be deleted or renamed at any time.
Rationale: plan-2: "Missing prose file (owner deleted it): producer -> RunnerError at `start` ('step X has no prose; override it or restore the file'); gate -> treated as disabled, lint warns." plan-3 chose the opposite for producers: "A producer step is present iff `<STEP>-OVERVIEW.txt` exists at `prose_commit`; absent -> skipped with a HISTORY line and a warning." merged-B settled it: "A producer with no `<NAME>-OVERVIEW.txt` at `prose_commit` is a `start` error naming the file ('restore it or override the step'); a gate with `gates: 1` and no prose file is disabled with a warning."
Spec conflict: None found. spec.yaml lists the prose files, so deleting one is visible, but nothing in the owner's files says whether a deleted file means "this step no longer exists" or "something is broken". The naming convention `<STEP>-OVERVIEW.txt` / `<STEP>.txt` is itself the runner's reading of the folder.
Other context: merged-A decision 6 rejected plan-3's silent skip explicitly - "treating a missing producer prose file as 'step skipped' (plan-3; silent). A missing producer prose file makes `start` refuse (plan-2); `override` is the explicit way to skip." An unknown prose file became a lint warning rather than an error in both merges, so an owner who adds a new step gets told about it without a round breaking. The implementation follows the plan down to the error text, and the gate's skip is recorded with source `no-prose` so the archive distinguishes it from a disabled or overridden gate.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2 lint list; plan-2 section 4.4; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.3; plan-5 section 5.2; plan-6 section 4; merged-A decision 6 and section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4; `pipeline.lint`, `pipeline.prose_file`, `pipeline.prose_owner`; `prompts.render_prompt`; `round.skip_source`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-4, JC-CODE-11

## JC-5: What it means that every gate is switched off (from JC-PLAN-5)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.4, plan-3 section 4.4, plan-4 section 4.3, plan-5 interpretation I4, plan-6 section 4.
Context: project.yaml ships with every gate set to 0. The owner's files never say what a 0 does beyond "whether they are enabled or not". A disabled gate could mean the step's output is accepted as-is, or that the round cannot proceed, or that the runner judges in the gate's place.
Rationale: plan-5's I4: "The gate step is skipped: recorded in HISTORY as `skipped (disabled)`, no findings, no spend, `step_commits` set. Mechanical checks still run (they are the runner's, not the gate's). The producer's prompt says the gate is disabled this round." plan-1 adds that the producer is still shown the gate's standard: the gate prose appears in the producer's prompt "annotated `(this gate is disabled this round; judge yourself by it)`".
Spec conflict: project.yaml says only "# The gates, in intended run order, and whether they are enabled or not. 1 = enabled". Every gate's own prose file assumes it is running.
Other context: All six agreed on the shape, and both merges kept it, adding a uniform findings file with `"source": "disabled"` so the round archive looks the same whether a gate ran or not. The decision to keep the mechanical checks running when the gate is off is what makes a gates-off round still safe.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.4; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.3; plan-5 I4; plan-6 section 4; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4.

## JC-6: What a disabled CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE means for the owner's approval (from JC-PLAN-6)
Who and where: plan-1 risk 14, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-6 section 12.2, and by implication all six planners.
Context: CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE is the mechanical gate that waits for the owner's approving word. It is set to 0 like the rest. If a disabled gate is skipped, a round can start with no owner word at all - which is either a convenience or a hole, depending on how you read it.
Rationale: plan-1 states it flatly as a risk and accepts it: "CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE disabled means no approval. -> As specified; `--delegated` still controls checkpoints; documented." plan-6 chose a middle course: "if disabled (today's config) `start` records `{word: approved, source: gate-disabled}`, so a gates-off round runs with checkpoints unless `--approval delegated` says otherwise."
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt makes approval central - "approve" / "approved" means "approved; the round pauses for the owner between steps (checkpoints)" - while project.yaml switches the gate that collects it off. The two files pull in opposite directions.
Other context: Both merges took plan-6's reading: an absent approval with the gate disabled is recorded as approved with the source noted, and the round keeps its review checkpoints. Both merges also flagged it to the owner - merged-B interpretation 4, merged-A risk 3 ("Confirm with the owner") - and the gap was written up again for the owner in CLARIFICATIONS.md (JC-276, JC-128).
Sources: plan-1 risk 14; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-6 section 12.2 and risk 23.4; merged-A section 2.9 step 3 and risk 3; merged-B section 2.9 step 2 and interpretation 4.

## JC-7: Which steps and gates the owner's "override" word may skip
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.4, plan-4 section 4.12, plan-5 interpretation I17, plan-6 section 4; implemented by the implementer in `pipeline.NOT_OVERRIDABLE` and `pipeline.is_overridable`, commit ef1296d.
Context: The owner may say "override" followed by step or gate names. The prose protects only the plan. The planners had to decide what else must never be skipped, because skipping some steps would leave the round with nothing to build from, and the runner needs an actual list.
Rationale: plan-3: "CHAT-TO-PLAN, CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE, CLEANUP-CHECK and LANDING cannot be overridden." plan-4 additionally refuses to let PLAN-TO-SPEC be skipped, "refused with a usage error since nothing can run without a spec". merged-B settled the list the code carries: "Overridable: every gate, PLAN-AGENTS ..., SPEC-TO-TESTS ..., TESTS-TO-SUITE ..., CLEANUP ..., POSTMORTEM .... Not overridable: CHAT-TO-PLAN, CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE, PLAN-TO-SPEC, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION, LANDING."
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says only: "'override' + steps or gates: the runner skips them this round; the plan itself cannot be skipped." Everything past the plan is left open; the four extra protected names are the planners' and the implementer's reading of what a round would still mean without them.
Other context: merged-A protected its own FINISH step as well. Later fix commits narrowed the word further at runtime: a62ca94 refuses an override of another step while a pending attempt has unrecorded work (JC-305), and b887f2a refuses an UPSTREAM that names an overridden step (JC-378).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.12; plan-5 I17; plan-6 section 4; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4; `pipeline.py` `NOT_OVERRIDABLE`, `is_overridable`; `round.override`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 38-39.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-7, JC-CODE-7

## JC-8: What an overridden producer's missing artifact falls back to (from JC-PLAN-8)
Who and where: plan-2 section 4.4 (the "Default when overridden" column), plan-5 interpretation I17, plan-6 section 4, and both merges.
Context: Skipping a step leaves a hole: PLAN-AGENTS produces the budget shares, TESTS-TO-SUITE produces the keep/archive decision, SPEC-TO-TESTS produces the tests. Each hole needs a default or the round cannot continue.
Rationale: plan-2's table gives one default per step: PLAN-AGENTS overridden means "defaults"; SPEC-TO-TESTS overridden means "no tests; frozen set empty"; TESTS-TO-SUITE overridden means "all round tests join the suite"; CLEANUP overridden means "skipped". plan-5's I17 gives the same reasoning in a sentence: "an overridden producer step is skipped and its artifact, if the runner needs it, is the previous round's or the defaults (PLAN-AGENTS skipped means default shares and agents)."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files say an overridden step is skipped and stop there; the consequences for later steps are not addressed anywhere.
Other context: The two merges adopted the same table. Test D's second round then walked into the hole the table left at SPEC-TO-TESTS (JC-361, JC-362), and a later fix commit, b887f2a, revisited that cell: an overridden SPEC-TO-TESTS now leaves the tests to SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION (JC-380), replacing "no tests at all" with "the implementation step writes them".
Sources: plan-2 section 4.4; plan-5 I17; plan-6 section 4; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4.

## JC-9: Where the owner's three checkpoints sit, and what they hang on
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.4, plan-4 runner.yaml `checkpointAfter`, plan-5 I3, plan-6 section 7; implemented by the implementer as `config.DEFAULTS.checkpointsAfter` (`[PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE, CLEANUP]`) and `round.complete`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner asks for a pause after the plan, after the spec and before landing. Two things were left open: whether the pause happens when the producer finishes or when its gate passes, and where that placement is recorded. The runner needs a list of step names, not sentences.
Rationale: All six hung the checkpoint on acceptance rather than on the producer's completion. merged-A puts the rule plainly: "`checkpoint` names the review checkpoint raised after acceptance unless delegated through the step; the step table is the only place checkpoint placement lives." Acceptance means the gate passed, or - when the gate is off - the producer finished and the mechanical checks were clean. merged-B decision 1 adds where the list lives: the review checkpoints "live in the runner, derived from prose sentences and `project.yaml` comments; the owner may adopt `checkpointsAfter` into `project.yaml`."
Spec conflict: Two owner files disagree about where the last one goes. PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt and CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt both say "Checkpoint after this step, with the owner", which puts the third checkpoint after CLEANUP; project.yaml's comment attaches it to a different step: "TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE: 0 # checkpoint before landing". The planners reconciled these by treating "before landing" as the intent and CLEANUP as the position. Nothing in the owner's files names a step key the runner could read.
Other context: Recording the placement in runner-owned config rather than in the owner's files was a second, deliberate choice; several plans offered the owner the option of taking it over. It was implemented exactly as planned, as a config list the owner can edit, and `pipeline.lint` warns when an entry names no step, so an owner's typo is visible rather than silent.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 sections 4.2 and 4.7; plan-5 I3; plan-6 section 7; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.4, 2.3 and interpretation 5; `config.py` `DEFAULTS.checkpointsAfter`; `round.complete`; `pipeline.lint`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 40 and 150.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-9, JC-CODE-3

## JC-10: Delegation removes review pauses only (from JC-PLAN-10)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.4, plan-4 section 4.7, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 4.
Context: The owner's word "delegate" means "approved; no checkpoints". Read literally that would also silence a producer's question, a blocked step, a budget overrun and a spec change. The planners had to decide how far the word reaches.
Rationale: plan-3: "Delegation skips only `review-spec` and `review-landing`; stop checkpoints are never skipped." plan-2 gives the reason in its risk 11: "delegation removes review checkpoints, not questions a gate judged worth the owner's cost".
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says only: "'delegate' / 'delegated': approved; no checkpoints." COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt at the same time tells a producer to end "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide and spending their time is sensible given their cost", and "BLOCKED when you cannot finish within budget or turns" - outcomes that have nowhere to go if delegation silences every pause.
Other context: Both merges carried it; merged-A's list of never-skipped checkpoints runs to eleven kinds. SPEC.md later records the same call in the owner's own words: "Delegation skips only review checkpoints; questions, blocks, limits and spec edits still stop the round." The one case the merges did leave to delegation - a producer's question with no gate to weigh it (JC-19) - was reversed after test D by fix commit 3f613d7 (JC-373).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 sections 4.9 and 9.11; plan-3 section 4.4; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 4; merged-A section 2.4; merged-B section 2.9.

## JC-11: Adding an "abandon" word the owner's list does not contain (from JC-PLAN-11)
Who and where: all six planners, in their owner-word tables - plan-1 runner.yaml `ownerWords`, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-4 runner.yaml, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 12.1, and plan-2's `abandon --reason` command.
Context: A round has to be able to end without landing. The owner's word list has no word for that, but the sibling project's write-up shows "owner word: abandon" in its lifecycle diagram as the way out.
Rationale: Not recorded as a decision by any planner; each simply lists `abandon` beside the owner's four words. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a round that cannot finish must have an exit that is not "leave it running forever", and the sibling's write-up supplied the word, so adopting it looked like completing the owner's list rather than adding to it.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt gives a closed-looking table headed "Words that count, needed for both plan and quote" listing only approve/approved, delegate/delegated, delegate through STEP, and override. The planners added a fifth word that the owner never wrote down.
Other context: plan-3 was alone in noticing the risk that the word could fire by accident and proposed a guard: "`abandon` additionally requires the message to be under 200 characters or to start with the word". The merges removed the risk a different way by taking the runner out of word-reading altogether (JC-49), so `abandon` became a driver-issued command with the owner's quote attached.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.7; plan-3 section 4.10 and risk 10; plan-4 section 4.12; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 12.1; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B section 2.8.

## JC-12: Writing the producer's final-message contract (from JC-PLAN-12)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.5, plan-2 Appendix B, plan-3 section 4.7, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.5, plan-6 section 8.4.
Context: AGENTS.md promises every agent that its prompt will name "the JSON your final message must be", and that the runner parses it. It does not say what that JSON contains. Every field name, and every rule about which fields go with which outcome, was invented here.
Rationale: Not argued as a choice; each plan simply specifies one. plan-6's version is representative: status, `notes`, `question`, `target`, `resolutions` keyed by finding id with `fixed`/`disputed`, and an optional cost. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the four outcome words are fixed by the owner's prose, so the contract had to carry the extra information each outcome implies - a question for NEEDS-OWNER, a target step for UPSTREAM, something to narrow for BLOCKED - and a place for the producer to answer the gate's previous findings.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md fixes only that a contract exists: "Your system prompt names your step, your inputs, where to write your artifact, and the JSON your final message must be. Follow it exactly; the runner parses it." COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt fixes the four status words and what each means, and nothing else.
Other context: The six independently produced near-identical shapes, which is why neither merge treated it as contested; merged-B section 2.5 settles the final wording. The choice to put this contract in runner-owned template files rather than in the owner's prose is JC-70.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 Appendix A and B; plan-3 section 4.7; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.4; plan-6 section 8.4; merged-A section 2.5; merged-B section 2.5.

## JC-13: Writing the gate's verdict contract (from JC-PLAN-13)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.5, plan-2 Appendix B, plan-3 section 4.7, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.4, plan-6 section 8.4.
Context: COMMON-GATE.txt describes how a gate should think - findings with suggestions, blocking or not, disputes ruled by quotation, a decision on a producer's request for the owner - without giving any of it a machine shape. The planners had to turn each of those ideas into a named JSON field.
Rationale: Each plan lists the same five parts: a verdict, findings carrying `id`, `quote`, `reason`, `suggestion` and `blocking`, `rulings` on earlier findings, a `needs_owner` decision, and free notes. plan-3 adds the rule that "Ids continue from the highest prior id", which is what lets findings be tracked across attempts.
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt supplies the concepts only: "Every finding carries a suggestion", "Rule on each disputed finding before raising new ones, upheld or withdrawn by quotation", "If the producer asked for the owner: uphold when spending the owner's time is sensible given their cost; otherwise withdraw with the answer to assume". Nothing names a field.
Other context: The `quote` field is the one that carries the most weight: COMMON-GATE tells a gate to "quote the text you judge", and the planners made that quote the identity of a finding, so a re-raised withdrawn finding can be recognised by its quote (JC-16).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 Appendix B; plan-3 section 4.7; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.4; plan-6 section 8.4; merged-A section 2.6; merged-B section 2.5.

## JC-14: Whether the gate's verdict or its blocking flags decide pass and fail
Who and where: plan-1 risk 5 alone among the plans; the other five took the opposite view - plan-2 section 4.6, plan-3 section 4.7, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.4, plan-6 section 8.4. Reopened at both merges, settled by the coordinating session in FINAL-PLAN amendment 1, and implemented by the implementer in `schemas.normalize_findings`, commit ef1296d.
Context: A gate returns both a verdict word and a set of findings marked blocking or not. The two can disagree. The runner has to pick one as the truth.
Rationale: plan-1: "Gate verdict vs `blocking` inconsistency. -> Verdict is authoritative; flags normalized; logged." The other five derive the verdict from the flags instead; plan-4 states it as a rule the gate is told: "Verdict is FAIL exactly when a finding is blocking or needs_owner is upheld." merged-B decision 12 defends that: "COMMON-GATE defines blocking by cost, so the flags are the primary datum." merged-A decision 9 answers it, and the final plan's amendment 1 settles it in those terms: "The gate's `verdict` field is the verdict. `blocking` flags are normalized to it: PASS with a blocking finding sets that finding non-blocking; FAIL with no blocking finding keeps the findings as given. Either mismatch is one HISTORY line. ... Reason: 'FAIL iff a blocking finding' encodes today's COMMON-GATE cost rule into the runner; the owner may reword that rule at any time."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt defines blocking in terms of money, which is exactly the wording that might be rewritten: "A FAIL costs a retry of the producing step (${{ step.retry_cost }}) plus this gate again: FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that; otherwise PASS with non-blocking findings. A finding is blocking when leaving it in would cost more than the retry." The file never says which of the two signals wins if they disagree.
Other context: The same amendment carved out one exception on routing grounds: "`needs_owner.upheld` still raises the `question` checkpoint whatever the verdict says, because that is routing, not judgment." SPEC.md later records the outcome: "The gate's verdict wins and blocking flags follow it, so a reworded cost rule cannot desync the runner." The implementation adds detail of its own: a FAIL carrying no findings at all is accepted with a third note, and finding ids that are not `F<n>` or that reuse a number already spent are renamed `<GATE>-<n>.F<k>` rather than refused.
Sources: plan-1 sections 5.4 and 9.5; plan-2 section 4.6; plan-3 section 4.7; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.4; plan-6 section 8.4; merged-A decision 9 and section 2.6; merged-B decision 12; FINAL-PLAN amendment 1; `schemas.normalize_findings`; `round.record` and `round.record_gate`; `plumbing/gate.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 71.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-14, JC-CODE-22

## JC-15: Giving a producer a way to say a finding cannot be fixed this round (from JC-PLAN-15)
Who and where: plan-1 (feature 8), plan-4 section 4.5, plan-6 section 11.2 - against plan-2, plan-3 and plan-5, which allow only "fixed" or "disputed".
Context: A gate raises a finding; the producer must answer it on the next attempt. If the only answers are "I fixed it" and "I disagree", a producer facing something real but out of scope has to lie one way or the other.
Rationale: plan-1 names the problem it is solving: "`deferred` resolution status - Producers had to mark unfixable findings 'fixed' every step". plan-4 defines the consequence: "deferred means it cannot be closed in this round and goes to the postmortem".
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt describes only two dispositions for a finding once raised - it is upheld or withdrawn by the gate - and says nothing about a producer's options. Nothing in the owner's files forbids a third answer or provides one.
Other context: Both merges adopted it (merged-A decision 16 area, merged-B decision 12), and the final plan kept it. It is a small widening of a contract the owner did not write, justified by an observed failure mode in the sibling project.
Sources: plan-1 section 3 item 8 and 5.4; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B decision 12.

## JC-16: Making the runner enforce two rules the owner addressed to the gate (from JC-PLAN-16)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.7, plan-4 section 4.7, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 11.2.
Context: COMMON-GATE tells a gate that a finding upheld twice is settled and that a withdrawn finding must not be raised again. These are instructions to a reasoning agent. The planners decided the runner would also keep score and act on it.
Rationale: plan-3 spells out both halves: a findings ledger tracking `upheld_count` with "settled at 2", and "A new finding whose `quote` equals a withdrawn finding's quote is dropped with a HISTORY note ('re-raised withdrawn finding F3 dropped')". plan-2 makes the count a config key, `settleAfterUpholds: 2`, and lists it in its "wording-adjacent rules" table so that a reviewer can find it if the owner rewords the sentence.
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt: "Rule on each disputed finding before raising new ones, upheld or withdrawn by quotation. Upheld twice is settled. Do not re-raise a withdrawn finding." Addressed to the gate, with no mention of the runner.
Other context: This sits close to the line the planners drew in JC-1 - a number taken from a prose sentence and hard-coded - which is precisely why plan-2 and both merges put it in the wording-adjacent table (JC-129). A related enforcement, refusing a producer's dispute of a settled finding, came with it.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 sections 4.9 and 4.16; plan-3 sections 4.7 and 4.10; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B section 2.6.

## JC-17: Pulling the JSON out of a message that is not only JSON (from JC-PLAN-17)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.6, plan-3 section 5.5, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.5, plan-6 section 15.3.
Context: The contract says the final message is exactly one JSON object. Language models routinely wrap it in a code fence or add a sentence. Treating every such message as a failure would waste paid runs.
Rationale: plan-4: "try the whole message; then a ```json fence; then the last balanced top-level `{...}`." plan-2 says the same and adds that applying it is logged: "Result normalization (mechanical; a HISTORY line when applied)".
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "Follow it exactly; the runner parses it", which on a strict reading leaves no room for recovery. Nothing says whether a near-miss should be salvaged or rejected.
Other context: Unanimous and uncontroversial; both merges kept it. It is a deliberate softening of an owner instruction in the agent's favour, logged each time it is used so the softening is visible. The rule the merges wrote down turned out to pick the wrong object when a message ends with a nested fragment, which review 2 found and its fixer replaced (JC-175).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.6; plan-3 section 5.5; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.5; plan-6 section 15.3; merged-A section 2.6; merged-B section 2.6.

## JC-18: What happens when no valid result can be had (from JC-PLAN-18)
Who and where: the planners split four ways - plan-1 and plan-6 refuse and consume nothing, plan-4 consumes the attempt with a synthesized finding, plan-5 books the cost as an infrastructure error, plan-2 adds an explicit `record --infra-error` command.
Context: An agent run can end with unusable output. Counting it as a failed attempt punishes the producer for a transport problem; ignoring it loses the money that was spent and can loop forever.
Rationale: plan-4: "Failure -> the attempt counts with synthesized finding `R1` ('final message was not the contract JSON') and the same step runs again." plan-5 keeps the attempt but counts an infrastructure error and stops after a bounded number: "after `infraRetries` failures raises checkpoint `infra`". merged-A rejects plan-4's reading - "consuming the attempt with a synthesized finding (plan-4; wastes a budgeted run on a transport failure)" - while merged-B rejects plan-1's and plan-6's - "exit 2 with nothing recorded (1, 6), which loses the spend".
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files describe what a good result looks like and say nothing about a broken one; the choice turns on who should bear the cost of a transport failure.
Other context: The final plan took merged-B, so the built runner books the cost, saves the raw output, and asks for the same attempt again. Both merges agree that a bounded number of these turns into a blocking finding rather than an endless retry. Review 1 then argued that reverting a producer's work on an invalid message cancels the saving this decision was meant to buy, and that finding was left unfixed (JC-143).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.7; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 15.3; merged-A decision 17; merged-B decision 13; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-19: What happens to a producer's question when no gate is there to weigh it (from JC-PLAN-19)
Who and where: plan-2 section 4.9 and risk 10, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-1 section 5.4, plan-4 section 4.7, plan-6 section 11.2; adopted by both merges.
Context: The owner's prose routes a producer's request for the owner through the gate, which decides whether the question is worth the owner's time. With every gate switched off there is no gate to decide, so the runner must choose between interrupting the owner and proceeding on the producer's stated assumption.
Rationale: plan-3 splits on delegation: "Gate absent: not delegated -> checkpoint `needs-owner`; delegated -> proceed on the assumption, append one line to UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md ('assumed: ...'), HISTORY line". plan-2 reasons the same way in its risk 10: "Continue on the stated assumption, list in `pending_questions` at the next checkpoint or done; approved mode asks."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt assumes a gate is always there: "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide ... you never contact them yourself, the gate weighs the question." COMMON-GATE.txt carries the matching instruction. project.yaml then turns every gate off.
Other context: Both merges took the split reading, as merged-B decision 17. It was reversed after test D: review 5 ruled it against the owner's own SPEC.md sentence and fix commit 3f613d7 removed the branch, so a question with no gate now stops the round delegated or not (JC-373). Test D's round 0002 is where the cost of the original reading showed: the owner's reserved decision was settled for them (JC-350, JC-357).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 sections 4.9 and 9.10; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A decision 13; merged-B decision 17.

## JC-20: Whether a blocked step goes to the owner or back to the agent (from JC-PLAN-20)
Who and where: plan-2, plan-3, plan-4 and plan-6 send it to the owner; plan-1 and plan-5 retry the step first.
Context: BLOCKED means the producer could not finish inside its budget or turns. Either the round pauses and the owner narrows the work, or the runner gives the step another attempt with the same instructions.
Rationale: plan-3 states the choice and its reason: "BLOCKED goes straight to a checkpoint rather than a retry. Recommendation: keep (cheapest, and narrowing is the owner's call); if delegated rounds stall on it too often, allow one retry under delegation." plan-1 instead treats it as a failure to retry: "`BLOCKED`: finding `B1`, `failures += 1`, retry."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says what BLOCKED means and what the agent should supply - "BLOCKED when you cannot finish within budget or turns: say what to narrow" - but not who narrows it or when.
Other context: Both merges took the checkpoint reading; merged-A decision 13 rejects the retry explicitly ("narrowing is the owner's call"). Note the interaction with JC-10: because a blocked step is a stop-class checkpoint, a delegated round still halts on it.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 sections 4.10 and 9.15; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A decision 13; merged-B decision 17.

## JC-21: Who an UPSTREAM may point at, and what is thrown away when it does (from JC-PLAN-21)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-4 section 4.7, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 11.2.
Context: A producer may say an earlier artifact is wrong. The runner has to decide which steps are legal targets, what happens to the work already done after that target, and what the earlier step is told.
Rationale: All six converge: the target must be an earlier producer; the complaining agent's notes become a finding attached to that step's next attempt; every counter downstream of the target is reset; and pointing at CHAT-TO-PLAN is special because the plan is the owner's, so it raises a checkpoint instead of re-running an agent. plan-3: "target of CHAT-TO-PLAN -> checkpoint `upstream-plan`".
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt gives only the outcome and the evidence required: "UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong: quote the contradiction." Nothing about targets, resets or the plan's special status.
Other context: The reset rule is what makes UPSTREAM expensive, which is why all six counted it against the round's re-entry limit (JC-45). A later fix commit, b887f2a, added one more rule for an overridden target: an UPSTREAM naming it is refused as a finding on the source instead (JC-378).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B decision 17.

## JC-22: Inventing the mechanical checks the owner's prose presupposes (from JC-PLAN-22)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.7, plan-2 section 4.10, plan-3 section 4.11, plan-4 section 4.8, plan-5 section 5.7, plan-6 section 11.3.
Context: COMMON-GATE tells a gate not to repeat checks that have already been run, which implies the runner runs some. The owner's files never say what they are. Each planner drew up a list.
Rationale: All six produced the same core four: the artifact matches its schema; the diff stays inside the step's declared paths; the frozen tests are unchanged; the verify command passes. plan-2 gives the economic reason for putting them before the gate: "Cheap deterministic rejections before paying a gate."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt presupposes them in one clause - "Do not repeat the mechanical checks already run." - and AGENTS.md supplies one of them in passing: "Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths plus the round folder." The rest is invention.
Other context: The lists then grew in every plan, to a dozen or more ids covering judgment-file tampering, agent commits, prompt size, spec-file edits and landing failures. Everything in JC-23 to JC-31 is a sub-decision of this one, each with its own recorded reasoning, which is why they are separate entries.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.7; plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.7; plan-6 section 11.3; merged-A section 2.10; merged-B section 2.10.

## JC-23: Which diff the confinement check measures (from JC-PLAN-23)
Who and where: plan-6 section 11.3 against plans 1, 3, 4 and 5; plan-2 sits between them.
Context: To check that an agent only touched what it was allowed to touch, the runner compares two points in the repository's history. Measuring from where the step first started accumulates everything since then, including merges from elsewhere; measuring only the current attempt's uncommitted changes sees just what this agent did.
Rationale: plan-6 states the design and its purpose in one line: "M1 diff touches paths outside the declared ones plus the round folder (computed from the uncommitted diff at `record`, so merges from main never trip it)". merged-B rejects the alternative with the evidence: "Rejected: cumulative `step_starts..HEAD` diffs with `mech_ok` bookkeeping in `next` (1, 3, 5): more state, and a cumulative base is exactly what made the sibling's conflict path non-convergent."
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md fixes what must be true - "Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths plus the round folder" - but not how it is measured.
Other context: Both merges chose the per-attempt diff, and it is the mechanism that makes the landing-conflict fix work at all (JC-55), because a merge brings in thousands of lines nobody on this step wrote. SPEC.md later records it: "Mechanical checks run at record on the attempt's own uncommitted diff". How the merge case is measured in particular was settled later, by the review-1 fixer (JC-149).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.7; plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.7; plan-6 section 11.3; merged-A decision 10; merged-B decision 8; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-24: A file touched outside the declared paths - revert it or fail the attempt (from JC-PLAN-24)
Who and where: plan-2 section 4.10 alone proposes reverting; plans 1, 3, 4, 5 and 6 fail the attempt.
Context: When an agent edits one file it should not have, the round can either throw the whole attempt away and pay for another one, or quietly undo that file and let the rest stand.
Rationale: plan-2: "Out-of-scope changes are reverted (`git checkout --` / delete untracked) so the tree stays reproducible; in-scope work is kept and committed." merged-A adds the arithmetic: "a retry costs more than reverting one file - the gate sees the note, and a stray that verify needed surfaces as M3."
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md states the rule but not the penalty for breaking it.
Other context: Both merges adopted the revert, and SPEC.md records it in the owner's list: "strays are reverted, not failed". The safety net merged-A names matters: if the stray file was actually needed, the verify command fails once it is removed, so the attempt still fails - just for the right reason. How the revert is actually carried out is JC-107.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.7; plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.7; plan-6 section 11.3; merged-A decision 10; merged-B decision 8.

## JC-25: Freezing the tests once they have been accepted (from JC-PLAN-25)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.7 (M2), plan-2 section 4.10, plan-3 section 4.11, plan-4 section 4.8, plan-5 interpretation I21, plan-6 section 11.3.
Context: The pipeline writes tests before the implementation exists. Nothing stops the implementing agent from editing a test until it passes. The planners all decided the runner would prevent that mechanically.
Rationale: plan-5's I21 states both the rule and the escape: "Kept, byte-frozen from the gate's PASS commit through LANDING ... A wrong test is UPSTREAM to SPEC-TO-TESTS."
Spec conflict: None found. SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-OVERVIEW.txt says the code is written "until the frozen tests pass", which names the tests as frozen but does not say from when, until when, or what enforces it. The owner's files contain no other mention.
Other context: The sibling project's write-up listed this rule among its own failures - "Freeze rules interact badly with everything ... a defect found mid-round often cannot be fixed in that round" - which is what prompted JC-26. Review 5 later found that the owner's "until the frozen tests pass" is simply empty when SPEC-TO-TESTS is skipped, and ruled that the truthful empty frozen list beside it is the right answer (JC-379).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.7; plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 I21; plan-6 section 11.3; merged-A section 2.10; merged-B section 2.4.

## JC-26: Giving an agent somewhere to record a defect in something it may not change (from JC-PLAN-26)
Who and where: plan-6 section 11.6 (ERRATA.md), plan-5 interpretation I22, plan-2 risk 19 - against plans 1, 3 and 4, which provide nothing.
Context: Freezing the tests and hashing the spec means an agent that spots a real defect in either has no way to note it without either breaking the freeze or sending the whole round backwards.
Rationale: plan-6: "A producer that finds a defect in a frozen input it may not change (a spec sentence, a plan item) records it there and proceeds on the stated reading; the gate sees `round.errata`; POSTMORTEM reads it. This replaces vision_harness's 'hash everything, fix nothing in-round' trap without loosening the freeze."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files neither create the trap nor solve it; the freeze itself was the planners' invention (JC-25), so the escape hatch is a second-order choice.
Other context: merged-A kept it as a round file; merged-B cut it in decision 26 with a one-line reason - "ERRATA.md (the owner's `UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md` already serves that purpose)" - and the final plan confirmed the cut in amendment 10. So the owner's own judgment-call file ended up carrying this load, which is what two test rounds then did with it (JC-338, JC-356).
Sources: plan-2 sections 4.6 and 9.19; plan-5 I22; plan-6 section 11.6; merged-A decisions 16 and 24; merged-B decision 26; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-27: Making the spec declare the paths and the verify command (from JC-PLAN-27)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.7, plan-2 Appendix B, plan-3 section 4.6, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.5, plan-6 section 9.
Context: AGENTS.md speaks of "your step's declared paths" without saying who declares them or where. The planners all put them in the round's own spec, produced by the PLAN-TO-SPEC step, along with the command that proves the round works.
Rationale: Every plan defines the same two fields and one rule: the implementation paths and the test paths must not overlap. plan-3: "Rules: paths inside R; implPaths/testPaths prefix-disjoint". plan-5 adds that they may not point at the round folder, the locked prose or a spec file.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md refers to declared paths as if they already exist: "Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths plus the round folder." project.yaml supplies `verifyTimeoutSeconds` without ever saying what is being timed.
Other context: This is the hinge between the owner's economics and the runner's rails: the declared paths bound the confinement check, feed the cross-round overlap warning (JC-58) and determine which files the living charge is measured over. Four spec agents in the real rounds then used the granularity of those paths to enforce a plan non-goal the runner cannot see (JC-194).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.7; plan-2 Appendix B; plan-3 section 4.6; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.5; plan-6 section 9; merged-A section 2.6; merged-B section 2.6.

## JC-28: Checking that the plan's non-goals are carried into the spec, word for word (from JC-PLAN-28)
Who and where: plan-3 alone, section 4.11, check M5.
Context: PLAN-TO-SPEC is told to carry the plan's non-goals into the spec. plan-3 decided the runner would verify it by string comparison rather than leave it to the gate.
Rationale: Not argued. plan-3 lists the check as "M5 | PLAN-TO-SPEC | a plan `non_goals` string missing verbatim from `nonGoals`". Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a non-goal dropped between plan and spec is the kind of silent scope creep the harness exists to catch, and comparing two strings the agents wrote is cheap - but it does sit awkwardly beside the same plan's rule that the runner never compares prose.
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says only "Carry the plan's non-goals in the spec." It does not say whether "carry" means verbatim or in substance.
Other context: Neither merge adopted the verbatim check; both left the non-goals to the gate and the schema. This is one of the few plan-level calls that neither merge picked up.
Sources: plan-3 sections 4.5 and 4.11.

## JC-29: How the suite selection is actually carried out (from JC-PLAN-29)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.4, plan-2 section 4.10, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-4 section 4.3, plan-5 section 5.5, plan-6 section 11.2.
Context: The owner says the runner moves archived tests into the round folder. Nobody says how the decision reaches the runner, when the move happens, or what happens if two archived files share a name.
Rationale: All six settled on the same mechanism: TESTS-TO-SUITE writes a keep/archive partition of exactly the test files this round changed; the runner applies it with `git mv` once the decision is accepted. plan-3 adds the collision rule: "basename; collisions get a numeric suffix". plan-2 adds a re-entry shortcut: if the same partition is already recorded, the step auto-passes rather than being paid for twice.
Spec conflict: project.yaml gives the destination and the actor only - "testsArchive: 'tests-archive/' # tests kept with the round, moved by the runner" - and TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt gives the judgment without a shape: "Choose where each test goes: In the permanent suite, or in the round archive?"
Other context: The same prose file asks the producer to "flag it" when there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner; the planners gave that a field in the artifact but no route, and a later fix commit, 403f237, closed the gap (JC-263).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.3; plan-5 section 5.5; plan-6 section 11.2; merged-A section 2.10; merged-B section 2.4.

## JC-30: Whether the refactor cap is enforced or only shown (from JC-PLAN-30)
Who and where: plan-2 (M4), plan-3 (M4), plan-4 (S3) and plan-5 enforce it; plan-6 section 5.2 deliberately does not; plan-1 does not check it.
Context: project.yaml caps refactoring at a fraction of a round's budget. The runner can either refuse work that exceeds it or simply tell the agents the number and let the gates judge.
Rationale: plan-6 argues for showing only: "`maxRefactorOverhead` (rendered; not enforced, the spec says it limits scope)". plan-3 enforces it against a declared figure instead: "M4 | PLAN-TO-SPEC | Sum of `refactor[].budget_usd` > maxRefactorOverhead x budget_usd".
Spec conflict: project.yaml describes the cap in words that point both ways: "# Percent of budget cap allowed for refactoring work during a round. # This cap is not reduced by gains; it acts as a limit on scope." COMMON-PROJECT.txt repeats it to the agents as a target: "Refactoring in a round is encouraged, capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget; the cap limits scope, and gains do not change it."
Other context: The merges took the enforced-against-a-declared-figure route (merged-B interpretation 10 states the limit honestly: "`maxRefactorOverhead` is enforced only on the SPEC's declared `refactor` budgets; the gate judges the rest"). That is a partial enforcement: an agent that refactors without declaring it is caught only by a gate. The implemented check is JC-111.
Sources: plan-2 section 4.10; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.5; plan-6 section 5.2; merged-A section 2.6; merged-B interpretation 10.

## JC-31: Undoing a commit an agent was not supposed to make (from JC-PLAN-31)
Who and where: plan-4 section 4.7, plan-5 section 5.7 and plan-6 section 11.1 - not in plans 1, 2 or 3.
Context: Agents are told never to commit. If one does anyway, the runner's own accounting of what changed during an attempt is wrong. The three later plans added a check that detects it and quietly undoes it.
Rationale: plan-4: "HEAD fence: `HEAD != pre_head` -> `git reset --soft <pre_head>` and FLAG 'producer committed; undone softly'." The soft reset keeps the agent's work and only removes the commit.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md states the rule that is being broken - "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner." - but says nothing about what to do when an agent breaks it.
Other context: Both merges adopted it. It is an example of a recurring pattern in this slice: taking a rule the owner addressed to agents and building a mechanical backstop for it rather than trusting the instruction. What the check compares against had to be changed once the runner's own commits were taken into account (JC-151).
Sources: plan-4 sections 3 item 6 and 4.7; plan-5 section 5.7; plan-6 section 11.1; merged-A section 2.10; merged-B section 2.10.

## JC-32: How a gate is actually kept read-only (from JC-PLAN-32)
Who and where: plan-1 and plan-2 rely on a read-only agent type; plan-3 adds a check that discards a gate's edits; plans 4, 5 and 6 write dedicated agent definition files.
Context: AGENTS.md requires gates to be read-only. Nothing in the owner's files says how that is achieved with the tools available, and the three mechanisms have different strengths.
Rationale: plan-4 chose definitions: ".claude/agents/shackles-gate.md with a tool allowlist for driver mode; `--tools Read,Grep,Glob` in headless mode". plan-3 chose a backstop instead: "G1 | any gate | the gate's run changed tracked files or left untracked ones (besides RESULT_FILE)", discarded as an infrastructure error. plan-2 hedges: "gates: a read-only agent type such as `Explore`, or an explicit READ-ONLY instruction".
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md gives the requirement in one parenthesis - "give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent (read-only for gates)" - with no mechanism.
Other context: Both merges took all three at once: definitions, tool flags in headless mode, and the discard check as a backstop. The definitions later became per-rung and generated (JC-89). In the real tests the definitions were never available to the drivers, so only the prompt and the discard check kept a gate read-only (JC-213).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.12; plan-2 section 4.13; plan-3 section 4.11; plan-4 sections 3 item 7 and 4.1; plan-5 section 5.13; plan-6 section 15.4; merged-A decision 15; merged-B decision 21.

## JC-33: Turning the owner's per-word prices into real ledger charges
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.6, plan-2 section 4.11, plan-3 section 4.12, plan-4 section 4.9, plan-5 interpretation I7, plan-6 section 12.4; implemented by the implementer in `round.start`, `round.accept_producer` and the `words_charged` flags, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner's prose tells the planning agent to assume a price per word of plan, and the spec agent a price per word of spec. That is a signal to the writing agent. The planners additionally decided the runner would count the words and book the money, and had to decide whether every attempt re-charges.
Rationale: plan-5's I7 is the most explicit: "Booked to the owner bucket as net deltas: at approval, words of the rendered plan text times `planCostPerWord`; at each PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE PASS (or skip), words of `SPEC.md` plus `SPEC.json.summary` times `specCostPerWord`, minus what was already charged for the spec this round." merged-B fixed that the word charges happen "once each": at `start` for the plan and at PLAN-TO-SPEC acceptance for the spec body.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt addresses the agent, not the runner: "Assume a cost of ${{ project.planCostPerWord }} per word in the plan, and the plan needs to read as plain English." project.yaml's comment is equally silent on who books it: "planCostPerWord: 0.1 # per word in a plan". The prose says "Assume a cost of ... per word in the spec" without saying per attempt or per round.
Other context: The planners also had to define "word"; all settled on whitespace-separated tokens. The implementer added an explicit `words_charged` flag in STATE so a second acceptance (after an out-of-band edit or an owner answer) does not re-charge. Test A's reviewer left the remaining ambiguity for the owner in CLARIFICATIONS.md: whether the spec charge is "the owner's reading time (skip it when delegated) or the standing weight of a contract (charge always)". Spec agents in three rounds then optimised against the price by moving detail into the uncharged machine-readable half (JC-230, JC-289, JC-355).
Sources: plan-1 sections 5.6 and 5.8; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 I7; plan-6 section 12.4; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B section 2.11; `round.start`, `round.accept_producer`; `ledger.word_cost`; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-33, JC-CODE-38

## JC-34: Reading "flat, per turn" as once per checkpoint raised (from JC-PLAN-34)
Who and where: all six planners - most explicitly plan-5 interpretation I6 and plan-3 section 4.12.
Context: project.yaml prices the owner's interruption "per turn" without defining a turn. It could be each owner message, each pause, or each round trip.
Rationale: plan-5's I6: "`costToWaitForOwner` is booked once per checkpoint raised (owner bucket)." No planner argued the alternative.
Spec conflict: project.yaml: "costToWaitForOwner: 5 # flat, per turn". "Turn" appears nowhere else in the owner's files.
Other context: The reading has a visible consequence: a checkpoint where the owner sends three messages costs 5, not 15. Both merges kept it, and both list the owner-cost model among the interpretations to confirm with the owner.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.6; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 I6; plan-6 section 12.4; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B section 2.11.

## JC-35: Whether to charge the owner's hourly rate at all, and what to assume they spend
Who and where: split among the planners - plan-1 (12 minutes per checkpoint), plan-3 (0.2 hours), plan-4 (0.1 hours) and plan-2 (a per-resume `--owner-minutes` figure) charge it; plan-5 (I6) and plan-6 (section 12.4) decline to charge it. Settled by merged-B at ten minutes and implemented by the implementer as `ownerMinutesPerCheckpoint: 10` in `config.py` and `ledger.owner_checkpoint_cost`, commit 05509b7.
Context: project.yaml prices an hour of the owner's attention but gives no way to know how many hours a checkpoint costs. To use the number at all, someone has to invent an estimate.
Rationale: plan-5 declines and says why: "`ownerHourlyRate` is not booked by the runner (no decision-time estimate exists in `project.yaml`); it is exposed to prose and to the postmortem, which also receives measured owner wait hours per checkpoint." plan-3 charges and names the invented key: "priced with `plumbing.ownerHoursPerCheckpoint = 0.2`. Recommendation: keep; the owner may add `ownerHoursPerCheckpoint` to project.yaml to override." merged-B section 5 item 3 settles it: "`ownerHourlyRate` has no attention estimate in `project.yaml`; the runner uses `ownerMinutesPerCheckpoint: 10`, which the owner may set in `project.yaml`."
Spec conflict: project.yaml: "ownerHourlyRate: 10 # per hour of the owner's attention", with no attention estimate anywhere in the owner's files.
Other context: Both merges chose to charge with an invented default (merged-A 0.1 hours, merged-B 10 minutes) and both flagged it to the owner as an interpretation, offering the key for adoption into project.yaml. The two halves of this call are separable - whether to charge, and what number to use - but the second only arises if the first is yes. The gap was written up for the owner in CLARIFICATIONS.md: "ownerHourlyRate is priced with ownerMinutesPerCheckpoint (10 by default); set it in project.yaml if another estimate fits."
Sources: plan-1 sections 5.2 and 5.6; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 sections 4.3 and 9.3; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 I6; plan-6 sections 12.4 and 23.6; merged-A decision 11 and risk 2; merged-B decision 19, interpretation 3, sections 2.3, 2.11 and 5.3; `config.py` `DEFAULTS`; `ledger.owner_checkpoint_cost`; `round.raise_checkpoint`; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-35, JC-CODE-2

## JC-36: The budget-share formula, and reading "minimum" shares as fallbacks
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.8, plan-2 section 4.11, plan-3 section 4.12, plan-4 section 4.9, plan-5 section 5.9, plan-6 section 13.2; implemented by the implementer in `ledger.shares`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner supplies two fractions that split a round's quote between work and gates, and a short table of shares for two steps headed "Minimum per-stage budgets." Turning that into a dollar figure for each of sixteen steps needs a formula the owner did not write, and somebody had to decide whether an agents plan that allots less than a stated minimum is refused, raised, or accepted.
Rationale: All six use the same shape: a producer gets quote x workFraction x its share, a gate gets quote x gatesFraction x its producer's share, with the shares coming from the round's agent plan if it exists and from `defaultShares` otherwise. merged-A adds the rule for steps nobody assigned: "an equal split of the unassigned remainder". merged-B 2.11 describes the precedence only - "shares from AGENTS-PLAN.json once accepted, else `defaultShares`, else an equal split of the remaining fraction over unassigned steps" - so the plan's shares replace the owner's floors wholesale. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for leaving the floors unenforced: the runner is not supposed to arbitrate a budget the gate is told to judge, and PLAN-AGENTS-GATE.txt already says "Small imbalances are non-blocking findings."
Spec conflict: project.yaml labels the table "# Minimum per-stage budgets." but supplies only two of the eight producers, and gives the fractions with a bare comment: "gatesFraction: 0.3 # sums to 1 with workFraction". The planners read a table of minimums as a table of defaults - a different thing, since a minimum is a floor that should survive the agent plan - and nothing in the runner enforces the floors.
Other context: The decision is "tell the agent, do not enforce": the floors do reach the planning agent as text, and a plan that ignores them is accepted by the schema check and can only be caught by a gate. The word "minimum" came back later: fix commit 9edb5ca puts the owner's floors, filtered to the steps that actually run, in front of the agent that sets the shares (JC-262).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.8; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 section 5.9; plan-6 section 13.2; merged-A section 2.11; merged-B section 2.11; `ledger.shares`; `checks.s1_agents_plan`; `prompts.step_context`; project.yaml `defaultShares`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-36, JC-CODE-34

## JC-37: What the gate is told a retry costs
Who and where: all six planners, with small differences - plan-1 ("the producer's per-run budget"), plan-2 ("producer share + driverUsdPerStep"), plan-4 ("budget_for(producer) + spawnCost + driverUsdPerStep"), plan-6 ("its producer's budget"), merged-B ("the producer's budget + spawnCost + driverUsdPerStep"); implemented by the implementer in `ledger.retry_cost`, commit 05509b7, and refined by the fix-3 fixer in commit 5acf17a.
Context: The owner's gate prose puts a number in front of the gate and asks it to weigh a defect against that number. The number is a template token the runner must fill. What counts as the cost of a retry is not obvious: the producer's run alone, or the run plus the overheads.
Rationale: Not argued by any planner; each simply defines it. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the figure exists to stop a gate failing an artifact over something trivial, so it should be the money a FAIL actually costs - which argues for including the spawn and driver overheads, as the later plans and merged-B do. The fix-3 fixer's correction is recorded as "a gate's prompt prices the producer's retry with the producer's spawnCost, so the two prompts agree".
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt leans on the number without defining it: "A FAIL costs a retry of the producing step (${{ step.retry_cost }}) plus this gate again: FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that". Note the prose adds "plus this gate again" itself, so the token must not double-count the gate. `subAgents.yaml` gives a `spawnCost` per rung and `project.yaml` a `driverUsdPerStep`, but nothing composes them.
Other context: A gate's whole threshold moves with this definition, so the small differences between plans are behavioural, not cosmetic; the fixer's correction, that the spawn cost used is the producer's rung and not the gate's, makes the price a gate sees equal what a retry would actually book. Test D's postmortem measured the figure against what runs really cost ("$26.26 vs $2.34") and proposed pricing it from recorded spend; review 5 refused, twice over (JC-386).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.8; plan-2 section 4.5; plan-3 section 4.5; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 section 5.3; plan-6 section 13.2; merged-A section 2.11; merged-B section 2.11; `ledger.retry_cost`; `round.step_context`; `plumbing/gate.txt`; commit 5acf17a.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-37, JC-CODE-36

## JC-38: What "remaining" means when several rounds are running (from JC-PLAN-38)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.2, plan-2 section 4.5, plan-3 section 2.4, plan-4 section 4.4.1, plan-5 interpretation I11, plan-6 section 8.2.
Context: The owner's prose shows every agent the project's remaining budget. There is no such key in project.yaml, so the runner computes it - and has to decide whether rounds that are still running count against it.
Rationale: plan-4: "`remaining` = `budget` minus the sum of finished rounds' totals minus live sibling rounds' totals (best effort, from their branches' STATE.json, cached per `next`)". plan-5's I11 adds a guard: computed keys are "added only when `project.yaml` lacks the key, never shadowing an owner value".
Spec conflict: COMMON-PROJECT.txt renders it - "Budget, dollars, a project target, not enforced: total {{ project.budget }}, remaining {{ project.remaining }}." - while project.yaml defines `budget` and nothing called `remaining`.
Other context: Counting live siblings is the interesting half: it means an agent in one round sees money another round has not finished spending. All six chose that reading, and both merges kept it.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.5; plan-3 section 2.4; plan-4 section 4.4.1; plan-5 I11; plan-6 section 8.2; merged-A section 2.5; merged-B section 2.3.

## JC-39: How a run's cost is established, and what the fallback guess is
Who and where: all six planners agree on the order of preference and split on the fallback - plans 1, 3 and 6 fall back to the roster's spawn cost; plans 2 and 4 fall back to the step's budget. Implemented by the implementer in `ledger.agent_cost`, commit 05509b7.
Context: In the primary operating mode the driver may not learn what a sub-agent's run cost. The ledger still needs a number, and the number feeds the hard stop and every spend figure a prompt shows.
Rationale: merged-A rejects the spawn-cost fallback with the consequence spelled out: "`spawnCost` as the estimate (plans 1, 3, 6; under-reports to near zero and blinds the hard stop)". plan-2's reasoning for the alternative is that an unmeasured run probably cost roughly what it was budgeted. merged-B decision 19 gives the chain the code follows: "`--cost` ... else `--tokens N` priced ... plus `spawnCost x spawns` ... else the step budget (source `agent-estimate`, flagged). Never below `spawnCost`."
Spec conflict: None found. subAgents.yaml supplies per-token prices and a spawn-cost floor - "spawnCost is USD, estimated: the floor for one spawn" - and project.yaml supplies `estOutputFraction` for pricing a bare token count, but nothing says what to record when nothing at all is reported.
Other context: Both merges chose the step budget and required that every entry be at least the spawn cost, so an unmeasured run is over-stated rather than invisible. Both also require the estimate to be flagged in the round's history; the implementation carries the note "no cost reported; the step budget is booked as an estimate", and TODO.md records the limitation for the owner: "Driver-mode costs are estimates unless the Agent tool reports tokens; headless mode books exact costs."
Sources: plan-1 section 5.8; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 section 5.9; plan-6 section 13.1; merged-A decision 11; merged-B decision 19 and section 2.11; `ledger.agent_cost`, `ledger.price_tokens`; `round.record`; harness/docs/TODO.md.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-39, JC-CODE-37

## JC-40: A flat driver charge versus measuring the driver's own transcript (from JC-PLAN-40)
Who and where: plan-4 section 4.9 measures it; plans 1, 2, 3, 5 and 6 charge the flat figure.
Context: The session that drives a round also costs money. project.yaml offers a flat per-step figure. plan-4 argued the real figure is available from the session's transcript and built a cursor to read it.
Rationale: plan-4 designed around the sibling's known bug: "the cursor is advanced in DRIVER.json (session-global), so two rounds driven by one session never book the same turn twice. No transcript -> `driverUsdPerStep` flat, never a $0 entry." The others declined; plan-3: "Deliberately excluded: transcript-based driver cost measurement (a flat per-step charge is what the owner's config expresses)."
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml states the flat figure - "driverUsdPerStep: 1.0 # runner overhead per step" - which permits but does not require a better measurement.
Other context: Both merges rejected the measurement, merged-A citing "the sibling's double-counting mess, and the owner configured a flat figure", and both seeded it as a deferred item. This is one of the clearest cases in the slice of a single planner going further than the others and being overruled on grounds of risk rather than value.
Sources: plan-1 section 3 (deferred list); plan-2 section 3; plan-3 section 3; plan-4 sections 3 item 9 and 4.9; plan-5 section 4; plan-6 section 17; merged-A decision 11; merged-B decision 19.

## JC-41: When the living-token charge is computed and against what
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.8, plan-2 section 4.11, plan-3 section 4.12, plan-4 section 4.9, plan-5 interpretation I8, plan-6 section 13.4; implemented by the implementer in `ledger.living_charge`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner prices every token of living code and prose, charged on creation and refunded on removal. When to compute it is open: after every step, or once at the end. Against what is also open: the commit the round started from, or the state of the main branch at the moment the round joins it. Nothing says how a token is counted beyond "a token is {{ project.tokenBytes }} bytes".
Rationale: All six charge once, at landing, against the tip actually merged. The reason is inherited from the sibling's invariant list, which plan-1 restates: the charge is "priced against the tip actually merged - so a round is never charged for a sibling's diff." merged-B 2.11 gives the whole formula the code follows, including the per-file price function, the base cost added for a new file and refunded for a deleted one, `testBaseCost` per new test function, and the exclusion of `__pycache__` and `*.pyc`.
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml gives the prices and the rule - "Living tokens are charged when created, refunded on removal, and count towards both prose and code." - with no timing, and sets "tokenBytes: 4 # estimated bytes per token" without ever saying the charge is a delta between two commits.
Other context: The consequence is that the cost of a round is unknown until it lands, which is why several plans added a preview command. plan-6 also flagged the size of the number to the owner: "at $0.25 per token, a 200-line test file is ~$800 in the ledger ... quotes will be dominated by living tokens." Two implementer details ride along: the diff is taken with `--no-renames`, so a renamed file is charged as a deletion plus an addition, and new test functions are counted in every changed file under `testPaths` rather than only in files the suite step kept. The refund half of the same formula is what paid an agent to delete the project's only test file in two rounds (JC-199).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.8; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 I8; plan-6 sections 13.4 and 23.9; merged-A section 2.11; merged-B section 2.11; `ledger.living_charge`, `ledger._tokens`, `ledger._price`; `landing.land`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 121-122.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-41, JC-CODE-39

## JC-42: How "a new suite test" is counted (from JC-PLAN-42)
Who and where: all six planners, each with a regular expression - plan-1 `testFunctionPattern`, plan-4 `testPatterns` per file extension, plan-5, plan-6 and the merges the same.
Context: project.yaml charges a fixed amount per new test in the permanent suite. Counting tests requires deciding what a test is, in a project whose language the harness does not know.
Rationale: plan-4 states the compromise and its limit: "`testBaseCost` counting by regex per extension is an approximation; recommend it, with a documented `testPatterns` override; an exact pytest collection count is a later TODO."
Spec conflict: project.yaml: "testBaseCost: 3 # per new suite test", with no definition of a test.
Other context: All six chose a regular expression over running the test framework, and made it configurable. The count only applies to tests that stay in the permanent suite, which ties this entry to JC-29 and JC-41. The price per test is also what several spec agents weighed when deciding how many test functions to write (JC-196, JC-231).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.3; plan-4 section 4.2 and risk 4; plan-5 section 5.2; plan-6 section 13.4; merged-A section 2.11; merged-B section 2.11.

## JC-43: How the hard stop behaves once it fires (from JC-PLAN-43)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.8, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.10, plan-4 section 4.9, plan-5 section 5.9, plan-6 section 11.4.
Context: project.yaml sets a multiple of the round's budget at which a round stops. Whether that is a hard halt or a pause the owner can lift, and how often it may fire, is not stated.
Rationale: All six made it a checkpoint raised once per round that the owner can clear, not a termination. plan-1: "`total > hardStopBudgetMultiple x budget_usd` -> checkpoint `hard-stop` once per round, evaluated on every entry and before rendering, never while a checkpoint is being raised."
Spec conflict: project.yaml's comment is terse and ungrammatical: "hardStopBudgetMultiple: 6 # multiple of a round's budget at which in a round stops". COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt pulls the other way for agents - "Budgets are targets, not gates" - which is why the planners made the stop a pause rather than a wall.
Other context: The "once per round" rule and the rule against raising it while another checkpoint is being raised are both invented; without them a round over budget would stop at every subsequent ledger entry. Review 2 later worked out that a realistic first task would hit the stop before landing and told the next phase to quote generously (JC-173).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.8; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 section 5.9; plan-6 section 11.4; merged-A section 2.11; merged-B section 2.11.

## JC-44: What "maximum gate rejections before a round should stop" counts (from JC-PLAN-44)
Who and where: per-producer in plans 1, 3, 4 and 5; round-wide in plans 2 and 6.
Context: The owner sets a limit of three. Counting failures per step and counting them across the whole round give very different rounds: three failures spread over three steps is normal in the first reading and fatal in the second.
Rationale: merged-A gives the argument that settled it: "Rejected: a round-wide FAIL total (plans 2, 6) because `PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW` says to expect one or two rejections per gated step." plan-6 had reasoned from the comment instead, reading "before a round should stop" literally as a round total.
Spec conflict: Two owner files point opposite ways. project.yaml: "maxFailuresBeforeStop: 3 # maximum gate rejections before a round should stop" - the word "round" supports the total. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt: "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them." - which makes three per round impossible to survive.
Other context: Both merges chose per-producer and both flagged the reading to the owner as an interpretation rather than a fact; it is one of the four interpretations the bootstrap wrote into CLARIFICATIONS.md (JC-128). This is the clearest instance in the planning slice of two of the owner's own files disagreeing.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.4; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.9; plan-5 I5; plan-6 section 11.4; merged-A decision 12; merged-B decision 18 and interpretation 1.

## JC-45: What "maximum round retries" counts (from JC-PLAN-45)
Who and where: re-entries to an earlier step in plans 1, 2, 3 and 4; owner resumes of a stop checkpoint in plans 5 and 6.
Context: The owner limits a round to two "retries" without saying what a retry is. Counting trips backwards through the pipeline and counting times the owner has restarted a stalled round are different meters with different failure modes.
