Rationale: plan-3 gives the reasoning for the re-entry reading and the consequence of the alternative: "read as the number of re-entries to an earlier step (UPSTREAM, out-of-band edit, landing failure) before a `round-limit` checkpoint, not total step attempts (which would stop every round at once)." plan-5's I5 takes the other reading: "`round_attempts` counts owner resumes of `failure-limit` and `round-limit` checkpoints".
Spec conflict: project.yaml: "maxRoundAttempts: 2 # maximum round retries before a runner should stop". Nothing else in the owner's files uses the word "retry" of a round.
Other context: Both merges chose the re-entry reading and flagged it. The choice interacts with JC-21 and JC-55: an UPSTREAM and a landing conflict both consume one of the two, so a round that hits both is at its limit. In test D it shaped an owner's answer: insisting on the ending the round had settled would have cost one of the round's two lives (JC-360).
Sources: plan-1 risk 3; plan-2 section 4.11 and risk 9; plan-3 sections 4.10 and 9.2; plan-4 section 4.7 and risk 3; plan-5 I5; plan-6 section 11.4 and risk 23.5; merged-A decision 12; merged-B decision 18 and interpretation 1.

## JC-46: Treating the turn limit as advice (from JC-PLAN-46)
Who and where: plan-5 interpretation I13 and plan-6 section 15.1 state it as a decision; plans 1, 2 and 4 had put a turn flag in the agent command.
Context: project.yaml caps turns per run. Whether the runner can enforce that depends on the tool it spawns agents with, and the planners' probes of the installed command-line tool disagreed about whether a turn flag existed.
Rationale: plan-5's I13: "Advisory (stated in the prompt); the CLI in use has no `--max-turns`. Enforced caps are `--max-budget-usd` and the wall clock `maxRunWallClockHours`."
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml gives the number - "maxTurnsPerRun: 120" - without saying it must be enforced; COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt refers to turns as a real limit ("BLOCKED when you cannot finish within budget or turns"), which is what makes leaving it unenforced a choice rather than an oversight.
Other context: merged-B settled the factual dispute in its own section 0 - "Plans 1, 2 and 4 put `--max-turns` in their agent command; plans 5 and 6 were right" - and both merges made the number advisory prompt text. The decision that remains a judgment call is telling the agent a limit the harness cannot enforce, rather than dropping it or substituting an enforceable one.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.13; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 I13; plan-6 section 15.1; merged-A decision 18; merged-B section 0 and interpretation 9.

## JC-47: Leaving the sub-agent cap to instruction (from JC-PLAN-47)
Who and where: all six planners - most explicitly plan-2 section 4.11 and plan-5 interpretation I14.
Context: The owner caps how many sub-agents a round may run at once. Nothing the runner controls can count them, because they are spawned by an agent inside its own session.
Rationale: plan-2: "`maxSimultaneousSubAgentsPerRound` - AGENTS-PLAN validation and a MUST-NOT line; enforcement by instruction." plan-5's I14 adds where the number is shown: "the runner prints `subagents_allowed` in the action and in the prompt; the driver enforces it."
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml sets "maxSimultaneousSubAgentsPerRound: 2" with no enforcement mechanism available to the runner.
Other context: The planners did what they could: the number is validated in the round's agent plan and printed in every prompt. It is an honest admission that one of the owner's limits is a request rather than a rail. In practice every real round's planner granted no sub-agents at all (JC-222).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 4.6; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 I14; plan-6 section 5.2; merged-A section 2.5; merged-B section 2.3.

## JC-48: Building a channel for the owner's words at all (from JC-PLAN-48)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.6, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.14, plan-4 section 4.12, plan-5 section 5.11, plan-6 section 12.3.
Context: The owner talks to the harness in a chat session. The runner is a command-line program that never sees that chat. Some bridge had to exist, and the owner's files do not name one.
Rationale: All six adopted the sibling's mechanism: a chat hook that appends every owner message, with a timestamp, to a file the runner can read. plan-3 describes it and its constraint: the hook "never raises (a failing hook would block the owner's chat)"; and "Only `next` reads outside the worktree, and only this file."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says the runner is the only party that contacts the owner - "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner." - without saying how the owner reaches it.
Other context: The file is deliberately kept out of version control and read from the main checkout even by a round working in a separate worktree, which is the single exception to the rule that a round touches nothing outside its own working directory. SPEC.md later records the mechanism: "A prompt hook logs the owner's chat to a gitignored OWNER.log; the runner verifies quotes against it." In every sandbox test no such log existed, so every owner word was recorded unverified (JC-121).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.6; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.14; plan-4 section 4.12; plan-5 section 5.11; plan-6 section 12.3; merged-A section 2.13; merged-B section 2.14.

## JC-49: Who reads the owner's words (from JC-PLAN-49)
Who and where: the runner, by regular expression, in plans 1, 3, 4, 5 and 6; the driver, in plan-2. plan-6 added a verification step that both merges then built on.
Context: The owner's words carry authority: they approve a round, delegate it, skip steps or end it. Someone must decide that a given sentence means "approved". Either the runner pattern-matches the log, or the chat agent judges and tells the runner what it heard.
Rationale: plan-2 assigns it to the driver: "The driver interprets them (its judgment call under the prose) and issues commands with `--quote`; the runner stores the quote." merged-B's rejection of the alternative is the fullest statement of the reasoning: "The word list lives in locked prose, the prose requires 'unambiguous conversational context' (a judgment call), and a renamed word would silently hang or phantom-approve a round." merged-A adds the corroborating history: "the sibling's phantom approval came from exactly that mechanism". Both merges kept plan-6's check: the runner refuses a quote that does not appear in the owner's log after the relevant time.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt lists the words and then makes recognising them a matter of judgment: "Words that count, needed for both plan and quote; needed with unambiguous conversational context". A regular expression cannot weigh conversational context; and because the word list lives in a file the owner edits freely, a runner that matches on it depends on wording - which is the line drawn in JC-1.
Other context: This is the decision that pulled the runner furthest out of the prose. SPEC.md later records it: "The driver interprets owner words and hands the runner verbatim quotes; the runner never parses prose." Both merges also noted the residual honesty problem - merged-A risk 8: "Quote verification is an honesty check, not security". The mechanics of the check itself are JC-120.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.6; plan-2 sections 4.9 and 9.3; plan-3 section 4.10; plan-4 section 4.12; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 12.2; merged-A decision 1; merged-B decision 2; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-50: Which lines in the log count as the owner speaking (from JC-PLAN-50)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-3 section 4.14, plan-4 section 4.12, plan-5 interpretation I19, plan-6 section 12.1.
Context: The log collects every prompt typed into a session in that repository, which includes system-generated envelopes and messages from before the plan existed. Not all of it is the owner deciding something.
Rationale: Two filters, each with its own reason. The time window is the one with recorded history - plan-5's I19 ties it to a real failure: the slice starts at "min(previous round's landed_at or abandoned_at, PLAN.json.presented_at)", which "fixes the sibling's phantom approval", and approving words "count only after `presented_at`". The envelope filter is listed rather than argued; plan-3 gives the list, skipping messages that begin with markers such as "<system-reminder" or "<task-notification".
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files addresses which of the owner's messages count or when; the whole channel is the planners' invention (JC-48).
Other context: plan-6 added a third filter - suppressing the hook entirely when the environment marks the session as a sub-agent - so that an agent's own prompts can never be mistaken for the owner's. Both merges kept all three. The envelope list ended up duplicated in the hook, for a reason of its own (JC-122).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.6; plan-3 section 4.14; plan-4 section 4.12; plan-5 I19; plan-6 section 12.3; merged-A section 2.13; merged-B section 2.14.

## JC-51: Putting undefined judgment calls in front of the owner (from JC-PLAN-51)
Who and where: plan-1 (feature 9), plan-3 section 4.15, and taken further by the coordinating session in FINAL-PLAN amendment 8.
Context: The project exists to monitor the judgment calls agents make. The owner's files tell agents to write them into two files in the round folder, and stop there. Whether anyone ever reads those files is left open.
Rationale: plan-3 designed the surfacing: "Every checkpoint's `owner_message` ends with the counts and the absolute path of the UNDEFINED file when it grew since the last checkpoint: the owner sees undefined calls before deciding." plan-1 lists the same feature with its reason: "Judgment-call monitoring ... It is the project's stated purpose."
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md and COMMON-PROJECT.txt require the records to be written; nothing requires them to be shown to anyone, and POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt only asks the postmortem to look for "the agent judgment call that disambiguated the issue without the owner's realization" after the fact.
Other context: The coordinating session made this its eighth amendment and widened it: counts and the last five undefined lines "appear in every checkpoint message, in `status`, in the `done` JSON and in HISTORY per attempt", and it committed to a later test that "will fabricate a situation that forces an undefined judgment call and check that the harness reports it" - which became test F (JC-476). A delegated round raises no checkpoints, so the amendment left a hole that review 3 found and fix 3 closed (JC-256).
Sources: plan-1 section 3 item 9 and 5.6; plan-3 section 4.15; merged-A section 2.13; merged-B section 2.14; FINAL-PLAN amendment 8.

## JC-52: A gate cannot write, yet is told to record its judgment calls (from JC-PLAN-52)
Who and where: plan-3 section 4.15 and plan-5 section 5.4 identified it; both merges adopted the fix.
Context: Every step is told to append a line to the round's judgment-call files. Gates are required to be read-only. A read-only agent cannot append to a file.
Rationale: plan-3's solution: "gates are read-only and return `judgment_calls` in their verdict, which `record` appends with the suffix ` (via runner, <GATE>-<attempt>)`." merged-B states the conflict plainly in its list of tensions to flag: "Gates are read-only (AGENTS.md) yet COMMON-OVERVIEW and COMMON-PROJECT ask every step to append judgment calls to the round files: gates return them in the verdict JSON and the runner appends them."
Spec conflict: A direct contradiction between two of the owner's files. AGENTS.md: "give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent (read-only for gates)". COMMON-PROJECT.txt, which is included in every gate's prompt: "Record any DEFINED JUDGMENT CALL in your round's DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md and any UNDEFINED JUDGMENT CALL in UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md: one short line appended per call."
Other context: Both merges took the same fix and both listed it for the owner rather than treating it as settled; it is one of the four interpretations the bootstrap put into CLARIFICATIONS.md (JC-128). The suffix marking runner-appended lines means the audit trail distinguishes what the gate said from what the runner wrote on its behalf - and a gate in test B copied the suffix into its own line, producing it twice (JC-237).
Sources: plan-3 section 4.15; plan-5 section 5.4; merged-A decision 14; merged-B decision 20 and interpretation 2.

## JC-53: The judgment-call files may only grow (from JC-PLAN-53)
Who and where: plan-5 interpretation I15 and its check M4; adopted by both merges.
Context: The two files are the audit trail of what agents decided on their own. An agent that can append can also rewrite, and a rewritten trail is worse than none.
Rationale: plan-5: "Created empty at `start`; append-only (mechanical check M4); their line counts are logged per attempt in HISTORY, carried into `index.jsonl`, and both files are named as inputs to POSTMORTEM." merged-B's check is stated as "the judgment files only grew ... a rewrite is reverted and reported".
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md and COMMON-PROJECT.txt say a line is appended per call; neither says existing lines are protected.
Other context: Both merges kept it as a blocking check, and both count the per-attempt growth so the owner sees how many undefined calls each attempt produced. Fix 3 later added a comparison between those counts and what the producer's message claimed (JC-277).
Sources: plan-5 I15; merged-A section 2.10; merged-B section 2.10.

## JC-54: Taking the sibling project's concurrency design whole (from JC-PLAN-54)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.9, plan-2 section 4.12, plan-3 section 4.13, plan-4 section 4.10, plan-5 section 5.8, plan-6 section 14.
Context: The owner's files say nothing about running several rounds at once - no locking, no branches, no worktrees. A supplied write-up of the sibling project describes a complete design for it. The planners could have built something simpler for a single round at a time.
Rationale: plan-5: "Reused from the summary unchanged: `main_root`, the id claim ... the record push fence, `check` never pushing or charging, the landing check phase ... the timid fast-forward-only `sync_main`, prompts rendered from `prose_commit`." plan-3 states the principle it buys: "git is the lock manager", with no daemon, database or lock server.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files are silent on concurrency entirely; project.yaml's round folder layout allows many rounds but does not require them to run together.
Other context: Adopting the design brought its invariants with it, including the deliberate absences - no lease expiry and no takeover (JC-59). SPEC.md later lists it with its provenance marked: "Git is the only lock: a CAS push claims a round, every state change pushes the branch as a fence. [other: inherited]". The planners did not copy it uncritically: the sibling document lists nine of its own failures, and JC-55 to JC-57 are each a plan's answer to one of them. The write-up itself was the coordinating session's substitute for letting planners read the sibling repository (JC-442).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.9; plan-2 section 4.12; plan-3 section 4.13; plan-4 section 4.10; plan-5 section 5.8; plan-6 section 14; merged-A section 2.12; merged-B section 2.12.

## JC-55: How a landing conflict is made to converge
Who and where: all six planners diagnosed the sibling's worst failure and fixed it differently - plan-1 (a snapshot of every file), plan-2 (commit the conflict markers), plan-3 (leave the tree conflicted, measure against the merged tip), plan-4 and plan-5 (measure against git's own auto-merged tree), plan-6 (stop and ask the owner to merge by hand). Implemented by the implementer in `landing.landing_check`, `round.route_landing_findings` and `round.prepare_attempt`, commit 05509b7.
Context: When a round's work collides with something that landed meanwhile, the sibling project's design sent the conflict back to the implementation step - where that step's own confinement and freeze checks rejected the only possible fix. It cost real money and could not resolve itself.
Rationale: plan-4's fix uses a git command that computes the merge without touching the working tree: "M1/M2 are measured as `git diff --name-only <automerge_tree> HEAD` (the hand edits) against write paths ... Byte-identical retries are impossible by construction because the merge base moved." plan-6 went the other way and made it the owner's problem: a "`landing-conflict`" checkpoint whose message is "Resolve by hand there ... then say approve; the runner never resolves conflicts." merged-B decision 9 settled it: "A landing conflict becomes one agent attempt measured against the auto-merge tree ... Rejected: a `landing-conflict` checkpoint asking the owner to hand-merge ...: resolving a merge is a judgment call, and a delegated round would stall; the hand-merge stays available for free because an already-merged tree makes LANDING's merge a no-op."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files describe neither landing nor conflicts. The conflicting requirements are all ones the planners themselves imposed: confinement, frozen tests, and no automatic merge resolution.
Other context: merged-A rejected plan-6's checkpoint in the same terms, quoting the invariant it would violate - "a conflict degrades to more agent work" - and kept the owner's hand merge as the exit of last resort. SPEC.md later records the outcome and how hard it was: "A landing conflict becomes one agent attempt on the conflicted files, judged against git's auto-merged tree. [hard]". The implementation includes the failure accounting: the conflict counts a failure against SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION and a round retry, so a round that keeps conflicting eventually reaches the owner rather than looping.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.9; plan-2 section 4.12 item 2; plan-3 section 4.13; plan-4 section 4.10; plan-5 section 5.8 item 3; plan-6 sections 14.4 and 14.5; merged-A decision 3; merged-B decision 9 and section 2.12; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10; `landing.landing_check`, `landing.merge_tree`; `round.route_landing_findings`, `round.prepare_attempt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 130.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-55, JC-CODE-74

## JC-56: How the main branch is advanced when a round lands
Who and where: plans 2, 4, 5 and 6 push the round's tip straight to the remote branch; plans 1 and 3 keep the sibling's approach of moving a local branch first. Implemented by the implementer in `landing.landing_target`, `landing.ff_local_main` and `landing.land`, commit 05509b7.
Context: Moving a local branch fails if that branch is checked out anywhere, which makes it an unenforced precondition that fails late. Pushing the round's tip to the remote avoids the local branch entirely. The harness must also work in a single checkout with no remote, and on a machine where `main` is checked out somewhere and possibly dirty.
Rationale: merged-B states it: "Rejected: `git branch -f main` first (the summary, plan 1): it needs `main` parked, an unenforced precondition that failed late in the sibling." plan-2 adds what the owner then does: "the owner's checkout just `git pull --ff-only`s (the `done` action says so)." merged-B 2.12 gives the no-remote path the code follows: land "Without an origin: `git branch -f <main> HEAD` when not checked out; when the main checkout is on `<main>` and clean, `git -C <main_root> merge --ff-only <branch>`; otherwise an error naming the fix."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files do not mention branches, landing, a main branch or any repository layout.
Other context: Both merges chose the push, and both kept a local fallback for the case where there is no remote at all. This is one of the sibling's listed rough edges - "Unenforced preconditions that fail late" - being designed out rather than documented. The implementation carries the refusal text "landing: <main> is checked out and dirty at <path>; commit or stash there, then rerun next", and with an origin the local branch is only updated best-effort with a FLAG telling the owner to pull, so the round never depends on the state of a checkout it does not own.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.9; plan-2 section 4.12 item 1; plan-3 section 4.13; plan-4 section 4.10; plan-5 section 5.8 item 1; plan-6 section 14.4; merged-A decision 2; merged-B decision 10 and section 2.12; `landing.landing_target`, `landing.ff_local_main`, `landing.land`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 129.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-56, JC-CODE-78

## JC-57: Which commit a round is cut from (from JC-PLAN-57)
Who and where: plan-2 and plan-6 cut from the remote's main branch; plans 1, 3, 4 and 5 cut from whatever the checkout is sitting on.
Context: Starting a round from the local checkout's current position means a round can be built on top of unrelated local work. Starting from the remote's main branch means every round begins from the same shared baseline.
Rationale: plan-6 records the reason as a local rule: base is "`origin/<main>` (**changed**: not the checkout's HEAD, per the machine's own rule 'cut from origin, never from what the checkout sits on')".
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files addresses where a round begins.
Other context: Both merges took the remote baseline. It interacts with JC-56: if a round starts from the shared baseline and lands by pushing to it, the local main branch is never needed at all. It is also what made the guard of JC-153 necessary, since an owner editing the checkout is editing something the round will not see.
Sources: plan-2 section 4.12 item 1; plan-6 section 14.2; merged-A section 2.9 step 5; merged-B section 2.12.

## JC-58: Two rounds that claim the same files get a warning, not a refusal (from JC-PLAN-58)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.9, plan-2 section 4.12 item 6, plan-3 section 4.13, plan-4 section 4.8, plan-5 section 5.8 item 2, plan-6 section 14.6.
Context: Nothing stops two live rounds declaring the same source files; the collision only surfaces at landing, sometimes hours later. The planners could have refused the second round, queued it, or simply said something.
Rationale: All six chose a non-blocking warning read from the sibling rounds' own specs. plan-3: "any prefix overlap of implPaths/testPaths -> non-blocking finding naming the round and paths, carried to the gate". plan-1 explains the restraint: preventing it is listed as a non-invariant inherited from the sibling - "no cross-round path arbitration beyond a warning."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files do not contemplate two rounds at once.
Other context: Both merges kept the warning. The reasoning is that the landing merge already arbitrates correctly, so the warning exists to let a human or a spec agent avoid the collision, not to enforce anything. Review 2 later leaned on this warning when deciding a related finding could be deferred (JC-170).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.9; plan-2 section 4.12 item 6; plan-3 section 4.13; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.8 item 2; plan-6 section 14.6; merged-A section 2.12; merged-B section 2.10.

## JC-59: No expiry, no takeover of a stalled round (from JC-PLAN-59)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 3 (deferred), plan-2 section 3, plan-3 section 3, plan-4 section 3, plan-5 section 4, plan-6 section 17.
Context: A round that crashes keeps its claim forever. The alternative is a lease that expires, which lets another runner take over - and brings clock skew and the risk of two runners working the same round.
Rationale: plan-6: "a lease/TTL for dead claims (`rounds --prune` reports them; expiry reintroduces clock skew)". plan-3 lists it among deliberate exclusions: "lease/TTL/stale-holder takeover (the summary's non-invariant, kept)".
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files say nothing about recovery from a crashed round.
Other context: All six compensated the same way, with a reporting command that lists claimed-but-unused round ids so a human can see the debris. The sibling document had recommended exactly that: "a `prune` command reporting claimed-but-never-committed ids would help." The consequence for the liveness test is JC-118.
Sources: plan-1 section 3; plan-2 section 3; plan-3 section 3; plan-4 section 3; plan-5 section 4; plan-6 section 17; merged-A section 2.12; merged-B section 2.12.

## JC-60: Keeping a one-line-per-round record across rounds (from JC-PLAN-60)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.3, plan-2 section 4.8, plan-3 section 4.12, plan-4 section 4.6, plan-5 section 5.9, plan-6 section 6.
Context: A round needs to know what earlier rounds quoted and what they actually spent, and the project needs a total. The planners all added a file of one JSON line per finished round.
Rationale: Inherited from the sibling with its stated reason, which plan-1 repeats in its own words: appended per finished round on the round branch and merged into main by landing. The sibling document explains the format choice: "Append-only JSONL is deliberate: two rounds appending different lines merge cleanly, where a single JSON array would conflict every time."
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml names the round folder and its files; nothing names a cross-round record.
Other context: The inherited reason turned out to be wrong, and both merges said so - see JC-87. The record also feeds the quote-versus-actual figures shown to the planning agent in later rounds.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.3; plan-2 section 4.8; plan-3 section 4.12; plan-4 section 4.6; plan-5 section 5.9; plan-6 section 6; merged-A decision 4; merged-B decision 11.

## JC-61: Whether paths are counted from the harness folder or the repository root (from JC-PLAN-61)
Who and where: plans 3, 4, 5 and 6 use the harness folder; plans 1 and 2 use the repository root.
Context: The owner's config lists paths like "src/" and "docs/" with no base. Everything downstream - which files are charged, which files a step may touch, what a finding names - depends on the answer.
Rationale: plan-5's I1: "The harness root `harness/`. The repository root is `git rev-parse --show-toplevel`." plan-6 gives the textual evidence: "`AGENTS.md` is titled `# harness/` and says `src/`, `docs/`, `tests/` are living and `roundPaths` are 'from the harness root'". plan-3 adds a consequence it wanted: allowing "../" so that "a target project whose code lives beside `harness/` declares `../app/`".
Spec conflict: project.yaml's path block is bare - "livingSourcePaths: ['src/', 'docs/', 'tests/']" - and its round-path comment says "`folder` is that folder, from the harness root", which supports the harness reading for round paths but does not settle the living paths. AGENTS.md's heading "# harness/" is the strongest evidence and it is a heading, not a statement.
Other context: Both merges chose the harness folder and rejected the repository root (merged-A decision 21). The "../" allowance is what lets the harness manage a project that is not itself, which the owner's vision sentence calls for: "The harness is intended to work with general software development." The two conventions still crossed once in a real round, in a prompt that printed a path the wrong way round (JC-207).
Sources: plan-1 section 2; plan-2 section 4.3; plan-3 section 4.2; plan-4 section 4.1; plan-5 I1; plan-6 section 3; merged-A decision 21; merged-B section 2.1.

## JC-62: A defaults table so a new config key cannot break a running round
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.1, plan-2 rule R1, plan-3 section 4.3, plan-4 section 4.2, plan-5 section 5.2, plan-6 section 5.1; implemented by the implementer as the `DEFAULTS` mapping and `load()` in `config.py`, commit 7c4c6ea.
Context: The owner's config changes often. A runner that reads a key directly crashes when the key is absent; one that reads through a table of defaults keeps going and says so. The runner also needs about thirty settings of its own - branch name, retry counts, timeouts, the headless command line - that the owner never wrote down.
Rationale: plan-5 names the failure it prevents: "Every `project.*` key the runner reads goes through `cfg.project(key)` with a documented default in a `DEFAULTS` table (the sibling broke an in-flight round on a new key; here a missing key is a default plus a `lint` warning, never a crash)." merged-B 2.3 gives the reason in one line: "Every key the runner reads is in `DEFAULTS` with today's value from `project.yaml`, so a sibling landing a new key never breaks an in-flight round."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's header is the requirement: "Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them." It does not say how. The owner's project.yaml simply does not mention the runner's own settings, and its header says only "This file contains (and only contains) user settings".
Other context: The sibling document lists this exact failure - "A sibling landing a new required config key broke an in-flight round (`KeyError` on a key the older branch's config lacked)" - and recommends the fix. All six took it, and both merges made every default equal to the value the owner's file has today, so the table is a mirror rather than an opinion; deleting an owner key therefore changes nothing. The implementation copies merged-B's block essentially verbatim, adding `carryForwardFiles` and `worktreeDir`, and later dropping two keys nothing read (JC-141).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.1; plan-2 section 4.2; plan-3 section 4.3; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 section 5.2; plan-6 section 5.1; merged-A section 2.3; merged-B decision 4 and section 2.3; `config.py` `DEFAULTS`, `load`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md "Config keys"; FINAL-PLAN amendments 2 and 5.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-62, JC-CODE-1

## JC-63: Pinning the prose a round renders from (from JC-PLAN-63)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.5, plan-2 section 4.5, plan-3 section 4.3, plan-4 section 4.2, plan-5 section 5.3, plan-6 section 8.1.
Context: AGENTS.md forbids the prose from changing mid-round. The owner edits those files in a working tree that every round can see. Something had to make the rule true rather than merely stated.
Rationale: All six read the prose out of a fixed commit rather than off disk. plan-2: "Makes 'prose does not change mid-round' true mechanically." The sibling document had already found the same need for a different reason: "a sibling round landing new prose mid-round cannot change what this round's agents are told."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md states the rule without a mechanism: "Prose under `locked_prose/` does not change mid-round."
Other context: Both merges kept it. It is the clean case of the pattern in JC-31: an owner rule addressed to people, turned into something the code guarantees.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 section 4.5; plan-3 section 4.3; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 section 5.3; plan-6 section 8.1; merged-A section 2.3; merged-B decision 5.

## JC-64: Whether the round's configuration is pinned too (from JC-PLAN-64)
Who and where: plans 1, 3 and 4 pin the configuration as well as the prose; plan-6 pins only the prose; the two merges split the same way.
Context: The prose is frozen mid-round by the owner's own rule. Nothing says the numbers are. Pinning them makes a round fully reproducible; leaving them live lets the owner change a gate flag or a price while a round is in flight.
Rationale: merged-A pins everything: "a sibling landing a config key mid-round is the summary's failure #2, and a gate flip mid-round has an explicit tool (`override`)." merged-B leaves config live: "AGENTS.md freezes only prose, a live config lets the owner flip a gate in the worktree mid-round, and a sibling's landing cannot reach the worktree before LANDING anyway."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md names one thing and not the other: "Prose under `locked_prose/` does not change mid-round." project.yaml and subAgents.yaml are listed in spec.yaml alongside the prose, which is why several planners read them as equally frozen.
Other context: The final plan took merged-B, so the built runner reads prose from a pinned commit and configuration from the working tree. merged-B flagged the split as something a test must pin: "a test asserts the split."
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-3 section 4.3; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 section 5.2; plan-6 risk 23.13; merged-A decision 7; merged-B decision 5; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-65: Where the record of the owner's files lives and what it holds (from JC-PLAN-65)
Who and where: all six planners built one and put it in four different places - plan-1 at the harness root, plans 2, 3 and 4 under the test folder, plan-5 and plan-6 under the archives folder, plan-6 additionally keeping a copy of every file.
Context: To notice that an owner file changed, the harness must remember what it used to look like. Where that memory lives matters, because anything under the living paths is charged per token, and a folder of file copies duplicates what version control already holds.
Rationale: merged-A rejects two of the four with reasons: "the baseline under `tests/` (plans 2, 3, 4; living and charged), file copies beside the manifest (plan-6; git already has the bytes)". plan-5 chose the archives folder and added an append-only record of each acceptance, which merged-B kept as "an append-only `harness/archives/spec-changes.jsonl` line per acceptance".
Spec conflict: None found. The requirement that such a test exist came from the owner outside these files - every plan calls it a hard requirement - but nothing in the files states it, and nothing constrains its shape.
Other context: The merges split, and the coordinating session mixed them in its third amendment: "`harness/archives/spec-baseline.json` and `harness/archives/spec-changes.jsonl`. The test stays `harness/tests/test_spec_baseline.py` and reads the baseline by path." The built repository has exactly those files. Review 1 later noted that the location is pinned in one more place than the owner's config suggests (JC-158).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.10; plan-2 section 4.14; plan-3 section 4.16; plan-4 section 4.14; plan-5 section 5.10; plan-6 section 16; merged-A decision 8; merged-B decision 6; FINAL-PLAN amendment 3.

## JC-66: What a changed owner file actually stops (from JC-PLAN-66)
Who and where: all six planners differ in emphasis - plan-1 and plan-2 refuse to start a round, plan-5 also raises a checkpoint mid-round and at landing, plan-6 only warns during a round, plan-2 blocks at every step.
Context: A failing test tells whoever runs the suite. It does not stop a round that is already moving, and it does not stop a new round being started on a spec nobody has looked at. Each plan drew the line somewhere different.
Rationale: plan-1 refuses at the start of a round and explains why that is enough: "`start` refuses stale specs ... the suite run at landing cannot be red for this reason because `start` already gated it and the manifest lands with the round." plan-6 declines to block mid-round: "Warn if `spec check` drifts (`warnings`), never block." merged-B rejects plan-2's mid-round block with a reason: "`next` blocking on drift mid-round (2): the worktree's spec only changes when the round changes it."
Spec conflict: None found. The alert is the planners' construction (JC-65); the owner's requirement, as every plan reports it, was that a change be surfaced, not that it halt anything in particular.
Other context: Both merges settled on: the test fails, `start` refuses unless the change is accepted in the same command, and a drift discovered at landing raises a checkpoint rather than a red suite. merged-A rejected warn-only in the owner's terms: "the owner asked for an alert an agent must act on." A second, different guard on `start` was added later (JC-153).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.10; plan-2 section 4.14; plan-3 section 4.16; plan-4 section 4.14; plan-5 section 5.10; plan-6 section 16; merged-A decision 8; merged-B decision 7.

## JC-67: Ignoring line endings when hashing the owner's files
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.10 records the machine fact behind it; plan-2 rule R5; plans 3, 4, 5 and 6 the same. Implemented by the implementer in `procs.read_bytes_normalized` and `specguard.check`, commit 7c4c6ea.
Context: Windows checkouts can rewrite line endings on their own, and an editor can add a byte-order mark without changing a word. A hash over raw bytes then reports a change the owner never made, and the alert cries wolf.
Rationale: plan-1 found the cause while probing the machine: "`core.autocrlf=true` comes from conda's system gitconfig, so byte hashes of spec files must normalize line endings." merged-B 2.13 fixes the form: "hashes are sha256 of bytes after a BOM strip and CRLF/CR -> LF."
Spec conflict: SPEC.md says "a test fails whenever one of them changes", where "changes" is undefined for whitespace the author never typed.
Other context: The trade is deliberate and both merges kept it: a change that is only line endings is invisible to the alert. Several plans made that an explicit test case - a file rewritten with different line endings must read as clean - and the implementation has a test for each case. The same normalization is used when reading prose, so the rendered prompt cannot differ from the hashed file.
Sources: plan-1 sections 1 and 5.10; plan-2 section 4.2 (R5); plan-3 section 4.16; plan-4 section 4.14; plan-5 section 5.10; plan-6 section 16; merged-A section 2.14; merged-B decision 6 and section 2.13; `procs.read_bytes_normalized`, `procs.sha256_file`; `specguard.check`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-67, JC-CODE-79

## JC-68: Whether a round may edit one of the owner's files, and how loudly
Who and where: plan-3 interpretation I16 and plan-5 interpretation I16 designed it; plans 1, 2, 4 and 6 either forbid it or do not address it. Implemented by the implementer in `round.landing_step` with the `spec_edits_approved` flag, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner permits edits to their own files when genuinely necessary, and demands that every one be flagged. That has to become a mechanism: something must detect the edit, show it, and make sure it is seen before the round lands - and the runner has to decide whether a delegated round may skip that.
Rationale: plan-5's I16: "Allowed, never silent: after every recorded attempt the runner diffs the spec files ... any change is appended to HISTORY as a fenced unified diff under `## SPEC EDIT` ... and the round cannot land until the owner has approved at checkpoint `spec-edits`, delegation or not." plan-3 additionally requires the round's spec to declare in advance that it intends to edit them. merged-B decision 7 fixes the rule the code follows: an in-round spec edit "forces a `spec-edit` checkpoint before LANDING regardless of delegation".
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's header sets the requirement without a mechanism: "Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly." CLAUDE.md at the repository root repeats it. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says "delegate" means "approved; no checkpoints"; the two cannot both be honoured, and the implementation chose the spec-file rule.
Other context: Both merges adopted the loud version. SPEC.md later records the family of decisions: "Spec guard: hashed baseline, failing test with diff, start refuses drift, edits checkpoint the owner", and its line 13 endorses the reading. The implementer's `spec_edits_approved` flag makes the checkpoint fire once per distinct set of edits rather than on every `next`; what counts as a distinct set, and what an undone edit means, were settled by the review-2 fixer (JC-165, JC-177).
Sources: plan-3 I16 and section 4.16; plan-5 I16; merged-A section 2.14; merged-B decision 7 and section 2.9; `round.landing_step`, `round.approve`, `round.e1`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 83 and 144; SPEC.md line 13.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-68, JC-CODE-73

## JC-69: What surrounds the owner's prose in a prompt (from JC-PLAN-69)
Who and where: plan-1 prepends the harness's AGENTS.md; plan-3 prepends and appends nothing; plans 2 and 4 append the mechanical block if the prose does not ask for it; plan-6 keeps AGENTS.md out entirely.
Context: A prompt is built from a prose file the owner wrote. Whether the runner is allowed to add anything around it decides how much of an agent's instructions the owner controls.
Rationale: plan-3 takes the strict line: "Nothing is prepended or appended; the owner's prose decides structure, and reaches the runner's text only through `plumbing.*`." plan-6 keeps AGENTS.md out for a specific reason: "`AGENTS.md` is never rendered (it mentions a token by name to explain it); agents read it from disk." merged-B chose to include it anyway: "Prompts open with `AGENTS.md` verbatim and unrendered", which solves plan-6's problem by not rendering it.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md describes the prompt as complete in itself - "Your system prompt names your step, your inputs, where to write your artifact, and the JSON your final message must be." - and the prose files each end with the mechanical-block token, which implies the runner fills a hole rather than adding a preface.
Other context: Both merges kept the safety net of appending the mechanical block when a prose file omits the token, with a warning (JC-100), so an owner edit that drops the token cannot leave an agent without its contract. The final plan confirmed the AGENTS.md preface in amendment 10.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 section 4.5; plan-3 section 4.5; plan-4 section 4.4; plan-5 section 5.3; plan-6 section 8.1; merged-A decision 16; merged-B decision 15; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-70: The machine-readable lines the runner writes and reads back (from JC-PLAN-70)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.5, plan-2 Appendix A, plan-3 section 4.5, plan-4 section 4.5, plan-5 section 5.4, plan-6 section 8.4.
Context: The runner has to tell each agent which step it is on, where its artifact goes and where its result goes, in a form that test doubles and later tooling can read back out of the prompt. It also has to put every machine-parsed word somewhere the owner's editing cannot reach.
Rationale: All six adopted the sibling's convention of fixed `KEY:` lines and put them in runner-owned template files. plan-6 states the purpose: "Every machine-parsed word (statuses, verdicts, finding fields, the `STEP:`/`ARTIFACT:` lines) is emitted by runner code into the `{{ plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS }}` block and parsed by runner code."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md defines the block's role in a way that constrains its content: "{{ plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS }} refers to the mechanical steps agents must follow given their current task. These steps never require judgment calls." What those steps are is left entirely open.
Other context: This is the practical half of JC-1: it gives the runner a place to put everything it must parse, so the owner's prose can be rewritten freely. The templates' own wording is explicitly free to change; only the key lines and the JSON contracts are load-bearing - which is why several later fixes are edits to those templates rather than to code (JC-259, JC-309, JC-385).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 Appendix A; plan-3 section 4.5; plan-4 section 4.5; plan-5 section 5.4; plan-6 section 8.4; merged-A section 2.5; merged-B section 2.5.

## JC-71: Showing a producer the standard it will be judged against
Who and where: all six planners, differing in method - plan-1 strips include-only lines, plan-3 rewrites includes as "(as above)", plan-4 renders each prose file at most once per document, plan-6 drops includes already present; implemented by the implementer in `render.Renderer.prose_token` and its `rendered` set, commit ef1296d.
Context: The owner's producer prose ends by saying the gate's prose will follow. Pasting the gate file in raw would repeat whole sections the producer has already read and would include the gate's own mechanical block, which is meaningless to a producer. The owner's prose files also include one another, so the same block can be reached twice by another route.
Rationale: plan-4's rule is the one both merges took: "Each prose file renders at most once per document: a second include of the same name renders empty (this is also what makes `plumbing.GATE-PROSE` embed cleanly)." merged-B decision 14 rejected plan-1's approach as too fragile: "stripping lines made solely of include tokens (1), a rule about line layout."
Spec conflict: The owner's prose asks for it without saying how: "The gate that judges you will have the following prose: {{ plumbing.GATE-PROSE }}", in every producer overview file, and the gate files it names include the same COMMON blocks the producer already included. No owner file says what a repeated include means.
Other context: A second choice rides along: what the block says when the gate is switched off. All six show the gate's standard anyway, with a line saying it will not run - so a gates-off round still tells the producer what good looks like. The implementation adds two of its own guards: a cycle renders `[cycle: NAME]` and depth eight renders `[depth: NAME]`, both with a warning, and neither ever raises.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 section 4.5; plan-3 section 4.5; plan-4 section 4.4; plan-5 section 5.4; plan-6 section 8.3; merged-A section 2.5; merged-B decision 14 and section 2.5; `render.py` `prose_token`, `plumbing_token`, `gate_prose`, `MAX_DEPTH`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-71, JC-CODE-12

## JC-72: What to do about a prompt that is too big (from JC-PLAN-72)
Who and where: plan-3 and plan-5 shrink it; plan-4 warns; plan-6 refuses with a finding; plan-1 relies on keeping bodies out of prompts.
Context: Prompts carry a round's plan, spec, findings and history. In the sibling project they reached tens of kilobytes. A prompt that does not fit is a real failure, but so is silently cutting an agent's inputs.
Rationale: plan-5 shrinks: "A prompt over `maxPromptBytes` is not an error: embedded artifacts ... are replaced by their paths, a HISTORY line records it." plan-6 refuses: "the runner raises mechanical finding `P1` ('artifact and inputs exceed one context; narrow or split into sequential rounds') to the producer instead of spending the gate."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE.txt makes size a matter for the gate's judgment: "Too large to review properly in your context? Fail." Nothing says the runner should act first.
Other context: Both merges chose a warning only, having first removed the cause: inputs are given as paths rather than pasted in. merged-A states the link: "shrinking oversized prompts (plan-5; a size warning suffices when bodies are not inlined)", and keeps the owner's gate criterion where the owner put it - "PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE's own 'too large' criterion stays with the gate."
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-3 sections 4.3 and 9.11; plan-4 section 4.8; plan-5 section 5.3; plan-6 section 8.5; merged-A decision 16 and risk 15; merged-B section 2.5.

## JC-73: Telling the driver the exact command to run and the exact words to relay (from JC-PLAN-73)
Who and where: plan-2 Appendix C and plan-3 section 4.9; adopted by both merges.
Context: The driver is an agent, and every judgment it makes is one the harness cannot see. Printing the next command verbatim and the message to relay verbatim leaves it almost nothing to decide.
Rationale: plan-3 states the goal in one sentence: "`record_command` is the exact `record` line to run next and `owner_message` the exact text to relay at a checkpoint: the driver's freedom is limited to spawning and relaying."
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md describes the driver's loop - run `next`, hand the prompt over, save the result, `record` - and forbids it from judging, but does not say how little latitude it should have.
Other context: merged-A made it decision 25 in the same words. It is a deliberate narrowing of the one agent in the system that is not covered by a gate. The six real test rounds show how much latitude was left anyway: which command answers a checkpoint, what the owner would say, and whether to re-price a run were all the driver's (JC-198, JC-224, JC-293).
Sources: plan-2 Appendix C; plan-3 section 4.9; merged-A decision 25; merged-B section 2.8.

## JC-74: How the prompt reaches the sub-agent (from JC-PLAN-74)
Who and where: plan-6 section 15.4 hands over the file path; plans 1 and 4 paste the content into the spawning message.
Context: AGENTS.md tells the driver to give the prompt "verbatim" to a fresh sub-agent. The tool used to spawn one takes a message, not a file, so "verbatim" can mean pasting the whole text or naming the file and telling the agent to read it.
Rationale: merged-B decision 22 gives both the cost and the reading of the word: "Rejected: pasting the content into the task (1, 4): it doubles the driver's context per step across the 20-40 spawns of a round; 'verbatim' is satisfied because nothing is altered or summarized."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md: "each `next` gives you a prompt file; give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent (read-only for gates)". Whether handing over a path is "giving it verbatim" is exactly the question.
Other context: merged-A took the opposite view implicitly by spawning with the task message "Prompt file: <path>" but describing the definition body as carrying the instruction to read it. The final plan confirmed the path form in amendment 10: "prompt handoff by path with the fixed one-line wrapper".
Sources: plan-1 section 5.12; plan-4 section 4.11; plan-6 section 15.4; merged-A section 2.13; merged-B decision 22; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-75: Accepting that the reasoning effort cannot be set when spawning (from JC-PLAN-75)
Who and where: plan-3 risk 5, plan-4 section 4.11, plan-5 risk 3.
Context: The owner's roster gives each rung a model and an effort level. In the primary operating mode the driver spawns agents with a tool that takes a model but, as these plans understood it, no effort. The choice is to accept the gap and document it, or to force every round through the headless mode where effort can be set.
Rationale: plan-3: "the action carries `effort` anyway; DRIVER.md notes the gap; revisit when the tool exposes it." plan-4 goes further and recommends where to use which mode: "recommend accepting estimates in driver mode and using headless mode for delegated rounds and all system tests, where cost and effort are exact."
Spec conflict: None found. subAgents.yaml defines effort as an API parameter - "`effort` the API parameter" - and project.yaml selects rungs by name; nothing says the effort must be honoured.
Other context: The premise turned out to be wrong. merged-A's decision 15 reports the discovery - "the Agent tool takes model and effort from the definition (plans 3-5 assumed effort could not be set)" - which is what produced the generated per-rung definitions in JC-89. SPEC.md later records the final understanding and marks it hard: "Agent definitions are generated per rung: the Agent tool takes effort only from one loaded at session start."
Sources: plan-3 risk 9.5; plan-4 section 4.11 and risk 6; plan-5 risk 3; merged-A decision 15; FINAL-PLAN amendment 4.

## JC-76: What the three agent-rung keys mean (from JC-PLAN-76)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.2, plan-2 section 4.11, plan-3 section 2.4, plan-4 section 4.2, plan-5 interpretation I12, plan-6 section 5.2.
Context: project.yaml names three rungs from the roster without saying what they are for or what happens when the round's own agent plan disagrees with them.
Rationale: plan-5's I12 sets a full fallback chain: "Gate: `AGENTS-PLAN.json.gateAgents[STEP]`, else `project.gateAgent`, else `project.maxAgent`. Producer: `AGENTS-PLAN.json.agents[STEP]`, else `project.maxAgent`. A candidate counts only if it is a key of `subAgents.yaml.agents`." The third key was read by all six as the rung that real-agent tests run at.
Spec conflict: project.yaml lists them bare: "maxAgent: max", "gateAgent: max", "systemTestAgent: medium". PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt gives the choosing agent wide latitude - "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision" - without saying whether the owner's keys are defaults it may override or limits it may not.
Other context: Reading `maxAgent` as a default rather than a ceiling is the substantive half; the name suggests a maximum. All six read it as a default, and both merges kept that - which is what let two real planners assign rungs below it (JC-218, JC-219).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.2; plan-2 section 4.11; plan-3 section 2.4; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-5 I12; plan-6 section 5.2; merged-A section 2.3; merged-B section 2.3.

## JC-77: Replacing repeated end-to-end rounds with a probe ladder (from JC-PLAN-77)
Who and where: all six planners, in their real-agent testing sections - plan-1 section 6.5, plan-2 section 6, plan-3 section 6, plan-4 section 5.6, plan-5 section 7.3, plan-6 section 20.
Context: The owner had described a way to validate the harness with real agents: run a tiny throwaway task end to end, gates off, then gates on, then variations. All six planners were asked to say whether something cheaper and more comprehensive existed, and all six said yes and designed one.
Rationale: plan-1 states the case against the owner's loop most directly: "The end-to-end round is the most expensive (~15 agent runs, $30-90, hours) and least attributable test: when something goes wrong you cannot tell which prompt, which gate, or which mechanism failed, and it has no ground truth for gate effectiveness." plan-5 names what the ladder adds: per-step probes are "the only stage that can measure whether a gate catches a planted defect."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files describe the process a round follows, not how the harness is validated; the plan being displaced was stated by the owner in chat, and each plan keeps it as a later rung rather than dropping it.
Other context: All six converge on the same shape - a free layer driven by a scripted stand-in, then single-step probes against both clean and deliberately defective inputs, then the owner's loop in a sandbox, then the real repository. merged-A's summary is accurate: "They differ only in packaging." Both merges kept the ladder, and every plan tied one specific downstream decision to it: nobody would touch the gate prose until the probes produced evidence (JC-84). The ladder's first rung was eventually walked as test E (JC-473).
Sources: plan-1 section 6.5; plan-2 section 6; plan-3 section 6; plan-4 section 5.6; plan-5 section 7.3; plan-6 section 20; merged-A section 7; merged-B section 8.

## JC-78: The shape of the test suite (from JC-PLAN-78)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 6.1, plan-2 section 5, plan-3 section 5, plan-4 section 5.1, plan-5 section 7.1, plan-6 section 19.
Context: The harness is mostly git operations and process orchestration. Tests can mock git, or build real throwaway repositories and drive the real program. The second is slower but tests what actually runs.
Rationale: All six adopted the sibling's approach, which plan-1 states: "Partial integration, no mocks of git: every test builds a throwaway repo in a temp dir ... and drives the runner." They added one thing of their own for this machine: driving the program in-process rather than as a subprocess, because process startup on Windows is slow. plan-4: "in-process test driving (process spawn is slow here)".
Spec conflict: None found.
Other context: The scripted stand-in agent is the centrepiece - one script that reads the prompt's key lines, writes a canned artifact, and can be told to misbehave in a couple of dozen specific ways. The coordinating session later made this a rule of its own (JC-95). The throwaway repositories then leaked until review 1 asked for them to be cleaned up (JC-157).
Sources: plan-1 section 6.1; plan-2 section 5; plan-3 section 5; plan-4 section 5.1; plan-5 section 7.1; plan-6 section 19; merged-A section 3; merged-B section 3.

## JC-79: How a race between two rounds is made repeatable in a test (from JC-PLAN-79)
Who and where: plan-3 designed a code seam because it found no shell on the machine; plans 1, 4, 5 and 6 used git's own hooks; plan-6 wrote the hooks in Python.
Context: Proving that two rounds cannot both claim the same id means making one of them lose, on demand. The sibling did it with shell scripts installed in a test repository. Whether that works depends on the machine.
Rationale: plan-3 probed and concluded: "no `sh`/bash on PATH, so git hooks are not a reliable test device here", and designed an in-code seam instead: "`gitutil.HOOKS = {'before_push': None, 'before_fetch': None}`". plan-6 probed differently and found hooks do run, including "with an absolute-interpreter shebang", so it wrote them in Python.
Spec conflict: None found.
Other context: merged-A settled it by removing the disagreement rather than picking a side: "Python-written origin hooks with an absolute-interpreter shebang ... Rejected: sh hooks as the only race device (plan-3 found no `sh`; others found it - Python hooks remove the question)." merged-B added a probe that skips those tests if hooks turn out not to fire. Both kept the sibling's trick of narrowing one clone's fetch configuration, which needs no hook at all.
Sources: plan-1 section 6.2; plan-3 sections 5 and 9.7; plan-4 section 5.1; plan-5 section 7.1; plan-6 sections 1 and 19.2; merged-A decision 19; merged-B section 3.1.

## JC-80: Which spec the mechanics tests run against (from JC-PLAN-80)
Who and where: plan-4 section 5.1 proposed a generated stand-in spec; the other five copy the owner's real files into their test repositories. The merges split.
Context: Every test needs a harness to drive, which means a project file, a roster and a prose file per step. Using the owner's real files makes the tests realistic; it also means a prose rewrite by the owner breaks hundreds of tests that have nothing to do with prose.
Rationale: merged-B states it as the point: "Mechanics tests run against a generated minimal spec (same structure, one line of prose per file); only the real-spec tests read the owner's files ... Rejected: copying the real prose into every fixture (1, 3, 5, 6): a prose rewrite should fail the baseline test, not two hundred mechanics tests."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's header again: "Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them." A test suite that breaks on a wording change is not robust to it.
Other context: merged-A kept the real files in its fixtures and so did not adopt this; the final plan took merged-B and made it a constraint: "the mechanics tests run on the generated minimal fixture spec ... not on the owner's files." SPEC.md later records it: "Mechanics tests use a generated one-line-per-file spec, so a prose rewrite fails only the drift test." Fix 3 extended the same idea to the sandbox and probe commands, after the owner's new root SPEC.md broke them (JC-268).
Sources: plan-4 section 5.1; merged-A section 3; merged-B decision 24 and section 2.17; FINAL-PLAN constraints section.

## JC-81: The runner's machine interface (from JC-PLAN-81)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.1, plan-2 section 4.7, plan-3 section 4.9, plan-4 section 4.13, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 10.
Context: The runner's caller is an agent, not a person. The planners all decided that every command prints exactly one JSON object, that human text goes to the error stream, and that a fixed set of exit codes carries the outcome - notably a distinct code meaning "the round is waiting for the owner".
Rationale: plan-6: "Every command prints exactly one JSON object on stdout (human text goes to stderr), so the driver parses stdout blindly." The codes are inherited from the sibling: 0 ok, 1 error, 2 bad usage, 3 a check failed, 10 a checkpoint.
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md names the commands `next` and `record` and says the runner parses the agent's JSON; it says nothing about what the runner prints.
Other context: The separate code for a checkpoint is the load-bearing part: it lets the driver distinguish "stop and talk to the owner" from "something broke" without reading the output. The rule that humans read stderr is also what left a JSON-only driver blind to several warnings, which later fixes had to correct (JC-209, JC-256, JC-375).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.1; plan-2 section 4.7; plan-3 section 4.9; plan-4 section 4.13; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 10; merged-A section 2.8; merged-B section 2.1.

## JC-82: Keeping a mode with no branch, no remote and no worktree (from JC-PLAN-82)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 5.9 item 5, plan-2 section 4.9, plan-3 section 4.9, plan-4 section 4.7, plan-5 section 5.6, plan-6 section 14.2.
Context: The full concurrency design needs a remote and a worktree per round. That is heavy for tests and impossible offline. The sibling had a stripped-down mode; the planners all kept it.
Rationale: plan-6: "`--no-branch`: id = highest local folder + 1, current branch, no worktree, no push (tests, offline)."
Spec conflict: None found.
Other context: It is the mode most of the test suite runs in, so it carries real weight: a large part of the round machinery is exercised only through a path the owner will never use. Both merges kept it and both then required the full mode to refuse if there is no remote, so the stripped-down mode has to be asked for explicitly.
Sources: plan-1 section 5.9; plan-2 section 4.9; plan-3 section 4.9; plan-4 section 4.7; plan-5 section 5.6; plan-6 section 14.2; merged-A section 2.9; merged-B section 2.9.

## JC-83: Fixing the prose rather than adding the missing setting (from JC-PLAN-83)
Who and where: all six planners, in their spec-edit sections - plan-1 section 7, plan-2 section 7, plan-3 section 7, plan-4 section 6, plan-5 section 8, plan-6 section 21.
Context: Two of the owner's prose files quote a price from a setting that does not exist. The harness can be made whole either by changing two words of the owner's prose or by adding the missing setting to the owner's configuration. Both are edits to files the owner owns.
Rationale: plan-5 rejects the configuration route with the clearest reason: "The alternative (adding `ownerReviewCostPerWord: 0.1` to `project.yaml`) is rejected because it duplicates a value the owner already split." plan-6 puts the positive case: "The minimal fix uses the owner's own, more specific keys." plan-1 and plan-3 present both and recommend the prose edit; plan-4 offers a third option if the owner refuses - record a waiver and leave the unresolved marker in place.
Spec conflict: The defect itself. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt and PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt referred to `{{ project.ownerReviewCostPerWord }}`, while project.yaml defines `planCostPerWord: 0.1` and `specCostPerWord: 0.1` and no such key.
Other context: All six also specified how to make the edit loud, as spec.yaml requires: a separate commit whose subject marks it as needing owner review, the diff in the commit body, and the diff repeated in the report to the owner; the coordinating session turned that into a standing procedure (JC-450). Commit 9a13bb2 does exactly that - "SPEC EDIT (owner review required): resolve ownerReviewCostPerWord (2 tokens)" - and it is the only change to the owner's files in the whole bootstrap. Review 1 then judged the edit necessary and minimal (JC-145).
Sources: plan-1 section 7; plan-2 section 7; plan-3 section 7; plan-4 section 6; plan-5 section 8; plan-6 section 21; merged-A section 4; merged-B section 4; FINAL-PLAN constraints section.

## JC-84: Leaving the gate language alone until there is evidence (from JC-PLAN-84)
Who and where: all six planners - plan-1 section 7 and risk 6, plan-2 section 7 and risk 4, plan-3 risk 9.4, plan-4 section 6 and risk 11, plan-5 section 8, plan-6 section 21 and risk 23.7.
Context: Every gate file tells the gate to fail on each of a list of criteria. Read literally, a gate could fail almost any artifact. Each planner noticed this and each had the option of proposing a softening edit now.
Rationale: All six declined, for the same two reasons. plan-4: "COMMON-GATE's cost rule ('FAIL only when the defect ... costs more than [a retry]') already tempers the per-line 'Fail' lists, and the gate replays will show whether relaxation is needed before anyone edits wording." plan-3 went further and defined what evidence would change its mind: "if a clean artifact fails twice in three probes, propose to the owner one edit per gate file of the form `Fail` -> `Finding` ... and apply only what the owner approves."
Spec conflict: Two of the owner's own sentences pull against each other. PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE.txt lists absolutes: "Ambiguous, so that two implementers could read it differently? Fail. ... Not sensible? Fail." COMMON-GATE.txt, included in the same prompt, restricts failure to a cost test: "FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that; otherwise PASS with non-blocking findings."
Other context: Both merges kept the restraint and both named the smallest edit they would eventually propose - to the shared cost rule, never to the per-gate lists. This is a case of six agents identifying a probable problem in the owner's files and deliberately not acting on it. Test E was the evidence they were waiting for, and every clean seed passed (JC-401, JC-406, JC-407, JC-417).
Sources: plan-1 section 7 and risk 6; plan-2 section 7 and risk 4; plan-3 risk 9.4; plan-4 section 6 and risk 11; plan-5 section 8; plan-6 section 21 and risk 23.7; merged-A section 4; merged-B section 4.

## JC-85: Where carried-forward TODOs and clarifications live
Who and where: plan-2 gives them their own living files; plans 1, 3, 5 and 6 parse them out of each postmortem's headings; plan-4 refuses to parse and feeds whole postmortems instead. Settled by the coordinating session in FINAL-PLAN amendment 2 and implemented by the implementer as `config.DEFAULTS.carryForwardFiles` with POSTMORTEM's write paths and CHAT-TO-PLAN's plumbing, commit 05509b7.
Context: The postmortem is told to write TODOs for the next round and clarifications for the owner. Where those go, and how the next round's planning agent gets them, is not stated. Parsing them out of a document means the runner reading prose written by an agent under headings nobody standardised.
Rationale: plan-4 rejects parsing for the reason that matters here: "parsing postmortem TODOs by heading (wording-dependent; whole files are fed instead, capped)". merged-A rejects both alternatives: "Rejected: parsing `## TODO` headings out of POSTMORTEM.md (plans 1, 3, 5; wording-dependent) and feeding the last three whole postmortems (plan-4; loses older items, bloats prompts)." merged-B rejects plan-2's files in turn: "separate `docs/TODO.md` files (2), a second home the prose does not name." The final plan's amendment 2 settles it: the two files "are living files, named in `DEFAULTS.carryForwardFiles`, created by the bootstrap with a one-line header each. POSTMORTEM's write paths include them. CHAT-TO-PLAN's plumbing feeds both files verbatim .... The runner never parses either file."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt names two destinations without giving either an address: "propose a sensible fix and add it to TODOs for next round" and "add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review". CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt expects them to arrive: "Suggest the highest-impact open TODOs; include only those the owner accepts." COMMON-ROUND.txt lists the round folder's files and does not include them.
Other context: The two merges split and the coordinating session chose merged-A's shape, adding rules the merges had not, including that edits to those files after landing "are exempt from the living charge". The built repository has harness/docs/TODO.md and harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md. One implementer detail is worth naming: `round.round_context` reads the two files by list position, so an owner who reorders `carryForwardFiles` swaps the two labels in the planner's prompt without any warning. The bootstrap then used the same files for its own open questions (JC-128), and a postmortem in test F had to decide whether a plan non-goal forbade writing them at all (JC-439).
Sources: plan-1 section 5.5; plan-2 section 4.15; plan-3 section 4.5.3; plan-4 section 3; plan-5 I24; plan-6 section 17; merged-A decision 5; merged-B decision 16; FINAL-PLAN amendment 2; `config.DEFAULTS.carryForwardFiles`; `prompts.step_context`; `round.round_context`, `round.planning_contexts`; `plumbing/POSTMORTEM.txt`, `plumbing/CHAT-TO-PLAN.txt`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-85, JC-CODE-17

## JC-86: What hard cap is put on a single agent run
Who and where: plan-2 section 4.11, plan-4 runner.yaml (`minRunUsd`), plan-6 runner.yaml (`runBudgetMultiple`); the merges differ from each other; implemented by the implementer in `ledger.budget_cap`, commit 05509b7.
Context: A step's budget is a target, not a limit, but a headless run can be given a real spending cap. Setting it at the budget would abort useful work the moment a step runs slightly long; leaving it off removes the only hard brake on a runaway run.
Rationale: plan-6 sets a separate multiplier and says why the two numbers differ: "`runBudgetMultiple: 2` (hard CLI cap = step budget x this; budgets are targets)". plan-2 reuses the owner's own round-level multiple for the per-run cap: "per-run agent cap `max_budget_usd = share x multiple`". plan-4 adds a floor so a tiny share cannot make a run impossible: "`minRunUsd: 1.0 # floor for a run's --max-budget-usd`". merged-B 2.11 gives the formula the code follows: "`budget_cap = max(minRunUsd, budget x hardStopBudgetMultiple)`".
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt tells agents the opposite of a cap - "Budgets are targets, not gates: size your work to your budget and to an estimate of value it creates" - while project.yaml supplies a multiple for stopping a round, not a run, commenting it as "multiple of a round's budget at which in a round stops". Nothing says a single run may be cut off.
Other context: merged-A kept plan-6's separate multiplier; merged-B reused the owner's round multiple and added plan-4's floor, and the final plan took merged-B. Either way an agent is told a target and given a different, larger hard limit, which is a small departure from what the owner's prose tells the agent to expect.
Sources: plan-2 section 4.11; plan-4 section 4.2; plan-6 section 5.3; merged-A sections 2.3 and 2.11; merged-B sections 2.3 and 2.11; `ledger.budget_cap`; `round.action_payload`; `agents.run_agent`; `config.DEFAULTS.minRunUsd`.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-86, JC-CODE-35

## JC-87: Keeping a cross-round record that two rounds can write at once (from JC-PLAN-87)
Who and where: merged-A decision 4 and merged-B decision 11 - the two merges found the same flaw in all six plans and fixed it in opposite ways.
Context: Every plan kept a file with one line per finished round, on the stated grounds that appending lines merges cleanly. Both merging sessions checked and found that it does not: two rounds that each append a line at the end of the same file conflict when git merges them.
Rationale: merged-A removed the file: "**Shared weakness**: all six kept the summary's append-only `index.jsonl` and its 'timid' `sync_main`; two rounds appending at EOF conflict in git ... `spend --project`, `round.history` and `rounds` aggregate `STATE.json` across round folders and live branches." merged-B kept the file and changed how git merges it: "All six repeated the summary's claim that append-only JSONL 'merges cleanly'; under git's three-way merge two rounds appending at EOF conflict. One `.gitattributes` line fixes it."
Spec conflict: None found. The record is the planners' own construction (JC-60); the error was inherited from the sibling project's write-up, which asserted the property without qualification.
Other context: The final plan took merged-B, so the file survives with a merge attribute that unions the two sides. The built repository has a .gitattributes at its root. Both merges arriving independently at the same diagnosis is the strongest sign in the planning slice that the flaw was real. Review 1 then noticed that the attribute is pinned to a fixed folder whatever the owner's config says (JC-158).
Sources: merged-A decision 4 and section 2.7; merged-B decision 11 and section 2.12; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.

## JC-88: How the commits a round makes after landing reach the main branch
Who and where: merged-A decision 4 (a new FINISH step) and merged-B decision 11 and section 2.12 (a bounded sync loop); implemented by the implementer in `landing.sync_main`, commit 05509b7.
Context: A round lands its work, then writes a postmortem and closes its books. Those last commits are made after landing and are not yet on the main branch. The sibling's answer was a deliberately timid fast-forward that gives up silently if another round moved the branch first - which leaves the postmortem stranded on the round's own branch. Failing to deliver them must not fail a round that has already landed.
Rationale: merged-A adds a step for it: "FINISH merges `origin/<main>` and pushes like LANDING ... re-running verify and the suite only when the tail touched files outside the archives and docs." merged-B keeps the sibling's command name and makes it a loop instead: "a bounded merge-and-push loop ... (after finish and at every `done`; never raises) ... a conflict -> abort, `main_synced: false`, HISTORY `tail commits arrive with the next landing`", with "No verify or suite: post-landing commits touch only the round folder and the baseline."
Spec conflict: None found. project.yaml places the postmortem after landing - "# Standard landing happens here, after TESTS-TO-SUITE." followed by POSTMORTEM-GATE - without saying how its output reaches the shared branch.
Other context: The final plan took merged-B. SPEC.md later records the fix and how widespread the flaw was: "Post-landing commits merge back into main through a bounded sync; every plan had orphaned them." The difference between the two merges is mostly cost: merged-A re-runs the tests when the tail touches code, merged-B never does, on the grounds that the tail touches only records. The `main_synced` field is how the driver learns to expect the tail later.
Sources: merged-A decision 4 and section 2.12; merged-B decision 11 and section 2.12; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10; `landing.sync_main`; `round.next`, `round.done_payload`; .gitattributes; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 134.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-88, JC-CODE-77

## JC-89: Generating one agent definition per capability rung (from JC-PLAN-89)
Who and where: merged-A decision 15; adopted and extended by the coordinating session in FINAL-PLAN amendment 4.
Context: The plans had assumed the effort level in the owner's roster could not be applied when the driver spawns a sub-agent, and had written one gate definition and one producer definition (JC-32, JC-75). merged-A found the assumption wrong and redesigned around it.
Rationale: merged-A: "generated `.claude/agents/shackles-{producer,gate}-<rung>.md` definitions carrying model, effort and tools from `subAgents.yaml` ... New: one definition per rung, generated by the runner, because the Agent tool takes model and effort from the definition (plans 3-5 assumed effort could not be set)." The coordinating session added the constraint that makes it awkward: "agent definitions register only at session start; one created mid-session is 'not found'", and a fallback for when the right type is unavailable - a general-purpose agent with the model set, accepting that the effort then comes from the driver's own session.
Spec conflict: None found. subAgents.yaml describes the rungs and their parameters - "Keys are rungs on a capability ladder ... `effort` the API parameter" - and says nothing about how they are applied.
Other context: The definitions had to be generated and committed rather than created on demand, precisely because of the session-start rule. The built repository contains eight of them, one per rung per kind. SPEC.md later records the whole thing and marks it hard. In practice not one real test round could use them, because every driver session was started outside the sandbox that held them (JC-215, JC-454).
Sources: merged-A decision 15 and section 2.13; FINAL-PLAN amendment 4.

## JC-90: Reading "the previous round" as the round that just finished (from JC-PLAN-90)
Who and where: merged-B interpretation 6.
Context: The postmortem step runs at the end of a round and is told to report on what went wrong in the previous round. Taken literally that means the round before this one, which would leave each round's own faults unexamined until the next round happens - and the first round's faults never examined at all.
Rationale: merged-B states the reading in one line: "POSTMORTEM's 'the previous round' is read as 'this round, now landed'." No argument is recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the step is placed inside the round it would be reporting on, its listed inputs are that round's own history and findings, and the prose asks it to trace issues "to the plan(s) that originated them" - all of which fit the round that has just landed and not a previous one.
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt: "Determine what, if anything, stood out as wrong in the previous round." project.yaml places the step at the end of the same round: "POSTMORTEM-GATE: 0 # Round ends here, all agents stop."
Other context: Every plan had implicitly assumed this reading by feeding the postmortem the current round's own history, findings and judgment-call files; merged-B was the first to notice it needed saying and to put it on the list of things to raise with the owner.
Sources: merged-B interpretation 6 and section 2.5.

## JC-91: Cutting features out of the bootstrap (from JC-PLAN-91)
Who and where: merged-B decision 26.
Context: Six plans between them proposed far more than one implementing agent could build in one session. Somebody had to decide what would not be built, knowing that each cut removes something a planner had argued for.
Rationale: merged-B lists the cuts with the reason for each in parentheses: "`prune --apply` and `clean` (the `rounds` report suffices), the `smoke` matrix (subsumed by `probe`), `spec adopt` mid-round, a `push` command (`next` pushes when the branch is ahead), `judgment-calls`/`schema`/`agents`/`log`/`cost` commands (`status`, `render` and `config` cover them), transcript costing, `ERRATA.md` (the owner's `UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md` already serves that purpose), the `SessionStart` hook and `DRIVER.json`, per-extension test patterns beyond Python, `tierOverride`."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files describe a process, not a feature list; what the first build must contain was left to the planners, and the constraint that forced the cuts - one implementing agent, one session - came from the owner outside the files.
Other context: merged-A made the opposite kind of choice, keeping more and naming what to drop if time ran short: "If the session runs short, milestones 6-7's `probe`/`sandbox`/headless parts are the ones to defer with a TODO; never the baseline test, the state machine, or the fence." The final plan adopted merged-B's cut list in amendment 10, which is why the harness has no errata file and no separate judgment-call command.
Sources: merged-B decision 26; merged-A section 6; FINAL-PLAN amendment 10.
