## JC-92: Making one merge the plan and the other a list of amendments
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in FINAL-PLAN, sections "Base" and "Amendments", commit fdcc0d6.
Context: Two merging sessions had each produced a complete, self-consistent plan from the same six inputs, and they disagreed on a dozen points. The coordinating session could have written a third plan, or taken each disagreement on its merits into a new document. It did neither.
Rationale: Stated as a method rather than argued: "This file is short on purpose: it names the base document and lists every amendment. Where this file and merged-B disagree, this file wins. Where merged-B is silent, merged-A is the second reference, then the six plans", with "**merged-B is the plan**, in full". Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a plan that is already coherent stays coherent if you name it as the base and list departures, whereas a fresh synthesis risks combining two designs into one that neither merge checked; and naming a fallback order means the implementer always has an answer without coming back to ask.
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files governs how the bootstrap was organised or which document wins a conflict.
Other context: The consequence is visible in the shape of the result: FINAL-PLAN is eight pages shorter than either merge and consists almost entirely of pointers, and the buildable specification is spread across three documents with a written precedence ladder that every later reviewer had to follow (review 1's process step 6 checks "FINAL-PLAN amendments 1-10 and the merged-B sections they modify"). Nine of its ten amendments take a merged-A position over merged-B's, which is why several entries above show merged-B losing on a specific point while remaining the base document. It was never revisited; the implementer built from it in one session.
Sources: FINAL-PLAN sections "Base" and "Amendments"; commit fdcc0d6; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 1 item 6.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-92, JC-COORD-5

## JC-93: Where a round's working directory lives (from JC-PLAN-93)
Who and where: the coordinating session, FINAL-PLAN amendment 5.
Context: Every plan and both merges put each round's separate working directory in a folder called `.worktrees` at the top of the repository. The machine this runs on has its own convention for where such directories go, and other agents share the machine.
Rationale: "`DEFAULTS.worktreeDir` is `.claude/worktrees` (this machine's rule: worktrees live at `.claude/worktrees/` inside the repo) and a round's worktree folder is `round-NNNN` under it ... Nothing else about the claim, fence or landing changes."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files name no working-directory layout; the constraint comes from the machine's own notes, which sit outside this repository.
Other context: It matters more than it looks, because the recovery path that cleans a dirty working tree would delete any sibling directory that is not ignored by version control - which is why every plan made "this folder must be ignored" a precondition checked before a round starts. Moving the folder moves that precondition with it.
Sources: FINAL-PLAN amendment 5; merged-A section 2.3; merged-B section 2.3.

## JC-94: What the test suite is allowed to fail on
Who and where: the coordinating session, FINAL-PLAN amendment 6, revising merged-B section 3.4; implemented by the implementer in `doctor.wording_warnings` and `doctor.overlap_warnings`, commit ef1296d.
Context: merged-B had a test asserting that the words the runner parses - the four producer outcomes and the two verdicts - still appear somewhere in the owner's prose, so that renaming one would fail a test rather than silently break the system. That makes the suite depend on the owner's wording, which is the thing the whole design avoids.
Rationale: "Make that a `doctor` warning instead. Tests fail only on: the baseline drift test, structural lint (steps, gates, prose file names, artifact keys, roster keys, spec files present), unresolved tokens outside the waiver file, and prompts that fail to render. No test asserts on any sentence of the owner's prose."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's header, once more: "Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them." A suite that goes red because the owner reworded a sentence is not robust to it - even when the test was written to protect the owner. The rule the implementer worked under conflicts with wanting to notice when the owner renames a status word.
Other context: This draws a sharper version of the line in JC-1: a rule may mirror the prose, and the harness may notice when the mirror cracks, but noticing is a warning and not a failure. The list of four things that may fail is the operative part, since it defines what a red suite means from then on. The consequence is honest and documented: if the owner renames "DONE", nothing fails; `doctor` says the word is missing from the prose and the runner keeps using its own word. Review 1 later read the amendment's spirit to let a second wording check stand as a warning too (JC-132).
Sources: FINAL-PLAN amendment 6; merged-B sections 2.5 and 3.4; `doctor.wording_warnings`; `contract.STATUSES`, `contract.VERDICTS`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 147-153; harness/tests/test_spec_structure.py.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-94, JC-CODE-88

## JC-95: The suite never spawns a real agent (from JC-PLAN-95)
Who and where: the coordinating session, FINAL-PLAN amendment 7.
Context: The plans had a scripted stand-in agent for the tests and a separate ladder of paid real-agent probes. Nothing had forbidden a test from spawning a real one, and a test that did would be slow, costly and non-deterministic.
Rationale: "**Simulated agents carry the suite.** ... Go as far as it can go: every routing row ..., every checkpoint kind, every mechanical check, every owner command, the caps and stops, the spec guard, both landing conflict paths, the races and the fence, all driven by the stub without any real agent. A real agent is never spawned by the test suite."
Spec conflict: None found.
Other context: The instruction is not only a prohibition; it is a demand for coverage, listing what the stand-in must be made to exercise. SPEC.md later records the same design: "A stub agent with many scripted behaviors drives every path; real agents run as probes with planted defects." The paid probes remain, outside the suite.
Sources: FINAL-PLAN amendment 7; merged-B section 3.1.

## JC-96: Requiring the unattended mode to work before the build is done
Who and where: the first coordinating session, FINAL-PLAN amendment 9 and its closing note.
Context: Every plan made the interactive driver the primary way to run a round and the unattended command-line mode secondary, tested only against the stand-in agent. That leaves the unattended path unproven, which is fine until it is needed. The session had to decide whether "secondary" meant "implemented and tested only by the fake agent" or "implemented and actually proved against the real command-line tool once".
Rationale: "Implement merged-B 2.15 as written; the suite exercises it only through the stub. `doctor` must find `claude.exe` here and `doctor --probe-cli` must succeed once during the build (one call, low rung, capped at a few cents). Reason: the test driver in later phases may have to run rounds headlessly if nested sub-agent spawning turns out to be unavailable."
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md describes the driver loop - "each `next` gives you a prompt file; give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent" - and is silent on any other way of running a step.
Other context: The stated worry was resolved before the file was finished: the closing note records a probe showing that a sub-agent can spawn its own sub-agents, so driver mode became the primary path for every later real-agent test (JC-446). The requirement was kept anyway - one real capped call during the build - so the fallback would be known to work rather than assumed to. It was never met on this machine, because the standalone command-line tool is not logged in, and the two sessions carried that as a disclosure rather than a blocker: the final report tells the owner that "headless mode is stub-verified only; `claude auth login` fixes it and `doctor --probe-cli` then confirms."
Sources: FINAL-PLAN amendment 9 and "Verified after writing the above"; merged-B section 2.15; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 7.
Merged from: JC-PLAN-96, JC-COORD-7


================================================================================
IMPLEMENTATION: THE RUNNER'S CODE, ITS PROMPT TEMPLATES AND ITS DOCS
================================================================================

## JC-97: A wrong-typed config value refuses, an unknown one is kept (from JC-CODE-4)
Who and where: the implementer, in `config.py` `_compatible` and `load`, commit 7c4c6ea.
Context: The owner edits project.yaml freely; the runner has to decide what to do with a value of the wrong shape (a string where a number belongs) and with keys it has never heard of.
Rationale: merged-B 2.1: "Unknown is a warning, never a crash: a missing config key takes its default ... a type mismatch is a usage error naming the key."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files say nothing about malformed values. What left it open is that project.yaml is hand-edited and its header forbids agents to change it.
Other context: Followed exactly, with added detail in `_compatible`: a boolean default accepts an integer, a numeric default refuses a boolean, and a default of `None` (`claudePath`, `agentOverride`) accepts anything. Unknown keys are kept and exposed to prompt templates, so an owner can add a key and reference it in prose the same day.
Sources: `config.py` `_compatible`, `load`; harness/tests/test_config.py::test_type_mismatch_is_a_usage_error_naming_the_key, ::test_unknown_keys_kept; merged-B 2.1 and 2.3.

## JC-98: Overriding a producer also skips its gate (from JC-CODE-8)
Who and where: the implementer, in `round.gate_runs` and `round.skip_source`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The owner may override a producer step. Its gate is a separate step with its own flag. Nothing said whether the gate should still run over an artifact that was never produced.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a gate judges a producer's artifact against the plan; when the producer never ran there is no attempt to judge, so running the gate would either fail on an absent artifact or pass vacuously while costing a real agent run.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says an override means "the runner skips them this round", naming "steps or gates" as two separate things, which reads as if each is skipped only when named.
Other context: `skip_source` reports the skip as `override` in the gate's FINDINGS file, so the archive shows why. A side effect is that the owner cannot override only a producer and still have its artifact judged; PROCESS.md line 35 states the rule ("neither it nor its producer is overridden"). Test D's driver used the distinction deliberately, overriding a gate in one round and a producer in the other (JC-341, JC-361).
Sources: `round.gate_runs`, `round.skip_source`, `round.skip_producer`; `prompts.step_table`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 35; harness/tests/test_round.py::test_overrides.

## JC-99: Which structural mismatches stop a round and which only warn (from JC-CODE-10)
Who and where: the implementer, in `pipeline.lint`, commit ef1296d.
Context: The lint compares the code's step table against the owner's data (the gate list, the prose file names, the artifact keys, the roster keys, the spec file list). merged-B listed the checks but not which ones are fatal.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a mismatch that would make the runner act on something that does not exist (a gate name with no step, a roster key with no entry, a missing spec file, a producer with no prose) has no safe interpretation and must stop `start`; a mismatch that has an obvious safe reading (a gate absent from the map means disabled, an unknown prose file means unused, a missing artifact key means take the default) is reported and carried on, which is merged-B 2.1's "Unknown is a warning, never a crash".
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files have no notion of lint severity. What left it open is merged-B 2.4's single sentence "Lint (run by `doctor`, strictly by `start`, as warnings elsewhere)", which fixes where lint runs but not what each item is.
Other context: The split means an owner who deletes a gate's prose file loses that gate silently but with a warning, while an owner who deletes a producer's prose file cannot start a round until they restore it or override the step - the asymmetry merged-B 2.4 did specify (JC-4).
Sources: `pipeline.lint`; `round.start`; `doctor.run`; harness/tests/test_pipeline.py lint cases; merged-B 2.4.

## JC-100: The process block is appended when the owner's prose drops its token (from JC-CODE-13)
Who and where: the implementer, in `render.assemble`, commit ef1296d.
Context: Every prose file today ends with `{{ plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS }}`, the token that pulls in the machine-readable contract the runner parses back. The owner may delete it.
Rationale: merged-B 2.5: "If the prose has no `plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS` token the block is appended after a blank line (lint warns)." The reason is the rule the implementer worked under: the code must survive arbitrary edits to the owner's files, and without the block an agent has no `RESULT_FILE`, no `WRITE_PATHS` and no result shape.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "{{ plumbing.PROCESS-INSTRUCTIONS }} refers to the mechanical steps agents must follow given their current task", which describes the token but does not require every prose file to carry it.
Other context: Implemented exactly; the append is recorded as a render warning that reaches HISTORY as a FLAG and `doctor` as a warning, so the owner learns their edit changed the prompt's shape. It is the safety net under JC-69.
Sources: `render.assemble`; `round.render_step` (warnings to HISTORY); `doctor.run`; harness/tests/test_render.py::test_assemble_raw_header_and_missing_process_token; merged-B 2.5.

## JC-101: An unresolvable token renders a visible marker instead of failing (from JC-CODE-14)
Who and where: the implementer, in `render.Renderer.miss` and `doctor.run`, commit ef1296d.
Context: Prose written by the owner can name a config key or a context value that does not exist - and did: two prose files named `project.ownerReviewCostPerWord`, which project.yaml does not define.
Rationale: merged-B 2.1: "an unresolvable token renders `[unresolved: ns.key]`" - unknown is a warning, never a crash.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt and PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt referenced a key project.yaml does not define; that is the defect the two-token spec edit of commit 9a13bb2 repaired (JC-83).
Other context: Implemented exactly; the markers are collected, printed to stderr, written to HISTORY as a FLAG and reported by `doctor`, where they are errors unless waived (JC-126). The two layers are deliberate: rendering never fails, and something else decides whether the resulting prompt is acceptable.
Sources: `render.py` `miss`; `round.render_step`; `doctor.run`; harness/tests/spec_waivers.json; merged-B 2.1 and 2.5; commit 9a13bb2.

## JC-102: What each step is shown - the inputs list (from JC-CODE-15)
Who and where: the implementer, in `prompts.step_context` (the `inputs` block), commit ef1296d.
Context: Every prompt tells its agent "Inputs, by path: ...". merged-B 2.5 lists `inputs` as a context key and says nothing about what goes in it; the owner's prose names artifacts loosely (COMMON-ROUND lists the whole round folder's contents) but does not say which step reads which.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): each step should see the artifacts it is built from and nothing more, both because reading costs tokens and because a step that sees a later artifact could copy rather than derive. The chain implemented is the pipeline's own dependency order - the plan for everyone; the agents plan from PLAN-TO-SPEC onward; SPEC.json and SPEC.md from SPEC-TO-TESTS onward; the frozen tests from SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION onward; SUITE.json for CLEANUP.
Spec conflict: COMMON-ROUND.txt lists every round-folder file to every step; SPEC-TO-TESTS-OVERVIEW.txt says the tests are written "from the spec alone" and SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-OVERVIEW.txt that the code is "the code the spec describes, and nothing else", which are narrower than COMMON-ROUND's list.
Other context: POSTMORTEM gets a different list entirely (HISTORY, FINDINGS, RESULTS, both judgment files, the owner-log slice, STATE, the plan, the spec body and the carry-forward files), matching POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt's demand to trace issues to plans and owner words. Gates get their producer's artifact first, plus the producer's own result file - the latter corrected by review 1 so a gate re-run after a FAIL does not read the wrong attempt (JC-154).
Sources: `prompts.step_context`; `plumbing/producer.txt` and `gate.txt` ("Inputs, by path"); harness/tests/test_render.py::test_step_context_write_paths_and_inputs; commit ef1296d.

## JC-103: CLEANUP's write paths (from JC-CODE-16)
Who and where: the implementer, in `prompts.step_context` (the `cleanup` entry), commit ef1296d.
Context: CLEANUP is the last code step; the owner's prose says only "Clean up the round, so that it can be landed without issue", which does not say what it may touch.
Rationale: merged-B 2.4's step table: CLEANUP writes "`implPaths` + living paths minus `testPaths` + `INDEX.md`".
Spec conflict: CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt is one sentence of scope with no paths; project.yaml defines `livingSourcePaths` but not who may write them.
Other context: Implemented exactly. The subtraction of `testPaths` is what keeps the frozen tests frozen through CLEANUP; `INDEX.md` is added so a round that adds a module can add its routing line. The width of this list is what two cleanup agents then had to confine themselves by judgment (JC-297, JC-366), what a postmortem proposed to narrow (JC-302), and what let a cleanup step rescue a round by writing its missing test (JC-365).
Sources: `prompts.step_context`; `pipeline.PIPELINE` (CLEANUP writes `cleanup`); merged-B 2.4.

## JC-104: The prompt tells each agent where the round goes next (from JC-CODE-18)
Who and where: the implementer, in `prompts.advances`, `after_done`, `after_needs_owner` and `after_pass`, commit ef1296d; corrected by the post-test-A fixer in commit 0bcc8fa.
Context: merged-B 2.5 lists `after_done`, `after_needs_owner` and `after_pass` as context keys without giving their text; the sentences an agent reads about the consequences of its own status are the runner's own words, not the owner's.
Rationale: The post-test-A commit records the correction: "The last producer and gate no longer read 'the round advances to None'" - `advances(None)` now renders "the round finishes".
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose describes each status (COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt: "End DONE when the artifact is complete. NEEDS-OWNER when ... UPSTREAM when ... BLOCKED when ...") but never says what the runner then does.
Other context: The sentences carry real information the prose does not: that a disabled gate means "a clean DONE is accepted", that a NEEDS-OWNER goes to the gate when one runs and to the owner when none does, and that a review checkpoint follows. They are generated per step, so a change to the gate flags changes what the agent is told without any prose edit. One of them, the review-checkpoint clause, was still wrong for a partly delegated round until fix 5 (JC-400).
Sources: `prompts.advances`, `prompts.step_context`; `plumbing/producer.txt` and `gate.txt` final lines; harness/tests/test_render.py::test_step_context_says_where_the_round_goes_next; commits ef1296d and 0bcc8fa.

## JC-105: A hand-written validator instead of a dependency (from JC-CODE-21)
Who and where: the implementer, in `schemas.validate` and `schemas.json_schema`, commit ef1296d.
Context: The runner validates six JSON shapes and also has to emit JSON Schema for the headless command line's `--json-schema` flag.
Rationale: merged-B 2.6: "A hand-written validator ... no jsonschema dependency; the same dicts are emitted as JSON Schema for `--json-schema`." FINAL-PLAN's constraints allow "PyYAML and pytest ... no other dependencies".
Spec conflict: None found; this is a constraint from the owner's machine, not from the spec files.
Other context: Implemented exactly. `schemas.describe` turns the same dictionaries into the one-line contract an agent reads in its prompt, so the prompt, the validator and the command-line schema cannot drift apart.
Sources: `schemas.py` `validate`, `json_schema`, `describe`; `prompts.artifact_contract`; `agents.run_agent`; merged-B 2.6; FINAL-PLAN "Constraints from the owner".

## JC-106: M1 exempts the owner's spec files from the stray revert (from JC-CODE-25)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.m1_strays` (the `exempt` parameter) and `round.record_producer`, commit 05509b7.
Context: M1 reverts every changed path outside the step's write paths. The owner's spec files are never in any step's write paths, yet AGENTS.md and the producer template both allow an agent to edit one "only when genuinely necessary".
Rationale: The docstring records it: "spec files are exempt (E1 shows them to the owner instead)". Without the exemption the two rules would cancel: an agent could edit a spec file and the same `record` would silently undo it.
Spec conflict: The tension is between spec.yaml's header - "Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly" - and merged-B 2.10's M1, which reverts everything outside the write paths. The owner's files never say the edit must survive; they say it must be flagged.
Other context: The exemption is by exact path, so only the files spec.yaml currently lists are spared. Everything else about the edit is handled by E1: the diff goes to HISTORY, `spec_edits` names the file, and the `spec-edit` checkpoint fires before LANDING whatever the delegation (JC-68).
Sources: `checks.m1_strays` docstring and `exempt`; `checks.e1_spec_edits`; `round.record_producer`, `round.e1`, `round.landing_step`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 103 and 107; spec.yaml header.

## JC-107: How a stray change is undone (from JC-CODE-27)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.revert`, commit 05509b7.
Context: M1, M2 and M4 all have to put a file back. A tracked file, an untracked file, a file the agent staged and a file inside a half-finished merge each need different treatment.
Rationale: merged-B 2.10: "the paths are reverted (`git checkout -- p` for tracked, delete for untracked) and listed"; and for a merge attempt, "strays are restored from the automerge tree (`git checkout <tree> -- p`)".
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not describe reverting at all.
Other context: Followed with added detail: a path the agent added to the index is first `git rm --cached` and then deleted, and a directory is removed with the read-only-tolerant `procs.rmtree`, both Windows-driven. The restore-from-tree branch is attempted first whenever a merge tree is supplied and the path exists in it. It is the mechanism behind the planners' choice to revert rather than fail (JC-24).
Sources: `checks.revert`; `procs.rmtree`; harness/tests/test_checks.py::test_m1_stray_reverted_and_in_scope_kept; merged-B 2.10.

## JC-108: A conflicted frozen test is a note, not a violation (from JC-CODE-28)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.m2_frozen` (the `T1` branch), commit 05509b7.
Context: Once the tests freeze, M2 reverts any change under `testPaths`. During a landing merge attempt git itself may put conflict markers into a frozen test, and the agent must edit it to resolve them.
Rationale: merged-B 2.10: "a conflicted test on a merge attempt is non-blocking `T1` instead".
Spec conflict: None found; no owner file mentions freezing or merging. What left it open is that the freeze rule and the conflict-resolution rule are both the runner's own and meet only here.
Other context: Implemented exactly, with the finding text "a frozen test was conflicted in the merge and hand-resolved; review it" and the suggestion "keep the resolution minimal". Because T1 is non-blocking it is carried forward to later producers and to POSTMORTEM rather than costing a retry.
Sources: `checks.m2_frozen`; `round.record_producer`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 104; merged-B 2.10.

## JC-109: L3 is decided by reading conflict markers out of the files (from JC-CODE-29)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.l3_unmerged`, commit 05509b7.
Context: After a merge attempt the runner must decide whether the agent actually resolved every conflicted file before committing a two-parent commit.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): merged-B 2.12 specified "`git ls-files -u` non-empty -> finding `L3`", but the index is only consulted after `git add -A`, and an agent that edits a conflicted file without staging it leaves the index unmerged while the file is fine - and vice versa. Reading the working-tree file for `<<<<<<< `, `=======` and `>>>>>>> ` lines tests what actually ships.
Spec conflict: None found; no owner file mentions merges. What left it open is that merged-B named a mechanism (`ls-files -u`) rather than a property.
Other context: This is a deviation from merged-B 2.12's stated mechanism. The cost is a false positive on a file that legitimately contains a line of seven equals signs - a documentation file or a test fixture about conflicts - which would fail the attempt with L3 and no way to pass. The check runs only over the paths git itself reported as conflicted, which narrows but does not remove the risk.
Sources: `checks.l3_unmerged`; `round.record_producer`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 110 and 132; harness/tests/test_concurrency.py::test_conflict_becomes_a_merge_attempt_that_converges; merged-B 2.12.

## JC-110: What S1 additionally requires of the agents plan (from JC-CODE-32)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.s1_agents_plan`, commit 05509b7.
Context: PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt tells the step to "choose the agent, sub-agents and budget share for each step and gate" and to "Optimize the budget, within reason", which is a judgment instruction with no checkable shape.

Spec conflict: project.yaml sets `maxSimultaneousSubAgentsPerRound: 2`, `gatesFraction: 0.3` and `workFraction: 0.7` but does not say the shares must be a partition; the prose says only "within reason".
Other context: Followed, with added detail from the later fix rounds: CHAT-TO-PLAN is exempted from the `agents` map (it runs in chat) but required in `shares.work`, and gate shares are required only for producers whose gate actually runs (JC-262). The 0.01 tolerance is the implementer's reading of "sums to 1" for floating-point fractions written by an agent. The exemption is what a real planner then had to reconcile with the owner's printed minimum for the same step (JC-286, JC-309).

## JC-111: What S1 additionally requires of the spec (from JC-CODE-33)
Who and where: the implementer, in `checks.s1_spec`, commit 05509b7.
Context: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt demands a spec that is "complete, unambiguous and sensible" - none of which a machine can check - so the mechanical check has to pick the structural properties that later steps depend on.
Rationale: merged-B 2.6: "paths inside the repo, prefix-disjoint, none inside the round folder or `lockedProsePath` or a spec file; sum of `refactor[].budget_usd` <= `maxRefactorOverhead x budget_usd`; `SPEC.md` exists and is non-empty."
Spec conflict: project.yaml sets "maxRefactorOverhead: 0.3 # Percent of budget cap allowed for refactoring work during a round" and COMMON-PROJECT.txt says refactoring is "capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget"; neither says what is measured, which is why the runner measures only the spec's own declared refactor budgets and leaves the rest to the gate (JC-30).
Other context: Followed exactly; merged-B section 5 item 10 flagged the narrow enforcement to the owner and PROCESS.md line 159 repeats it. The protected-path rule is what keeps a spec from declaring the locked prose or the owner's own files as implementation paths.

## JC-112: The order the checks run in, and which are skipped once something already failed (from JC-CODE-43)
Who and where: the implementer, in `round.record_producer`, commit 05509b7.
Context: A producer's record runs up to seven checks, two of which (M3's verify and M5's whole permanent suite) can take minutes. Nothing said what order they run in or whether an already-failing attempt should still pay for them.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the cheap file-level checks come first and revert what they find, so the expensive ones run against a tree that is already in scope; M3 is skipped when the artifact is invalid or the merge is unresolved, because verify would be measuring a tree the agent is going to have to redo; M5 is skipped whenever any blocking finding already exists, because the attempt is failing anyway and the suite costs minutes.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files list no checks. What left it open is that merged-B 2.4's table lists the checks per step as a set, with no order.
Other context: The order is M4 (judgment files), the claimed-counts comparison, E1 (spec edits), M2, M1, the commit, S1, M3, M5. Committing before S1 and M3 is deliberate: the checks measure the uncommitted diff, and the artifact and verify then run against what was committed.
Sources: `round.record_producer`; `pipeline.checks_for`, `pipeline.ALWAYS`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 111.

## JC-113: `answer` is offered at a review checkpoint and re-opens the accepted producer (from JC-CODE-58)
Who and where: the implementer, in `round.RESUME` and `round.answer`, commit 05509b7.
Context: A `review` checkpoint pauses after a step has been accepted. merged-B 2.9 lists `answer` only for `question` and `blocked`.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a review is the owner reading an artifact, and the natural response to reading something wrong is to say what to change; without `answer` the owner's only options would be to approve it anyway or abandon the round. So the runner turns the text into finding `O1`, clears the producer's and its gate's acceptance, and re-runs the producer with the owner's words as a blocking finding.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose says only "Checkpoint after this step, with the owner" and "When a checkpoint is reached, wait in chat", never what the owner may say.
Other context: This is an addition beyond merged-B, and it is the one checkpoint transition that un-accepts an already accepted step. Neither PROCESS.md nor DRIVER.md says that `answer` at a review reverses an acceptance; PROCESS.md line 88 states the generic rule only.
Sources: `round.RESUME`, `round.answer`; `round.resume_commands`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 88; merged-B 2.9.

## JC-114: Which owner commands each checkpoint kind offers (from JC-CODE-59)
Who and where: the implementer, in `round.RESUME` and `round.HINTS`, commit 05509b7; extended by the fix-5 fixer, commit 3f613d7.
Context: Every checkpoint message ends with "Resume with one of:" and a list of runnable commands. Nothing said which commands belong to which kind.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the list is what the runner will actually accept and act on at that kind, so offering a command that would be refused wastes the owner's turn; `round-limit` and `hard-stop` are budget facts nothing but approval or abandonment can change, while `question` and `blocked` are producer-level and take an answer or an override.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose names four words ("approve", "delegate", "override", and by implication abandonment) without attaching them to situations.
Other context: The fix-5 fixer added `override` to the `question` list because "the runner already accepted there" - the offered list had been narrower than the accepted set, which is the failure mode this table is meant to prevent. The hints are complete command lines with the runner path and `--root` quoted, so the owner can copy them. Test F's driver found the opposite problem: at a question the list offers `answer` and `approve` side by side with nothing marking the difference, and one approving word would have shipped the choice the owner had reserved (JC-293).
Sources: `round.RESUME`, `round.HINTS`, `round.resume_commands`, `round.checkpoint_message`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 83-85; commits 05509b7, 5acf17a and 3f613d7.

## JC-119: `spec accept` records the HEAD it was accepted at, not the commit that will carry it (from JC-CODE-81)
Who and where: the implementer in `specguard.accept`, commit 7c4c6ea; explained by the fix-3 fixer, commit 5acf17a.
Context: Accepting writes a new baseline file that must itself be committed. The `accepted_commit` field can only name a commit that already exists.
Rationale: Quoted from the fix commit: "spec accept prints `head_at_accept`; PROCESS.md says the acceptance lands in the next commit, so spec diff shows it once more."
Spec conflict: None found; this is the guard's own bookkeeping.
Other context: The effect is that `spec diff` immediately after an acceptance still shows the accepted change, because it diffs against the commit before the acceptance. Rather than complicate the mechanism, the fixer documented the behaviour and made the command print the sha it used.

## JC-120: How an owner's words are verified against the log (from JC-CODE-82)
Who and where: the implementer, in `owner.verify_quote` and `round.owner_command`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The driver interprets the owner's words and passes a quote; the runner must decide whether that quote really came from the owner, using only a log of chat messages with timestamps.
Rationale: merged-B decision 2 and 2.9: the words, "whitespace-normalized", must "occur in a line dated >= `presented_at`" for `start`, and after `checkpoint.at` for an owner command.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says only the runner "contacts the owner" and that the driver relays; CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt requires the owner's words "with unambiguous conversational context", which is a judgment the runner is forbidden to make. Verification of a substring is the mechanical residue of that sentence.
Other context: Implemented exactly. It is a containment check, not an equality check, so a quote lifted from a longer message passes; and the time window is what stops an old "approve" from being reused. merged-B decision 2 rejected parsing the log for the words themselves precisely because "a renamed word would silently hang or phantom-approve a round" (JC-49).
Sources: `owner.verify_quote`, `owner.normalize`, `owner.slice_since`; `round.owner_command`, `round.start`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 152; harness/tests/test_owner.py::test_verify_quote_windows; merged-B decision 2 and 2.9.

## JC-121: Without an owner log the words are recorded unverified rather than refused (from JC-CODE-83)
Who and where: the implementer, in `owner.verify_quote` (returning `None`), `round.owner_command` and `round.start`, commit 05509b7.
Context: The log only exists if the owner's chat session runs the hook, which requires the session to have started in this repository. A sandbox, or a session started one folder up, has no log at all.
Rationale: merged-B 2.14: "`doctor` warns when the hook is not configured or the log does not exist (then quotes are recorded unverified with a FLAG)."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files know nothing of the log. What left it open is that the verification mechanism is itself optional infrastructure.
Other context: The three-valued return (`True`, `False`, `None`) is the implementer's way of keeping "not in the log" (a refusal) apart from "no log" (a FLAG). Every sandbox round in the test programme ran this way, so no owner word in the whole test programme was ever verified. Test A's reviewer judged it worth raising, and CLARIFICATIONS.md asks "A round starts and lands on unverified owner words whenever no owner log exists ...; PROCESS.md permits that with a FLAG. Should a missing log stop `start` instead?"
Sources: `owner.verify_quote`; `round.owner_command`, `round.start`; `doctor.run`; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 13; merged-B 2.14.

## JC-122: The hook carries its own copy of the envelope list (from JC-CODE-85)
Who and where: the implementer, in `harness/src/owner_log_hook.py` (`ENVELOPES`) against `config.DEFAULTS.envelopePrefixes`, commit 05509b7.

Other context: The duplication is guarded by a test - test_owner.py::test_hook_matches_the_runner_prefix_list - so the two copies cannot drift. The hook also never raises and returns `None` on every error path, which is the same "lose nothing, break nothing" reasoning. The filter itself is JC-50.

## JC-123: The text of the generated agent definitions (from JC-CODE-86)
Who and where: the implementer, in `agentdefs.definition`, `GATE_BODY` and `PRODUCER_BODY`, commit 05509b7.
Context: FINAL-PLAN amendment 4 required per-rung definitions generated from the roster, with frontmatter and tool restrictions; the body text an agent reads before it opens its prompt file is not specified anywhere.
Rationale: FINAL-PLAN amendment 4 gives the frontmatter: "`model` (alias from `modelAliases`), `effort`, and for gates `tools: Read, Grep, Glob`; for producers the git-command restrictions merged-B lists." The body is the implementer's; expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the definition is a second, independent statement of the two invariants that matter most - read-only for gates, no git for producers - so that an agent that never reads its prompt file properly still has them.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md states both invariants in prose ("Only the runner ... commits, pushes"; producers "edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths"). The definitions restate them as tool restrictions, which is the mechanical form.
