## JC-124: The headless loop's stop conditions and its action cap (from JC-CODE-87)
Who and where: the implementer, in `agents.run_loop`, commit 05509b7.
Context: `run --until checkpoint|step|done` drives next/agent/record automatically. Nothing said how many actions it may take or which exit codes end it.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): an unbounded loop on a paid agent is the one failure mode that costs real money without anyone watching, so a hard cap of 200 actions is a backstop independent of the budget caps; exit 2 (an invalid result) must not end the loop, because the correct response is to rerun the same attempt, which is exactly what the next iteration does.
Spec conflict: None found; project.yaml bounds a run (`maxTurnsPerRun`, `maxRunWallClockHours`) and a round (`hardStopBudgetMultiple`) but not a loop.
Other context: The loop re-opens the round from disk before each `next` and again before each `record`, so a crash mid-loop leaves consistent state. The cap is exposed as `--max-actions`, and hitting it returns `{"kind": "stopped"}` with exit 1 rather than pretending to finish.
Sources: `agents.run_loop`; `commands.add_parsers` (`run`); harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 53; harness/tests/test_headless.py.

## JC-125: An eight-word shared window is the mechanical proxy for "nothing depends on the wording" (from JC-CODE-89)
Who and where: the implementer, in `doctor.overlap_warnings` and `doctor.windows`, commit 14c1f8f.
Context: The governing rule is that no mechanical behaviour may depend on the wording of the owner's prose. That is a property of intent, not of text, so it cannot be checked directly.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the observable symptom of a dependency is a source file containing a phrase copied from the prose - a regular expression, a string comparison, a duplicated sentence - so any eight consecutive lowercase words shared between a source file and a prose file is treated as a signal worth a human look. merged-B 3.6 proposed the same measure as a test; amendment 6 moved it to a warning (JC-94).
Spec conflict: None found; the rule itself is the implementer's brief, not a sentence in the owner's files.
Other context: Eight is arbitrary and the check is one-directional (source against prose), so it catches copied phrases and not a regular expression that encodes a rule in different words. It is a heuristic presented as a heuristic - a warning in `doctor`, never a failure - and review 1 accepted that form as within the spirit of the amendment (JC-132).
Sources: `doctor.overlap_warnings`, `doctor.windows`; harness/docs/TESTING.md; merged-B 3.6; FINAL-PLAN amendment 6.

## JC-126: An unresolved token is a `doctor` error unless it is waived (from JC-CODE-90)
Who and where: the implementer, in `doctor.run` and harness/tests/spec_waivers.json, commit ef1296d.
Context: Rendering never fails on an unknown token (JC-101); something else has to decide whether the resulting prompt is acceptable.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a prompt containing `[unresolved: project.somethingElse]` is a prompt that silently fails to tell an agent a number it was meant to have, so it must block `start` through `doctor`'s exit code - unless the owner has knowingly declined to fix it, which is what the waiver file records with a reason.
Spec conflict: merged-B 2.1 says "Unknown is a warning, never a crash", which the renderer obeys; making the same condition an error one layer up is the implementer's reconciliation of that principle with the need to notice.
Other context: The waiver file exists and is empty, because the one real case - `project.ownerReviewCostPerWord` - was fixed by the two-token spec edit instead (JC-83). merged-B section 4 describes the waiver as the fallback "If the owner declines the edit".
Sources: `doctor.run`, `doctor.waivers`; harness/tests/spec_waivers.json; harness/tests/test_spec_structure.py::test_every_prompt_renders_without_unresolved_tokens; merged-B section 4; commit 9a13bb2.

## JC-127: The probe repository runs with every gate on and delegated (from JC-CODE-92)
Who and where: the implementer, in `probe.prepare`, commit 037a6cb.
Context: A probe plays a temporary repository forward with the stub to just before one step, then runs one real agent there. The gate flags and the approval mode of that temporary round had to be chosen.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): probing a gate requires that gate to be enabled, and probing any step requires the round to reach it without stopping, which delegation guarantees; enabling every gate uniformly means one code path serves both producer and gate probes.
Spec conflict: None found; project.yaml's gate flags are all 0 today, so a probe deliberately runs a configuration the owner does not currently use.
Other context: The consequence is that a probe never measures the harness as configured - it measures each prompt in isolation, which is the point of Level 1 in merged-B section 8 ("which prose sentence a failure belongs to"). Test E ran twelve such probes and reported "12 of 12 as expected".
Sources: `probe.prepare`, `probe.cmd`; harness/docs/TESTING.md "Real-agent ladder"; merged-B section 8; commit 037a6cb.

## JC-128: The bootstrap's own unresolved questions were written into the owner's carry-forward files (from JC-CODE-102)
Who and where: the implementer in harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md and docs/TODO.md, commit 14c1f8f; extended by the post-test-A fixer, commit 9b282a9.
Context: merged-B section 5 listed ten interpretations and tensions "to flag to the owner (no edits)". A list inside a bootstrap plan is read once; the harness has a mechanism for questions that must reach the next planner.
Rationale: The post-test-A commit records the routing decision: "the two policy questions the sandbox postmortem left for the owner go to CLARIFICATIONS.md." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for the original choice: CHAT-TO-PLAN's prompt feeds both files verbatim to the next planner, so putting the bootstrap's own open questions there makes them impossible to miss at the next round rather than archived in `bootstrap/`.
Spec conflict: None found; the files are the runner's own invention (JC-85). What left it open is that merged-B's section 5 had no destination.
Other context: The effect is that the harness's first round begins by showing the owner four interpretations the bootstrap made on their behalf - the two caps read per step and per re-entry (JC-44, JC-45), the ten-minute checkpoint estimate (JC-35), gates returning judgment calls in their verdict (JC-52), and `start` requiring no owner word while CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE is 0 (JC-6) - plus the two test-A questions about unverified words and the spec word charge.
Sources: harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md; harness/docs/TODO.md; `plumbing/CHAT-TO-PLAN.txt`; `round.round_context`, `round.planning_contexts`; merged-B section 5; commits 14c1f8f and 9b282a9.

## JC-129: PROCESS.md names every rule that mirrors a sentence of the owner's prose (from JC-CODE-103)
Who and where: the implementer, in harness/docs/PROCESS.md ("Wording-adjacent rules"), commit 14c1f8f.
Context: The governing rule is that nothing mechanical depends on the wording of the locked prose. Some runner rules nonetheless exist only because a prose sentence exists - two upholds settling a dispute, the review checkpoints, gates being read-only.
Rationale: merged-B 2.16 requires the section: "the wording-adjacent table (each runner rule that mirrors prose: settled at 2 upholds, the status and verdict words, review checkpoints, step order, gates read-only, the owner word list interpreted by the driver)".
Spec conflict: The tension is internal: the runner must not read the prose, yet several of its constants are answers to sentences in it. The section makes the coupling visible instead of denying it.
Other context: Implemented exactly, with the carry-forward files added as a sixth entry. It is the closest thing in the harness to a list of the implementer's own interpretive decisions, which is why an owner rewording a prose sentence is told by the failing baseline test to read it (JC-142).
Sources: harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 147-153; `specguard.INSTRUCTION`; merged-B 2.16.


================================================================================
REVIEWS 1 AND 2, AND THE COMMITS THAT FIXED THEM
================================================================================

## JC-130: Inventing the severity scale and deciding where its lines fall (from JC-R12-1)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, a fresh read-only session, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 1 "Process", final paragraph.
Context: The reviewer had to rank whatever it found so that the people who read the review would know what had to be fixed before real agents were let near the harness. No one had handed it a ranking scheme, so it had to invent the levels and decide what put a defect in each one.
Rationale: Quoted: "Severity scale: **blocker** = the next phase cannot succeed or would be misled into a false conclusion with no workaround; **major** = a real defect that costs an agent run, corrupts round records, or misleads the driver, with a workaround; **minor** = wrong but cheap; **nit** = cleanliness." The lines are drawn in money and wasted agent runs rather than in how wrong the code is.
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files says how a review of the harness is written or ranked. The nearest thing is a rule for gates inside a round, COMMON-GATE.txt: "A FAIL costs a retry of the producing step ($...) plus this gate again: FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that". That governs a gate judging an artifact, not a reviewer judging the harness, so the choice was open.
Other context: Review 2 adopted the same scale without restating it ("Severity scale as in review 1"), and reviews 3, 4 and 5 transcribed it in turn, so every fix ordering in the whole bootstrap rests on this one decision. Both early reviews reported zero blockers, which is what allowed the bootstrap to move from reviewing to real-agent testing.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md sections 1 and 2; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 1; commit 8ed4870.

## JC-131: A sensible departure from the plan is not a finding, but a wrong plan is named (from JC-R12-2)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 1 item 6 and section 4.
Context: The implementer had been told to build what bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md said. In several places the code did something different. The reviewer had to decide whether a departure from the plan was in itself a defect worth writing down.
Rationale: Quoted: "**Plan deviations**: FINAL-PLAN amendments 1-10 and the merged-B sections they modify, checked against the implementation; where the implementation deviated sensibly it is not a finding; where the plan itself was wrong it is said." Section 4 then lists four departures as "Sensible deviations, no action" and two places where the plan itself was blamed.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; they never mention the bootstrap plan at all. What left the choice open is bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, which says "Where this file and merged-B disagree, this file wins" but never says what to do when the finished code disagrees with both.
Other context: This is what made section 4's four "no action" lines possible, among them the suite taking seven minutes where the plan had said four and the three documents quoting three different figures, all of which the reviewer called "both acceptable" rather than findings. Review 2 kept the same policy, adding only "R1's list of sensible deviations stands."
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md sections 1 and 4; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 5; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md.

## JC-132: Reading the spirit of an amendment so a missing test became an acceptable warning (from JC-R12-3)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 4, first bullet of "Sensible deviations, no action".
Context: The base plan asked for a test that the runner's source and the owner's prose share no eight-word window. The implementer had built it as a warning printed by the `doctor` command instead (JC-125). The reviewer had to decide whether a warning satisfied an instruction that said "test".
Rationale: Quoted: "the 8-word prose/source overlap check is a `doctor` warning (`doctor.py:44-60`), not a test (merged-B 3.6 wanted a test; FINAL-PLAN amendment 6's spirit is the warning)." The reviewer read the amendment's intent rather than its letter and let the deviation stand.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The conflict is between two bootstrap documents: FINAL-PLAN amendment 6 says wording-adjacent checks are warnings, not test failures, and lists what tests may fail on, while the base plan section it amends asked for this particular check as a test. Amendment 6 does not name the eight-word check, so whether it was covered was genuinely open.
Other context: The check survived as a warning; nothing later reversed this. The owner's own concern that the harness must survive edits to the prose (SPEC.md: "Everything else in the repository is derived from them and must survive arbitrary edits to them") is what made the question worth asking at all.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 4; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendment 6.

## JC-133: Ranking the merge-attempt stray bug major rather than a blocker (from JC-R12-4)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M1 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: On a landing merge the runner was reporting the other round's changes as files the agent had wrongly touched, which failed the attempt. The reviewer had to decide whether this stopped the next phase (a blocker under its own scale) or merely cost money (major).
Rationale: Quoted: "The merge commit is made anyway, so attempt 3 (a full-price agent run that has nothing to do) passes and the round converges; the cost is one wasted attempt, one spurious `failures` increment toward `maxFailuresBeforeStop`, and a finding that tells the agent it changed files it never touched." Because the round still finishes, the reviewer priced it as waste rather than as a dead end.
Spec conflict: None found; no owner file describes merge attempts. The owner's project.yaml sets `maxFailuresBeforeStop: 3`, which is why one spurious failure mattered enough to weigh at all.
Other context: The fix was made in commit 6086792 (JC-149) and re-verified in review 2. Review 2 then found a second half of the same problem in the gate's view of the diff (JC-170, JC-182), which the reviewer of review 1 had not looked for.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M1); harness/project.yaml; commit 6086792.

## JC-134: Blaming the plan for the lost merge record, and accepting a known cost in the fix (from JC-R12-5)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M3 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: When an agent left conflicts unresolved, the next command threw away the whole record of that attempt. The reviewer had to say whose mistake it was, and its proposed fix created a second effect it had to decide whether to accept.
Rationale: Quoted on blame: "The plan is at fault here: merged-B 2.9 step 4(a) stated exactly this condition; the implementer followed it." Quoted on the side effect: "Consequence to accept: the following attempt's prompt commit is then skipped by `commit()` while the merge is in progress, which is what PROCESS.md:124 already promises". The reviewer chose to keep the half-finished merge alive and pay for it with a missing commit rather than lose the agent's work.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The base plan's own words, "the merge stays in progress" (its section 2.12), sat against its section 2.9 step 4(a), which treated exactly that state as unexpected and aborted it; the plan contradicted itself and the reviewer had to pick a side.
Other context: The session that fixed this went the other way on the central question (JC-150): it aborts the merge and commits the record, so the reviewer's "consequence to accept" never arose. Review 2 re-traced the whole area and called the fixer's deviation right.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M3) and section 4; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md sections 2.9 and 2.12; commit 6086792.

## JC-135: Deciding what belongs in the diff a gate is asked to judge (from JC-R12-6)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M4 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: The file handed to a code gate as "the artifact" contained the runner's own bookkeeping: the round's state file, its history, the producer's prompt and the producer's result, next to the real code change. The reviewer had to decide what a gate should be shown, and what should be dropped.
Rationale: Quoted: "A gate whose prose says 'Line by line: is it not sensible? Fail.' is invited to judge STATE.json." The reviewer read the owner's gate prose as meaning the gate will judge whatever it is given, so anything the producer did not write must be kept out. It deliberately left one part open: "If the judgment-call lines are wanted in the diff, add the two judgment files explicitly."
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE.txt says only "Line by line: * Is it not sensible? Fail. * Is it outside of the spec? Fail." and never says what "it" is. AGENTS.md says "Your system prompt names your step, your inputs, where to write your artifact", which leaves the runner to decide what the inputs are.
Other context: The fix removed the whole round folder and did not add the judgment-call files back (JC-152). Review 2 checked the result and reported "DIFF_FILE without the round folder" as sound.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M4); harness/locked_prose/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE.txt; commit 6086792.

## JC-136: Refusing the command rather than making the flag work (from JC-R12-7)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M5 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: Starting a round in a separate worktree took the owner's settings from what had been pushed to the server, not from the checkout the owner was sitting in, so a gate the owner had just switched on stayed off. There were two ways out: carry the owner's edited files into the new worktree, or refuse to start until the edit is committed and pushed.
Rationale: Quoted: "Fix: in worktree mode refuse when any spec file in `root` differs from `origin/<main>` ('commit and push the spec change first'), and reserve `--accept-spec` for `--no-branch` or for drift that is already committed on the target". The reviewer chose a loud refusal over silently copying the owner's work, matching the harness's stated habit that unknown situations stop the command rather than guess.
Spec conflict: None found. No owner file mentions worktrees or the `--accept-spec` flag. What left it open is that harness/project.yaml holds the gate switches the owner flips, and nothing says which copy of that file a round reads.
Other context: The fix went wider than the finding (JC-153), and review 2 later asked for the refusal's wording to mention pulling as well, which commit 94a6095 did.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M5) and section 5 note 2; commits 6086792 and 94a6095.

## JC-137: Freezing the machine-local settings at the moment a round starts
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M6 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2, and the session that fixed it in `round.start`, commit 6086792.
Context: The machine-local settings file harness/local.yaml is deliberately not committed, so a round running in a fresh worktree never saw it, while two documents told the owner it was the place to put such settings. Every local override - the resolved `claude` path, a forced rung - would vanish for the round.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix: in `start`, after `worktree add`, copy `<main_root>/harness/local.yaml` into the worktree when it exists (it stays ignored, so it is never committed); one `shutil.copyfile`. Say in PROCESS.md that a worktree round reads the copy taken at `start`." The reviewer chose a one-line copy plus a sentence of documentation over changing where the runner reads settings from, and accepted that the round's local settings are then a snapshot that later edits in the main checkout will not reach.
Spec conflict: None found; harness/local.yaml is not one of the owner's files and is not named in spec.yaml. What left it open was the documents' promise, not the owner's files.
Other context: Implemented as written, together with the sentence now at harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 27: "A worktree round reads the copy of `harness/local.yaml` that `start` takes from the checkout it runs in; edit the copy to change the round's local config." A related guard from the same commit refuses `--no-branch` from a linked worktree, so the two checkout modes cannot be confused.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M6); `round.start`; `config.load`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 27; harness/tests/test_concurrency.py::test_worktree_round_reads_the_local_yaml_copied_at_start; commit 6086792.
Merged from: JC-R12-8, JC-CODE-67

## JC-138: Reporting a guard bug that only bites when the driver is careless (from JC-R12-9)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-M7 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: A safety check meant to protect an agent's unsaved work only protected it when every changed file was in scope; a single stray file anywhere caused the whole tree, in-scope work included, to be wiped. The reviewer had to decide whether a guard that only fires when the driver skips a step deserved to be a major finding.
Rationale: Quoted: "This only bites when the driver runs `next` before `record`, but that is the guard's whole purpose." The reviewer weighed the narrow trigger against the fact that the narrow trigger is the only case the guard exists for, and reported it at major with a one-word fix.
Spec conflict: None found. AGENTS.md says "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner", which puts the agent's unsaved work in the runner's care, but says nothing about what the runner does with a stray file.
Other context: Fixed exactly as suggested (JC-160). The reviewer also carried it into its notes for the next phase as "Always `record` before another `next`", and review 2 restated the same advice.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M7) and section 5 note 6; commit 6086792.

## JC-139: Making the runner tolerant of an agent that answers in the wrong shape
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-m2 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2, and the review-1 fixer in `schemas.SCHEMAS["RESULT"]` and `round.claimed_calls`, commit 6e98e69.
Context: Producers and gates both report judgment calls under the same key name, but one is asked for counts and the other for lines of text. A producer that answered in the gate's shape had its work thrown away and the run charged as a failure. The choice was between tightening the instructions to agents and loosening what the runner accepts.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Two contracts with the same key name and different shapes is a foot-gun for real agents. Fix: accept `int` or `list` in RESULT and count a list (`schemas.py:121`)". The fix commit records the same decision: "a producer's judgment_calls may be counts or the gate's lists; the runner counts the files, so the message's shape is not an infra error." The reviewer chose leniency in the runner over stricter prose, on the ground that the shape clash is the harness's own fault.
Spec conflict: The owner's AGENTS.md requires the record but not its shape: "append a small record with context inside your round's UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md file". COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt likewise says only "Note the outcome and rationale for any DEFINED JUDGMENT CALL you make". Neither says anything about counts in a final message, so the shape was the runner's own invention and the runner could relax it freely.
Other context: The decision rests on the runner counting the files rather than trusting the message, which makes the message's shape cosmetic; the counts are still compared against the files and a mismatch is flagged (JC-277). A sentence was added to the process document: "The producer's `judgment_calls` are counts, and a gate-shaped list of lines is tolerated; the runner counts the files, not the message."
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m2) and section 5 note 5; harness/AGENTS.md; `schemas.SCHEMAS["RESULT"]`; `round.claimed_calls`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 70; commit 6e98e69.
Merged from: JC-R12-10, JC-CODE-24

## JC-140: Letting a producer's silence close a gate's finding
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-m3 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2, and the review-1 fixer in `round.apply_resolutions` and the plumbing text `plumbing/producer.txt`, commit 6086792; the prompt wording extended by the fix-3 fixer, commit 1849698.
Context: The producer's instructions said every gate finding "must appear in your resolutions", but nothing checked it, so a producer could ignore a finding and the round would move on with it still open. The choice was between making the rule real and making the instruction honest.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Either enforce (one blocking S1-style finding listing the ids) or, cheaper, treat omitted ids as `fixed` with a HISTORY note and say so in the prompt." The reviewer declined to pick outright but attached a price label to one option, which in practice decided it; the fix commit records the outcome: "findings omitted from resolutions count as fixed with a HISTORY line." Blocking would have spent another full agent run on a bookkeeping slip.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; the "must appear" sentence is in the runner's own plumbing text, not in the owner's prose. COMMON-GATE.txt only says a gate must "Rule on each disputed finding before raising new ones", which is the gate's duty, not the producer's, and nothing says what an unmentioned finding means.
Other context: The effect is that silence from a producer now closes a gate's finding, which is a meaningful change in who has the last word: an agent that silently drops a finding is recorded as having fixed it, and only the FLAG in HISTORY shows otherwise. Only open gate and owner findings are swept this way; mechanical findings never needed a resolution entry, and the template now says so. A dispute of a settled finding, or of a finding that is not a gate's, is ignored with a FLAG rather than refused, for the same reason.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m3); `round.apply_resolutions`; `plumbing/producer.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 75-78; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_findings_omitted_from_resolutions_count_as_fixed; commits 6086792 and 1849698.
Merged from: JC-R12-11, JC-R12-26, JC-CODE-44

## JC-141: Deleting two documented settings rather than making them work
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-m7 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2, and the review-1 fixer in `config.py`, `gitops.py` and harness/docs/PROCESS.md, commit 6e98e69.
Context: Two settings, a git timeout and a git identity, were defined in the code and listed in the process document, but nothing read them. The choice was between wiring them up and taking them away.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Either wire them (two lines in `config.load`) or delete both keys and the doc mentions; deleting is simpler." The fix commit records the act - "R1-m7: gitTimeoutSeconds and gitIdentity did nothing; deleted with their PROCESS.md mentions" - and the choice it embodies is that a configuration key the code ignores is worse than no key at all, because it promises control the owner does not have.
Spec conflict: None found. Neither key appears in the owner's project.yaml; both were the runner's own defaults, and the process document listed them among "runner keys with defaults in `config.DEFAULTS`", so nothing of the owner's was being taken away. That is what left the choice open.
Other context: The hard-coded values stayed: the runner always commits as `shackles-runner <runner@shackles.local>` with a fixed 300-second timeout per git command, and the owner cannot change either without editing gitops.py, which is a living, charged file. The fixer also removed the now-unreachable function that would have applied the identity.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m7); harness/project.yaml; `gitops.py` `IDENTITY`, `TIMEOUT`, `git_proc`; `config.py` (absence of the keys); harness/docs/PROCESS.md; merged-B 2.3; commit 6e98e69.
Merged from: JC-R12-12, JC-R12-29, JC-CODE-5

## JC-142: Calling a fragile design a documentation problem and telling the fixer not to refactor
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-m8 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2; the sentence written by the implementer in `specguard.INSTRUCTION`, commit 7c4c6ea, and extended by the review-1 fixer, commit 6e98e69.
Context: Documents told the owner that renaming a step meant editing one file, when in fact the step names are spelled out in seven files and the test fixtures. The reviewer had to decide whether to ask for the code to be restructured so the promise became true, or to change the promise. The failing guard's message is also where a reviewer is told what "reviewed" means, which is the guard's whole purpose.
Rationale: Quoted: "It is loud (lint refuses `start`), never silent, so this is a documentation fix: name the files, or, if wanted later, hoist the per-step special cases ... behind `pipeline.Step` fields (`inputs`, `writes`), which would leave `pipeline.py` as the single place. Do not do the latter now." The reviewer weighed the fact that the failure is noisy against the size of the refactor and explicitly forbade the larger change at this point. The fix commit records the result: "the spec-change instruction and PROCESS.md name every file a step rename touches."
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md says of the spec files "Everything else in the repository is derived from them and must survive arbitrary edits to them", and harness/project.yaml is one of those files, with the step and gate names in its `gates:` block. A strict reading says a rename must be survivable; the reviewer read "survive" as "fail loudly", not "need no code change". SPEC.md also says a change stands "until the change is reviewed and accepted" without saying what reviewing consists of.
Other context: The instruction now names the concrete files a structural rename reaches - pipeline.py and the step names in round.py, prompts.py, landing.py, checks.py, config.py, the stub and the fixtures - which is the closest the harness comes to enumerating its own coupling to the owner's vocabulary, and the companion to the wording-adjacent table (JC-129). Nobody went back and did the refactor.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m8) and section 7; SPEC.md; harness/project.yaml; `specguard.INSTRUCTION`; harness/tests/test_spec_baseline.py; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 32; harness/docs/TESTING.md "Baseline procedure"; merged-B 2.13; commit 6e98e69.
Merged from: JC-R12-13, JC-CODE-80

## JC-143: Narrowing a blanket rule so paid work is not thrown away (from JC-R12-14)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-m12 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: When an agent's final message was badly formed, the runner undid everything that agent had changed before anyone could look at it. The reviewer had to decide whether that was right for every kind of agent.
Rationale: Quoted: "For gates this is G1 and right. For producers it throws away paid work because of a formatting slip; merged-B decision 13 chose 'rerun the same attempt' *because it is cheaper than a retry cycle*, and the revert cancels that saving. Fix: revert only for gates". The reviewer read the plan's own reasoning and concluded that the code defeated it.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The plan's decision 13 gives the reason the reviewer leaned on: "a fresh agent on the same prompt is cheaper than a retry cycle" (JC-18). The owner's files say only, in AGENTS.md, that "Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths", which is about scope, not about undoing work.
Other context: This is the one finding of review 1 that was not fixed; the decision to skip it is JC-159. Review 2 records "m12 skipped by agreement" and, when a costlier defect appeared in the same area, "it makes R2-M1 more expensive than it needs to be, which is one more reason to fix R2-M1 first". It remains unfixed at the commit audited.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m12); bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 and 3; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md decision 13.

## JC-144: Calling a test the plan asked for ceremony, and asking for its removal (from JC-R12-15)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, finding R1-n4 in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 2.
Context: The suite contained a test asserting that the documents stayed under fixed line counts. The reviewer had to decide whether a test that the plan had called for, and that was currently passing, earned its place.
Rationale: Quoted: "`test_docs.py:41-46` asserts line counts of the docs (PROCESS <= 200, DRIVER <= 80 ...); this is ceremony from the plan, not a property of the software. Drop it, or keep only the one-sentence-per-line rule if that is wanted." The reviewer weighed the test against what it protects and judged that it protects nothing anyone cares about.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files never mention document length. AGENTS.md does say "Be dense, not clever", and COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt repeats it, which is the nearest thing to an argument for keeping the test; the reviewer did not treat it as one.
Other context: Commit 6e98e69 deleted the test entirely, keeping nothing. The documents have grown since: harness/docs/PROCESS.md is now 167 lines, still inside the old limit, so nothing was lost in practice.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-n4); harness/AGENTS.md; commit 6e98e69.

## JC-145: Passing judgment on the only two edits made to the owner's own files (from JC-R12-16)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 3 "The two spec edits (commit 9a13bb2)".
Context: The implementer had changed two of the owner's locked prose files, one word in each, because both referred to a setting that does not exist (JC-83). The owner's standing instruction is that such edits happen only when genuinely necessary and minimally, so somebody had to judge whether these two passed that test.
Rationale: Quoted: "Necessary and minimal. ... The zero-edit alternative, a runner default or a derived context key, would render one number for both the plan and the spec sentence while the owner has deliberately split the two rates (`planCostPerWord`, `specCostPerWord`); the edit replaces each stale token with the owner's own key for that sentence, one token per file, in its own commit with the diff in the body ... Nothing smaller exists that keeps the owner's split."
Spec conflict: The rule being applied is the owner's, in spec.yaml: "Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly." The words "genuinely necessary" and "minimally" are what the reviewer had to interpret; the owner gives no test for either.
Other context: The edits replaced `{{ project.ownerReviewCostPerWord }}` with `{{ project.planCostPerWord }}` and `{{ project.specCostPerWord }}` in CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt and PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt. Both stand today, and they are the only agent-made change to an owner file in the whole bootstrap. The reviewer also checked that the waiver file was empty, "as it should be".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 3; spec.yaml; commit 9a13bb2; harness/locked_prose/CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt and PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt.

## JC-146: Telling the next phase to run in a throwaway copy rather than the real repository (from JC-R12-17)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 5 note 1.
Context: The next phase was going to drive a real round with real agents. The reviewer had to decide where that should happen, knowing that a round in the real repository pushes branches to the shared server and eventually merges into the main line.
Rationale: Quoted: "**Use the sandbox, not the repository.** `sandbox --dir <a folder outside any git repository, e.g. %TEMP%\shackles-sb>` builds `repo/` and `origin.git`; a `start` without `--no-branch` in the real repository pushes `round/NNNN` and later `main` to GitHub, and `--no-branch` from a linked worktree hits R1-m11."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files describe the harness, not how it should be exercised. What left the choice open is that two of the reviewer's own findings, R1-M5 and R1-m11, made both in-repository routes awkward at the time of writing.
Other context: This shaped the entire test phase: all six real-agent tests ran in sandboxes, and the coordinating session later made it a standing rule (JC-465). Review 2 repeated and strengthened the advice ("Decide where the task runs, and always pass `--root`") and added the observation that a defect it found, R2-M2, is "Not reachable in the sandbox", so the choice also hid one problem from testing (JC-164).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 5 note 1; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 (R2-M2) and 7 note 1.

## JC-147: Certifying a long list as sound so the next reviewer would not repeat it (from JC-R12-18)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 6, headed "Verified sound (the next reviewer need not repeat)".
Context: The reviewer had exercised far more of the harness than it had found fault with. It had to decide how much of that to certify, knowing the certificate would be believed and would decide what the next reviewer spent its budget on.
Rationale: The heading is the reasoning: the list exists to tell the next reader what not to look at again. It covers around fifteen areas, including the claim "**No mechanical behaviour depends on prose wording**", which is the owner's chief stated concern, and the full routing table, concurrency, costs, Windows behaviour and the documents.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files do not describe reviews. What left the choice open is that nothing set a standard for how much evidence makes a "verified" claim; the reviewer had run the suite once and read the code.
Other context: Review 2 acted on it: "it builds on `review-1.md` and does not repeat that review's 'verified sound' list", and headed its own section 4 "Verified and found sound (beyond review 1's list)". Review 2 nonetheless found two majors in areas review 1 had walked through, which shows what the certificate cost.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1, 3 and 4.

## JC-148: Judging the leftover coupling to the owner's files acceptable (from JC-R12-19)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 1, in bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md, section 7 "Brittleness to changes in the owner's prose or config", the table and the closing sentence.
Context: The owner's stated worry is that the harness must keep working when the owner rewrites the prose or the settings. The reviewer built a table of sixteen kinds of owner change, labelled each "loud", "soft" or "silent", and then had to say whether what was left was good enough.
Rationale: Quoted: "Nothing in the mechanics matches a phrase of the prose; the residual coupling is names (files, steps, keys) and it fails loudly except for the four rows marked silent, each covered by a minor finding or a one-sentence doc note above." The reviewer accepted four silent failure modes on the grounds that each had a written note against it.
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md is absolute: "Everything else in the repository is derived from them and must survive arbitrary edits to them". "Survive" is not defined, and the reviewer's three-way loud/soft/silent scale is its own reading of it.
Other context: Review 2 kept the table and added nine sharpened rows, including one that reports the harness's own source being a living path as "silent" (JC-174). Reviews 3, 4 and 5 each kept a table of the same shape. Nothing in the review chain reversed any of the four accepted silent rows.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 7; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 6; SPEC.md.

## JC-149: Fixing the merge-attempt strays by filtering the list of changed files, not by the method the plan gave
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in the new `Round.snapshot` method of harness/src/shackles/round.py; extended to the gate's diff by the review-2 fixer in commit fd6d47a.
Context: When a sibling round has landed, the merge brings its changes into the worktree; measured against the step's own start, every one of the sibling's files looks like a stray the agent wrote. The plan had said the runner should build a tree from the agent's work and compare that tree against the automatically merged tree. The reviewer had instead offered a shorter route: take the list of dirty files the runner already has and drop the ones that match the merged tree. The fixer had to choose, and had to decide what to do with files git does not track at all.
Rationale: The commit body records the outcome, not the choice: "R1-M1: on a merge attempt the M1/M2 snapshot keeps only what differs from the automerge tree (plus untracked paths), so the sibling's own changes are never reported as strays (merged-B 2.10 as written)." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the reviewer's route touches two lines in a method that already exists and needs no extra git command, and an untracked file cannot be the other round's work by definition, so keeping untracked paths in is the safe side of the "plus" in the commit message. The review-2 fixer's addition is recorded as "after a merge commit the producer's step starts at the automerge tree, so the gate's DIFF_FILE never shows the sibling's changes."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; merge attempts are entirely the runner's invention. What left the choice open is that the plan's section 2.10 prescribes a mechanism ("compare the tree that `git write-tree` produces after `git add -A` against `merge_pending.automerge_tree`") while describing the goal in the same sentence ("so the sibling's changes never count"), and the two can be met separately.
Other context: Review 2 re-traced it and agreed: "conflicted files the agent resolved differ from the tree (which holds the markers) and stay in; untouched conflicted files drop out and L3 catches them. Correct." The commit's own claim that this is "merged-B 2.10 as written" is not accurate about the mechanism, only about the goal. Two parts of this audit disagreed about who wrote the mechanism: the code slice attributed `round.snapshot` to the implementer's commit 05509b7, the reviews slice to this commit. The repository settles it for the reviews slice - 05509b7's round.py contains no `snapshot` method, and 6086792 adds it - although 05509b7 did already read `merge_pending.automerge_tree` when restoring reverted files.
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M1); bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md section 2.10 and decision 9; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 3; `round.snapshot`, `round.record_producer`; `checks.m1_strays`, `checks.m2_frozen`, `checks.revert`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 130 and 133; commit fd6d47a.
Merged from: JC-R12-20, JC-CODE-42

## JC-150: Ending a half-finished merge rather than preserving it, against both the plan and the review
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in `Round.tree_state`, `Round.infra`, `Round.record_producer` and the new `gitops.abort_merge`.
Context: When an agent leaves conflict markers behind, the runner has to decide whether to keep the half-finished merge in the working tree until the next attempt, as the plan and the reviewer both wanted, or to unwind it and start it again next time.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "a merge attempt that does not end in the merge commit (L3, an invalid result, M0, UPSTREAM, BLOCKED) aborts the merge and commits its record as usual; `next` re-establishes the merge for the next attempt. This deviates from merged-B 2.12 ('the merge stays in progress'): keeping it would leave the runner's own uncommitted files to be flagged as M1 strays at the next record." The fixer also found and solved a practical obstacle: "`gitops.abort_merge` first restores index versions of merge-staged paths an agent edited, without which `git merge --abort` refuses".
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The conflict is inside the plan: its section 2.12 says the merge stays in progress, while its section 2.9 step 4(a) treats a merge in progress as unexpected and aborts it.
Other context: The reviewer's own proposal was the opposite ("That preserves the merge and the uncommitted round files", JC-134), so the fixer overrode the reviewer on the central point while using the reviewer's one-line change to the condition. Review 2 checked it and accepted: "deviating from merged-B 2.12 for the reason the commit message gives". The instruction to agents follows from the decision: `plumbing/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION.txt` tells a merge attempt to "resolve exactly those files, keep both intents, run verify, and run no git command at all; the runner stages and commits the merge."
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M3); bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md sections 2.9 and 2.12; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 3 and 5; `round.record_producer`, `round.infra`, `round.tree_state`, `round.prepare_attempt`; `gitops.abort_merge`; `plumbing/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 132.
Merged from: JC-R12-21, JC-CODE-41

## JC-151: Rewriting a check nobody had reported, inside a commit answering reported findings
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, the new `Round.runner_head` method and the reordering of `Round.record`; the regex anchor made explicit by the review-2 fixer in commit 94a6095.
Context: While fixing the merge findings the fixer noticed that the check meant to catch an agent committing on its own (JC-31) compared the current commit against the prompt commit, and that the runner's own commits moved things past it. It had to decide whether to widen the commit's scope, and what to compare against instead.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "Found on the way: M0 compared HEAD against the prompt commit, but the runner's own commits while an attempt is pending (an invalid result, `approve` at an infra checkpoint) move HEAD past it, so every infra rerun ended in a spurious M0 soft reset, M1 reverting the runner's files and a phantom failure ... M0 now compares against the runner's newest commit (`runner_head`), runs before anything is booked, and on a merge attempt hard-resets to it; the snapshot precedes every HISTORY write of a record."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not describe commits by the runner. AGENTS.md does supply the principle the check enforces, "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner", but gives the runner no way to tell its own commits from an agent's.
Other context: The mechanism chosen is a commit-message convention - every runner commit is titled `round NNNN: ...` and `runner_head` finds the newest one with a grep over the log - which makes the harness's own commit titles load-bearing, the price of not storing a sha in STATE. Review 2 singled this out for re-examination and concluded it "is sound on every path that creates a non-runner commit", leaving one residual worry that became R2-n3 (JC-183).
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1 and 3; harness/AGENTS.md; `round.runner_head`, `round.record`, `round.commit`, `round.snapshot`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 102; commit 94a6095.
Merged from: JC-R12-22, JC-CODE-40

## JC-152: Leaving the judgment-call files out of the diff the gate reads (from JC-R12-23)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in `Round.render_step`.
Context: The reviewer had asked for the round's own folder to be removed from the diff a code gate is given (JC-135), and had left one question open: whether the two judgment-call files, which live in that folder and are written by the agent under judgment, should be put back.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says only "R1-M4: the gate's DIFF_FILE excludes the round folder." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the gate prompt already lists the two judgment-call files among its named inputs, so adding them to the diff as well would duplicate them; and the smaller change is the one that answers the finding exactly.
Spec conflict: The owner's AGENTS.md tells every agent to "append a small record with context inside your round's UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md file" and COMMON-GATE.txt tells a gate "You see the artifact, its inputs, the intent, and prior findings with their resolutions" without saying whether the judgment-call record is part of the artifact or part of the inputs.
Other context: Review 2 read the resulting prompt as an agent would and reported it sound. The judgment-call files remain in the gate's list of inputs, so a gate can still open them - and two gate agents in test E did exactly that, quoting the file's header line in findings of their own (JC-409, JC-414).
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M4); harness/locked_prose/COMMON-GATE.txt; harness/AGENTS.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 4.

## JC-153: Making the start guard wider and unconditional
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in the `start` function of harness/src/shackles/round.py; the remedy added to the message by the review-2 fixer, commit 94a6095.
Context: A worktree round is created from the remote's main branch, not from the checkout the owner is editing, so a spec change sitting uncommitted or unpushed would silently not be in the round. The reviewer asked that starting a round in a worktree be refused in that case (JC-136). The fixer had to decide how far the guard reached: only the prose and settings files, or also the list of those files and the file of accepted hashes; and whether the guard runs always or only when a flag is used.
Rationale: Not recorded beyond the effect. The commit body says "R1-M5: a worktree round refuses while a spec file, `spec.yaml` or the baseline differs from origin/<mainBranch>." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the round is created from the server's copy of the branch, so anything the round will read and any record of what has been accepted must match, or the round starts on a different set of the owner's files than the owner is looking at.
Spec conflict: The owner's spec.yaml names its own files and says "The files listed here are 'the spec.yaml files'", so extending the guard to spec.yaml itself and to the runner's baseline file goes past that list. Nothing in the owner's files forbids it or asks for it. SPEC.md's own guard covers drift from the baseline, not drift from origin, which is a different way for a round to run on the wrong spec.
Other context: The refusal text became a real obstacle later: review 2 pointed out it also fires when the checkout is merely behind, and commit 94a6095 added "(or pull, when origin is ahead)". Both reviews' notes to the next phase then had to explain the required order: edit, accept, commit, push, start, which DRIVER.md line 23 now spells out. The cost is that an owner who forgot to push cannot start a round at all.
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-M5); spec.yaml; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-n7); `round.start`; `checks.e1_spec_edits`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 48; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 23; harness/tests/test_concurrency.py::test_worktree_start_refuses_spec_files_that_differ_from_origin; commit 94a6095.
Merged from: JC-R12-24, JC-CODE-66

## JC-154: Correcting the stale file name rather than removing it (from JC-R12-25)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in `prompts.step_context`.
Context: A gate's list of inputs named the producer's result file using the gate's own attempt number, so after any retry the path was wrong. The reviewer offered two repairs: pass the producer's real attempt number through, or drop the entry altogether because the same content is already pasted into the prompt.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says "R1-m1: the gate's inputs name the producer's own attempt." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a gate that wants to re-read the producer's message from disk can still do so, and a correct path costs one extra argument, whereas dropping the entry would silently narrow what the gate can reach.
Spec conflict: The owner's AGENTS.md says "Your system prompt names your step, your inputs, where to write your artifact, and the JSON your final message must be", which requires the inputs to be named but does not say which inputs.
Other context: Review 2 confirmed the result while reading the rendered prompt: "SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE (inputs name the producer's own attempt ...)". It is one detail of the wider inputs design (JC-102).
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m1); harness/AGENTS.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 4.

## JC-155: Having the runner guess at the driver's available agent types from its own folder (from JC-R12-27)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in `Round.action`.
Context: The definitions that give a spawned sub-agent its model and its restricted tools only register when the chat session was started inside the repository. The reviewer asked the runner to warn when that is likely to be wrong. The fixer had to decide what evidence the runner would use, since the runner cannot see the chat session at all.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says "R1-m5: an `agent_type` warning when the definition is absent under cwd". The warning text it wrote asserts a conclusion: "no .claude/agents/<type>.md under <cwd>, so a session started here has no such type (definitions register at session start from the session's project directory); spawn general-purpose with model <alias>".
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files do not mention agent definitions. AGENTS.md only says the driver should "give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent (read-only for gates)", leaving how the sub-agent is made to the runner and the driver.
Other context: This was reversed during the test rounds: the guess was wrong in the sandbox, and commit 6b82c29 replaced it with a plain second name in the action (JC-208), after which the process document says the opposite of the guess - "the runner cannot see the session's agent list, so it never guesses which applies".
Sources: commit 6086792; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m5); commit 6b82c29; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 61.

## JC-156: Deleting a second do-nothing flag that no finding had named (from JC-R12-28)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6086792, in harness/src/shackles/commands.py.
Context: The reviewer had asked for one do-nothing command-line flag to be removed, `doctor --json`. A second flag of exactly the same kind, `status --json`, existed and had not been reported. The fixer had to decide whether the finding was about that one flag or about the pattern.
Rationale: Not recorded; the commit body says only "R1-n2: the no-op --json flags", plural. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): every command already prints JSON, so both flags mislead in the same way, and leaving one behind would invite the same finding next time.
Spec conflict: None found. Neither flag appears in the owner's files.
Other context: The consequence is small but real: a driver that had typed `status --json` now gets a usage error instead of the output. Neither the reviewer of review 2 nor any later round objected.
