## JC-157: Cleaning up leaked test folders at exit, and letting the cleanup fail quietly (from JC-R12-30)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6e98e69, in harness/tests/fixtures.py and harness/src/shackles/agents.py.
Context: Each run of the test suite left about seventy throwaway repositories behind in the machine's temporary folder. The reviewer named two possible repairs: register a removal to run when the process ends, or restructure the fixture so the test framework owns the folder. The fixer also had to decide what happens when the removal itself fails, which on Windows it can.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says "R1-m6: the suite's template repositories are removed at exit; probe_cli removes its prompt folder." The code swallows the failure: `except OSError: pass`. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the at-exit hook is a two-line change that leaves the caching of built repositories untouched, and a cleanup that raised at interpreter shutdown would turn a tidiness problem into a test failure.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files say nothing about the test suite's temporary files.
Other context: Review 2 confirmed the effect by measurement: "this review's run left none", and noted that the seventy-eight folders it did find predated the fix.
Sources: commit 6e98e69; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m6); bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1 and 3.

## JC-158: Answering a hidden coupling with a sentence rather than a check (from JC-R12-31)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 1's findings, in commit 6e98e69, in harness/docs/PROCESS.md.
Context: Two things are pinned to the fixed path `harness/archives/` regardless of what the owner sets: the git attribute that merges the round index (JC-87), and the file of accepted hashes (JC-65). The reviewer offered either a sentence in the documents or a warning computed by the `doctor` command. The fixer picked.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says "R1-m10: PROCESS.md says the union attribute and the baseline are pinned to harness/archives/." The sentence added is: "The union attribute and the spec baseline are pinned to `harness/archives/` whatever `roundPaths.folder` or `archivesPath` say, so an owner who moves the rounds folder out of `archives/` moves the `.gitattributes` line with it." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a sentence costs nothing and the situation it warns about needs the owner to do something the runner could not do for them anyway.
Spec conflict: The owner's harness/project.yaml defines both `archivesPath: "archives/"` and `roundPaths.folder: "archives/rounds/NNNN/"`, so the owner plainly expects to be able to move them; SPEC.md says the rest of the repository "must survive arbitrary edits" to those files. The fix documents the exception instead of removing it.
Other context: Review 1 had already listed this row of its brittleness table as one of the four accepted "silent" failures (JC-148). Review 2 did not reopen it.
Sources: commit 6e98e69; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md section 2 (R1-m10) and section 7; harness/project.yaml; SPEC.md.

## JC-159: Leaving one finding unfixed
Who and where: the sessions that fixed review 1's findings, across commits 6086792 and 6e98e69, which never mention R1-m12; recorded second-hand in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1 and 3, and treated by the first coordinating session as a standing example.
Context: Review 1 had asked that a producer's work no longer be thrown away when its final message is malformed, leaving the revert only for gates (JC-143). Review 1 raised twenty-four findings in all, and the session and its fixer had to decide whether every one was worth fixing before the next review. That change was not made, and the omission was deliberate rather than missed.
Rationale: Not recorded by the fixers. Review 2 records only that it was agreed: "every R1 finding (M1-M7, m1-m11, n1-n5; m12 skipped by agreement)" and "**m12** skipped as agreed; it makes R2-M1 more expensive than it needs to be, which is one more reason to fix R2-M1 first." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): keeping one rule for producers and gates is simpler to reason about and to test, and the cost it was accused of - a wasted rerun on a behaviour change to a rarely-hit path - was believed rare before review 2 found a defect that made it common.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, which are silent on how a malformed agent message is handled. What left the choice open is the plan's stated reason for rerunning an attempt at all, "a fresh agent on the same prompt is cheaper than a retry cycle", which the revert partly cancels but does not contradict in words.
Other context: This is the earliest recorded instance of the pattern the final report later formalises as "findings the fixers skipped, with the reason". The next review reported the cost of the skip rather than re-raising it, which is the behaviour the standing rule for reviews asks for (JC-253). The entry for R1-m12 also does not appear in review 2's list of fixes verified, which is where it would otherwise have been recorded; it remains unfixed at the commit audited.
Sources: commits 6086792 and 6e98e69; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md finding R1-m12; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1, 2 and 3; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md decision 13; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 5.
Merged from: JC-R12-32, JC-COORD-15

## JC-160: `next` refuses when any dirty path lies inside the pending attempt's write paths (from JC-CODE-60)
Who and where: the review-1 fixer, in `round.tree_state`, commit 6086792.
Context: `next` finds a dirty tree either because an agent's work has not been recorded yet or because something crashed. merged-B 2.9 said to refuse when "every dirty path" is inside the pending step's write paths and to reset otherwise.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "`next` refuses (instead of resetting) when any dirty path lies inside the pending attempt's write paths."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not describe recovery. What left it open is that merged-B's "every dirty path" would reset an agent's real work the moment one unrelated file was also dirty - the defect review 1 reported as R1-M7 (JC-138).
Other context: This inverts the plan's quantifier, and the safer direction was chosen: work is never destroyed without `--discard`. The refusal names the step, the attempt and the remedy. A dirty tree that touches nothing in the pending scope is still reset, scoped and counted (JC-115).
Sources: `round.tree_state`, `round.pending_allowed`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 49; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 44; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_pending_work_refused_without_discard; commit 6086792.

## JC-161: How deeply to re-check the previous round's fixes before looking for new faults (from JC-R12-33)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, a fresh read-only session, in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 1 item 1 and section 3.
Context: Review 2 arrived after two commits had changed the runner in answer to review 1. The reviewer had a fixed budget and had to split it between re-checking twenty-four old findings and hunting for new ones, and had to decide which of the fixes deserved more than a reading.
Rationale: Quoted: "**The two fix commits first.** Read `6086792` and `6e98e69` against every R1 finding (M1-M7, m1-m11, n1-n5; m12 skipped by agreement): is each fix present, correct, complete, and does it introduce anything new? The M0 rework (`runner_head`) and the merge-attempt rework (abort and re-establish) got a full re-trace because both changed behaviour beyond the finding." The test applied was whether the fix had moved beyond what was asked; the rest were checked by reading and by the new tests.
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files describes reviewing, re-reviewing or when reading is enough.
Other context: The resulting verdict is section 3's opening, "All present and correct", and it is what let the bootstrap move to real-agent testing after one more fix round. The two reworks the reviewer chose to re-trace are exactly the two entries at JC-150 and JC-151, so the choice of what to re-trace was aimed at the right places.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 1 and 3; commit 6bbf15d.

## JC-162: Ranking the JSON extraction defect major on the last review before real agents ran (from JC-R12-34)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-M1 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.
Context: The runner picked the wrong object out of an agent's final message whenever the message carried a nested object of its own, which is exactly what a producer answering a gate, or a gate ruling on a dispute, has to send. The reviewer knew this was the last review before real agents ran and had to decide whether this rose to the level that stops the next phase.
Rationale: Quoted on the cost: "The cost is a full producer rerun per occurrence (for a code step its work is reverted, R1-m12 as accepted) and, after `infraRetries`, an `infra` checkpoint blamed on the agent." Quoted on why it matters: it hits "exactly the messages the docs promise to tolerate ... and exactly the attempts that matter". The reviewer still put it at major, since a rerun is a workaround, and carried it into its notes as the first thing to check if anything went wrong.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; the shape of a final message is entirely the runner's. AGENTS.md says only "Your system prompt names ... the JSON your final message must be. Follow it exactly; the runner parses it."
Other context: Review 2's note to the next phase says "Apply the four-line fix before the gates-on run, or expect one rerun per occurrence", which is a stronger instruction than the severity label implies. It was fixed before the tests ran (JC-175).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-M1) and section 7 note 3; harness/AGENTS.md; commit dc035b7.

## JC-163: Proposing "the longest object" as the rule for finding an agent's answer (from JC-R12-35)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-M1 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.
Context: Having found that the runner's backwards scan picks a nested object, the reviewer had to propose a rule that reliably picks the whole message instead. Several rules would work: the outermost object, the last top-level object, the longest object.
Rationale: Quoted: "Fix (`schemas.py:221-229`, four lines): among the balanced objects that carry `status` or `verdict`, return the **longest** one (the outer object always contains its nested ones), e.g. collect `(end - start, obj)` over `starts` in forward order and return the max; keep the final any-dict fallback." The reviewer reasoned from containment: an object that contains another is longer than it.
Spec conflict: None found. The plan is what was wrong here, as the review itself says: "merged-B's 'the last balanced object' extraction rule (PROCESS.md:66) is the wrong rule for nested JSON; the plan is at fault, the implementer followed it" (JC-17).
Other context: The fixer did not take this rule; it rewrote the scan to skip nested objects and take the last top-level one (JC-175). The two rules differ when a message contains two complete answers: "longest" picks the bigger, "last top-level" picks the later.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 (R2-M1) and 5; commit dc035b7.

## JC-164: Reporting a deadlock the next phase could not reach (from JC-R12-36)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-M2 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.
Context: A round that edits one of the owner's files cannot get past the cleanup step on the harness's own repository, because the permanent suite contains the guard that fails on exactly such an edit, and the owner only approves the edit at a checkpoint that comes later. The reviewer had to decide whether to report at major a defect that the sandbox it had just recommended could never hit.
Rationale: Quoted: "Not reachable in the sandbox (its suite is the toy's), so the next phase only meets it if the tiny task runs on the real repository and an agent edits an owner file; but it is the harness's headline promise (PROCESS.md:139) and it deadlocks on its own repository." The reviewer weighed reachability against the promise being broken and chose the promise.
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md names this among the architecture features: "Spec guard: hashed baseline, failing test with diff, start refuses drift, edits checkpoint the owner." The owner's spec.yaml adds "Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly", so an agent edit is expected to happen and to be survivable. Neither says how the guard and the round's own test run are meant to coexist.
Other context: Review 2's note to the next phase turned the finding into an instruction: "avoid any task that touches an owner file (R2-M2)". It was fixed before any test round ran (JC-165).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 (R2-M2), 5, 6 and 7; SPEC.md; spec.yaml; commit dc035b7.

## JC-165: A round that edits an owner file provisionally re-accepts the baseline on its own branch
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2 proposed it in finding R2-M2; the session that fixed review 2's findings built it in the new `Round.e1` method and the `specguard.accept` call it makes, commit dc035b7.
Context: The permanent suite contains the drift test, and the checks at CLEANUP and LANDING run the permanent suite. A round that legitimately edits a spec file therefore makes its own suite red and can never land. There were several ways out: move the owner's checkpoint earlier, take the guard out of the permanent suite, widen what the cleanup step may change, or let the round record its own acceptance of the edit on its branch and have the owner confirm it later. Having chosen the last, the fixer still had to decide the exact trigger.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix (`round.py:882-885`, one line): when E1 records a new or changed edit, provisionally accept the baseline on the branch ... The snapshot was taken before `record_producer` runs, so M1 never sees the baseline files, `commit_work` commits them, the branch's guard is green, and the owner's `approve` at `spec-edit` re-accepts with their words exactly as today; `abandon` discards the branch and main's baseline is untouched." Quoted from the fix commit: "E1 accepts the branch's baseline provisionally whenever the set of spec-file edits changes, so M5 at CLEANUP and L2 at LANDING are green on a repository whose suite carries the baseline guard". Every acceptance is written into the audit file with the note "round NNNN: provisional, pending the owner's spec-edit checkpoint; edited: ...".
Spec conflict: The owner's spec.yaml requires that agents "flag every edit loudly" and SPEC.md lists "edits checkpoint the owner" as a feature; SPEC.md also says "a test fails whenever one of them changes until the change is reviewed and accepted", while the owner's review happens at the `spec-edit` checkpoint, after CLEANUP - inside the window in which the round still has to run the test. A machine-written acceptance sits close to that line.
Other context: This is the runner writing to the baseline on behalf of an agent's edit, before any human has seen it. The safety is that the acceptance is on the round branch only, the `spec-edit` checkpoint still fires before LANDING regardless of delegation (JC-68), and the owner's `approve` re-accepts with their words in the note. It is worth flagging that between the provisional acceptance and the checkpoint the guard is silent on that file. The sentence at harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 144 now records it; nothing later reversed it.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-M2); commit dc035b7; `round.e1` docstring and body; `specguard.accept`; `round.landing_step`, `round.approve`; spec.yaml; SPEC.md; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 144; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_spec_edit_keeps_the_branch_guard_green_until_the_owner_accepts.
Merged from: JC-R12-37, JC-R12-49, JC-CODE-31

## JC-166: A question nobody rules on stands on its assumption and is logged as an undefined judgment call
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-m2, and the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-M2; built by the review-2 fixer in `round.settle_question` and `round.record_gate`, commit fd6d47a, and extended by the fix-4 fixer in `round.skip_gate`, commit c1e7b8c.
Context: When a producer ends NEEDS-OWNER it states an assumption and the next gate is supposed to uphold or withdraw the question. A gate can return a verdict without mentioning it, and a gate can be skipped or overridden before it ever sees it. Nothing said what the runner should do with silence, or with a gate that never runs.
Rationale: Quoted from review 2: "The gate prompt says 'Omit rulings, needs_owner and judgment_calls when empty', so a real gate that considers the assumption fine and PASSes without a ruling is likely. ... Fix ... flag '{step} attempt {attempt}: no ruling on the producer's question; the assumption stands', append that line to `UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md` ... and clear `pending_question`." The review-2 fix commit states the rule: "a gate verdict that neither upholds nor withdraws the producer's question settles it: the assumption stands, logged as an undefined judgment call, and no later gate is asked to rule on it." Review 4 found the same hole on the skipped-gate path and asked for a line naming the producer, and the fix-4 commit records why it mattered: "skip_gate and skip_producer never touched pending_question, so the next gate that ran was asked another producer's question and, giving no ruling, earned that producer a false undefined line."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt covers only the two answers: "If the producer asked for the owner: uphold when spending the owner's time is sensible given their cost; otherwise withdraw with the answer to assume". It does not cover a gate that does neither, or one that does not run. COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt tells the producer "you never contact them yourself, the gate weighs the question", which assumes a gate exists. AGENTS.md supplies the rule used for the fallback: "when the spec, plan, code, tests, or docs conflict or fall silent on something that impacts behavior, the judgment call is UNDEFINED".
Other context: The line the runner writes names who assumed what and why nobody ruled, and lands in UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md - the runner writing an undefined judgment call on an agent's behalf, which AGENTS.md describes as something the agent does. This is the harness's own answer to a gap in its own prose, surfaced rather than hidden. Review 2's brittleness table records the coupling it creates: rewording the gate prose's "omit when empty" sentence makes the situation more common. The delegated-round branch of the same mechanism was later removed by review 5 (JC-373), leaving these two callers.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 (R2-m2) and 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-M2; harness/locked_prose/COMMON-GATE.txt; harness/AGENTS.md; `round.settle_question`, `round.skip_gate`, `round.record_gate`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 90; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_override_settles_or_drops_the_producers_open_question; commits fd6d47a and c1e7b8c.
Merged from: JC-R12-38, JC-R345-25, JC-CODE-45

## JC-167: When an override at a checkpoint resumes the round
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-m3, and the review-2 fixer in `round.owner_command`, commit fd6d47a.
Context: Every checkpoint message offered `override` as a way to continue, but running it printed the same checkpoint again. Sometimes an override removes the very thing the checkpoint is about; sometimes it is about a later step and the checkpoint still needs an answer.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix (two lines in `owner_command` after `self.override(...)`): resume when the checkpoint's step, or its producer for a gate, is now overridden ...; otherwise keep the checkpoint and say so in PROCESS.md:86 ('at a checkpoint, `override` of another step is followed by `approve`')." The fix commit repeats it: "override at a checkpoint resumes the round when the checkpoint's own step is now overridden (another step's override is followed by approve)." The reviewer split the behaviour by whether the override actually removes the reason the round stopped, and answered the rest with a sentence rather than code.
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt defines the word only as: "'override' + steps or gates the runner skips them this round; the plan itself cannot be skipped." Nothing says whether skipping a step also means carrying on from a stop.
Other context: When the checkpoint stands, the payload carries the note "overrides recorded; the checkpoint at <STEP> stands: approve to resume" under `warnings` and exits 10 again, so a driver reading only JSON still learns what happened. A `review` checkpoint can never be resumed this way, because its step is already accepted and therefore cannot be overridden. Test D's driver exercised exactly this sequence with a single owner sentence carrying two acts (JC-344). Later rounds tightened the same command twice more (JC-305).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m3); harness/locked_prose/CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt; `round.owner_command`, `round.override`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 89; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 40; commits fd6d47a and 1e567e5.
Merged from: JC-R12-39, JC-CODE-55

## JC-168: A hand merge returns the round to landing without any command
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-m4, and the review-2 fixer in the new `Round.hand_merged` method, commit fd6d47a.
Context: The process document promised the owner that finishing a conflicted merge by hand needs no command from them, because the landing merge becomes a no-op. That held in one situation and not in another: after a landing finding had already set up a merge attempt, the next command tried to establish a merge that was already done and would have paid an agent to resolve conflicts that no longer existed. The reviewer could have narrowed the promise in one sentence instead.
Rationale: The reason given for choosing code is the cost of leaving it: "One paid, confused agent run; a DONE from it does land." The fix proposed is precise: "if `merge_pending` is set, no merge is in progress and ... `is_ancestor(root, merge_pending['target_sha'], 'HEAD')`, clear `merge_pending`, write a HISTORY line, set `step` to `resume_step or 'LANDING'` and continue the loop." The fix commit records the result: "a hand merge after L1 is seen by next (the target is already in HEAD) and returns the round to LANDING instead of a phantom merge attempt."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, which do not mention merges or landing. The promise is the runner's own, at harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 131. What left it open is that merged-B's promise was stated for the case before the landing finding and not re-checked for the case after it.
Other context: This is the opposite choice from the neighbouring R2-m5 (JC-169), made in the same review, where the reviewer preferred to change the sentence because "the doc fix is smaller". The difference is that here the wrong promise costs an agent run and there it costs nothing. The fixer also closed the open landing findings, which the review had not asked for (JC-180).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m4); harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 131; `round.hand_merged`, `round.mechanical_loop`; harness/tests/test_concurrency.py::test_hand_merge_after_l1_returns_to_landing; commit fd6d47a.
Merged from: JC-R12-40, JC-CODE-75

## JC-169: An out-of-band edit counts only once it is committed
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-m5; the mechanism written by the implementer in `round.out_of_band` and `round.spec_hash`, commit 05509b7, and documented by the review-2 fixer, commit fd6d47a.
Context: The owner may edit the plan or the spec in the worktree mid-round, and the runner hashes them at each `next` and sends the round back when they change. But an uncommitted edit is also what a dirty tree looks like, and the documents promised the owner the runner would notice either way: in fact an uncommitted edit was quietly wiped and charged as an infrastructure error. The choice was between making the runner spare those two files and correcting the promise.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix: one word in PROCESS.md:94 and DRIVER.md ('a **committed** out-of-band edit'), and the reset note in HISTORY should list the paths it reverted ... so an owner sees what happened. Exempting the plan and spec artifacts from the reset is the alternative; the doc fix is smaller."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not describe mid-round edits. The promise is the runner's own.
Other context: An uncommitted edit is reset by the dirty-tree recovery instead, with the paths named in HISTORY (JC-115, JC-181) - so the two mechanisms partition cleanly and the owner is told which one applied. The consequence is that the owner is now told, in two documents, to commit an edit before expecting the runner to see it. The related rule that other steps' artifacts are runner-owned (JC-255) is what keeps an agent's edit from being read as the owner's.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m5); `round.out_of_band`, `round.spec_hash`, `round.tree_state`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 97; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 45; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_out_of_band_plan_and_spec_edits; merged-B 2.9; commit fd6d47a.
Merged from: JC-R12-41, JC-CODE-72

Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-m6 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.

Other context: The fixer did not take the offer of deferral and implemented it instead (JC-182), which is the reverse of the usual direction in this slice.

## JC-171: Siding with the code against a number the owner had set (from JC-R12-43)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-n4 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.
Context: The owner's settings file allocates a share of the budget to the gate that judges the plan, but that gate is done by the runner with no agent involved, so the money is never spent and the allocation is silently dropped. The reviewer had to decide whether the owner's number should start being honoured or whether the prompt should stop implying it exists.
Rationale: Quoted: "`step_table` ... reports `gate_runs: true` for CHAT-TO-PLAN when its flag is 1, though it is mechanical and `ledger.shares` ... never allocates a gate share to it; the PLAN-AGENTS prompt then asks for a share the ledger drops, and the owner's `defaultShares.gates.CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.02` ... is silently ignored for the same reason. Report the approval gate as not running (one condition in `step_table`); say in PROCESS.md:112 that only LLM gates take gate shares."
Spec conflict: Directly with the owner's file. harness/project.yaml sets, under "Minimum per-stage budgets", `gates: CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.02`, and its `gates:` block marks the same gate "mechanical, run by the runner". The owner's own file both allocates the money and says no agent will run there; the reviewer resolved that against the allocation.
Other context: Implemented in commit 94a6095 (JC-184), with the process document's sentence now reading "only LLM gates take gate shares (CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE is mechanical, so a `CHAT-TO-PLAN` gate share is ignored)". The owner's number is still in project.yaml and still ignored; the change makes the plan-writing agent stop being told about it. Two real rounds had already logged an undefined judgment call on the same line (JC-192, JC-223), and review 3 filtered the whole minimum-share table for the same reason (JC-262).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-n4) and section 6; harness/project.yaml; commit 94a6095; harness/docs/PROCESS.md.

## JC-172: Narrowing a stated safety guarantee rather than making it true (from JC-R12-44)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, finding R2-n6 in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 2.
Context: The process document listed "agents get no push credential" among the harness's invariants. That is true when the runner starts an agent itself, and not true in the normal path, where a sub-agent inherits the chat session's credentials. The reviewer had to choose between closing the gap and rewording the claim.
Rationale: Quoted: "PROCESS.md:132 lists 'agents get no push credential' as an invariant; that holds for headless mode (`scrubEnv`) and not for driver mode, where sub-agents inherit the session and Git Credential Manager, and only the definition's `disallowedTools`, G1 and M0 stand in the way. Say so." The reviewer treated the three remaining barriers as enough and asked only that the document stop over-claiming.
Spec conflict: The owner's AGENTS.md states the rule as absolute: "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner." The reviewer's answer leaves that as a rule agents are told to follow rather than one the machine enforces.
Other context: Commit 94a6095 changed the invariant to "headless agents get no push credential" and added a sentence naming what stands in the way in driver mode. The plan had already said as much in its risk list - "the runner inherits the session's git credentials; `scrubEnv` applies only to spawned agents" - so the over-claim was the implementer's.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-n6); harness/AGENTS.md; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md risk 13; commit 94a6095; harness/docs/PROCESS.md.

## JC-173: Naming a figure for what the next phase should quote (from JC-R12-45)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 7 note 2.
Context: The harness stops a round when spending passes a multiple of the quote the owner approved (JC-43). The reviewer worked out that a realistic first task would hit that stop before landing, and had to decide what number to tell the next phase to use.
Rationale: Quoted: "**Quote generously or pass `--tokens`.** Without `--cost`/`--tokens`, every `record` books the whole step budget as an estimate, plan and spec words cost $0.1 each, and each checkpoint costs about $6.70; a $10 quote with a 300-word plan and a 500-word spec hits the `hard-stop` (6 x quote) before landing. Quote at least $50 for the sandbox task, or pass the Agent tool's token totals."
Spec conflict: None found; the numbers are the owner's but the conclusion is not. harness/project.yaml sets `hardStopBudgetMultiple: 6`, `planCostPerWord: 0.1`, `specCostPerWord: 0.1`, `ownerHourlyRate: 10` and `costToWaitForOwner: 5`; the owner's CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt tells the planner only to "Size the quote to the value of the change", which is the opposite instruction from "quote at least $50 so the machinery does not stop you".
Other context: This advice governed the real-agent test rounds that followed, all of which ran in sandboxes with quotes chosen by the driver rather than by the owner - $120, $150, $120, $150, $120 and $130 (JC-188, JC-217, JC-280, JC-330).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 7 note 2; harness/project.yaml; harness/locked_prose/CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt.

## JC-174: Treating the harness's own source being inside a round's reach as brittleness, not a fault (from JC-R12-46)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 2, in bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md, section 6 (the `livingSourcePaths` row) and section 7 note 7.
Context: On the harness's own repository the owner counts `src/` among the living paths, so a round can edit the very runner that is driving it, and a bad edit breaks the round in the middle. The reviewer had to decide whether to raise this as a finding to be fixed or to record it as a hazard for whoever drives the next round.
Rationale: Quoted from the table: "the runner itself is living: a CLEANUP or SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION that edits `src/shackles/` runs the edited runner for the rest of the round (the worktree's `run.py` is the driver's runner); a broken edit breaks `record` mid-round", classed "silent". Quoted from the advice: "**On the real repository**: choose `implPaths` outside `src/shackles/` (self-hosting hazard, section 6)". Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the setting is the owner's to make, and the harness is meant to develop arbitrary software including itself, so a guard would be the runner overruling the owner.
Spec conflict: The owner sets it directly in harness/project.yaml: `livingSourcePaths: ["src/", "docs/", "tests/"]`, and AGENTS.md repeats the principle: "`src/`, `docs/`, and `tests/` are living". Nothing says the runner's own source is excluded.
Other context: Recorded, not fixed, and still open at the commit audited. The choice fits the same review's decision to run the tests in a sandbox, where the runner is not the thing under development - and in a sandbox the two owner files appear to disagree, which review 5 later had to explain away (JC-389).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 6 and 7; harness/project.yaml; harness/AGENTS.md.

## JC-175: Taking the last outermost object rather than the longest one
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit dc035b7, in `schemas.extract_json`.
Context: The reviewer had proposed picking the longest object carrying an answer (JC-163). The fixer had to choose an actual rule, and chose a different one: walk the message forwards, skip anything inside an object already decoded, and take the last of the objects that remain.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "R2-M1: extract_json takes the last top-level balanced object that carries status or verdict; a nested resolution, ruling or needs_owner never wins." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): "top-level" states the property that actually matters, where "longest" only happens to correlate with it, and the forward scan that skips decoded ranges gives the nesting test for free. The fixer also wrote the difference into the process document.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "Your system prompt names ... the JSON your final message must be. Follow it exactly; the runner parses it", which presumes a clean message and does not say what to do with a dirty one. The plan's rule, "the last balanced object", is what was being replaced.
Other context: The two rules part company when a message contains two complete answers: the new test asserts the later one wins. The fallback when nothing carries `status` or `verdict` is still the last top-level object, so a merely mislabelled message fails schema validation with a readable message rather than disappearing (JC-176). Unchanged since; across tests B, C, D and the probes the extractor coped with every message it was given (JC-274).
Sources: commit dc035b7; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-M1); `schemas.extract_json`; `round.record`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 67; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 31; harness/tests/test_schemas.py::test_extract_json_prefers_the_top_level_object_over_nested_ones.
Merged from: JC-R12-47, JC-CODE-23

## JC-176: Changing what happens when a message carries no answer at all (from JC-R12-48)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit dc035b7, in the last line of `schemas.extract_json`.
Context: The rewrite of the scan also touched the fallback used when no object in the message has a status or a verdict. Before, the fallback returned the first object found anywhere in the message, nested ones included. After, it returns the last top-level object. The reviewer had asked only to "keep the final any-dict fallback".
Rationale: Not recorded; the commit body does not mention the fallback. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): once the scan collects only top-level objects in order, the last of them is the consistent partner of the main rule, and the old behaviour of reaching inside an object was the same mistake the finding was about.
Spec conflict: None found; the fallback is entirely the runner's own.
Other context: The fixer's own new test pins the changed behaviour, with the comment "the top-level object is the message". This only affects messages that are already going to be rejected as invalid, so the practical effect is which error the agent is shown.
Sources: commit dc035b7; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-M1); harness/tests/test_schemas.py.

## JC-177: Letting an edit that is undone before the end of the round need no approval
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit dc035b7, in `checks.e1_spec_edits` and `Round.e1`.
Context: Until this commit, once a file of the owner's had been touched its name stayed on the list for the rest of the round, so the owner was stopped at a checkpoint even if the file had since been put back exactly as it was. The reviewer had not asked for this.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "spec_edits now lists the files that differ from base_commit, so an edit undone later needs no approval." The code writes a history line for the reversal too: "SPEC EDIT <path> undone: the file is back at base_commit".
Spec conflict: The owner's rule in spec.yaml is about edits, not about net differences: "Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly." Under the new behaviour an edit that is made and then reverted is written to the round's history but never put in front of the owner; a file that is byte-identical to where the round started is arguably not an edit at all, which the owner's sentence does not settle.
Other context: The hash of each diff is kept so HISTORY records a new or changed diff once, and the set changing at all clears the approval flag, so a second edit after the owner approved the first stops the round again. The fixer wrote a test for exactly this case. It is the clearest example in the review-2 fixes of a change that goes past what the review asked for; the commit body states it plainly, which is why it is separable from JC-165.
Sources: commit dc035b7; `checks.e1_spec_edits`; `round.e1`, `round.landing_step`; spec.yaml; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 107; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_spec_edit_undone_later_in_the_round_needs_no_approval.
Merged from: JC-R12-50, JC-CODE-30

## JC-178: Standing the real repository's seven-minute suite in for something a test can run (from JC-R12-51)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit dc035b7, in harness/tests/test_outcomes.py.
Context: The deadlock of JC-164 only exists where the permanent test suite contains the guard that checks the owner's files. The fixer had to prove the fix without making a test run the harness's own suite, which takes about seven minutes.
Rationale: Not recorded in prose beyond the code comment. The test defines `GUARD_SUITE = [sys.executable, "src/run.py", "spec", "status"]` with the comment "the baseline guard as the permanent suite, as on the real repository", and builds a throwaway repository configured to use it. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): what matters to the deadlock is that the suite goes red exactly when the owner's files differ from the accepted hashes, and the `spec status` command is the cheapest thing with that property.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files do not describe the test suite. The nearest constraint is the bootstrap plan's rule that "the mechanics tests run on the generated minimal fixture spec, not on the owner's files" (JC-80), which this respects.
Other context: The two tests added are the only place in the suite where the self-hosting situation is exercised. They assert a proxy, not the real suite, so they would not catch a way for the real suite to go red that the `spec status` command does not share.
Sources: commit dc035b7; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md (constraints section).

## JC-179: Redefining when a step counts as already accepted
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit fd6d47a, in `Round.override`.
Context: The command that skips a step refused any step that had already recorded a clean commit. That meant a producer whose gate had failed it could not be skipped, which is exactly the situation the owner is in when the round stops at the failure limit. The reviewer had not raised this; the fixer met it while making the command resume the round (JC-167).
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "override treats a producer as accepted by its gate, not by its clean commit, so a producer whose gate failed can be overridden at failure-limit." The code carries the same sentence as a comment on the line.
Spec conflict: The owner's CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says only "'override' + steps or gates the runner skips them this round; the plan itself cannot be skipped", with no notion of a step being too far along to skip. The idea of acceptance is the runner's, and harness/docs/PROCESS.md defines it as "its gate's PASS, or its clean DONE when the gate does not run", which is what the fixer aligned the command with.
Other context: A producer that already ran and waits for its gate gets a different refusal naming the remedy - "already ran; its gate <GATE> is pending: override the gate instead" - because overriding the gate accepts the artifact as it stands. That distinction was sharpened again by review 4 and fix 4 (JC-312). The fixer's test asserts both halves.
Sources: commit fd6d47a; harness/locked_prose/CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt; `round.override`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 89; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 42; harness/tests/test_round.py::test_overrides; commit c1e7b8c.
Merged from: JC-R12-52, JC-CODE-54

## JC-180: Closing the landing findings when the owner finishes a merge by hand (from JC-R12-53)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit fd6d47a, in the new `Round.hand_merged` method.
Context: The reviewer had described four lines that notice the merge is already done and send the round back to landing (JC-168). The fixer had to decide what to do with the findings the landing step had raised about the conflict, which would otherwise stay open with nobody to answer them.
Rationale: Not recorded. The commit body says only "R2-m4: a hand merge after L1 is seen by next (the target is already in HEAD) and returns the round to LANDING instead of a phantom merge attempt." The code goes further, marking every open finding of source `landing` on that step as fixed. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the findings describe conflicts that no longer exist, and leaving them open would carry them into every later prompt and into the postmortem.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not describe landing or merges.
Other context: The fixer's test asserts the extra behaviour directly, checking that every landing-sourced finding ends marked fixed. The process document's promise now covers both timings.
Sources: commit fd6d47a; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m4); harness/tests/test_concurrency.py; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 131.

## JC-181: Capping the list of reverted files at twenty (from JC-R12-54)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit fd6d47a, in `Round.tree_state`.
Context: The reviewer asked that the note written when the runner wipes a dirty working tree name the files it reverted instead of counting them (JC-169). A wipe can involve hundreds of files, so the fixer had to decide how much to print.
Rationale: Not recorded; the commit body says "R2-m5: the dirty-tree reset names the paths it reverted". The code prints the first twenty and then "and N more". Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the point of the finding was that the owner should be able to see what happened, and twenty names is enough to recognise the situation without turning one line of the round's history into a page.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's COMMON-ROUND.txt describes the history file only as "the log of attempts, verdicts, final messages and spend, appended by the runner".
Other context: The number twenty appears nowhere else and is not documented; an owner whose wipe touched more than twenty files is told a count for the rest. The fixer's test checks three specific names appear, so it would not notice if the cap were wrong.
Sources: commit fd6d47a; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m5); harness/locked_prose/COMMON-ROUND.txt; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py.

## JC-182: Building the deferrable fix, and building it out of the step's start marker (from JC-R12-55)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit fd6d47a, in `Round.record_producer`.
Context: The reviewer had said this one could be left for later with a sentence in the documents (JC-170). The fixer chose to build it, and then had to choose a mechanism: the reviewer had suggested a new piece of round state called `gate_diff_base`; the fixer instead moved the existing marker that records where the step began.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "R2-m6: after a merge commit the producer's step starts at the automerge tree, so the gate's DIFF_FILE never shows the sibling's changes." The code carries the reason as a comment. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for building rather than deferring: the gate prose fails anything outside the round's spec, so leaving it would have meant a gate failing the merge resolution for the other round's work during the real-agent tests that were about to begin; and for the mechanism: one existing field already means "where this step's diff starts", so a second field would have needed its own lifetime and its own clearing.
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE.txt is what makes the wrong diff dangerous: "Line by line: * Is it not sensible? Fail. * Is it outside of the spec? Fail."
Other context: The choice of mechanism means the step's recorded start is no longer a commit but a tree, which anything else reading that field would have to cope with; nothing else reads it today. The fixer extended the existing conflict test to prove it, turning the code gate on and adding a file inside the round's own paths so that the other round's work could be seen if it leaked in.
Sources: commit fd6d47a; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-m6); harness/locked_prose/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE.txt; harness/tests/test_concurrency.py.

## JC-183: Disproving a reported fault and changing the code anyway (from JC-R12-56)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit 94a6095, in `Round.runner_head`.
Context: The reviewer had warned that a user's git setting could make a pattern search behave differently and silently disable one of the runner's checks (JC-151), and had asked for a fixed-string search. The fixer checked the claim, found it wrong, and then had to decide whether to leave the code alone, make the requested change, or do something else.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "R2-n3: runner_head names its regex flavor (--extended-regexp). Verified on git 2.45 that rev-list --grep ignores grep.patternType (only git log reads grep config), so the reviewer's failure mode did not exist; the flag only makes the anchor explicit." The fixer did not take the fixed-string search the reviewer asked for, because the pattern uses an anchor that a fixed-string search would treat as an ordinary character.
Spec conflict: None found; this is entirely internal to the runner.
Other context: This is the one place in the review-1 and review-2 slice where a fixer recorded a reason for not following a finding as written while still acting on it. It is also the residual risk review 2 had flagged against the rework at JC-151: "R2-n3 is the one residual dependency."
Sources: commit 94a6095; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md sections 2 (R2-n3) and 3; harness/src/shackles/round.py.

## JC-184: Aligning the table of steps with the runner's own rule instead of patching the one reported case (from JC-R12-57)
Who and where: the session that fixed review 2's findings, in commit 94a6095, in `prompts.step_table`.
Context: The table of steps handed to the budget-planning agent said a gate would run in two cases where no gate agent is ever spawned: the approval gate, which the runner does itself, and any gate whose producer has been skipped. Only the first was reported (JC-171).
Rationale: Quoted from the commit body: "R2-n4: step_table reports the approval gate as not running, and a gate as not running when its producer is overridden, matching Round.gate_runs and the ledger, so PLAN-AGENTS is never asked for a share the ledger drops". One reason covers both changes: the table should say what the rest of the runner already decides.
Spec conflict: The owner's PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt asks the agent to "choose the agent, sub-agents and budget share for each step and gate of this round" and to "Optimize the budget, within reason", which only works if the table it is given is true. The owner's harness/project.yaml still allocates a share to the gate that never runs.
Other context: The fixer added a test that pins both cases, and the process document now records the rule. Later rounds added more to the same prompt about where the skipped share goes (JC-309).
Sources: commit 94a6095; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md section 2 (R2-n4); harness/locked_prose/PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt; harness/project.yaml; harness/tests/test_render.py; harness/docs/PROCESS.md.

## JC-185: The planning prompt prints an absolute, quoted start command with `--root` (from JC-CODE-99)
Who and where: the review-2 fixer in `plumbing/CHAT-TO-PLAN.txt`, commit dc035b7; the path shape unified by the fix-4 fixer, commit dddbaf2.
Context: The driver runs `start` from a chat session whose working directory may be anywhere. Without `--root` the runner finds the nearest ancestor holding spec.yaml - which, for a session testing a sandbox, is the real repository.
Rationale: Quoted from the review-2 commit: "the CHAT-TO-PLAN prompt prints the runner, --root and the draft plan absolutely and quoted, so a start rendered for a sandbox cannot claim a round on the real repository." Quoted from the fix-4 commit: "The CHAT-TO-PLAN plumbing built the draft's path with a forward slash on a backslash path: round.draft_plan is built with os.path.join once, in empty_round_context" (JC-328).
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files contain no commands.
Other context: This is a safety property expressed entirely in a prompt template - the runner cannot enforce it, because a driver may always type the command itself. The template also states the precedence rule the two delegation flags create: "The plan's approval decides; --delegate [--through STEP] on that command line means the same and overrides the plan's mode."
Sources: `plumbing/CHAT-TO-PLAN.txt`; `prompts.empty_round_context` (`draft_plan`); harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 22; commits dc035b7 and dddbaf2.


================================================================================
TESTS A AND B: THE FIRST TWO REAL-AGENT SANDBOX ROUNDS, AND THE FIXES BETWEEN THEM
================================================================================

## JC-186: Reading the owner's "add a function shout(text)" as a change to the function that already existed (from JC-AB-1)
Who and where: the driver of test A - a session that acted both as the harness driver and as the top-level agent in the owner's chat - during CHAT-TO-PLAN, the planning step that happens in chat before the round is started.
Context: The owner's first message asked for "Add a function shout(text)" and said "Keep it tiny." The toy project the sandbox builder had just created already contained a function of exactly that name and a test asserting its behaviour. The driver had to write a plan saying what the round would do, and the two readings produce different rounds: edit the function that is there, or add a second function beside it. The owner's words do not distinguish them.
Rationale: The report records the decision but not the driver's reasoning; it says only that "The driver has to decide, unprompted, that 'add' means 'change the one that is there'." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a project this small has no room for two functions with the same name, so "add shout" can only mean "make shout do this"; and the owner's "Keep it tiny" points at the cheaper of the two readings.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left the choice open was the sandbox fixture colliding with the most natural first request: the fixture seeds `shout`, and the version of harness/docs/TESTING.md in force at the time did not mention that it does. The prose that hands the decision to the driver is CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt: "Ask the owner what is sensible to ask now; decide the rest and write it down."
Other context: The choice silently decided how much the round would cost downstream - the driver notes that it "determines whether SPEC-TO-TESTS rewrites the existing test (cheap) or adds a second one ($3 + tokens)". Every later step followed the reading. The driver raised the collision itself and asked that either the fixture or the docs change; the fix was to document the fixture (JC-212). Not labelled as a judgment call by anyone at the time.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 3.4; section 1 (CHAT-TO-PLAN); section 8, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC and SPEC-TO-TESTS; section 5 (the landed code).

## JC-187: Settling the three behavioural questions about shout in the plan instead of asking the owner (from JC-AB-2)
Who and where: the driver of test A, CHAT-TO-PLAN.
Context: "Add a function shout(text)" does not say what the function should return for an empty string, nor whether text that already ends in an exclamation mark gets a second one. The driver had to either put a question to the owner and wait, or decide and write the decision into the plan. It decided, listing three assumptions inside the plan it presented.
Rationale: Not recorded in the driver's own words. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the harness charges the owner's time (a checkpoint costs about $6.67 in the harness's cost model), and the planning prose tells the driver to ask only what is worth asking now; three small semantic details on a two-line function are not worth a round trip, and writing them into the plan puts them in front of the owner anyway when the plan is presented.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files deliberately leave it open. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says "Plan what is sensible given the vision and the owner's words: scope, validation steps, non-goals, assumptions, quote" and "Ask the owner what is sensible to ask now; decide the rest and write it down". AGENTS.md warns that such a blanket instruction "defines how you choose, not the line between the two".
Other context: The assumptions propagated all the way to the landed code and tests. The round's POSTMORTEM went looking for where one of them came from; its HISTORY entry was cut mid-sentence by the runner's note cap, so what it concluded is only in the archived result file - the defect that was later marked (JC-116). Not labelled.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 1 (CHAT-TO-PLAN); section 8, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC, SPEC-TO-TESTS and POSTMORTEM; section 5 (the landed code).

## JC-188: Quoting the round at $120 and holding the plan to about 125 words (from JC-AB-3)
Who and where: the driver of test A, CHAT-TO-PLAN.
Context: The plan carries a quote, and every later budget in the round is a fraction of it: each step's allowance, the per-run cap, the hard stop, and the retry cost the gates are shown. The driver had to pick a number for a change that turned out to be one line of Python. It also had to decide how long to make the plan, because the plan is charged to the owner by the word.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the quote is not a cap on real spending but the yardstick everything else is measured against, so it has to be large enough to leave every step a workable allowance while still reading as a plausible price for a small change; $120 gives the two most expensive steps about $13 and $27 each.
Spec conflict: None found. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt leaves the number entirely to the driver: "Size the quote to the value of the change, calibrated by what information is readily available", together with "Assume a cost of $0.1 per word in the plan". No rule connects the size of a change to a number of dollars.
Other context: The round finished at $69.98 against the $120 quote, so the quote was generous by about 40 percent; of that spend $39.73 was the owner's own charges. The report says the plan was 125 words while the booked owner spend implies 123. Test B made the same call differently, quoting $150 for a comparable task (JC-217), and review 2 had advised quoting at least $50 (JC-173). Not labelled.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 1 (CHAT-TO-PLAN); section 2 (both checkpoint messages); section 5 (the `done` JSON and index.jsonl).

## JC-189: Writing an empty string for "no through step" in the plan's approval block (from JC-AB-4)
Who and where: the driver of test A, CHAT-TO-PLAN, when turning the owner's second message ("delegate") into the plan's machine-readable approval.
Context: The driver recorded the approval as `{"mode": "delegated", "through": "", "overrides": [], "words": "delegate"}`. The `through` key names the step up to which review checkpoints are skipped, and is only meaningful for the phrase "delegate through STEP". The owner had simply said "delegate", so there was no through step, and the driver had to pick a representation for "none": omit the key, write `null`, or write `""`. The version of harness/docs/DRIVER.md in force told it to "Fill `approval` (`mode`, `through`, `overrides`, `words` verbatim)" without saying what "none" looks like.
Rationale: Not recorded as reasoning; the driver later described `""` as "the obvious value for 'no through step' when the doc tells you to fill the key".
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files - this is a runner-and-docs question, not a spec question. What left it open was DRIVER.md's instruction to fill the key while saying nothing about the empty case, against the runner's `round.delegated_through`, which accepted only `None`.
Other context: This is the most expensive call in either of the two rounds. Because `""` is neither `None` nor a known step name, delegation was silently switched off and both review checkpoints fired on a round the owner had explicitly delegated. The driver measured the cost at $13.33 of a $69.98 round, or 19 percent, plus two round trips the owner had declined. Passing `--delegate` on the command line did not rescue it, because the command layer falls through to the plan's value. The round's own POSTMORTEM found the same defect independently and filed it as a TODO. It was fixed immediately after test A (JC-254), and DRIVER.md now says "omit `through` otherwise (null or empty means the same: every review skipped)". Test B's driver omitted the key and both reviews were correctly skipped.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 1 (CHAT-TO-PLAN); section 3.1; section 2 (both checkpoints); section 8, the HISTORY CHECKPOINT entries and the POSTMORTEM entry.

## JC-190: Choosing each step's agent rung by how much judgment it needs rather than by how much work it is (from JC-AB-5)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: PLAN-AGENTS has to assign one agent rung from the roster (max, high, medium, low) to every producer step of the round. The whole round was one function and one test, so a size-based rule would have put everything on the cheapest rung. The agent had to decide what the rung should track.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "rungs chosen by judgment density rather than size of the change - max for PLAN-TO-SPEC and POSTMORTEM, medium for SPEC-TO-TESTS, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION and TESTS-TO-SUITE, low for CLEANUP, and PLAN-AGENTS recorded at the rung that actually ran it". Its final message expands the reason: PLAN-TO-SPEC writes "the contract the tests and then the code are each built from alone", is not overridable, and every spec word costs the owner $0.1; POSTMORTEM "reads the whole round, traces issues to owner words, and writes the charged carry-forward files".
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose explicitly hands this over. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision, and anything else you deem reasonable to consider. Optimize the budget, within reason." harness/project.yaml sets `maxAgent: max` and `gateAgent: max` as defaults but does not require them (JC-76).
Other context: The rungs were never actually honoured, because every sub-agent in the test ran on one model regardless (JC-193). Test B's planner answered the same question one rung lower throughout (JC-218). Logged by the agent as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 2; section 8, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS entry; section 6.

## JC-191: Putting the spare budget on POSTMORTEM rather than on the steps that write code (from JC-AB-6)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The work budget is split as fractions that must sum to one across the producer steps. Two steps had already spent their floor. The remaining surplus could go to the steps that write the code and tests, or elsewhere.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "work shares keep the two already-spent minimums, fund every remaining step above its expected real cost, and put the surplus on POSTMORTEM (0.32) instead of the code steps, whose living tokens are charged; subAgents omitted, so 0 everywhere." Its final message adds that a larger target on a code step "would invite a larger diff", and that "nothing here parallelises and a spawn costs more than it would save".
Spec conflict: None found. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt asks it to "Optimize the budget, within reason" and COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says "Budgets are targets, not gates: size your work to your budget and to an estimate of value it creates" - which is exactly why a generous share on a code step is a real risk and why the trade-off had to be judged. The floors come from `defaultShares` in harness/project.yaml.
Other context: The reasoning is a deliberate bet that agents spend up to their budget, so budget is a lever on diff size, not just a cap. POSTMORTEM did in fact become the largest single run of the round. Test B's planner made the opposite call, concentrating the surplus on the steps most likely to be re-entered (JC-220). The sub-agent half of the same logged line is JC-222. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 3; section 8, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS entry; section 6.

## JC-192: Filling in gate budget shares for six gates that were not going to run (from JC-AB-7)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Every gate was switched off for this round, so no gate would consume any budget. The prompt nonetheless asked for a `shares.gates` map and said each map must sum to one. The agent had to decide between leaving the map empty (accurate, but arguably breaking the "sums to one" rule and the schema) and filling it for gates that would never run.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "no gate runs this round, so nothing says what shares.gates should hold while the schema still requires the key and the prompt says each map sums to 1; filled it for the six LLM-gated producers, keeping the stated minimums, so a split already exists if a gate is enabled mid-round."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, but harness/project.yaml contributes to the confusion: its `defaultShares` block declares gate minimums the agent is shown. The sentence that actually forced the choice was in the runner's own generated prompt text, which at that commit read "shares.gates maps every producer step whose gate runs to a fraction of the gates budget ... each map sums to 1" - silent about the case where no gate runs at all.
Other context: This was the round's only undefined judgment call until POSTMORTEM, and it is the line quoted back at the owner in every checkpoint message and in the `done` payload. The prompt was amended before test B to say "({} when no gate runs); each non-empty map sums to 1", which closed this exact hole - and test B's planner then hit the neighbouring one instead (JC-223). Test C's planner, facing the same gates-off round after the amendment, left the map empty and said why (JC-285). Logged by the agent as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 2; section 2 (checkpoint 1); section 8, every HISTORY entry; section 5 (the `done` JSON).

## JC-193: Booking every run at the rung the plan named, although all seven actually ran on one model (from JC-AB-8)
Who and where: the driver of test A, at each of the seven `record` commands.
Context: As the test was set up, every sub-agent was spawned as a general-purpose agent on one model, whatever rung the action named. The `record` command has an `--agent RUNG` flag that re-prices a run at the rung that actually ran; the driver had run `record --help` at the start of the session and had seen it. The version of harness/docs/DRIVER.md in force did not mention the flag; it said only to add "`--tokens N` when the tool reported a token total". The driver had to decide whether to correct the ledger or to let it price four of the seven runs at rungs that did not run, and disclose the gap in its report. It chose the latter.
Rationale: Not recorded as reasoning at the time. The report states the consequence plainly: "The dollar figures in STATE/HISTORY are therefore priced at the wrong rung for four of the seven runs." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): DRIVER.md is the loop the test was meant to exercise, and it did not ask for `--agent`; using an undocumented flag would itself be a deviation, so the honest move was to follow the documented loop and record the distortion.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left it open was the gap between DRIVER.md's record instruction, which named only `--tokens`, and `record --help`, which offered `--agent`.
Other context: This is the single decision on which the two rounds differ most - test B's driver, reading a DRIVER.md that by then documented the flag, passed `--agent medium` on eight of thirteen records (JC-224), and test C's driver, on a one-rung round, passed it nowhere (JC-281). Neither approach makes the ledger right: review 3 later found that the runner prices at `--agent` with no comparison to the action's rung and no warning that the plan was not honoured, and added a flag for exactly that (JC-257). Not labelled; disclosed as a deviation.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 4, deviation 1; section 1 (the record rows); section 6 (the table of rungs the runner priced).

## JC-194: Scoping the spec's write paths to the two single files instead of the two directories
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agents of test A and test B (round 0001, attempt 1 in each), and again in both rounds of test D.
Context: The spec declares the implementation paths and the test paths, which become the write paths the runner enforces mechanically for every later step. The agent could declare the directories or the two files themselves. Each round's plan said the round should add no new files, and the runner has no separate way to enforce that.
Rationale: Test A's agent, in its own logged words: "implPaths and testPaths scoped to the two files themselves (../src/toy/text.py, ../tests/toy/test_text.py) rather than their directories, so the plan's 'no new files' is enforced mechanically". Test B's agent reached the same conclusion independently: "implPaths and testPaths are the two single files the plan names, not their directories, so M1 mechanically reverts the new files the non-goals forbid." Test D's agent used the same words in both of its rounds: "so M1 reverts any stray new file and the plan's 'no new file' non-goal is carried mechanically."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's prose does not discuss path granularity at all; the only constraint, in the runner's own prompt text, is that the paths be harness-relative, prefix-disjoint and outside the round folder and the spec files, and harness/docs/PROCESS.md allows either form. The idea of using path scope to enforce a non-goal the runner cannot read is the agents' own.
Other context: Four independent agents converging on the same non-obvious technique is worth noting, because it suggests the technique is now effectively part of how the harness is used without appearing in any document. It worked in tests A and B, where the implementation diff was one file and the stray check had nothing to revert. In test D's second round it was the proximate cause of the round's largest tangle: because the implementation step's writable paths are the spec's implementation paths alone, skipping the test-writing step left no step able to write the round's test before cleanup - which three agents logged as an open question (JC-362, JC-363, JC-365) and review 5 turned into a finding (JC-380).
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 4 and the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC entry; bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 11; bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (sixth line), section 11.3 (fifth line), sections 7.8, 11.4 and 11.6; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md finding R5-m2.
Merged from: JC-AB-9, JC-AB-36, JC-D-8, JC-D-31

## JC-195: Writing the verify command with a portable launcher and cutting its timeout to two minutes (from JC-AB-10)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The spec carries a `verify` command that the runner runs, under a timeout, at the implementation step and again at cleanup. The project's configured suite command used a machine-specific interpreter path. The agent had to decide what to write, and what timeout to set against the configured default of 600 seconds.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "verify is the toy unittest suite run from HARNESS, the suiteCommand argv with the `py -3.13` launcher instead of a machine-specific python path, at a 120 s timeout (checked: S1 clean, verify green)". Its final message records that it ran the command green from the worktree before writing it.
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files names a launcher or a timeout; the timeout default lives in harness/project.yaml as `verifyTimeoutSeconds: 600`, which the spec may override.
Other context: A wrong verify command here is expensive - it is what the two check steps run, so it decides whether the round can land at all. The agent removed that risk by running it first. Test B's spec agent made the opposite call on the launcher, copying the configured suite command verbatim so that verify and the suite would be literally the same command (JC-229), while making the same 120-second call on the timeout. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 4; section 8, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC, SPEC-TO-TESTS, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION and CLEANUP.

## JC-196: Putting three test cases inside the one existing test function instead of writing new ones (from JC-AB-11)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The spec's test plan decides how many test functions the round creates, and the harness charges the owner $3 for every new test function that joins the permanent suite, on top of the living tokens it costs. The agent had to choose between three small, separately named tests and three assertions inside the test that already existed.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "test plan held to three cases - 'hi', empty text, and text already ending in '!' - added to the existing test rather than as new test functions ($3 each plus tokens); non-string input left untested per the plan's assumption; refactor left empty, since the plan rules out harness and docs changes and one toy function has nothing to consolidate."
Spec conflict: A real tension inside the owner's files, though the agent did not call it one. PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Exhaust edge cases, within reason", which pushes towards more cases, while COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "A new living file costs 10 beyond its tokens; a new suite test 3. Add either only when it earns that", which prices each one. Neither says where the line falls.
Other context: Exactly this tension became an explicit undefined judgment call in test B, logged twice - once by the spec agent and once by the gate that judged it (JC-231, JC-234) - and it reached the owner as a clarification at the end of that round. Logged here as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 5; section 8, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC and SPEC-TO-TESTS; section 5 (the landed test).

## JC-197: Holding the owner-facing spec to about 140 words and quoting the return expression rather than describing it (from JC-AB-12)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: SPEC.md is the half of the spec the owner reads, and it is charged to the owner at $0.1 a word at the moment the step is accepted. The agent had to choose a length, knowing that every extra sentence is money and every missing sentence is ambiguity the tests and the code could read two ways.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "SPEC.md held to 140 words ($14 of the $99.3 remaining) covering the behaviour rule, the three test cases and the non-goals; the exact return expression is quoted so no wording has to carry the upper-casing semantics."
Spec conflict: A tension inside the owner's files rather than a silence. PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt both demands completeness - "Break the plan into implementation steps until the spec is complete, unambiguous and sensible" - and prices it - "Assume a cost of $0.1 per word in the spec, and the spec needs to read as plain English". The gate that would have judged it is told to fail anything "Ambiguous, so that two implementers could read it differently", which sharpens the same trade-off without resolving it.
Other context: The spec was charged at 141 words, $14.10, which the round's POSTMORTEM then raised as part of its complaint that the owner paid 61 percent of the round. Test B's spec agent made the same call at 195 words and added a further move, pushing the exact expected strings into the machine-readable half, which is not word-charged (JC-230); test C and test D's agents did the same (JC-289, JC-355). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 6; section 8, the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC and POSTMORTEM entries; section 2 (checkpoint 1's spend line).

## JC-198: Answering both review checkpoints as the owner would, instead of stopping and relaying them (from JC-AB-13)
Who and where: the driver of test A, at checkpoint 1 (raised after the spec gate) and checkpoint 2 (raised after cleanup).
Context: The owner was absent, and had left a standing instruction that "their word for any review is `approve`". The version of harness/docs/DRIVER.md in force said the opposite of acting on it: "Exit 10 from `next` or `record`: relay `message` to the owner verbatim and stop; when the owner speaks, run the matching command with their exact words". The checkpoints were also anomalous - the round had been delegated and should not have stopped at all (JC-189). The driver had to choose between stopping the round and reporting a harness that was not behaving as documented, and continuing on the standing instruction so the test would reach its end.
Rationale: The report gives the decision as owner substitution: "The owner was absent. Owner messages 1 and 2 were used verbatim as given. At both checkpoints the standing instruction 'their word for any review is `approve`' was used, run as `approve --quote 'approve'`." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for continuing rather than stopping: the point of the test was to find out whether a round can reach `done`, which stopping would have defeated, and the standing instruction was exactly the owner's answer for this case.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The conflict was between the owner's standing instruction and DRIVER.md's "relay ... and stop".
Other context: Before answering the first checkpoint the driver ran a read-only `status` to check whether delegation had been recorded, which is how the bug of JC-189 was diagnosed. Answering `approve` rather than re-issuing `delegate` made no difference: the runner falls back to the plan's value either way. The two checkpoints cost $13.33 and each recorded a flag that the owner's words could not be verified, because no owner log existed (JC-121). Not labelled; disclosed as a deviation.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 4, deviations 2 and 3; section 1 (the command rows); section 2 (both checkpoint texts); section 5 (the round's OWNER.log).

## JC-199: Keeping the project's only test file in the permanent suite when the cost model paid to delete it
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE producer sub-agents of test A and test B (round 0001, attempt 1 in each) and of test C.
Context: TESTS-TO-SUITE decides, per file, whether a test changed this round joins the permanent suite or is moved into the round's archive. In each of the three rounds the only test file in the round was also the toy project's only test file. Archiving it would have moved the whole file, and the harness's ledger refunds living tokens for files that leave the living paths, so archiving pays.
Rationale: Test A's agent kept it because "it is the toy's only test and predates the round, so archiving it would empty the very directory SPEC.verify and suiteCommand discover (a vacuous green at M5 and L2, shout uncovered), while keeping costs nothing new: three assertions inside the existing test_shout, no new def test_*, so no testBaseCost and no added runtime per round." Test B's agent priced the alternative and said so in as many words: "The ledger points the other way and I judged against it deliberately ... about $44 better, a one-time credit bought by deleting every future regression check", adding that archiving "takes test_shout with it and empties the suite that suiteCommand discovers ... the plan's validation also requires the whisper test in the suite." Test C's agent priced the other side: "if it were not in the suite nothing would stop a later round quietly returning to plain ABK, and re-establishing the owner's answer costs an owner checkpoint (about $6.67) plus the second PLAN-TO-SPEC run it already took this round - more than double the $3 on the first regression alone."
Spec conflict: A direct conflict between two of the owner's files. COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Living tokens are charged when created and refunded on removal, in code and prose alike", which pays for archiving; TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "Do what is sensible.
