Sensibility involves a realistic analysis of expected long-term costs and benefits" and "Tests outside the suite mean the test will likely be ignored forever", which forbids it. Neither yields to the other.
Other context: The test B report ranks this as one of the two things that round most clearly exposes - "the living-cost ledger pays about $44 for deleting the project's only test file, which TESTS-TO-SUITE declined only by making a logged judgment call against its own incentive" - and observes that "A less careful agent on a cheaper rung takes the $44". The round's POSTMORTEM ranked it first of seven and sent it to the owner (JC-242); review 3 ruled it the owner's numbers rather than a harness defect and made no change (JC-260); it is the first of the four open questions in the bootstrap's final report (JC-478). Test C's agent also concluded the alternative was not actually reachable in that project, since archiving would empty the suite, and raised that separately (JC-296). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in all three rounds.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 7 and the HISTORY TESTS-TO-SUITE entry; bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 16, section 5 item 14, section 10; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 7, section 11.3, section 9.5.
Merged from: JC-AB-14, JC-AB-47, JC-C-23

## JC-200: Leaving the "flag it for the owner" field of SUITE.json empty (from JC-AB-15)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: SUITE.json has a `raise_with_owner` field, and the step's prose invites the agent to use it. The agent had just made a decision (JC-199) that went against the harness's own cost model, which is arguably exactly the kind of thing an owner would want to hear about. It decided not to raise anything.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "raise_with_owner left empty, since keep/archive is per file and one file with one test function has nothing to split."
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "If there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner, flag it" - the sentence is about a middle ground between keeping and archiving, and is silent on whether a conflict in the owner's own rules counts as something to flag. The agent read the sentence narrowly.
Other context: This is the clearest place where the two rounds diverge on the same sentence. Test B's agent read the same prose more broadly, found the same absence of a middle ground, and flagged the cost-model conflict itself (JC-239) - which turned out to matter, because review 3 then discovered that nothing in the runner ever read the field (JC-263). Test A's narrower reading meant the harness's blind spot went unexercised in this round. Test F's agent later read it narrowly again, for different reasons (JC-435). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call, in the same line as JC-199.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 7; section 8, the HISTORY TESTS-TO-SUITE entry.

## JC-201: Writing the path form the mechanical check wants rather than the form the prompt printed, and reporting the clash in a note (from JC-AB-16)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The agent found that its own prompt printed the changed test file one way (relative to the repository) while the check that would judge its artifact resolves the listed paths a different way and therefore wanted the harness-relative form. Copying the path the prompt showed would have failed the check, cost a FAIL and a retry. The agent had to decide which form to write, and separately how to report a harness defect it had found: end the step normally with a note, or stop the round by returning BLOCKED or UPSTREAM.
Rationale: In the agent's own words in its final message: "Paths in the artifact are H-relative, the form `checks.s2_suite` resolves through `cfg.repo_rel` (the prompt's changed-file line shows the same file repo-relative, which would fail S2); noted in SUITE.notes for POSTMORTEM."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; this is a defect in the runner's own prompt. COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt gives the reporting options and their thresholds - "UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong", "BLOCKED when you cannot finish within budget or turns" - neither of which fits a prompt that is merely misleading, which is why the agent had to invent the third route.
Other context: The agent was right, and its choice saved a paid retry. The driver ranked the underlying defect and observed that "a cheaper rung very plausibly would not [notice], and would burn a FAIL + retry"; POSTMORTEM confirmed it independently and filed a TODO. It was fixed before test B by making the prompt print the form the check resolves (JC-207), and test B's agent then passed the check at the first attempt using the path the prompt printed. Not labelled as a judgment call by the agent.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 3.3; section 8, the HISTORY TESTS-TO-SUITE and POSTMORTEM entries; section 6.

## JC-202: Deciding that the cheapest correct cleanup was to change nothing at all (from JC-AB-17)
Who and where: the CLEANUP producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: CLEANUP is a whole paid step whose prose asks the agent to clean up the round so it can land. The agent surveyed the round, found the diff was exactly the two edits the spec asked for, found verify and the suite green and the tree clean, and decided that the right output was no change. The alternative - tidying something, adding a docstring, touching the docs - was available and is what a step called "cleanup" invites.
Rationale: In the agent's own words in its final message: "CLEANUP found the round already landable and changed nothing, which is the cheapest correct outcome here ... nothing in the round became obsolete, there is no dead code or stale artifact to remove, SPEC.refactor is empty and the plan rules out harness and docs changes, so any edit I made would only add living tokens against a $6.25 living preview with spend at $51.02 of the $120 quote."
Spec conflict: Two of the owner's instructions pull opposite ways. CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt says only "Clean up the round, so that it can be landed without issue", while COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says "Living tokens are charged, and refunded on removal: be dense, not clever, and remove what your work makes obsolete". Nothing says that doing nothing is an acceptable answer.
Other context: The agent explicitly declined to log this, writing "No choice here impacted behaviour, so neither judgment-call file was appended" - which under a reasonable person's definition is wrong: this was a choice between reasonable alternatives that nothing settled. The step still cost $0.40 and a checkpoint. Review 3 later observed that any prose inviting a later step to "fix" an earlier artifact was dangerous, because such an edit re-enters the round as an owner's out-of-band edit (JC-255) - so restraint here was also lucky. Test C's and test F's cleanup agents reached the same conclusion with fuller reasoning (JC-297, JC-436). Not labelled; the agent said no call was made.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, the HISTORY CLEANUP entry; section 1; section 2 (checkpoint 2); section 7.

## JC-203: Ranking the postmortem's issues by harm to the vision rather than by dollars (from JC-AB-18)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: POSTMORTEM must rank the round's issues by importance and then write the surviving ones into two carry-forward files that the next round's planner reads. The round's most expensive defect was the delegation bug at $13.33; its most serious was that none of the owner's three authorisations could be verified, which cost nothing. The agent had to pick a ranking axis, and separately to decide how much to write into files that are charged by the token.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "issues ranked by harm to the vision rather than by dollars, so the unverifiable owner words outrank the $13.33 delegation defect; the carry-forward lines are held to pointers with the fix and the cost in them, since docs/ is living at $0.0625 a byte and the round folder is charged nothing, so the evidence, quotes and traces stay in POSTMORTEM.md."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says "First, just identify issues, and rank them by importance" without defining importance; the project vision in harness/project.yaml speaks of "a carefully monitored process", which is the phrase the agent used as its axis.
Other context: The ranking is what the owner sees first, so it materially changes what gets attention. The same step in test B chose a third axis - what leaving each issue in would cost over later rounds (JC-241) - and test C and test D chose that one again (JC-300, JC-345, JC-368). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 8; section 8, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM entry; section 3.9.

## JC-204: Splitting one issue across both carry-forward files rather than choosing one (from JC-AB-19)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The postmortem prose offers two mutually exclusive destinations for each issue: a TODO when everything was in alignment and something was simply missed, or a clarification when something is in conflict or ambiguous and the owner does not know. The round's first issue - that none of the owner's words could be verified because no owner log existed - had one half of each shape: installing the prompt hook is an uncontroversial fix, but whether a missing log should stop a round is a policy only the owner can set. The agent had to pick a branch or break the rule.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "the prose offers TODO or CLARIFICATIONS per issue, and issue 1 (owner words no log can verify) has a missed-alignment half and a policy half; split it across both files rather than lose one half, since the hook is uncontroversial to install and only the owner can say whether a missing log should stop a round."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt presents the two branches as alternatives - "for each issue, decide: 1) ... propose a sensible fix and add it to TODOs for next round. or 2) ... add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review" - and is silent on an issue that is both.
Other context: This was the second and last undefined judgment call of test A, and it is one of the two lines the `done` payload handed back to the owner. Test D's postmortem made the same split for a different issue (JC-369), and test F's had to do it again (JC-437). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 3; section 8, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM entry; section 5 (the `done` JSON's undefined tail).

## JC-205: Routing the round's four remaining issues between the TODO file and the clarifications file (from JC-AB-20)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM producer sub-agent of test A, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Having ranked six issues, the agent had to apply the same two-branch rule to the other four: the delegation bug, the owner's share of the round's cost, the misleading test-path line in the TESTS-TO-SUITE prompt, and the gate shares with no gate enabled. Each required deciding whether the owner's files were in alignment and something had been missed, or whether they were in conflict and the owner did not know.
Rationale: In the agent's own words in its final message: the delegation bug went to a TODO because the behaviour contradicts the harness's own process document and "no test covers the plan-sourced path"; the misleading prompt path and the inert gate shares likewise went to TODOs; but the cost issue went to a clarification - "the owner paid $39.73, 61% of the round, including $14.10 of spec words that accept_producer charges whether or not the round is delegated, a price PLAN-TO-SPEC sized SPEC.md against in a logged defined call, with testBaseCost likewise collapsing three cases into one test function."
Spec conflict: For the cost issue, the tension is between harness/project.yaml's own prices (`specCostPerWord: 0.1`, `testBaseCost: 3`) and what those prices push agents to do - write less spec and fewer tests - which no sentence in the owner's files addresses. For the other three the agent judged the owner's files to be in alignment.
Other context: Reported here as one entry because the report records the four routings only as a bundled list, without separate reasoning for each; the same agent in test B recorded six such routings separately, and each is its own entry (JC-242 to JC-247). None of the four was labelled as a judgment call by the agent, although its own line about ranking (JC-203) covers the same paragraph.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 8, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM entry; section 3.9; section 7.

## JC-206: Keeping the driver tool's warning banner out of the saved result file (from JC-AB-21)
Who and where: the driver of test A, when saving the POSTMORTEM sub-agent's final message.
Context: The sub-agent's final message came back with a banner prepended by the driver's own tooling, because the postmortem's text mentions a settings file. The version of harness/docs/DRIVER.md in force said "Save the sub-agent's final message to `result_file` exactly as returned". Saving it exactly as returned would have put an unrelated block of prose into the archived result; the driver saved the message without it.
Rationale: Not recorded as reasoning. The report states the outcome and the risk: "The JSON itself was intact and unaltered; the prefix was not saved to the result file ... a less careful driver might save the annotation into the result file and get an `invalid` verdict." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the banner is not part of what the agent said, and the rule the driver is really being held to - never edit a result - is about the agent's own content.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The conflict was between DRIVER.md's "exactly as returned" and the fact that what was returned was not only the agent's message.
Other context: The report does not state outright that the driver deliberately stripped the banner, so it is possible it never reached the text the driver copied; it is listed because the report treats it as a thing a less careful driver would have got wrong, which implies a choice was made. The instruction was softened after test A: DRIVER.md now says "as returned" and explains that `record` extracts the JSON from any surrounding text, "a tool-side annotation before or after it included" (JC-175). Not labelled; not listed among the report's own deviations.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 2, "One annotation from the driver's own tool harness"; section 4.

## JC-207: Prompts print paths in the form the checks resolve them in (from JC-CODE-6)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `config.Config.harness_rel` and its three callers, commit 0bcc8fa.
Context: A real sandbox round failed a check because "the prompt printed the changed test files repo-relative while S2 resolves SUITE.json paths HARNESS-relative, so the printed form failed the check" (JC-201). Either the check could accept both forms or the prompt could print the form the check wants.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "Config.harness_rel is the inverse of repo_rel; the prompt, the S2 message and landing's conflicted-file list use it." The note adds that "(the sandbox producer noticed; a cheaper rung would have bought a retry)" - the defect cost nothing only because a strong model worked around it.
Spec conflict: None found; harness/project.yaml fixes no path convention beyond `roundPaths`. merged-B 2.1 says "Paths in config, artifacts, prompts, findings and STATE are H-relative", which the prompt was violating (JC-61).
Other context: The fixer chose one canonical direction rather than a forgiving check, so a mismatch anywhere else still shows up as a failure rather than being silently absorbed. The stub agent was changed to copy the printed list so the stub round now exercises the printed form.
Sources: `config.py` `harness_rel`; `checks.s2_suite`; `round.step_context`; `landing.landing_check`; `plumbing/TESTS-TO-SUITE.txt`; commit 0bcc8fa.

## JC-208: The action stops guessing what the driver's session can spawn (from JC-CODE-65)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `round.action_payload` and `doctor.run`, commit 6b82c29.
Context: The printed action named an agent type such as `shackles-producer-max` and warned when it thought the session lacked that definition; it inferred that from the shell's working directory (JC-155).
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "The action's 'agent_type' warning guessed from the shell's cwd whether the session had the shackles-* definitions. The runner cannot see the session's project directory (CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR reaches hooks only), so it guessed wrong in the sandbox round: no warning, and no such types. The heuristic is gone. The action now carries the whole path: `fallback_agent_type` (general-purpose), `task` (the wrapper with prompt_file filled in) and `rungs` (rung -> model alias), and the session's own agent list is the authority."
Spec conflict: None found; nothing in the owner's files concerns agent registration. What left it open is that the runner and the driver are different processes with different views.
Other context: The decision replaces a guess with a complete set of alternatives and an explicit statement of who decides, which PROCESS.md line 61 records: "the runner cannot see the session's agent list, so it never guesses which applies." `doctor` was changed in the same commit to say that its agent-definitions check measures drift in this repository only.
Sources: `round.action_payload`; `agentdefs.FALLBACK`; `doctor.run`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 61; harness/docs/DRIVER.md lines 8-11; commit 6b82c29.

## JC-209: `start` reports its own flags as warnings (from JC-CODE-68)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `round.start`, commit 9b282a9.
Context: `start` writes FLAG lines into HISTORY for things the driver should know - notably that the owner's approval words could not be verified because no owner log exists. A driver reading only the JSON never saw them.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "start prints its FLAGs as `warnings`, so 'approval words recorded unverified: no owner log' reaches the driver".
Spec conflict: None found; no owner file describes the command's output. What left it open is merged-B 2.1's rule that "Every command prints exactly one JSON object on stdout; humans read stderr", which leaves a JSON-only driver blind to stderr (JC-81).
Other context: A small decision with a real consequence: starting a round on words nobody verified is exactly the case the owner would want to hear about, and it is the normal case in a sandbox (JC-121). CLARIFICATIONS.md carries the open question the same round raised: "Should a missing log stop `start` instead?"
Sources: `round.start`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 47; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 13; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md; commit 9b282a9.

## JC-210: `status` stops previewing the living charge once the round has landed (from JC-CODE-69)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `round.status_payload`, commit 9b282a9.
Context: `status` shows what the living charge would be if the round landed now, computed from the round's base commit to the current one. After landing, the real charge is already booked against the tip actually merged, and the preview disagrees with it.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "status stops previewing the living charge once the round landed, when the booked figure is the number."
Spec conflict: None found; the living charge is the runner's own bookkeeping (JC-41). What left it open is that the preview and the booked charge are computed from different bases.
Other context: The code comment states the rule - "the charge the diff from base_commit would book; once landed the booked living_usd is the figure" - and PROCESS.md repeats it. Two numbers for one concept is the failure this removes.
Sources: `round.status_payload`; `ledger.living_charge`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 52; commit 9b282a9.

## JC-211: Long Windows paths are a `doctor` warning and a sandbox setting (from JC-CODE-91)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `doctor.run` and `probe.sandbox`, commit 2d1387b.
Context: Round worktrees nest several folders deep under a temporary directory, and Windows refuses paths over 260 characters unless git is configured otherwise. A sandbox round failed on it.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "sandbox ... sets core.longpaths on both repositories it creates; doctor warns on Windows when core.longpaths is not true".
Spec conflict: None found; this is the machine, not the spec.
Other context: The split is deliberate - the sandbox sets the option on repositories it creates itself, and only warns about the owner's, because changing a git setting in the owner's repository is not the runner's to do. PROCESS.md's "Windows notes" section records it.
Sources: `doctor.run`; `probe.sandbox`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 165; commit 2d1387b.

## JC-212: The sandbox says what the toy project already contains (from JC-CODE-95)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in `probe.sandbox` (the `toy` field) and the `sandbox` help text, commit 2d1387b.
Context: A sandbox round begins with a plan written in chat about a toy project the planner has never seen. Test A showed a planner writing a plan to "add" a function that already existed (JC-186).
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "sandbox --help and its JSON say that the toy already holds shout(text) and print the toy's files, so the plan can say whether 'add' means change".
Spec conflict: None found; this is test scaffolding. What left it open is that CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt tells the planner to "Plan what is sensible given the vision and the owner's words" without any mechanism for seeing the code.
Other context: The same information went into TESTING.md, so the fixture's content is documented rather than discovered.
Sources: `probe.sandbox`; `commands.add_parsers` (`sandbox` help); harness/docs/TESTING.md line 17; commit 2d1387b.

## JC-213: DRIVER.md tells the driver what its own session has, and does not have (from JC-CODE-100)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in harness/docs/DRIVER.md ("What your session has"), commit cd2be94.
Context: Test A found the driver spawning agents that did not exist, because agent definitions and the owner-log hook register only at session start and only from the session's own project directory.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "What a session has (definitions and hook register only from its own project directory; the session's agent list is the authority), how to fill approval, the gate-flip sequence before start, the spawn (agent_type or fallback_agent_type, model_alias, the action's task), record with --tokens and --agent, and what each record exit code means: exit 10 is an accepted attempt followed by a checkpoint, not a failure."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md describes the driver's job in one bullet ("each `next` gives you a prompt file; give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent (read-only for gates), save its final message to the result file, then `record`") and says nothing about what a session can spawn.
Other context: The section records verified facts about the environment, including the measured fallback behaviour and the honest consequence that with the fallback "a gate keeps every tool, so only the prompt and G1 ... keep it read-only" - which is the state every real test round actually ran in (JC-215, JC-454). A later fix round corrected four more places where the document read as wrong in practice.
Sources: harness/docs/DRIVER.md lines 6-13 and 34-45; `round.action_payload`; commits cd2be94 and 1e567e5.

## JC-214: `check` is not read-only at landing, and the docs say so (from JC-CODE-101)
Who and where: the post-test-A fixer, in harness/docs/DRIVER.md, commit 7c2486f.
Context: `check` is advertised as a safe command to run at any time. At LANDING it merges the landing target into the worktree before running verify and the suite, which changes the tree.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "check is not read-only at LANDING. It writes nothing shared, as PROCESS.md says, but merges the landing target into the worktree; doctor joins the read-only list."
Spec conflict: None found; this is the harness describing itself.
Other context: The fix is to the document, not the code - the merge is deliberate, since merged-B designed the check phase that way so that a hand merge and a checked merge converge (JC-168). What changed is that DRIVER.md no longer promises something the code does not do.
Sources: harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 4; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 52; `landing.landing_check`; `round.check_payload`; commit 7c2486f.

## JC-215: Driving the round from a session outside the sandbox, so every sub-agent ran on one model (from JC-AB-22)
Who and where: the driver of test B - a chat session started in the real repository, not in the sandbox - across the whole round.
Context: The per-rung agent definitions register only for a session started in the folder that holds them (JC-89). This session had been started elsewhere, so none of the per-rung types existed for it. Its options were to start a fresh session inside the sandbox, which harness/docs/DRIVER.md explains would register the definitions and the owner-log hook; or to continue and spawn the documented fallback for every action. It continued, and ran all thirteen sub-agents as general-purpose agents on one model.
Rationale: In the driver's own words: "My session was started in `C:\Users\twolf\Claude\shackles_harness`, not in the sandbox, so the sandbox's `.claude/agents/` definitions were not registered - exactly the case DRIVER.md line 8-10 describes."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left the choice open was DRIVER.md, which described the fallback without saying that restarting in the sandbox was the better move; that advice was only written down afterwards, in review 3's notes.
Other context: The driver presented this as forced, and only the missing definitions were. The single model was not: the same DRIVER.md sentence it followed says the fallback takes the model per action, so it could have spawned the other rungs' models and still used the fallback type. Review 3 said as much, noting that the model choice "was the test's own instruction, not a harness defect, but the harness stayed silent about it" (JC-257). The consequence, which the report states plainly, is that "the `max` and `low` rungs, and therefore the rung-pricing path the ledger cares about, went untested". Not labelled; disclosed as a deviation.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 6, deviation 1; section 8; section 10; section 5 item 3.

## JC-216: Writing "one unit test for whisper" into the plan (from JC-AB-23)
Who and where: the driver of test B, CHAT-TO-PLAN.
Context: The owner asked for a `whisper` function "with a test". The driver had to turn that into a plan sentence, and chose to scope it as "one unit test for whisper". Later steps are built from the plan alone, so the phrase became a constraint on how much test coverage the round could have.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the owner asked for a small change and one test, the plan is charged by the word, and naming a number keeps the round from growing; "one unit test" reads as the smallest faithful rendering of "with a test".
Spec conflict: The phrase collides with PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt, which tells the spec writer to "Exhaust edge cases, within reason". Nothing says which wins, or what "one test" means when the tool being used counts a method, not an assertion, as a test.
Other context: This single phrase produced two of the round's nine undefined judgment calls - one by the spec agent, which resolved it by writing one test method holding three assertions (JC-231), and one by the gate that had to rule on that reading (JC-234). POSTMORTEM ranked it fifth of seven and sent it to the owner as a clarification, tracing it back to the owner's own words "with a test" (JC-246). It is listed in the bootstrap's final report as one of four questions the rounds raised for the owner (JC-478). Not labelled as a judgment call at the time it was made.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md lines 3 and 4; section 9, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC, PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE and POSTMORTEM; section 5 item 12.

## JC-217: Quoting the round at $150 and writing a 137-word plan with two non-goals (from JC-AB-24)
Who and where: the driver of test B, CHAT-TO-PLAN.
Context: The same sizing decision as JC-188, made by a different session for a comparable task - one small function and one small test. This driver chose $150 rather than test A's $120, and a 137-word plan (charged to the owner at $13.70), and wrote two non-goals forbidding new files and new packaging or documentation.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): with every gate switched on the round would run thirteen paid agent runs instead of seven, so the quote had to stretch over roughly twice as many budgets while still leaving each gate a workable share.
Spec conflict: None found; CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt leaves both the quote and the plan's length to the driver ("Size the quote to the value of the change", "Assume a cost of $0.1 per word in the plan").
Other context: The round finished at $86.19, 57.5 percent of the quote. The non-goals turned out to do real work: the spec agent cited them when it refused a refactor and refused an integration test (JC-227, JC-228), and the cleanup agent cited them when it left the docs alone. Not labelled.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 7; section 8 (the ledger table); section 9, the HISTORY entries for PLAN-TO-SPEC and CLEANUP.

## JC-218: Putting every producer step below the configured default rung (from JC-AB-25)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: harness/project.yaml sets `maxAgent: max`, the top rung, as the default for producers. The round was one function and one test. The agent had to decide whether to accept the default or plan cheaper agents, knowing a weaker agent is likelier to produce something a gate rejects, which costs a retry plus the gate again.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "producer rungs below the maxAgent default - medium for PLAN-AGENTS, PLAN-TO-SPEC and POSTMORTEM (a judgment shapes what follows), low for SPEC-TO-TESTS, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION, TESTS-TO-SUITE and CLEANUP (a few mechanical lines each); the whole plan is one function and one test."
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision" and "Optimize the budget, within reason", while harness/project.yaml offers `maxAgent` as a default rather than a requirement (JC-76).
Other context: The same axis as test A's call (JC-190) but applied one rung lower throughout - two sensible agents making the same decision differently, which is the clearest evidence in either round that this is a genuine judgment call. In practice it changed nothing, because every run was on one model anyway (JC-215). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 2; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS entry; section 8.

## JC-219: Putting the gates below their default rung too, and deliberately accepting a weaker judge (from JC-AB-26)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: `gateAgent: max` is the configured default for gates. This was the first round in which gates actually ran, so their rungs mattered for the first time. A weak judge can miss a defect; a strong judge can also raise a nitpick that costs a retry.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "gate rungs below the gateAgent: max default - medium on PLAN-AGENTS-GATE, PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE and POSTMORTEM-GATE, low on SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE and TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE; max effort on a five-line diff buys little and risks a nitpick FAIL that costs a retry plus the gate again."
Spec conflict: None found. The argument rests on COMMON-GATE.txt's cost rule - "A FAIL costs a retry of the producing step ... plus this gate again" - which prices a wrong FAIL but says nothing about how to price the risk of a missed defect.
Other context: The gate that judged this plan disagreed and said so: its non-blocking finding held that "SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE is the acceptance that freezes the tests, yet it was given the cheaper `low` rung while SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE got `medium`; the rungs read backwards" (JC-226). That disagreement, between two agents looking at the same line, is the definition of a judgment call being met in front of a witness. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 3; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS and PLAN-AGENTS-GATE entries.

## JC-220: Concentrating the work budget on the two steps most likely to be re-entered (from JC-AB-27)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The same allocation problem as JC-191, with a different answer. The agent had to decide where the surplus should sit once the two floors were honoured.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "work shares - mass on SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.22 (re-entered on a gate FAIL and on a landing conflict) and PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.20 (not overridable, defects propagate downstream), floors kept at CHAT-TO-PLAN 0.05 and PLAN-AGENTS 0.03, remainder by expected effort."
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt's "Optimize the budget, within reason" is the whole instruction, and the floors come from `defaultShares` in harness/project.yaml.
Other context: The opposite of test A's answer, which put the surplus on POSTMORTEM specifically to keep it away from the code steps (JC-191). The gate that judged it filed a non-blocking finding against the underlying reasoning, holding that "a work share is a per-run budget, not a pool drawn down across attempts", so sizing a share for retries "puts the largest per-run target ($23.10) on the smallest per-run job" (JC-225). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 4; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS entry.

## JC-221: Sizing the gate shares for "one or two rejections" that the ledger does not work that way (from JC-AB-28)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate budget also had to be split. The agent chose to weight it towards the two steps it expected to be rejected.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "gate shares - PLAN-TO-SPEC and SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.27 each, POSTMORTEM 0.18, SPEC-TO-TESTS 0.15, TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.10, PLAN-AGENTS at its 0.03 floor; sized for one or two rejections on the two pivotal steps."
Spec conflict: This is the clearest case in either round of an owner instruction that cannot be carried out. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them", but a share is a per-run target that the runner re-issues in full on every retry, so there is nothing to size. The agent followed the sentence anyway.
Other context: The gate's first finding named exactly this, and the round's POSTMORTEM ranked it fourth of seven and sent it to the owner as a clarification (JC-245). It is one of the four questions the bootstrap's final report puts to the owner (JC-478), and review 3 classified it as "silent, the owner's to settle" with "nothing mechanical to fix" (JC-267); review 4 reached the compatible verdict that with no gated step the sentence is vacuous (JC-318). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 5; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS and POSTMORTEM entries.

## JC-222: Allowing no step to run sub-agents of its own
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agents of test B, test C, both rounds of test D, and test F, each at attempt 1 of its round.
Context: The agents plan may grant each step a number of simultaneous sub-agents, up to a configured cap of two. Granting one would buy parallel work at the cost of a spawn charged to the step's own budget. Five planners in five rounds met the same choice.
Rationale: Test B's agent: "subAgents omitted, so every step gets 0 - no step this round splits into two independent halves worth a spawn, and a sub-agent's cost is the step's own." Test C's: "sub-agents - none at any step; the round's whole diff is one function line and one assert, so a spawn would cost its parent's budget and buy nothing." Test D's, in both rounds: "one function and one test method fit one context, and a spawn buys a hand-off and a spawnCost for no fan-out." Test F's: "no sub-agents for any step (one line of code and one test method do not repay a spawn) and no gateAgents map (no gate runs, so a rung there would be dead config)."
Spec conflict: None found; harness/project.yaml sets `maxSimultaneousSubAgentsPerRound: 2` as a ceiling and nothing sets a floor or a rule for when a spawn earns itself. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt names sub-agents as one of the things to choose and says only "Optimize the budget, within reason".
Other context: Test A's planner reached the same conclusion inside its work-shares line, with the same reason (JC-191). The decision is small only because these rounds were small; on a larger round it is the difference between one agent and three. Nothing in any round disturbed it: no sub-agent ever spawned a sub-agent. It also means the owner's cap (JC-47), which the runner cannot enforce anyway, was never tested against a plan that used it. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in every round.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 6; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 4, section 10; bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (third line) and section 11.3 (second line); bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11 (second line), section 10.
Merged from: JC-AB-29, JC-C-9, JC-D-5, JC-D-27, JC-EF-29

## JC-223: Leaving CHAT-TO-PLAN out of the gate shares although the owner's config names a minimum for it (from JC-AB-30)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The prompt shows the agent a list of minimum shares taken straight from the owner's configuration, and that list includes a gate share for CHAT-TO-PLAN. But CHAT-TO-PLAN's gate is mechanical - it never runs as an agent - and the same prompt sentence says the gate shares map only producers whose gate runs. Including it would honour the stated minimum but strand two percent of the gate budget on a gate that cannot spend it; omitting it would ignore a minimum the owner's own file declares.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "the prose names a minimum gate share for CHAT-TO-PLAN yet also says shares.gates maps only producers whose gate runs, and CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE does not run this round; omitted it, because the runner ignores a CHAT-TO-PLAN gate share and including it would strand 2% of the gates budget."
Spec conflict: A direct one, inside the owner's own file: harness/project.yaml declares `defaultShares.gates.CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.02` while the runner's own prompt says the map covers only producers whose gate runs, and harness/docs/PROCESS.md states that "only LLM gates take gate shares (CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE is mechanical, so a `CHAT-TO-PLAN` gate share is ignored)".
Other context: Both rounds tripped over the same configuration line from different directions - test A's planner logged the no-gates-at-all version of it (JC-192). Review 3 recorded that "both real rounds logged an undefined judgment call on it" and classified it "silent, recurring"; it was fixed by filtering the minimums shown to the steps actually present (JC-262). The gate that judged the plan agreed with the omission: "CHAT-TO-PLAN is rightly absent from shares.gates so nothing strands". Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 2; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS and PLAN-AGENTS-GATE entries; section 5 item 12.

## JC-224: Re-pricing eight of thirteen runs at the rung that actually ran (from JC-AB-31)
Who and where: the driver of test B, at the `record` command for each action whose planned rung was not the rung whose model the sub-agent ran on.
Context: The mirror image of JC-193. By test B the harness's driver documentation had been amended to describe `--agent RUNG`, but applying it still needed judgment: the driver had to decide that the rung is defined by the model the sub-agent ran on rather than by the effort level it ran at, map the model it had onto the roster's `medium` rung, and decide that a truthful ledger was worth overriding the accepted agents plan on eight of thirteen records.
Rationale: In the driver's own words: it passed "`--agent medium` wherever the action's rung (`max` or `low`) was not the rung whose model the sub-agent actually ran on (`opus` = `medium`). Eight records carried `--agent medium`; five did not."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. DRIVER.md at that commit told the driver to pass `--agent` "when the sub-agent ran on another rung's model than the action's", which settles the mechanics but not the judgment about what counts as the same rung.
Other context: The driver then found and reported the cost of its own choice - that `--agent` "silently re-rungs everything", so "the ledger then prices *the whole run* at that rung and ... the HISTORY line gets no FLAG saying the plan's rung was not honoured". The round's POSTMORTEM ranked the resulting divergence third of seven (JC-244), and the gate above it found a factual error in how the postmortem described the mechanism (JC-250). A flag was added afterwards (JC-257). Not labelled as a judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 2; section 5 item 2; section 6, deviation 1; section 8; section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM entry.

## JC-225: Judging the plan's budget-sizing error worth a note rather than a rejection (from JC-AB-32)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate found that the agents plan had been sized on a wrong model of how the budget works - a share is a per-run target, not a pool drawn down across attempts - so the reasoning "sized for one or two rejections" was unsound and had put the largest per-run target on the smallest per-run job. The gate had to decide whether that was worth failing the step, which would cost a retry of PLAN-AGENTS plus the gate running again.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "judged F1 non-blocking - shares are per-run targets, so a misordered allocation costs nothing unless a producer spends to it, well under the $4.36 retry plus this gate."
Spec conflict: None found; the rule the gate applied is COMMON-GATE.txt's "FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that; otherwise PASS with non-blocking findings." Estimating what a mis-sized budget costs downstream is the judgment, and the rule cannot make it.
Other context: The gate's verdict is authoritative in this harness (JC-14), so this call decided whether the round paid for a retry. It was right on the money: the budgets were never approached. The finding itself then had nowhere to go, because no later producer owned the file it was about (JC-238, JC-248). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call, appended by the runner from the gate's verdict.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 7; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS-GATE entry.

## JC-226: Judging the gate rungs "backwards" but not worth a rejection either (from JC-AB-33)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate disagreed with the plan's rung assignment (JC-219), holding that the gate which freezes the tests had been given the cheaper judge while a less consequential gate got the dearer one. Again it had to price its disagreement against the cost of a FAIL.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "judged F2 non-blocking - a low-rung judge on a one-test diff risks little with the implementation gate and the suite behind it, below the cost of a FAIL."
Spec conflict: None found; the same cost rule in COMMON-GATE.txt applies, and the judgment about how much a weaker judge risks is not one any sentence can settle.
Other context: The gate's argument turns on defence in depth - the later gate and the suite would catch what this one missed - which is a claim about the whole pipeline, not about the line it was judging. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 8; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-AGENTS-GATE entry.

## JC-227: Writing no integration test into the spec
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agents of test B and test C, each at attempt 1 of its round.
Context: The spec's test plan is what the test-writing step is built from alone, so if the spec does not ask for an integration test the round will not have one. The step's prose asks for integration tests where sensible. Each round added one pure function to a two-file toy project.
Rationale: Test B's agent, in its own logged words: "no integration test - the prose asks for one where sensible, and whisper is one pure function in one module with no caller, no I/O and no second system to cross; the non-goals forbid adding one." Test C's agent, in its history entry: "no integration test is sensible for a pure function in a single-module toy project whose only crossing, the import path, the verify command already exercises."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Include integration tests that cross multiple systems, where sensible", which is an instruction whose condition the agent must evaluate for itself; in test B the plan's non-goals pulled the same way. The words "where sensible" hand the decision to the agent without a threshold.
Other context: The gate accepted it in test B, and no gate or later step questioned it in test C, where the landed test file has one new method and nothing else. The interesting part is that both agents recorded a decision not to do something, which is the kind of call that usually leaves no trace at all - though test C's agent did not write it to a judgment-call file. Test D's and test F's spec agents declined the same invitation inside broader lines (JC-336, JC-354, JC-432). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in test B; unlabelled in test C.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 9, and the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC entry; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3 (the PLAN-TO-SPEC attempt 2 entry), section 9.5.
Merged from: JC-AB-34, JC-C-15

## JC-228: Declaring no refactoring in the spec, with a refactor allowance available
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agents of test B, test C and both rounds of test D, each at attempt 1 of its round.
Context: The spec carries a `refactor` list, and the project allows a round to spend up to 30 percent of its quote on refactoring - $45 in test B. Each agent had to decide whether anything nearby was worth consolidating, against a plan whose non-goals forbade touching it.
Rationale: Test B's agent: "no refactor, refactor is [] - the only neighbour is shout, two lines, and the non-goals forbid changing it; the 0.3 x 150 = 45 cap buys nothing here." Test C's agent: "No refactor items: the round's whole diff is one line and one test method, and the non-goals forbid touching anything else." Test D's agent, in both rounds: "refactor is empty; the round adds a function and a test and leaves nothing obsolete, so the 0.3 cap stays unused."
Spec conflict: Two owner instructions point opposite ways without a tie-breaker. PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Refactor where sensible" and COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Refactoring in a round is encouraged, capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget"; each round's plan forbade touching the neighbouring code. Nothing states which wins; the agents read the plan as the narrower and therefore governing instruction.
Other context: Test B's agent read the cap correctly as a limit on scope rather than a target, which COMMON-PROJECT.txt states ("the cap limits scope, and gains do not change it"). In test C the same tension reappeared at cleanup, where the agent again read the plan's non-goals as the limit even though its permitted write paths were far wider (JC-297). The parallel decision by the planning step, to reserve no refactor budget in advance, is JC-334. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in tests B and D; unlabelled in test C.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 10, and the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC entry; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3 (the PLAN-TO-SPEC entries), section 2.3, section 9.5; bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (tenth line) and section 11.3 (ninth line).
Merged from: JC-AB-35, JC-C-16, JC-D-12, JC-D-35

## JC-229: Making verify literally identical to the project's own suite command, and shortening its timeout
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agents of test B and of both rounds of test D.
Context: The spec must name a command the harness runs to decide whether the round's work is correct, and set a time limit for it. The agent could write a narrow command that runs only the round's new test, adapt the project's standing suite command, or copy it word for word; the configured timeout default is 600 seconds.
Rationale: Test B's agent, in its final message: "verify is project.yaml's suiteCommand copied verbatim (the toy suite via unittest discover over ../tests/toy), which I ran from HARNESS and confirmed green, with verifyTimeoutSeconds 120 instead of the 600 default because the suite takes under a second; that makes M3 at SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION and M5 at CLEANUP the same command, so a green verify is exactly the plan's validation." Test D's agent, in round 0001: "verify is project.yaml's own suiteCommand ..., timeout 120 s; one command for verify and the permanent suite cannot drift apart, and the toy suite runs in well under a second", and in round 0002 the same sentence "as in round 0001".
Spec conflict: None found; nothing in the owner's files names a verify command, and the 600-second timeout in harness/project.yaml is a default the spec may override.
Other context: The point of copying verbatim - that it collapses two different checks into one command, so a green verify is the same fact as a green suite - is an argument test A's agent did not make when it wrote a portable launcher into the command instead (JC-195), and it is the better of the two answers for a reason neither document states. Test D's round 0002 agent had a third round's precedent available and followed it deliberately, which is itself a decision: nothing carries an earlier round's reasoning forward automatically. Not labelled in test B, although the neighbouring decisions in the same paragraph were; logged as a DEFINED judgment call in both of test D's rounds.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC and CLEANUP entries; bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (fifth line), section 11.3 (fourth line), section 11.5 and 11.6.
Merged from: JC-AB-37, JC-D-7, JC-D-30

## JC-230: Holding the owner-facing spec to 195 words by moving the exact test strings into the file that is not charged (from JC-AB-38)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The same length-against-ambiguity trade-off as JC-197, with an extra move available. The spec is split into two files, one the owner reads and pays for by the word and one the machine reads that is not charged. The agent had to decide what belongs in which.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "SPEC.md sized at 195 words, about $19.50 at $0.1 a word - the exact expected test strings live in SPEC.json, which is not word-charged, and SPEC.md keeps only what the owner reads and what removes ambiguity."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt charges only "per word in the spec" and does not say which of the two files is "the spec"; the cost falls on the prose half alone.
Other context: This is an agent optimising against the owner's own price list, and the same agent saw it later: POSTMORTEM ranked "specCostPerWord falls on SPEC.md alone, so the authoritative values moved into the uncharged SPEC.json" as its seventh issue and sent it to the owner. It also has a downside that showed up in the same round: because the prose half carried only a loose description of the third test case while the machine half pinned the exact string, the two disagreed, which the gate caught (JC-233) and the test-writing step then had to resolve (JC-235). Test C's and test D's spec agents made the same move for the same reason (JC-289, JC-355). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 12; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC and POSTMORTEM entries.

## JC-231: Reading "one unit test" as one test method holding three assertions (from JC-AB-39)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The approved plan said "one unit test for whisper" (JC-216); the step's own prose said to exhaust edge cases within reason. Three cases needed covering. Three test methods would break the plan's count and cost three charges of $3; one method with three assertions would arguably break the prose's spirit. The agent had to choose where a test ends.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "the plan scopes 'one unit test for whisper' while the step prose asks to exhaust edge cases within reason; wrote one test method test_whisper holding three assertions (ordinary, empty, padded-and-period), which honours both and costs one testBaseCost instead of three."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt's "Exhaust edge cases, within reason" against the approved plan's "one unit test", with COMMON-PROJECT.txt's "a new suite test 3. Add either only when it earns that" putting a price on the difference. Nothing defines the unit.
Other context: The gate had to rule on the same question and logged its own undefined call (JC-234); POSTMORTEM then sent it to the owner as a clarification, noting that "two agents logged it undefined" (JC-246). It is one of the four open questions in the bootstrap's final report (JC-478). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 3; section 9, the HISTORY PLAN-TO-SPEC, PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE and POSTMORTEM entries; section 5 item 12.

## JC-233: Letting the two halves of the spec describe the same test case differently (from JC-AB-41)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate found that the prose half described the third test case as text "padded with spaces and ending in a period" while the machine half pinned the actual string, which ends in spaces, not a period. Its own instructions tell it to fail anything two implementers could read differently.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "judged F4 non-blocking - SPEC.md's loose description of the third test case, while SPEC.json pins the exact string the test writer uses, so no ambiguity reaches the tests."

The gate declined, reasoning that the machine-readable half removed the ambiguity before it could reach anyone. Nothing in the owner's files says the two halves of the spec can disagree, or which wins.
Other context: The gate's bet came off, but only because the next agent made the matching judgment call in the same direction (JC-235). Had the test-writing step trusted the prose instead, the round would have produced a test asserting the wrong string. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.

' case, with no assertion on a text that already ends in dots; judged the component covered rather than raising a finding, because SPEC.json's testPlan enumerates exactly these three assertions and beyond the plan is a defect."
