## JC-248: Writing no resolutions at all for four findings rather than risk four "unknown finding" flags (from JC-AB-57)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The producer contract says every gate or owner finding must appear in the final message's resolutions. Four findings were carried to POSTMORTEM, all belonging to other steps, and the runner accepts a resolution only for the producer's own step. Listing them risked four warnings; omitting them risked looking as though they had been ignored.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "the prose is silent on how a postmortem disposes of carried non-blocking findings, and round.py accepts a resolution only for the producer's own step (F1/F2 are PLAN-AGENTS', F3/F4 PLAN-TO-SPEC's); omitted resolutions rather than draw four 'unknown finding ignored' flags, and disposed of all four in POSTMORTEM.md instead."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The ambiguity is in the runner's producer prompt, which lists carried notes with the same identifiers as open findings under an instruction that "every gate or owner finding id must appear in your resolutions".
Other context: The same underlying hole as JC-238, met at the last step by the one agent that could have written something about it; the report calls it "a real hole: F1-F4 were carried to two producers, neither of which could close them, and they end the round neither fixed nor closed". Review 3 ruled they had in fact ended carried, which is their terminal state, and that the prompt wording was the defect; a clause was added to the template (JC-259). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 7; section 5 item 12; section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM entry.

## JC-249: Putting a candidate fix into every clarification although the prose reserves fixes for the other branch (from JC-AB-58)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM producer sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The postmortem prose gives the fix-proposing half to the TODO branch and asks the clarification branch only for the plans and the owner's exact words. Five of the seven issues went to clarifications. The agent had to decide whether following the prose literally - presenting the owner with a conflict and no candidate answer - served the owner better than adding a suggestion the prose did not ask for.
Rationale: In the agent's own logged words: "the prose reserves 'propose a sensible fix' for the TODO branch and asks only for the plans and owner words in the CLARIFICATIONS branch; put a one-line candidate fix in each clarification anyway, so the owner can settle it with a yes rather than a round-trip."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt's two branches - "propose a sensible fix and add it to TODOs for next round" against "add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review with the plan(s) and exact owner words that led to the conflict" - are silent on whether the second may also carry a proposal.
Other context: The argument is entirely about the owner's own cost, which COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt makes an explicit consideration ("spending their time is sensible given their cost"). The gate accepted it. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 8; section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM and POSTMORTEM-GATE entries.

## JC-250: Letting a factually wrong sentence stand inside an otherwise correct postmortem (from JC-AB-59)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate found that the postmortem had described the runner's behaviour wrongly, saying a ledger note appears only when the recorded rung differs from the planned one, when in fact it appears whenever the flag is passed at all. The gate checked the line in the source, re-did the postmortem's arithmetic to confirm the issue survived the error, and had to decide whether a wrong mechanism sentence justified a rejection.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "judged F5 non-blocking - one wrong mechanism clause inside an otherwise complete trace whose conclusion, figures and TODO fix are all correct, far below the $16.81 retry plus this gate."
Spec conflict: None found; the cost rule in COMMON-GATE.txt is what it applied, against POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt's "Is the trace incomplete? Fail."
Other context: The report singles out this finding as proof the gates were doing real work rather than rubber-stamping - "F5 cites `round.py:797` by line and quotes the expression". The same inaccuracy was independently confirmed by review 3. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 26; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM-GATE entry.

## JC-251: Deciding what "the trace is incomplete" means when the trace is complete but wrong (from JC-AB-60)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate's whole instruction for each issue is two questions, one of which is whether the trace is incomplete. The postmortem's trace named every component, plan and owner phrase it was supposed to, but one sentence describing a component was false. The gate had to decide whether "incomplete" covers "present but wrong" - and the answer determined whether a mandatory FAIL applied or the discretionary cost rule did.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "the criterion says 'Is the trace incomplete? Fail' but not whether a factually wrong sentence about a traced component makes the trace incomplete; read incomplete as a missing component/plan/owner-words link and routed the inaccuracy to a non-blocking finding under the cost rule."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt's "Is the trace incomplete? Fail" is a mandatory rule with an undefined term, sitting beside COMMON-GATE.txt's discretionary cost test. Which applies depends entirely on how the word is read.
Other context: This is the sharpest example in either round of an agent choosing the scope of a rule that is written as though it left no choice - and choosing the reading that let it exercise judgment instead of being bound. A gate in test E met the mirror image and decided that a false premise is gateable after all (JC-419). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 9; section 3; section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM-GATE entry.

## JC-252: Deciding that a small item demoted to a "Smaller things" list owes no trace at all (from JC-AB-61)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM-GATE sub-agent of test B, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The postmortem had put one small defect - the doubled runner suffix of JC-237 - under a "Smaller things" heading while still filing it as a TODO. The gate's instructions demand a complete trace "for each issue found by the postmortem". The gate had to decide whether a demoted item is an issue for that purpose, and therefore whether the postmortem owed a trace back to a plan and to the owner's words for a one-line typo.
Rationale: In the gate's own logged words: "the criterion does not say whether an item the postmortem demotes to 'Smaller things' (the doubled '(via runner, ...)' suffix, which still became a TODO) is an 'issue' owing the full trace; required none, since no plan or owner words originated a one-line runner typo."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt says "For each issue found by the postmortem: Is the trace incomplete? Fail", and POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says "For each issue, trace the component(s) touched by the issue to the plan(s) that originated them, and the owner words that led to the plan(s)" - neither defines an issue, and neither allows for a defect that no plan or owner word originated.
Other context: The gate's reasoning identifies a genuine limit of the tracing rule: some defects have no owner-words ancestor at all, so the rule cannot be satisfied for them. Test D's two postmortems met the same limit from the producer's side and answered it differently, by tracing to the spec files instead (JC-349, JC-372), and a clause was eventually written into the postmortem template for it (JC-385). Nothing was changed in response to this one. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-B.md section 9, UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md line 9 (second POSTMORTEM-GATE line); section 9, the HISTORY POSTMORTEM-GATE entry.


================================================================================
REVIEW 3 AND FIX 3
================================================================================

## JC-253: Reviews may only clarify, simplify or correct, and must say what not to build
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, in bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 1, and the first coordinating session, which wrote the same rule into the handoff it left behind (git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md, "Rules that still apply") for reviews 4 and 5 to carry.
Context: The two sandbox test rounds and their automatic postmortems had proposed several new features for the harness, and reviewers and postmortems generate suggestions constantly. Before writing any finding, review 3 had to decide how to treat those proposals; and the coordinating session had to decide whether the review chain could grow the harness or only repair it.
Rationale: The reviewer wrote "Findings that simplify or fix are preferred over findings that add; three suggestions from the sandbox postmortems are recommended *against* below for that reason." The handoff states the same rule as a standing instruction: "Nothing mechanical may depend on the wording of the locked prose. Reviews only clarify, simplify, or correct; no added complexity."
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files says how a reviewer should weigh new machinery against restraint; the nearest owner statement is the cost rule in COMMON-PROJECT.txt, "A new living file costs {{ project.livingFileBaseCost }} beyond its tokens; a new suite test {{ project.testBaseCost }}. Add either only when it earns that", which prices additions for agents inside a round but says nothing about reviewers of the harness itself.
Other context: The order matters and the review-3 auditor's account of it is not quite right: review 3 was committed before the handoff was written, so the reviewer adopted the rule first and the session then made it standing. Review 3's own statement that nothing in its assignment said it is therefore true for review 3 and not for reviews 4 and 5, whose briefs carried it ("Prefer fixes that remove or simplify; say when a suggestion from a test report or postmortem should not be built"). The rule produced the three "do not build" verdicts of review 3 (JC-258, JC-259, JC-260) and every later refusal of the same kind, and it is why the final report can list ten suggestions that were deliberately not built, each with a reason, instead of a harness that grew a feature per round. No later review or fix reversed it.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 1; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md, "Rules that still apply" bullet 2; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 1 step 11 and section 3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 1 step 11 and section 3; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 5.
Merged from: JC-R345-1, JC-COORD-14

## JC-254: Reading "delegate through STEP" as allowing the producer's name, and anchoring the comparison there
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-M1; built by the fix-3 fixer in `round.delegated_through`, commit 2aea88d; the empty-value half added by the post-test-A fixer, commit 619b0a4.
Context: The owner can approve a round with the words "delegate through STEP", which switches off the review pauses up to and including that step. The runner compared the owner's word with the name of the gate that raises each pause, so naming the producer left the pause in place while naming the gate removed it. The reviewer had to decide which of the two readings the harness should honour, and whether to fix the code or to document that only the gate's name works. Separately, a plan that recorded no through step as an empty string rather than as nothing at all had silently switched delegation off (JC-189).
Rationale: Quoted from the finding, the behaviour "is the opposite of the owner's words", and "The CHAT-TO-PLAN prose says only 'no checkpoints up to and including STEP' ... and nothing tells the driver that STEP must be the gate." The fix commit records the mechanism: "delegated_through compared the checkpointsAfter entry (the gate, index 5) with `through`, so 'delegate through PLAN-TO-SPEC' (index 4) left the review after the spec in place, the opposite of the owner's words; the comparison is anchored on the producer of the checkpoint's step". The earlier commit records the empty-value half: "A plan with approval.through '' (or null) is fully delegated. round.py's delegated_through treated only None as 'no through step', so both review checkpoints fired on the delegated sandbox round ($13.33 of $69.98)."
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt line 16 is silent on which name STEP may be, and gives the three words and their meanings with no step vocabulary at all; the owner would naturally say a producer's name, while the runner's checkpoints hang on gates.
Other context: The alternative the reviewer did not take was to teach drivers to write the gate's name, which would have been cheaper in code and worse for an owner speaking plainly. The cost of the second half is recorded in dollars: a delegated round stopped twice for an owner who had delegated. The same fix refuses `--through` with an unknown step name "instead of silently disabling delegation", and the planning prompt now tells the driver to omit `through` unless the owner said "delegate through STEP". Review 4 checked the anchor rule and found it sound; test D exercised both names in the field and review 5 recorded the path as working.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-M1, brittleness table row 1, section 6 note 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md items table; `round.delegated_through`, `round.owner_command`; `schemas.SCHEMAS["PLAN"]`; `plumbing/CHAT-TO-PLAN.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 87; harness/docs/DRIVER.md lines 20-21; harness/tests/test_round.py::test_delegation_from_the_plan; commits 2aea88d and 619b0a4; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3.
Merged from: JC-R345-2, JC-CODE-50

## JC-255: Making an earlier step's finished artifact untouchable by machinery rather than by instruction
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-M2; built by the fix-3 fixer in `checks.m1_strays` (`owned_extra`) and `round.owned_artifacts`, commit 366da07.
Context: Every producer's write paths include the whole round folder, and every artifact lives in it, so any step could edit any other step's artifact. A late step that politely corrected a word in the already-accepted specification had its change committed and then read back by the runner as though the owner had edited the specification behind the harness's back, which sent the round back six steps. The choice was between telling agents not to touch those files and making the runner refuse the change.
Rationale: The reviewer's fix is introduced as "about five lines, no new rule for agents to learn": the runner marks the other steps' artifacts as its own, reverts such an edit and reports it as an ordinary out-of-scope change, "so ... `out_of_band` only ever sees the owner's committed edits". The cost of leaving it is stated: "A cheaper agent applies the gate's own suggestion ... and buys six steps." The fix commit records the same reasoning: "record_producer passes the artifacts of every step but the producer's own ..., so the edit is reverted with one M1 finding naming the file and out_of_band only ever sees the owner's committed edits."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md bullet 1 says only "Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths plus the round folder", and the round folder is exactly where the other artifacts live, so the owner's rule permits the edit it did not intend. COMMON-ROUND.txt lists every artifact to every step as part of the round folder without saying who may write which.
Other context: The reviewer still asked for one clause in the machine-written part of the prompt ("the artifacts of accepted steps are runner-owned after acceptance"), so the fix is belt and braces. The round's own spec file is mapped to the spec artifact's owner so the spec step may write both. The rule also protects the out-of-band detector, whose whole value depends on only the owner being able to trip it (JC-169). Test B had avoided the trap only because its cleanup agent read the runner's source before deciding (JC-238); test C confirmed in the field that the late step left the round folder alone. The fixer applied the rule on the failure paths as well as the success path (JC-272).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-M2, brittleness table row 2, section 6 note 2; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `round.owned_artifacts`; `checks.m1_strays`; `round.record_producer`; `plumbing/producer.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 21; harness/tests/test_checks.py::test_m1_reverts_an_edit_of_an_earlier_steps_artifact; commit 366da07; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md sections 4 and 5.
Merged from: JC-R345-3, JC-CODE-26

## JC-256: Delegating the reviews does not delegate the monitoring
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-M3; built by the fix-3 fixer in `round.record` (`undefined_new`), `round.judgment_report` and `commands.finish`, commit 4c4cf1e, and completed by the fix-4 fixer, commit 736112e.
Context: When an owner delegates a round there are no pauses, and the pause message was the only place that carried the list of judgment calls the agents had recorded. In a delegated round the owner therefore learned nothing about them, although the plan's amendment 8 had required undefined judgment calls to surface loudly (JC-51). The reviewer had to decide whether that was the intended consequence of delegation or a hole.
Rationale: The reviewer's own words, which the fixer then copied into the driver's instructions: "relay `undefined_new` to the owner as it appears, delegated or not: the owner delegated the reviews, not the monitoring". The evidence cited is the sandbox round's own postmortem: "with no checkpoint, the round's five undefined judgment calls and this flag reached the owner nowhere." The fix commit records the mechanism: "The recorded payload now carries undefined_new ... and undefined_file; finish prints the same lines on stderr. DRIVER.md tells the driver to relay undefined_new as it appears, delegated or not." The fix-4 commit closed a residue on the path where a command both records and pauses: "The attempt entry in HISTORY read '(0 undefined since the last checkpoint)' on the attempt that raised the checkpoint, because raise_checkpoint had reset the baseline before the entry rendered: record now passes the baseline it read before routing."
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md line 13 says "Delegation skips only review checkpoints; questions, blocks, limits and spec edits still stop the round", which lists what still stops a round but says nothing about reporting inside a round that does not stop. AGENTS.md requires the record to be appended but says nothing about who reads it; the whole delivery path is the harness's own invention.
Other context: The resulting sentence in DRIVER.md - "Relay `undefined_new` ... to the owner as it appears, delegated or not: the owner delegated the reviews, not the monitoring" - is a policy statement the owner's files do not contain. Test F was run specifically to confirm that an undefined judgment call surfaces, and traced one line through seven places (JC-429).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-M3 and section 6 note 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `round.record`, `round.judgment_report`, `round.done_payload`, `round.checkpoint_payload`, `round.status_payload`; `commands.finish`; harness/docs/DRIVER.md lines 35 and 49; harness/tests/test_checks.py::test_record_reports_the_undefined_lines_the_attempt_added; FINAL-PLAN amendment 8; commits 4c4cf1e and 736112e; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m2.
Merged from: JC-R345-4, JC-CODE-63

## JC-257: A run on a rung other than the action's is re-priced and flagged
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m1 and its first verdict on test B; built by the post-test-A fixer in `round.record` and `commands.add_parsers`, commit 6b82c29, and completed by the fix-3 fixer, commit 3c3f077.
Context: In sandbox round B every sub-agent was run on one model, although the plan for that round had assigned different models to different steps (JC-215, JC-224). The test report filed this as a harness problem. The reviewer had to decide whether the harness was at fault, whether the test was, or both; and the ledger had to decide what to do when the rung that ran is not the rung the action named.
Rationale: The reviewer's verdict: "that was the test's own instruction, not a harness defect, but the harness stayed silent about it". It then asked for the smallest possible change, "one FLAG in `record` when `agent` differs from ... the action's rung", and for the cost note to be written plainly only when the two disagree, "so the note means what the postmortem thought it meant". The earlier commit had added the flag itself: "`record --agent RUNG` prices the run at the rung that actually ran and refuses a name outside the roster instead of silently pricing at maxAgent." The fix-3 commit completed it: "record now compares: when the rung differs it flags 'ran on rung X, the action named Y' (HISTORY and the payload's warnings) and keeps the ledger suffix; when they agree the note is plain."
Spec conflict: None found. subAgents.yaml prices four rungs and project.yaml names which rung each kind uses; neither anticipates that the session doing the spawning might not have the model, and nothing says what should happen when the model that actually ran differs from the one planned.
Other context: DRIVER.md adds the standing advice that follows from it: "A session that can run one model only should `start --agent <rung>` ..., so every action names that rung, PLAN-AGENTS is told it, the ledger prices every run at it and `record --agent` is never needed." Review 4 confirmed the flag and found only that the command's help text still described the old behaviour, which fix 4 corrected. Test C used the one-model start option instead and needed no flag - and had to infer that from the action payloads, because the two documents disagreed (JC-281).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m1 and verdict 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `round.record`; `commands.add_parsers` (`--agent` help); harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 50; harness/docs/DRIVER.md lines 12 and 32; harness/tests/test_round.py::test_record_agent_names_the_rung_that_ran; commits 6b82c29 and 3c3f077; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-n1.
Merged from: JC-R345-5, JC-CODE-64

## JC-258: Declining to build the proposal that the driver announce its model before the round is planned (from JC-R345-6)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, at the end of finding R3-m1.
Context: The automatic postmortem of sandbox round B proposed a new mechanism by which the driving session would declare, before planning, which models it could actually run. The reviewer had to decide whether to endorse it.
Rationale: Quoted: "Do not build the 'driver reports it before the round is planned' TODO from sandbox B; `start --agent` already is that." The existing option forces one model for the whole round and tells the planning step about it.
Spec conflict: None found. The proposal concerned a capability of the driving session, which no owner file describes; what left the choice open is that the existing option was undocumented for drivers, so the postmortem did not know it existed.
Other context: The reviewer paired the refusal with a documentation fix ("one sentence in DRIVER.md"), which fix 3 wrote and test C then used successfully (JC-281), so the refusal was validated in the field. No later review revisited it.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m1 and verdict 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 12; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4.

## JC-259: Carried notes are already closed, so the prompt is wrong and no new closing path is needed
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m2 and its second verdict; built by the fix-3 fixer in `plumbing/producer.txt`, commit 1849698.
Context: A reviewing gate that passes a step may still leave notes. Those notes are shown to every later step with the same identifiers as live findings, and two real agents recorded that they could not tell whether they were expected to answer them (JC-238, JC-248); the sandbox postmortem proposed giving the final step of a round the power to close them. The reviewer had to decide whether the runner was wrong, the prompt was wrong, or neither.
Rationale: Quoted: "So the runner is not defective and F1 to F4 did not 'end the round open'; they ended it carried, which is their terminal state. The gap is the prompt." And on the proposal: "do not build it; there is nothing to close, and it adds a resolution path for a state that does not need one." The fix commit records what the prompt now says: "Carried notes carry F<n> ids and the same fields as open findings, and the resolution rule above them reads as though it covers them, so two agents logged undefined judgment calls on exactly this. producer.txt now says they are already accepted with their artifact, deferred, flagged for the owner, or the owner's answer; that they need no resolution entry; and to act on one only inside the step's own WRITE_PATHS, else leave it for POSTMORTEM."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt distinguishes a blocking finding from a note ("otherwise PASS with non-blocking findings") but never says what a later step owes a note, and treats findings as things to be resolved; the contradiction was inside the runner's own generated prompt, not the owner's prose.
Other context: Two real agents recording an undefined judgment call on the same sentence is the evidence. The commit also records a decision not taken: "No close path for carried findings is built (review 3, verdict 2)" - carried notes stay carried for the round and reach the postmortem. Review 4 recorded that test C's late step then "left the carried flag alone 'for POSTMORTEM', as the clause says", and reviews 4 and 5 both recorded that the proposed closing path was still not needed.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m2 and verdict 2; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `plumbing/producer.txt`; `round.step_context` (`carried`); harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 77; commit 1849698; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md sections 3 and 4.
Merged from: JC-R345-7, JC-CODE-96

## JC-260: The ledger paying an agent to delete tests is the owner's arithmetic, not a bug to patch (from JC-R345-8)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, its third verdict, with the matching brittleness row.
Context: The harness refunds the cost of text an agent removes. A real agent calculated that archiving the round's only test file was worth about forty-four dollars of refund against sixteen for keeping it, and said so (JC-199). The reviewer had to decide whether to add a rule preventing the refund, or to leave the numbers alone and send the question to the owner.
Rationale: Quoted: "the owner's numbers and prose ... `ledger.living_charge` ... implements the refund exactly as written and the archive move is a removal from the living paths. No harness change; the CLARIFICATION the postmortem filed is the right channel. If the owner wants a guard, the smallest is a few-line exemption ... which is theirs to decide."
Spec conflict: Two owner sentences pull against each other. COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Living tokens are charged when created and refunded on removal, in code and prose alike", while TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt asks for "a realistic analysis of expected long-term costs and benefits" when deciding where a test goes. Neither says the refund should not apply to archiving.
Other context: The reviewer named the exact patch it was refusing to write, so the owner could ask for it. Fix 3 recorded "no `living_charge` exemption added". No later review reopened it; the question stands in the harness's clarifications file for the owner, and it is the first of the four the final report puts to them (JC-478).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md verdict 3 and brittleness table; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md items table; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md.

## JC-261: The first gate staying mechanical is design, and only a trace of it is owed (from JC-R345-9)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m3 and its fourth verdict.
Context: The owner's settings list seven gates. One of them, the check on the plan, is performed by the runner itself rather than by an agent, and it left no attempt count, no log line and no verdict file, so a driver who switched "every gate on" could not confirm from the harness's own status that it had run. The reviewer had to decide whether the gate should become a real agent gate or whether the gap was only one of record-keeping.
Rationale: Quoted: "Design-wise the gate is mechanical and PROCESS.md:34 says so". The fix asked for is two lines that count an attempt and write a log line; the reviewer also traced why the driver had not understood this, namely that the sandbox builder rewrote the owner's settings file and dropped the comment that says "mechanical, run by the runner".
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml lists `CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE: 0` inside the block of gates with the comment "# mechanical, run by the runner; checkpoint after plan", so the owner's own file both lists it as a gate and says it is not an agent gate.
Other context: The same finding refutes a budget figure the test report gave, on the evidence of the archived prompt. Fix 3 built the trace (JC-271) and the comment-preserving sandbox copy (JC-269). Review 4 then found that one sentence in harness/docs/PROCESS.md still claimed every gate leaves a verdict file, which fix 4 corrected by changing one word to "An LLM gate".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m3 and verdict 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commits b8c4433 and 7936c64; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m7.

## JC-262: Filtering the misleading budget line in the generated prompt rather than the ledger or the owner's settings
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m4; built by the fix-3 fixer in `prompts.step_context` (`minimum_shares`), `prompts.step_table` and `plumbing/PLAN-AGENTS.txt`, commit 9edb5ca.
Context: The prompt for the planning step printed the owner's table of minimum budget shares raw, including a minimum for a gate that never runs. The ledger ignores that entry. Both real rounds recorded this as an unresolved question (JC-192, JC-223). The reviewer had three places it could change: the owner's settings file, the ledger, or the prompt.
Rationale: The reviewer chose the prompt, asking for "a `minimum_shares` step-context key for PLAN-AGENTS filtered to the steps present ... rendered in place of `project.defaultShares`; four lines in `prompts.step_context`, one token change in the plumbing". The unstated but visible premise is that the owner's file and the ledger are both behaving as written, and only the display misleads. The fix commit records the same: "The PLAN-AGENTS plumbing rendered project.defaultShares raw, including a gate share for CHAT-TO-PLAN whose gate never runs and which the ledger drops; both real rounds logged an undefined judgment call on it. A minimum_shares step-context key filters the owner's floors to the producers not overridden (work) and to the producers whose LLM gate runs (gates)."
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml carries `defaultShares.gates.CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.02` for a gate the same file marks as mechanical, so the owner's file contains the contradiction; the owner's files are the ones agents must change "only when genuinely necessary" (spec.yaml header), which pushed the fix elsewhere.
Other context: The same fix round added a helper naming the gates that take a share and a flag in the step table so that the ledger, the prompt and the artifact check all agree on which gates take one (JC-184). Review 4 then found a second half the filter had left open, namely whether the planning step should list the first step's share at all, which it judged a money question rather than a display one (JC-309); the fixer's own choice inside the filter is JC-273.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m4 and brittleness table; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `prompts.step_context` (`minimum_shares`), `prompts.step_table`; `pipeline.llm_gates`; `ledger.shares`; `checks.s1_agents_plan`; `plumbing/PLAN-AGENTS.txt`; harness/tests/test_render.py::test_step_context_minimum_shares_are_filtered_to_the_steps_present; commits 9edb5ca and 94a6095; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m3.
Merged from: JC-R345-10, JC-CODE-20

## JC-263: Giving the owner's "flag it" sentence a route rather than deleting the field it fills
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m5; built by the fix-3 fixer in `round.accept_producer` (the `raise_with_owner` loop), commit 403f237.
Context: The owner's prose tells the step that sorts tests to flag anything worth raising with the owner; the data format has a field for it; nothing in the runner read the field, so in a delegated round the flag reached the owner only by accident (JC-239, JC-243). The reviewer set out two fixes and recommended one.
Rationale: Quoted: "Fix, the smaller of two: at TESTS-TO-SUITE's acceptance ... write each item to HISTORY as 'flagged for the owner: ...' and append it to `st['carried']` ... or delete the field from the schema and let NEEDS-OWNER be the only channel. Either is a few lines; the first keeps the owner's sentence honest." The fix commit repeats it: "At TESTS-TO-SUITE's acceptance each item goes to HISTORY as 'flagged for the owner: ...' and to the carried list with source 'flag', so every later producer, POSTMORTEM and a reader of HISTORY see it; the smaller of the review's two fixes, keeping the owner's sentence honest."
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "If there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner, flag it", without saying where a flag goes or who reads it; the owner's files define no channel to the owner except the checkpoint, which this deliberately does not use.
Other context: The alternative - raising a checkpoint - would have stopped a delegated round over a test-placement opinion, at the owner's per-checkpoint cost. The route chosen reaches the owner only through the round's history and the postmortem, which is slower and weaker; the fixer called it "the smaller of the review's two fixes". The rejected alternative would have removed an owner-visible capability to save code. Review 4 found the route "sound; test C exercised it end to end" (JC-296), and review 5 recorded a second field confirmation from test D (JC-364).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m5; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; `round.accept_producer`; `schemas.SCHEMAS["SUITE"]`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 77; harness/tests/test_checks.py::test_raise_with_owner_is_flagged_in_history_and_carried; commit 403f237; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3.
Merged from: JC-R345-11, JC-CODE-49

## JC-264: Re-running a whole step after the owner answers a question is routing, not a defect
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m7, and the reviewer of review 5, in the closing cost note of its own major finding R5-M1.
Context: When a step asks the owner a question and the owner says "carry on", the runner discards the work and runs the whole step again with the answer attached, which costs a full paid agent run whose content is already known. Review 3 documented the behaviour and had to decide whether to propose changing it. Review 5 then restored the pause in delegated rounds (JC-373), which makes owners meet this cost far more often, and had to decide whether its own fix obliged it to change the routing too.
Rationale: Review 3: "If the re-run at `question` is judged too expensive, the smaller change is at `approve`: with the gate off, accept the attempt as built and log the assumption as an owner-approved defined call; with the gate on, re-run only the gate. Not recommended now: it is routing, not a defect." Review 5 kept the same answer and priced the alternatives for the owner instead: "`approve` at a `question` re-runs the producer with 'proceed on your stated assumption' as O1 (R3-m7, routing, unchanged); a delegating owner who wants the assumption kept as built pays one producer run for it, or answers with `override` where the step is overridable. Not a defect, said in the final-test notes."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt tells a step to "build on your best assumption meanwhile and state it", which implies the work already exists when the answer arrives, but no owner sentence says whether an approving answer should keep that work or discard it.
Other context: Review 3's only action was to correct the driver's documentation, which fix 3 did. Review 5's restatement came with a larger consequence, since the pause now happens more often, and it drove the related finding that the pause's list of resume commands omitted one of the two cheaper routes (JC-114). Neither review proposed the cheaper route it had itself described.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m7 and section 6 note 4; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M1 and section 7; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md items table; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commits 1e567e5, 7b45814.
Merged from: JC-R345-12, JC-R345-52

## JC-265: Grading a round that can start and land without any owner word as a minor (from JC-R345-13)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, finding R3-m9 in section 2 "Minor".
Context: Passing the delegate flag on the start command let a round begin with no recorded owner words at all, even with the approval gate switched on, and with no warning. The reviewer had to place this on its own severity scale, where "major" means a defect that costs an agent run, corrupts round records or misleads the driver.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the reviewer graded by consequence to the next test runs rather than by principle. The hole needs a driver to pass a flag that the owner did not ask for, the fix is one condition removed, and the sandbox rounds are unaffected because their approval gate is off, so nothing on the planned path would draw a false conclusion from it; that fits "wrong but cheap".
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt lists the owner's words that count and says they are "needed for both plan and quote", but the owner's files never say that the runner must refuse to start without them; the gate that would check it is one the owner can also switch off.
Other context: The grading did not change the outcome, because fix 3 fixed every finding of every severity (JC-276), and review 4 confirmed the plan's approval words are now required whenever that gate is on. Against the same scale, one could argue that a round landing on the shared branch with no owner word recorded corrupts the round record, which would have made it a major.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-m9; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commit 1ac860a; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4.

## JC-266: Accepting a standing contradiction between the owner's prose and the runner's own prompt in a delegated round (from JC-R345-14)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, section 5 "Brittleness", the row for "Checkpoint after this step, with the owner."
Context: Two of the owner's step descriptions end with the sentence "Checkpoint after this step, with the owner." In a delegated round no such pause happens, and the machine-written part of the same prompt says the round simply advances, so the agent reads two contradictory statements. The reviewer had to decide whether to raise this as a finding.
Rationale: The reviewer classified it as "soft" rather than "silent", with the note "the prose says a checkpoint follows, the plumbing line says the round advances; agents in round B trusted the plumbing". In this review's own scheme, "soft" means the harness "degrades with a warning or a truthful plumbing line", which is explicitly not a defect.
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt and CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt both say "Checkpoint after this step, with the owner.", while the owner's SPEC.md allows delegation to skip review pauses; the two owner files are not reconciled anywhere.
Other context: The reading was confirmed in the field twice, and review 5 later found that in a "delegate through STEP" round the machine-written line was not in fact truthful for the steps after the named one (JC-400). So the tolerance held, but only after the plumbing line was made accurate.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 5; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m3; commit 762f2c3.

## JC-267: The owner's sentence about expecting one or two gate rejections is the owner's to settle (from JC-R345-15)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 3, section 5 "Brittleness", the row for PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.
Context: The owner tells the planning step to "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them", but a step's budget is a target re-issued in full on each retry rather than a pot drawn down, so an agent that sizes for rejections over-allocates. A real gate raised this as a finding in round B (JC-221, JC-225). The reviewer had to decide whether the harness should change its budget arithmetic, change the prose, or leave it.
Rationale: Quoted: "a share is a per-run target re-issued on every retry; F1 in round B, a CLARIFICATION is filed; nothing mechanical to fix ... the owner's to settle."
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt's "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them" against harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 115, which gives each step its share of the quote per run.
Other context: Review 4 revisited the same sentence for a round with every gate switched off and reached a compatible but distinct verdict, that with no gated step the sentence is "vacuous rather than contradictory" and the agents' recorded uncertainty was the right outcome (JC-318). Neither review proposed an edit to the owner's prose, and the question is one of the four the final report puts to the owner (JC-478).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 5; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md.

## JC-268: Repairing the sandbox and probe builders although no finding asked for it, driven from the owner's own file list (from JC-R345-16)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, first row of the items table, commit 7936c64.
Context: The fixer's assignment was review 3's findings and the open test-B items. While working, it found that the two tools that build throwaway copies of the repository copied a fixed list of files and so missed the owner's newly added repository-level SPEC.md; a fresh sandbox then failed the harness's own environment and drift checks. The fixer had to decide whether to treat this as in scope, and how general the repair should be.
Rationale: The commit message records both halves: the builders "copied harness/ plus a fixed list of root files, so the owner's new root SPEC.md was missing and a fresh sandbox failed spec status, doctor and start; both now copy spec.yaml and every path it lists, wherever spec.yaml puts them, on top of what they copied before."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml is a list of the owner's files that is expected to change ("They belong to the owner and change often. Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them"), which the fixed list in the builders did not honour; no owner sentence told the builders to read that list, so the tools had been written against a snapshot of it.
Other context: This was scope the review did not name, and it went in first because the later real-agent tests all run in sandboxes. The first coordinating session had diagnosed the same defect precisely and left it for this fixer rather than fixing it itself (JC-466). Review 4 checked it and found it sound, noting one place the same pattern had been missed, which fix 4 then closed (JC-329).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md items table and "What the docs do not say"; commit 7936c64; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4 and R4-n8.

## JC-269: Editing the owner's settings file textually so the comments survive, and accepting a known limit in the method
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, in `probe.set_yaml_keys` and `probe.sandbox`, commit 7936c64; extended to the probe fixtures by the fix-4 fixer, commit dddbaf2.
Context: The sandbox builder used to rewrite the owner's settings file through a YAML library, which silently dropped every comment, including the one that explains that the first gate is mechanical - a comment a real driver had needed and not seen (JC-261). Review 3 asked for the comments to survive. The fixer had to choose how much YAML handling to write and how much fragility to accept.
Rationale: Recorded in the fix report: "`probe.set_yaml_keys` replaces a top-level key's block (the key line plus the indented or `- ` lines under it) or appends the key; a column-0 comment inside a key's value would end the block early, a shape the owner's file does not have." The commit states the property: "The sandbox edits livingSourcePaths and suiteCommand in project.yaml textually (set_yaml_keys) instead of re-dumping the file, so the owner's comments survive the copy."
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml's header - "This file contains (and only contains) user settings, and is not to be edited without owner approval" - and its per-key comments are part of what the owner wrote; a probe or sandbox that drops them is showing an agent a different spec than the real one. The owner's files do not say that comments are part of what agents must preserve.
Other context: The fixer chose a small, shape-dependent text editor over either a comment-preserving YAML dependency, which the plan forbids ("PyYAML and pytest are installed there; no other dependencies"), or a full parser of its own. A test counts the comment lines so the property cannot regress (JC-270). Fix 4 later had to narrow the same method again for a block whose inline comments it would have destroyed (JC-329).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md "What the docs do not say"; commit 7936c64; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md "Constraints from the owner"; `probe.set_yaml_keys`, `probe.sandbox`; harness/tests/fixtures.py `write_spec`; harness/tests/test_probe.py::test_set_yaml_keys_replaces_or_appends_and_keeps_comments; commit dddbaf2.
Merged from: JC-R345-17, JC-CODE-94

## JC-270: Letting the new test count the owner's comments but never read them (from JC-R345-18)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, in harness/tests/test_probe.py, recorded in bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md under "What the docs do not say".
Context: Having made the sandbox preserve the owner's comments (JC-269), the fixer needed a test that would catch a regression. The obvious test asserts that a particular comment is present, which would tie the test suite to the wording of a file the owner may rewrite at any time.
Rationale: Recorded: "The real-repository sandbox test asserts only that the copy has at least as many `#` lines as the source, never on any comment's wording."
Spec conflict: The governing rule is the plan's rather than the owner's files: FINAL-PLAN amendment 6 says "No test asserts on any sentence of the owner's prose" (JC-94). The owner's SPEC.md says only that the repository "must survive arbitrary edits to them", leaving the test author to decide what counts as depending on the wording.
Other context: Review 4 checked exactly this and cleared it (JC-322). Fix 4's own comment-count test for the probe repositories followed the same pattern. The boundary is defensible but not forced: deleting one comment does break this test.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; harness/tests/test_probe.py; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendment 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 6.

## JC-271: Counting the mechanical gate's passes instead of stamping the attempt number the review specified
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, in `round.mechanical_loop` (the approval branch), commit b8c4433.
Context: Review 3 asked for two lines, one of them literally "set `st['attempts']['CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE'] = 1`" (JC-261). The fixer noticed that this gate can pass more than once in a round.
Rationale: Recorded in the fix report: "the mechanical gate's attempt is incremented per pass, not set to 1 as the review wrote, because the gate passes again after an out-of-band plan edit or an UPSTREAM to CHAT-TO-PLAN and attempt numbers never restart." The commit states what the gate now leaves behind: "It now counts one attempt each time it passes ... and writes 'CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE passed mechanically (PLAN.json valid; approval: <mode> <words> from <source>)'."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; the rule the fixer invoked is the harness's own, stated in harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 41: "Attempt numbers of a step never restart, so `PROMPTS/`, `RESULTS/` and `FINDINGS/` names stay unique even after UPSTREAM or an out-of-band edit." harness/project.yaml's own comment says the gate is mechanical, but it sits in the same list as the other gates and so is expected to leave the same trace.
Other context: Review 4 checked the departure and endorsed it: "the attempt is counted per pass (the fixer's reason is right: attempt numbers never restart)". The fixer also enriched the log line beyond the review's text, adding the source of the approval, which fix 5 later relied on. No verdict file is written for this gate, which PROCESS.md line 34 states.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commit b8c4433; `round.mechanical_loop`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 34 and 41; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md.
Merged from: JC-R345-19, JC-CODE-53

## JC-272: Applying the new ownership rule on the failure paths as well as the success path (from JC-R345-20)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, commit 366da07 and the note in bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md.
Context: Review 3's fix for the earlier-artifact problem (JC-255) described the change at the point where a step finishes normally. A step can also end by pointing at an earlier step or by declaring itself blocked, and its files are processed then too.
Rationale: Recorded: "`owned_extra` is passed on the UPSTREAM/BLOCKED path as well as on DONE, so the revert rule is the same whatever the status; the M1 finding is blocking, so the producer re-runs (attempt 2) with the finding, as for any stray."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's AGENTS.md states a single rule about where a producer may write and does not vary it by how the step ends; the runner's code had two paths, which is what left the choice open.
Other context: Review 4 verified the wider application and found it sound. harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 111 now states the symmetry for the pre-existing checks as well.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commit 366da07; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4.

## JC-273: Keeping the first step in the printed work minimums while dropping it from the gate minimums (from JC-R345-21)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, commit 9edb5ca and the note in bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md.
Context: The filter the review asked for (JC-262) had to decide, for each of two tables, which steps belong. The review's wording was "work: producers not overridden; gates: producers whose LLM gate runs", which leaves the first step, planning, ambiguous because it runs before the round starts.
Rationale: Recorded: "`minimum_shares.work` keeps CHAT-TO-PLAN (the reviewer's 'producers not overridden'; its floor is charged at `start`, and round B's plan honoured it); `minimum_shares.gates` lists only producers whose LLM gate runs, so CHAT-TO-PLAN never appears there."
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml sets both `defaultShares.work.CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.05` and `defaultShares.gates.CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.02` while the same file marks that gate mechanical, so the owner's file supports keeping one entry and not the other only once you know which of the two the ledger reads.
Other context: Review 4 examined the money consequence and endorsed the choice explicitly: "the money reading (R4b) says the fixer's choice to keep it was right and the prompt should say why", and raised the missing explanation as a finding of its own (JC-309). Fix 4 then wrote the explanation into the prompt. Test C's planner had already met the same ambiguity from the other side and kept the share for the same arithmetic reason (JC-286).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commit 9edb5ca; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m3 and section 4; `plumbing/PLAN-AGENTS.txt`.

## JC-274: Refusing to strengthen the instruction that an agent's last message be only JSON
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, items table row "test-B 5.10", and the driver of test C, writing its report after the round finished.
Context: Two of thirteen agents in sandbox round B wrapped their final machine-readable answer in prose, contrary to the instruction. The test report raised it; review 3 did not make it a finding. The fixer had to decide whether to act on the test report's item on its own. One round later, one of eight replies in test C broke the same rule and the runner extracted it correctly anyway; the driver had to decide whether that evidence argued for revisiting the decision or supported it.
Rationale: The fixer recorded in the status column: "skipped ... extraction coped every time and every message was valid at attempt 1; a stronger wrapper is a guess with no evidence it lands better". Test C's driver reached the same conclusion from the new data: "PLAN-TO-SPEC a1 opened with a sentence of prose and wrapped the object in a ```json fence. `record` extracted it correctly, and the Agent tool's `agentId: ...` suffix was extracted correctly all eight times. Test B saw 2 of 13; fix 3 deliberately declined to strengthen the wrapper, and 1 of 8 is consistent with that decision rather than against it."
Spec conflict: None found. The instruction being disobeyed is the harness's own generated prompt, not an owner file - "Final message: exactly one JSON object and nothing else"; what left the choice open is that the harness already tolerates the disobedience (JC-17, JC-175), so there is no measurable failure to fix against, and nothing says how often a breach must occur before the mechanism should change.
Other context: The rate was very similar in the two rounds, so the evidence genuinely pointed either way and the driver had to choose a reading. The same call was then re-affirmed twice more: review 4 listed "a stronger JSON wrapper" among the suggestions not to build, noting "1 of 8, extraction coped, fix 3's reasoning stands"; review 5 recorded "no change; the extractor's record is 36 of 36 across B, C and D". This is the one decision in the bootstrap that four separate agents endorsed in turn.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md items table; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 7 item 11 and section 10; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3.
Merged from: JC-R345-22, JC-C-33

## JC-275: Accepting that the new honesty check can fire on an attempt that was already corrected (from JC-R345-23)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 3, commit adf7bb0 and the note in bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md.
Context: Review 3 asked for a warning when an agent's claimed count of judgment calls differs from the number of lines the files actually gained. One of the harness's existing checks restores a judgment-call file that an agent rewrote rather than appended to. The fixer had to decide where in the sequence the comparison runs, knowing the two can interact.
Rationale: Recorded: "the comparison runs after M4 restored a rewritten file, so an M4 attempt may also show a claims FLAG; both are honest."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. AGENTS.md asks agents to "append a small record" per call, and the plan's amendment 8 asks for the counts to be surfaced; neither says what to report when an agent both miscounts and rewrites.
Other context: The choice makes a double warning possible on one attempt, which the fixer judged acceptable because each warning states a true fact. Review 4 confirmed the ordering and raised only that the prompt should ask for a per-run count rather than a round total, because an agent reporting its running total on a second attempt would earn a warning it did not deserve. The check itself is JC-277.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md; commit adf7bb0; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-n6 and section 4.

## JC-276: `--delegate` never substitutes for the owner's word (from JC-CODE-52)
Who and where: the fix-3 fixer, in `round.validate_plan`, commit 1ac860a.
Context: When the approval gate is enabled the plan must carry the owner's approval. The first implementation skipped that requirement whenever the delegate flag was passed, so a round could start with no owner words at all (JC-265).
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "a delegated start satisfied CHAT-TO-PLAN-GATE without any owner words, unverified and unflagged. The flag overrides the mode, never the owner's word: the check no longer looks at it."
Spec conflict: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt lists "delegate" / "delegated" as words that mean "approved; no checkpoints", which are the owner's words; a command-line flag is the driver's.
Other context: The code comment states the principle in six words - "--delegate overrides the mode, never the owner's word" - and it is the same distinction the whole owner-quote mechanism rests on (JC-49). With the approval gate at 0, as project.yaml has it today, no word is required at all; that gap is written up in CLARIFICATIONS.md (JC-128).
Sources: `round.validate_plan`, `round.start`; harness/docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md; harness/tests/test_round.py::test_start_requires_approval_words_when_the_gate_is_enabled; commit 1ac860a.

## JC-277: The message's claimed judgment-call counts are compared with the files (from JC-CODE-62)
Who and where: the fix-3 fixer, in `round.claimed_calls`, commit adf7bb0.
Context: The producer's final message reports how many judgment calls it made; the files it appended to are the record. The two can disagree.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "record validated the producer's judgment_calls counts and then ignored them, so a message claiming calls the files do not show (or the reverse) was silent. After M4, the counts (ints, or list lengths) are compared with what the two files gained since the prompt commit, and a mismatch is one FLAG."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md requires the agent to "append a small record with context inside your round's UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md file" but the message shape the runner invented also asks for counts; nothing reconciles the two (JC-139).
Other context: The choice is to flag rather than fail, because the files are the authority and a wrong count is a reporting error, not a defect in the work. The comparison runs after the rewrite check has already restored any rewritten line, so it measures the file the runner will keep - which can produce two warnings on one attempt (JC-275).
Sources: `round.claimed_calls`, `round.record_producer`; `plumbing/producer.txt`; harness/tests/test_checks.py::test_claimed_judgment_calls_are_compared_with_the_files; commits adf7bb0 and b3a6f96.

TEST C: THE SANDBOX ROUND WITH GATES OFF, THE WORD "APPROVE", AND ONE FORCED QUESTION

## JC-278: Fast-forwarding the driver's worktree so the round would test the post-fix-3 harness (from JC-C-1)
Who and where: the driver of test C, before it built the sandbox, in its own worktree.
Context: The driver's worktree had been created from the branch as it stood on the shared server copy and was ten commits behind the copy of the project on this machine, whose newest commit contained fix 3. Because the sandbox is built by copying the driver's own worktree, the driver had to decide which version of the harness the test would actually exercise: the older one already in its tree, or the newer one on the machine. It also had to decide whether to stop and ask the owner, since the newer work existed only on this machine and had not been pushed anywhere.
Rationale: "Without the fast-forward the sandbox would have been built from the pre-fix-3 runner - the wrong system under test - and `bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md`, which the assignment told me to read, would not have existed in my tree. `ca24820` is a strict ancestor of `255461d` and my tree was clean, so `git merge --ff-only main` was a pointer move with no conflict and no local work at risk."
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files says which revision of the harness a test should run against. What left the choice open is the workspace instruction file at C:\Users\twolf\Claude\CLAUDE.md, which says "Before working on any project, pull the latest main. Alert the owner if it's a surprise that there are local, unmerged changes." That tells the agent to pull and to raise the surprise, but not whether to halt and wait for the owner before spending tens of dollars of test.
Other context: The driver did not halt; it fast-forwarded, ran the whole test, and raised the unpushed local work as a written flag in the report instead - which is the visible consequence of the coordinating session's rule that work leaves the machine only after a passing review or at a pause (JC-464). Every finding in test C therefore describes the harness at the newer commit, which is what review 4 then checked line by line. The coordinating session later made the fast-forward a standing rule for every sub-agent (JC-468). The harness applied no label: it happened outside the round.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 8 item 1; section 3; section 11.

## JC-279: Designing test C's round: the toy task, and one behaviour question the plan forces to the owner
Who and where: the second coordinating session, which set the round's shape in the brief, and the driver of test C, which wrote the owner's two chat messages and the draft plan before the round started.
Context: The handoff asked for "a tiny throwaway task with the owner's word 'approve' ... include one question checkpoint answered with `answer`". The concrete round was still open: which task, how a question could be forced to arise at all, which single detail to leave undecided, and how to tell a real answer from a coincidence. A question only arises if some behaviour-affecting detail is genuinely undecided and the step that would otherwise decide it is told to stop.
Rationale: Recorded in the report: "The design of the round is entirely in the second message and the `summary`: one behaviour-affecting detail is left open and PLAN-TO-SPEC is told, in the owner's voice, to end NEEDS-OWNER rather than choose. `initials` was picked because `whisper` was used by tests A and B; the separation question was picked because it is the function's headline behaviour, has exactly two sensible answers, and is one line either way." The plan's own summary states the instruction: "One detail is deliberately left open: whether the initials come back plain, as ABK, or separated by periods with a trailing one, as A.B.K. The owner has not decided that and will not have it decided for them, so PLAN-TO-SPEC must stop and ask: it must finish with status NEEDS-OWNER naming the two choices, not pick one. Every other detail is the round's to settle." Four assumptions pinned the details that could otherwise have become second questions.
Spec conflict: None found. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says "Ask the owner what is sensible to ask now; decide the rest and write it down", which is exactly the judgment being exercised and does not settle which detail qualifies; and it presupposes owner words that in this test did not exist independently of the driver. The owner's files describe how the harness must behave, not how it should be exercised.
Other context: It worked as designed: the spec step's first attempt ended NEEDS-OWNER, the runner raised the only question checkpoint of the round, and the answer became a blocking finding and reached the landed test. The choice fixed everything downstream - the function, the one test method, the eleven-line landed change - and because a new function was chosen rather than the one the earlier tests had used, the round could not accidentally repeat their behaviour. The round produced all three checkpoint kinds the handoff wanted, and its evidence fed review 4 directly; the same design was reused and extended for test F (JC-476). The harness applied no label; this was all before the round opened.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md lines 1 to 9, section 1, sections 2.3 and 2.4, section 4.4, section 11; commit a44867e.
Merged from: JC-C-2, JC-C-3, JC-COORD-34

## JC-280: Setting test C's quote at $120 (from JC-C-4)
Who and where: the driver of test C, in the draft plan written before the round started.
Context: Every budget in the round is a fraction of the quote, so the number had to be chosen before any work began, and only the driver could choose it. Too small and the steps would be told they had almost nothing to spend; too large and the ledger would say nothing useful about whether the round was affordable.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the driver sized the quote from the two earlier sandbox passes so that a round of this shape would finish inside it with a little room, and round numbers make the ledger easy to read. The round in fact spent $112.26, which is 93.6 percent of the quote.
Spec conflict: None found in the sense of a contradiction, but the owner's prose hands the judgment over without a rule: CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt says "Size the quote to the value of the change, calibrated by what information is readily available." The change was one line of code; the quote was $120, of which agent work took 11 percent.
Other context: The size of the quote is what made the round's loudest reported problem visible. The step budgets are the quote times a work fraction times the step's share, so they divide the whole quote among the steps, while the same quote is also charged for the owner's time ($62.10), the living cost of the new code ($28.25) and elapsed time. POSTMORTEM ranked that first among its five issues and said the last step "was told its budget was $16.80 when $10.27 of the quote remained" (JC-300). Review 4 agreed it is a question only the owner can answer and declined to change anything (JC-315, JC-316, JC-317). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 2.3; section 1; section 7 item 13; section 10; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md, "The sandbox postmortem's issue 1".

## JC-281: Never passing `record --agent`, on the strength of an inference from the action payloads (from JC-C-5)
Who and where: the driver of test C, from the first action of the loop onward, at all eight `record` commands.
Context: The driver's session could only run one model, so it had started the round with `start --agent medium`, which makes that rung every action's own rung. Two sentences of the harness's own documentation pull in different directions about whether `record --agent` should then also be passed on every record. The driver had to decide whether to pass it for safety or leave it off.
Rationale: "DRIVER.md line 12 tells a one-model session to `start --agent <rung>` but not the consequence - that `record --agent` then drops out of the loop entirely... A driver who reads line 12 and line 32 together may pass `--agent medium` on all eight records for safety, which per the help text then flags eight runs that were exactly on plan. I inferred the rule from the actions' `"agent": "medium"` and passed it nowhere."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; the conflict is between two derived documents. DRIVER.md line 32 says to add "`--agent RUNG` when the sub-agent ran on another rung's model than the action's... with a FLAG in HISTORY and `warnings` when it is not the action's", while the runner's own `record --help` text says `--agent` is "the roster rung that actually ran, when it is not the action's; the run is priced at it and HISTORY flags it", which read literally flags on every use.
Other context: The inference was right: every action reported the same rung, and no spurious flag appeared. Review 4 confirmed the code flags only on a mismatch and recorded the help text as the wrong one of the pair, and the missing sentence in DRIVER.md as a second nit, both citing test C. A side effect the driver noted itself is that nothing in the round exercised the flag, so the disagreement stayed untested. This is the third of three different answers three drivers gave to the same question (JC-193, JC-224). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 2.4; section 3; section 7 items 3 and 4; section 10; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md findings R4-n1 and R4-n2.

## JC-282: PLAN-AGENTS choosing a capability rung for each step (from JC-C-6)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The step's whole job is to assign each producer step one of four rungs on a capability-and-price ladder, from the cheapest model to the dearest. Nothing in the plan or the spec says which step deserves which rung; the agent had to weigh how much judgment each step carries against what a mistake there would cost.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "rungs - max for PLAN-TO-SPEC, the step that must ask rather than decide and whose SPEC.md words cost the owner $0.10 each; medium for PLAN-AGENTS, TESTS-TO-SUITE and POSTMORTEM, where the judgment is small but lasting; low for SPEC-TO-TESTS, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION and CLEANUP, work the spec dictates and the frozen test, M3 and M5 check."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose asks for the weighing without giving a rule. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision, and anything else you deem reasonable to consider. Optimize the budget, within reason."
Other context: Because the driver had forced one rung for the whole round, the four steps planned at the dearest and cheapest rungs never ran at them; every step in fact ran on the forced rung (JC-283). The rungs still had an effect, because the runner prices a retry at the planned rung (JC-37). It is the third answer to the same question, after tests A and B (JC-190, JC-218). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 2; section 11.3, the PLAN-AGENTS attempt 1 entry; section 11.1's closing note.

## JC-283: Recording differentiated rungs as intent although one rung was already forced for the round (from JC-C-7)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1; not written to either judgment-call file, so the auditor found it in the agent's artifact note and history entry.
Context: The agent discovered, by reading the round's state file, that the driver had forced every step onto one rung for this round. That gave it two sensible ways to write its plan: put that rung everywhere, so the artifact describes what will really happen; or write the rungs it would have chosen, so the artifact records the intent and prices a retry correctly. It chose the second and said so in the artifact without being asked to.
Rationale: From its history entry: "One fact recorded in the artifact: STATE.agent_override is medium, so every step will actually run at medium until that is lifted, and the rungs are the intent and the price of a retry."
Spec conflict: None found. The gap is in derived material: the PLAN-AGENTS prompt never tells the agent that a rung has been forced for the round, so the agent had to find that out for itself and then decide what to do about it.
Other context: Review 4 treated the fact that the agent had to make this decision at all as a defect in the prompt, finding that "PLAN-AGENTS is not told when `start --agent` forces one rung for the round...; test C's agent found `STATE.agent_override` itself and planned rungs that cannot run", and proposed a three-line change so a future agent "can skip that judgment" - which fix 4 wrote into the same commit as its neighbouring prompt change (JC-309). The test report calls the behaviour correct and notes the agent "said so unprompted". The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3, the PLAN-AGENTS attempt 1 entry; section 11.1; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md finding R4-n7.

## JC-284: PLAN-AGENTS dividing the work budget between the steps (from JC-C-8)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: Separately from the rungs, the agent had to split one budget into fractions that sum to one, deciding how much of the round's money each step may spend. This is an independent decision from the rung choice with its own consequence: the rung sets the price per unit of work, the share sets how much work is paid for.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "work shares - PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.36 for its two certain runs (ask, then pin), POSTMORTEM 0.20 and TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.14 for judgment with permanent cost, 0.08/0.08/0.06 for the dictated code steps, the stated 0.05 and 0.03 minimums kept."
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt asks the agent to "Optimize the budget, within reason" and gives no allocation rule.
Other context: The shares held up: every step finished inside its budget and the agent total was $12.33 of an $84 work budget. POSTMORTEM later named this very line as the judgment call that hid the deeper budget question: "Owner's own project.yaml keys and COMMON-OVERVIEW conflict, so it went to CLARIFICATIONS, naming PLAN-AGENTS a1 'work shares' as the call that hid it" (JC-301). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 3; section 11.3, the PLAN-AGENTS and POSTMORTEM entries; section 7 item 13.

## JC-285: PLAN-AGENTS leaving the gate budget and gate agents entirely unspecified (from JC-C-10)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: Thirty per cent of the quote is reserved for gates. Every gate was switched off for this round, so none would run. The agent had to decide whether to plan for them anyway, in case one were switched on later, or to leave the gate map empty and let the runner's own defaults apply.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "gate agents - gateAgents omitted and shares.gates left empty because no gate runs this round; a gate enabled later falls back to the runner's gateAgent (max) and an equal split of the gates budget."
Spec conflict: None found. The prompt permits an empty gate map ("shares.gates maps every producer step whose gate runs to a fraction of the gates budget ({} when no gate runs)") but says nothing about whether to fill it defensively.
Other context: This is the opposite of the answer test A's planner gave to the same question before the prompt was amended (JC-192); the amendment is what made the empty map obviously legal. No gate ran and no gate was enabled mid-round, so the fallback was never tested, and thirty per cent of the quote sat unassigned - part of the arithmetic behind the postmortem's first issue (JC-300). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 5; section 11.3, the PLAN-AGENTS attempt 1 entry; section 1.

## JC-286: Keeping a budget share for the planning step, against the runner's own validation rule (from JC-C-11)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The agent's prompt printed a table of minimum shares that included 0.05 for CHAT-TO-PLAN, the planning step the driver does in chat. The runner's validation rule, which the artifact must pass, does not require an entry for that step at all (JC-110). Because the shares must sum to exactly one, including or excluding that 0.05 changes every other step's budget. The agent had to choose which of the two to believe.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "the artifact contract requires no shares.work entry for CHAT-TO-PLAN (S1 excludes it) while the prompt states a 0.05 minimum for it, and neither AGENTS.md nor the locked prose settles which wins; kept at 0.05 because the ledger already booked $4.20 against that share."
Spec conflict: The 0.05 comes from the owner's own configuration file, whose `defaultShares` block reads "work: CHAT-TO-PLAN: 0.05". Nothing in the owner's files says whether the planning step belongs in the plan's own share map, and the derived validation code excludes it, so two artefacts derived from the owner's files disagree.
Other context: The agent chose the arithmetic that keeps the ledger honest and its artifact still passed validation, so nothing broke. Review 4 confirmed both shapes pass, showed that the money genuinely differs, and called the agent's choice the right one: "Test C's agent chose the honest shape and logged it as undefined; the stub and a literal reader of S1 choose the other." It proposed a one-line prompt fix, which fix 4 wrote (JC-309). The same call had been logged in an earlier round, so this is a repeat. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 5 item 1; section 6; section 7 item 12; section 11.2 line 1; section 1; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md finding R4-m3.

## JC-287: Reinterpreting an instruction about gate rejections in a round with no gates (from JC-C-12)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS step agent of test C, attempt 1.

Other context: The substitute the agent chose turned out to be accurate: the spec step did run twice and every other step ran once, with no failures. Review 4 ruled that no change is needed, because with no gated step the sentence is "vacuous rather than contradictory: no change, the agents' logged call is the right outcome" (JC-318). The gates-on version of the same problem is JC-221. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
