## JC-288: Choosing which of the two separations to build on while the owner was asked (from JC-C-13)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The agent was required to stop and ask the owner how the initials are separated, but it was also required to hand on a complete spec meanwhile. So it had to write the whole specification, including a test plan with exact expected values, on one of the two answers it was not allowed to choose between.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "interim separation - the prose says to build on a best assumption while the owner is asked, so the spec, test plan and implementation are written on plain (ABK); the owner named plain first in their own words and in the plan, and it is the plainer reading of 'the initials of the words in text'. One sentence of SPEC.md and one set of expected values flip if the answer is periods."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's prose requires an assumption but cannot say which. COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says to end "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide and spending their time is sensible given their cost: build on your best assumption meanwhile and state it".
Other context: The owner then chose the other option, so the assumption was discarded at attempt 2. That is exactly what the test was built to demonstrate, and the report says so: "The owner deliberately chose the option the producer had not assumed, so this is a real carry, not a coincidence" (JC-292). The agent also stated the cost of being wrong inside the checkpoint message the owner read, which is what let the owner weigh the question. Test F's spec agent faced a sharper version of the same bind, where the plan forbade it to choose at all (JC-430, JC-431). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.

## JC-289: Putting the test plan in the machine-read spec file instead of the owner-read one (from JC-C-14)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 1; not written to either judgment-call file, so the auditor found it in the agent's history entry.
Context: The step writes two files: a structured one the later steps read, which costs nothing, and a prose one the owner reads, which is charged to the owner at ten cents a word. The owner's prose asks for a test plan "in prose", which points at the expensive file. The agent had to decide where to put a detailed list of test cases that no owner would want to pay to read.
Rationale: From its history entry: "The test plan sits in SPEC.json rather than SPEC.md because SPEC.md costs the owner $0.10 a word and the plan is for the test-writing step, not the owner". At attempt 2 it repeated the reasoning: "the detailed test plan stays in SPEC.json, which costs nothing per word and which SPEC-TO-TESTS and SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION both receive as inputs."
Spec conflict: Two sentences of PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt pull apart. One says "Add a high-level test plan in prose covering the whole implementation", the other "Assume a cost of ${{ project.specCostPerWord }} per word in the spec, and the spec needs to read as plain English." Neither says which of the two spec files holds the plan, and the structured file has an optional test-plan text field, so both are allowed.
Other context: The saving was real: the prose file came to 191 words at attempt 1 and 153 at attempt 2, about $15.30 of the owner's money, while the full case list lived in the free file and reached the two steps that needed it. It is the same optimisation test B's and test D's spec agents made (JC-230, JC-355). Nothing later revisited it. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3, the PLAN-TO-SPEC attempt 1 and attempt 2 entries; section 10.

## JC-290: Pinning what happens when a word does not start with a letter (from JC-C-17)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The plan said the function returns the initials of the words, and defined the words as whatever Python's own splitting finds. It said nothing about a word like "(ada)" whose first character is a bracket. The agent had to pin one behaviour, because the test plan it was writing needed exact expected values.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "the plan is silent on a word whose first character is not a letter, e.g. '(ada)', and two implementers could differ (take it, or skip to the first letter); pinned as the first character exactly as it is, upper-cased, because the plan adds no validation and lets str.split() decide the words, so '(ada) byron' gives '(B'."
Spec conflict: The plan's assumptions are silent. They say only that the words are what Python's splitting finds and that the initials are upper-cased; neither covers a non-letter first character. Nothing in the owner's spec files reaches a toy project's string behaviour.
Other context: The rule survived the owner's answer and is in the landed test, which asserts a visible behaviour the owner never saw or approved. The agent also chose to make it an asserted test case rather than leave it unspecified, which is why it is visible at all. The postmortem did not raise it (JC-299). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.2 line 3; sections 4.1, 4.2 and 4.3; section 5; section 9.5; section 11.3.

## JC-291: Leaving bad input out of the test plan (from JC-C-18)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The plan said no validation would be added and that bad input raises whatever Python raises. The agent had to decide whether the single new test method should nevertheless assert which exception type comes back, which would turn an incidental detail into a pinned promise.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "the plan is silent on whether the one test method also asserts what bad input raises; left out, because the round adds no validation, so there is no new behaviour to hold, and asserting an exception type would pin a detail the plan hands to Python."
Spec conflict: The plan's fourth assumption says what the code does but not what the test should check. The owner's instruction to "Exhaust edge cases, within reason" (PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt) pulls the other way without settling it.
Other context: The decision held through to the landed test, which asserts five values and no exceptions. The agent stated the same reasoning again in its attempt 2 spec and the test-writing step repeated it verbatim when explaining why it added nothing. Test D's spec agent met the same silence and answered it the same way, twice (JC-336, JC-354). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.2 line 4; sections 4.1 and 4.2; section 5; section 11.3.

## JC-292: Answering, as the owner, with the option the producer had not assumed
Who and where: the driver of test C in its owner role, at the round's only question checkpoint, and the driver of test F in its owner role, at the question checkpoint of its round.
Context: In each round the runner had stopped and printed the producer's question, which offered two answers and named the producer's own assumption. The driver was both driver and owner and so wrote the answer. Answering with the same choice the step had assumed would have made the round cheap and the test almost vacuous - nothing in the landed result would show whether the answer travelled or the assumption simply survived. Answering with the other forces a rewrite and tests whether the owner's words reach the specification, the frozen test and the landed code.
Rationale: Both reports state the reasoning in the same words: "The owner deliberately chose the option the producer had **not** assumed, so this is a real carry, not a coincidence." Test C's owner answered "A period after every initial, including the last: ada byron king gives A.B.K., a single word gives A., and no words still gives the empty string"; test F's answered "Lower-case it: first_word of 'Hello World' gives 'hello', and a word that is already lower-case comes back unchanged."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's answer is by definition the thing no rule settles. What made it a choice for the driver rather than for a real owner is that the driver had designed the question in the first place (JC-279, JC-427) and was answering its own design.
Other context: Both answers went beyond the two-way question they were given: test C's pinned the single-word and no-words cases as well, removing two further decisions from the producer, and test F's covered the already-lower-case case, which is why the landed test asserts it. In both rounds the answer became a blocking finding in the next attempt's prompt, was resolved as fixed with the assumption withdrawn, and is visible in the landed test - the harness's promise that an owner's word reaches the code, demonstrated twice. The harness applied no label; owner words are not judgment calls in its scheme.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 1, section 4.1, section 4.4, section 9.5; bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 4, section 1, section 3, section 9.
Merged from: JC-C-19, JC-EF-34

## JC-293: Resuming with an answer rather than an approval, and carrying the whole sentence as both
Who and where: the driver of test C, resuming the round immediately after its question checkpoint, and the driver of test F, resuming after its own.
Context: The checkpoint message lists several resume commands with nothing to distinguish them. In this harness, approving at a question means proceeding on the step's stated assumption - which in both rounds was the opposite of what the owner wanted. Each driver had to read the owner's words, pick the command that matched, and decide what to put in the quote field the runner records as the owner's verbatim words: the round's standing approval word, or the new sentence.
Rationale: Both drivers wrote the same sentence: "this is an answer, not an approval, so `answer --text` is the matching command and the owner's exact words are both the answer and the quote." Test C's report adds the distinction it rests on: "The round's approval word is `approve` and that is what `PLAN.json.approval.words` and both `approve --quote` calls carry. At a `question` the owner is answering, not approving, so the quote is the sentence they actually said." Test F's adds the counterfactual: "`approve` here, which PROCESS.md line 85 defines as 'proceed on your stated assumption', would have shipped the choice the owner reserved."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, and the harness explicitly hands this choice to the driver: DRIVER.md line 20 says "Interpreting the owner's words is your judgment call", and PROCESS.md confirms what `approve` means at a question. The question checkpoint's list of resume commands prints no such mapping, which is what makes the choice live.
Other context: Getting this wrong would have silently landed the wrong behaviour in both rounds. Test F's report treats the near miss as a finding in its own right: the approving command "is offered beside `answer` with nothing marking the difference - here one approving word would have shipped the exact choice the owner had reserved" (JC-114). In both rounds the full sentence, carried verbatim, became the text of the owner's finding, and the producer quoted it back into the spec; in both the runner could not verify the quote, because the sandbox had no owner log, and recorded it with a flag (JC-121). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 4.1, section 8 item 5, section 3, section 1; bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 4, section 1, section 7 item 4, section 11.
Merged from: JC-C-20, JC-EF-35

## JC-294: Deciding what "one line" means in the shape of the code (from JC-C-21)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 2.
Context: The plan asked for the function to be added "as one line". That can mean one physical line of Python, or the two-line shape the neighbouring function already uses, a definition line and a return. The agent had to pin one so the implementation step could not choose differently.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "the plan's 'one line' is silent on shape and two implementers could differ (one physical line, or a def and a return as shout is written); pinned to shout's shape, a def line and a single return, because the plan says to write it beside shout and the toy module has no other style."
Spec conflict: The plan's own words are the ambiguity: its scope reads "Add initials(text) to src/toy/text.py as one line, beside the existing shout." Nothing in the owner's spec files touches code style.
Other context: The landed code follows the shape chosen. The implementation step recorded that after this the only freedom left was how the expression was spelled, which could not change behaviour, so it logged nothing at all. Test F's test-writing step logged the same class of question about the shape of a test method (JC-433), and its implementation step did not (JC-434); the test F report notes this is the third round in a row where "shape of the code the spec does not pin" produced an undefined line. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 5 item 3; section 6; section 11.2 line 5; section 11.3; section 9.5.

## JC-295: Extending the owner's period rule to a case the owner had not been shown (from JC-C-22)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC step agent of test C, attempt 2.
Context: The owner's answer gave three examples, all of ordinary words. The agent had already pinned that a word starting with a bracket contributes that bracket (JC-290). It had to decide whether the owner's "a period after every initial, including the last" applies to a bracket too, which changes the asserted value.

## JC-296: Raising the structural problem for the owner instead of leaving it unsaid (from JC-C-24)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: Having found that keep-or-archive is decided one file at a time while this project's whole suite is a single file, the agent had a choice about what to do with that observation. It could act on it, which its non-goals forbade; say nothing, since it had made the only available choice (JC-199); or use the artifact's channel for flagging something to the owner, which costs nothing immediately but is carried to later steps.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "flag - one raise_with_owner item, that keep/archive is per file while this suite is one file, so no archive was reachable and this keep records no exercised choice; flagged rather than left silent because a flag is carried to POSTMORTEM without costing an owner checkpoint."
Spec conflict: The owner's prose invites a flag only for a narrower thing. TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "If there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner, flag it", and the agent's point was not a middle ground but the absence of any choice. Its history entry says so: "the middle ground the prose imagines, a test worth running sometimes but not every round, has no mechanism either."
Other context: This was the first time the flag route (JC-263) was exercised end to end, and it worked: the runner recorded the flag in the history, carried it to the two later steps, the cleanup step left it alone, and the postmortem discharged it into the clarifications file for the owner. The driver reported that as a fix from the previous round confirmed working. Review 4 declined to build the finer-grained mechanism the flag implies, calling it "a design change, not a fix" (JC-314). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 8; section 11.3; section 7 items 13 and 14; section 11.4; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3.

## JC-297: Confining the cleanup step to the round's non-goals although it was allowed to change far more (from JC-C-25)
Who and where: the CLEANUP step agent of test C, attempt 1; not written to either judgment-call file - the agent explicitly said it had made no judgment call - so the auditor found it in the history entry.
Context: The runner grants the cleanup step write access to everything the project counts as living code and documentation (JC-103). It builds that list from configuration and never reads the round's plan. The plan's non-goals forbade touching the harness, its documentation, its prose or its configuration. So the only thing keeping the agent inside the round's scope was its own reading of the plan.
Rationale: From its history entry: "Nothing needed changing, so this attempt's diff under WRITE_PATHS is deliberately empty; the round is already landable and I spent the step verifying that... No judgment call was made and no line appended to either judgment-call file - every living path I could otherwise tidy is closed, ../tests/ by FROZEN_PATHS and docs/ and INDEX.md by the plan's non-goal 'No change to the harness, its docs, its prose or its config'". It also handed the observation on: "POSTMORTEM may also want to note that CLEANUP's WRITE_PATHS are wider than the round's non-goals allow."
Spec conflict: The owner's cleanup prose is one working sentence - "Clean up the round, so that it can be landed without issue" - and COMMON-PROJECT.txt separately says "Refactoring in a round is encouraged, capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget". Neither says the step must stay inside the plan's scope, and the mechanical write-path list did not enforce it.
Other context: POSTMORTEM ranked this second of five issues precisely because nothing but judgment held the line, and filed a proposed fix (JC-302). Review 4 declined to build the fix and used this very attempt as the evidence: "Test C shows the judgment holding" (JC-313). The agent's own claim that it made no judgment call is, on a reasonable person's definition, wrong: choosing to leave a wide permitted area untouched, and saying why, is a choice between reasonable alternatives. Test F's cleanup agent reached the same conclusion and did log it (JC-436). The harness applied no label, by the agent's own decision.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3, the CLEANUP attempt 1 and POSTMORTEM attempt 1 entries; section 7 item 13; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md verdict 4.

## JC-298: Deciding not to shrink the code to reduce its ongoing charge (from JC-C-26)
Who and where: the CLEANUP step agent of test C, attempt 1; not written to either judgment-call file, so the auditor found it in the history entry.
Context: The project charges a permanent cost for every token of living code, and the harness repeatedly tells agents to be dense. This round's new code and test were about to be charged $28.25. The agent had the opportunity to shorten identifiers or tighten the code before landing, which would reduce that charge, and had to weigh the saving against readability and against the spec's pinned shape.
Rationale: From its history entry: "The living preview is $28.25 ($5.50 text.py, $19.75 the test file, $3 testBaseCost) and I did not trim it: the test file is frozen and the only freedom left in text.py is identifier spelling, which would buy cents against the spec's pinned shape and the module's readability."
Spec conflict: The instruction it had to weigh is the owner's, in COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt: "Living tokens are charged, and refunded on removal: be dense, not clever, and remove what your work makes obsolete." The same sentence's "be dense, not clever" is its own counterweight, and nothing sets a threshold below which a saving is not worth taking.
Other context: The $28.25 was booked unchanged at landing and is a quarter of the round's total cost, the second largest item after the owner's own time. Nothing later revisited it. This is a separate decision from JC-297 with a separate consequence: that one is about which files to touch, this one about how tightly to write the files the round did produce. Test F's cleanup agent weighed the same question and recorded it (JC-436). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3, the CLEANUP attempt 1 entry; section 10; section 3.

## JC-299: Deciding what counted as an issue, and reporting nothing about the round's own code (from JC-C-27)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The step is told to identify what stood out as wrong, and separately to find the agent judgment call that settled something without the owner realising. The round contained two such calls about the toy code itself - that a word beginning with a bracket contributes the bracket, and that the owner's trailing-period rule extends to it (JC-290, JC-295) - behaviour the owner never saw. The agent had to decide whether those, or anything else about the round's own code, belonged in the report.
Rationale: The logged line ends: "nothing about initials itself is reported, the code matches the owner's answer."
Spec conflict: The owner's prose pulls both ways. POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says "Determine what, if anything, stood out as wrong in the previous round", which supports reporting nothing when the code is right, but its routing rule also says "If possible, find the agent judgment call that disambiguated the issue without the owner's realization", which is a description of exactly the bracket rule.
Other context: The consequence is that the owner has never been told what the landed function returns for a bracketed word. All five issues the report did raise are about the harness and its process, not the work the round was for. No later review revisited this; review 4 adjudicated the five issues that were raised and did not ask for a sixth. The harness's own label: part of the logged "ranking" line, which bundles this with the ordering decision of JC-300; they are separated here because they have separate reasons and separate consequences.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 9; section 11.2 lines 3 and 6; section 9.5; section 7 item 13.

## JC-300: Ranking test C's five issues by what each would cost if left alone (from JC-C-28)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The step is told to rank the issues it finds by importance, and importance is not defined. The five issues were of very different kinds: a budget rule that mismeasures every round, a permission that only judgment kept in bounds, a structural gap in how tests are kept, a pricing belief that was wrong, and a cosmetic counter in the log. The agent had to choose a yardstick and then apply it.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "ranking - five issues, ordered by what they cost if left: the step-budget rule that partitions the whole quote first (it misprices every round and already handed this step $16.80 against $10.27 remaining), CLEANUP's path width second (only judgment held it)".
Spec conflict: None found; POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says only "First, just identify issues, and rank them by importance. Log them." It does not say what importance means.
Other context: Putting the budget rule first is a case of the agent ranking an issue that had affected itself. The driver endorsed the ranking and carried the top issue to the next review as its own headline item. Review 4 devoted a section to it, agreed it is an owner question rather than a bug, and declined to build anything (JC-315, JC-316, JC-317). It is the same yardstick test B's and test D's postmortems chose (JC-241, JC-345, JC-368). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 9; section 11.3; section 1 and section 7 item 13; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md, "The sandbox postmortem's issue 1".

## JC-301: Routing test C's five issues between the fix list and the owner's questions list (from JC-C-29)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: The step's prose gives two destinations and a test for choosing between them: an issue that everyone's intentions agree on and that was simply missed becomes a proposed fix for the next round, while an issue where something is genuinely in conflict or ambiguous and the owner does not know goes to the owner for a decision. The agent had to apply that test to five issues at once, deciding in each case whether the conflict sat in the owner's own files or only in the code derived from them.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "routing - CLEANUP's path width, POSTMORTEM's carry-forward pricing and HISTORY's checkpoint counter go to TODO, because plan, owner words and vision agree and only derived code missed it; the quote's two readings and keep/archive granularity go to CLARIFICATIONS, because the conflict sits inside the owner's own spec files (project.yaml, the locked prose) and only the owner can settle it."
Spec conflict: None found; the rule the agent applied is the owner's own, in POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt, which lists the two options as "(1) ... propose a sensible fix and add it to TODOs for next round. or 2) Something is in conflict or ambiguous, and the owner is unaware: add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review".
Other context: This is recorded as one entry rather than five, because the agent recorded one reason per destination rather than a separate reason for each issue. Three fixes and two clarifications were appended to the carry-forward files under a round heading, where the next round's planner reads them. Review 4 agreed with the two clarifications and with one of the three fixes, and told the project not to build the largest of them (JC-313). The driver independently endorsed the hardest routing call, writing that "POSTMORTEM's issue 1 is an owner question, not a bug". Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.1 line 10; section 11.3; section 11.4; section 1; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md verdicts 4 and 5.

## JC-302: Proposing a new mechanism to narrow the cleanup step's permissions (from JC-C-30)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM step agent of test C, attempt 1; not written to either judgment-call file, so the auditor found it in the history entry.
Context: Having decided that the cleanup step's write permissions were too wide (the issue the cleanup step itself had passed to it, JC-297), the agent had to propose a fix. It could have proposed something small, such as a warning or a sentence in the prose, or it could design a new field. It chose a new field: a list that the specification step would fill from the plan's scope, which the runner would then use to narrow the permissions.
Rationale: From its history entry: "CLEANUP's WRITE_PATHS included docs/, INDEX.md, all of ../src/ and all of ../tests/ in a round whose non-goal forbade any harness change; prompts.py builds them from livingSourcePaths and never reads the plan's scope or non-goals, so only judgment held the line - TODO with a cleanupPaths fix in derived code."
Spec conflict: None found; the instruction is simply "propose a sensible fix", in POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt, with no guidance on how large a fix may be.
Other context: Review 4 rejected the proposal in terms, and its reasoning shows that a different-sized fix was available: "do not build. CLEANUP's paths are wide by design..., the runner never parses a plan's scope or non-goals..., and a new SPEC field plus a check plus a prose obligation adds a rule for agents to learn in order to enforce a non-goal that judgment, the living charge and the gate (when on) already hold" (JC-313). Recorded separately from the routing entry because it is a design choice with its own recorded reasoning and its own downstream consequence, and because the review had to adjudicate it. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 11.3, the POSTMORTEM attempt 1 entry; section 11.4; section 7 item 13; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md verdict 4.

## JC-303: Adding a recurring problem as a note under this round rather than as a new item (from JC-C-31)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM step agent of test C, attempt 1.
Context: A warning about the owner's words being recorded unverified had fired four times in this round and was already an open item in the owner's questions file from an earlier round (JC-121). The step's prose tells it where new issues go but says nothing about an issue that is already open. The agent had three options: open a duplicate item, say nothing, or attach a note to the existing one.
Rationale: The logged line reads: "the prose routes each issue to TODO or to CLARIFICATIONS and is silent on an issue already open in CLARIFICATIONS from an earlier round; the recurring unverified-owner-words FLAG is added as a one-line addendum under round 0001 rather than as a new item or left out, so the next planner sees that it fired and landed without the owner being asked the same question twice."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt describes only two destinations for a fresh issue, and the mechanical instruction in the step's prompt says each goes "under a '## round {{ round.id }}' heading" - a per-round structure with no place for a note about an older item. Neither says what to do when the issue is already open.
Other context: The addendum went into the clarifications file under the round heading, so the next round's planner reads that the warning fired four times and the round landed anyway. Test D's and test F's postmortems made the same choice for longer lists of open questions (JC-348, JC-371, JC-438). The unverified recording itself is expected in a sandbox, since no owner-log hook is registered there. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 5 item 5; section 6; section 11.2 line 7; section 11.4; section 8 item 3.

## JC-304: Judging the round a pass and the one real defect cosmetic (from JC-C-32)
Who and where: the driver of test C, writing the report after the round finished.
Context: The round had turned up one genuine runner defect: the history file's per-attempt count of judgment calls "since the last checkpoint" reads zero on exactly the attempt that raised a checkpoint. The driver had to decide how much weight to give it - whether the round counted as a clean pass, whether the defect deserved to be raised as something that must be fixed before further testing, or whether it was a cosmetic blemish. It also had to decide how to present eight other places where the documentation was unclear or wrong.
Rationale: "What broke: nothing stopped the round. One real, cosmetic runner defect, which POSTMORTEM found independently and filed as a TODO... This is the only thing this round found broken." The driver's own summary opens "Verdict: the round reached `done`", and it separated the eight documentation problems into one-line items rather than defects.
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files or in the harness's documentation tells a driver how to grade a test round; the whole verdict was the driver's to form.
Other context: The grading held: review 4 confirmed the defect, agreed it was cosmetic - "Cosmetic; the message the driver relays is correct" - and sized the fix at three lines. The same review turned each of the eight documentation items into a finding or a nit and ruled on all of them, so the driver's decision about what to raise set the next agent's agenda. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 1; section 7 item 9; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md finding R4-m1 and the verdict table.

REVIEW 4 AND FIX 4

## JC-305: What happens to an agent's unrecorded work when the owner overrides a step
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-M1, and the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-M3; built in `round.override` by the fix-4 fixer, commit 30e4fe9, and the fix-5 fixer, commit a62ca94.
Context: Owner commands commit whatever they find in the working tree. When the owner skips a step that an agent has already worked on but whose work has not yet been recorded, the command's own commit picked up everything in the tree, so the agent's files were committed without passing any of the harness's mechanical checks; a stray file and an unchecked source edit reached the shared branch in review 4's reproduction, and a stray reached it again in review 5's when the owner skipped a different step. The obvious cheap repair is to make the shared commit routine selective; each reviewer had to decide between that and a change local to the skip command, and what the right outcome is in each of the two cases.
Rationale: Review 4 ruled out the general repair: "Do not make `commit()` selective: the sweep is right for every other owner command (an edited PLAN.json at `upstream-plan` is meant to be committed by `approve`)", and asked instead for "about six lines in `round.py:override`", discarding the pending work before the commit. The fix-4 commit records it: "Overriding the step whose attempt is pending reverted nothing before the owner command's commit swept the tree, so an agent's files landed without M1, M3 or M5. override now reverts them (HISTORY lists the paths) before clearing attempt_pending." Review 5 met the other half and chose refusal rather than discarding, with the message "record it first, or override {pending step} to discard it", stating what it rejected: "Do not make `commit()` selective (review 4's reasoning stands) and do not defer the commit: an unsaved STATE with dirty HISTORY is exactly R5-M2." The fix-5 commit records the result: "naming any other step reached the owner command's `git add -A` commit, which swept the agent's unrecorded files in unchecked ... Now the pending attempt's unrecorded work ... refuses the override with the paths listed."
Spec conflict: None found. harness/docs/PROCESS.md says an overridden step is "skipped" and that checks run at `record` on the uncommitted diff of the attempt, but neither the owner's files nor the plan say what happens to work that exists when the owner skips the step; the owner's files do not describe uncommitted state at all. What left it open is AGENTS.md's rule that "Only the runner ... commits", which makes every owner command a commit point.
Other context: Two rounds of review were needed to reach the pair of rules, and the asymmetry is deliberate: overriding the step whose work it is means discarding that work on purpose, while overriding anything else means the work still matters and must be recorded properly first - because the owner skipping one step should not destroy another step's finished work. Review 5's refusal depends on its own other major being fixed first, which it said: "with R5-M2 in place the refusal is clean" (JC-375). Fix 5 made two small departures of its own in building it (JC-392, JC-393). Test D's driver avoided the whole area by only ever overriding between recording a step and asking for the next one (JC-340).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-M1 and section 7; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M3, section 3, section 7 note 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `round.override`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 89; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 41; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_override_of_the_pending_step_discards_its_unrecorded_work, ::test_override_of_another_step_is_refused_while_an_attempt_has_unrecorded_work; commits 30e4fe9 and a62ca94.
Merged from: JC-R345-24, JC-R345-54, JC-CODE-56

## JC-306: Overriding the questioner itself makes its question moot, dropped with only a log line
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, the third call site of the helper proposed in finding R4-M2; built by the fix-4 fixer in `round.skip_producer`, commit c1e7b8c.
Context: An open question from a producer can be ended by the owner skipping the step that asked it, as well as by a gate that never rules (JC-166). Here the reviewer had to decide whether the assumption still deserves a recorded judgment call.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "`skip_producer` (clear with one HISTORY line: an overridden producer's question is moot, its artifact is not used, see R4-n9)." The fix commit records the outcome - "an overridden producer drops its question with a HISTORY line" - without arguing the distinction further. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): an assumption is only worth recording when work was built on it; an overridden producer's artifact is discarded, so there is nothing standing on the assumption and an undefined judgment-call line would be noise.
Spec conflict: None found. No owner sentence covers a question whose asker is removed from the round; what left the choice open is that "override" in CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt is described purely as skipping, and the owner's instruction to record judgment calls does not say whether a call that no longer affects anything should still be recorded.
Other context: The consequence is a deliberate asymmetry: a skipped gate leaves a recorded call, a skipped producer does not, and harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 90 states both in one sentence. Fix 4 implemented both through one helper and noted the separate wording: "`<STEP> overridden: its open question is dropped`". Review 5 found the asymmetry consistent and reported the field confirmation from test D.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-M2; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; `round.skip_producer`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 90; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 37; commit c1e7b8c; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m4 and section 4.
Merged from: JC-R345-26, JC-CODE-46

## JC-307: Ruling that the test fixture is wrong and the owner's gate prose is right (from JC-R345-27)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-M3.
Context: The harness ships a fake, "clean" example of each step's output so that a gate can be tested against something that ought to pass. The clean example for the step that writes tests contained a test file whose only assertion was that true is true. The owner's prose for that gate says to fail a test that is not sensible. So the clean example would rightly fail, and the planned experiment on gate effectiveness would have measured the fixture rather than the gate.
Rationale: Quoted: "A gate that FAILs this seed is right, and test E would either report 'the gate fails clean seeds' or the driver would have to argue the fixture away by hand." The fix changes the fixture to "one real but redundant assertion".
Spec conflict: SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt says "For each test: * Is it not sensible? Fail." The owner's prose is not in doubt; the conflict is between it and a fixture written before the prose was applied to a real gate.
Other context: The reviewer weighed and named the alternative it rejected, which was to document in the testing guide that this seed is expected to fail. Fix 4 rewrote the fixture and review 5 confirmed the new seed in its own reproduction. The same reasoning was applied to two further fixtures, once by review 4 (JC-310) and once by review 5 (JC-382), and the fixture programme's limit was drawn at a fourth (JC-383). Test E then ran the repaired fixtures and every clean seed passed (JC-406, JC-407).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-M3, section 6 and section 7 note 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit 1a6b840; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.

## JC-308: Constraining the fixture repair so the harness's own cost accounting does not move (from JC-R345-28)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, inside finding R4-M3.
Context: The harness charges a fixed amount for each new test added to the permanent suite, and several existing tests depend on this fixture. Rewriting the fixture could easily have added or removed a test function.
Rationale: Quoted: "Keep one `def test_` so the living charge's test count is unchanged."
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml sets `testBaseCost: 3 # per new suite test` and COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "a new suite test {{ project.testBaseCost }}. Add either only when it earns that"; the owner's rule prices tests but says nothing about fixtures that only pretend to be tests, so the reviewer chose to keep the fixture neutral rather than reason about whether the rule applies to it.
Other context: Fix 4 honoured the constraint ("still one `def test_`") and fix 5 honoured it again when it thickened a different fixture (JC-398). It is the clearest case in the review chain of a fixture change being kept inside the owner's cost model on purpose.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-M3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md "Where I departed"; harness/project.yaml.

## JC-309: Settling a budget ambiguity with a sentence of prose rather than a mechanical check
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-m3; built by the fix-4 fixer in `plumbing/PLAN-AGENTS.txt`, commit 028a3a7.
Context: The prompt for the planning step implied that every producing step belongs in the budget table, while the harness's own validity check excluded the first step, and both shapes passed. The difference is real money: the first step's share is charged when the round starts, so a plan that omits it hands the whole work budget to the other seven steps. Both real rounds recorded the ambiguity (JC-286). The reviewer could add a check, change the prompt, or both.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix (one line of plumbing): in `PLAN-AGENTS.txt` say that CHAT-TO-PLAN's share is already spent ... If a mechanical check is wanted instead, `s1_agents_plan` can require the `shares.work` entry ... the prose fix alone is enough for the agents that logged it." The fix commit gives the arithmetic the sentence carries: "start books that step's minimum share before any plan exists: a plan that omits it over-commits the seven later budgets by that amount, one that lists it keeps the sum honest."
Spec conflict: The owner's harness/project.yaml gives the first step a work minimum but the owner's files never say whether that step's share belongs in a round's own plan; PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says only "choose the agent, sub-agents and budget share for each step and gate of this round", and that step is one that is already finished and already paid for when the plan is written.
Other context: The two alternatives have different failure modes: prose persuades an agent, a check refuses a plan. The reviewer chose the one that costs nothing when an agent gets it right. The template now says it in the agent's own terms: "CHAT-TO-PLAN at its minimum above (that share is already spent, booked at start, and listing it keeps the other budgets honest)". The same commit added the forced-rung line so a planner does not choose rungs the session cannot run (JC-283). Review 5 recorded that both real planning agents then listed the step at its floor.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m3, section 3 item 3, section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; `plumbing/PLAN-AGENTS.txt`; `checks.s1_agents_plan`; `round.start`; commit 028a3a7; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.
Merged from: JC-R345-29, JC-CODE-97

## JC-310: Changing the simulated agent's example plan rather than documenting that the example is unrealistic (from JC-R345-30)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-m6.
Context: The clean example plan used to test the gate that judges plans gave every step the most expensive model and split the budget equally, for a one-line task. The owner's prose tells that gate to fail anything not sensible. The reviewer offered two fixes and recommended one.
Rationale: Quoted: "Alternatively say in TESTING.md that this seed is an equal split and that a FAIL there judges the fixture; the stub change is the one that keeps the probe honest for the TODO.md item ('measure whether clean artifacts pass the gates as worded')."
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-GATE.txt ("Is anything about it not sensible? Fail.") against PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt, which asks the planner to choose "given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task"; the example plan obeyed neither, and no owner file describes what a test fixture must look like.
Other context: The deciding consideration is the harness's own carried-forward task list, which says "Measure whether clean artifacts pass the gates as worded (Level 1 probes) before proposing any prose relaxation" - an experiment that is worthless if the clean artifact is indefensible (JC-84). Fix 4 changed the simulated agent and also wrote the documentation half, and made the stub obey the new prompt instruction at the same time (JC-326). When test E ran the probe, the gate still raised five objections to the repaired plan and ruled every one non-blocking (JC-401).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m6 and section 7; harness/docs/TODO.md; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commits ef824c8, 3a3ce76; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.

## JC-311: Making a half-given delegation instruction a refusal rather than honouring it
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-m4; built by the fix-4 fixer in `round.start`, commit 972dce4.
Context: The start command accepts a "delegate" flag and a "through STEP" flag. Given the second without the first, it validated the step name and then silently ignored it, so a driver got an ordinary approved round and no word about the flag. The reviewer could make the second flag imply the first, or refuse the combination.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix (two lines in `round.py:start`): refuse `--through` when neither `--delegate` nor a delegated plan is present". The reasoning given is the driver's experience: someone following the documentation "who drops the first flag gets an approved round and no word about the second". The fix commit states the general principle it serves: "start validated --through and applied it only under --delegate, so a driver who dropped the first flag got an approved round and no word about the second. It is now refused unless --delegate is given or the plan is delegated, and applied whenever accepted (the command line replaces the plan's through)."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, which do not describe the command line at all. CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt lists "delegate through STEP" as a single owner phrase, which arguably makes the second flag imply the first; the runner's two separate flags are a derived design, and two independent flags encoding one owner sentence is what left it open.
Other context: Refusing is the safer reading because a silent promotion to delegated would remove owner pauses that were never asked for; it is also the general rule the runner follows everywhere, that a half-understood instruction is a refusal rather than a silent reinterpretation. The fixer went one step further and applied the flag where it is meaningful without its partner (JC-325). Review 5 confirmed the refusal happens "before any git command".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; `round.start`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 47; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 22; commit 972dce4; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.
Merged from: JC-R345-31, JC-CODE-51

## JC-312: Keeping a refusal judged correct and changing only its wording (from JC-R345-32)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-m5.
Context: An owner who tries to skip a step that has already run, but whose gate has not yet judged it, is refused with the message "already accepted", which is untrue: nothing accepted it (JC-179). The reviewer had to decide whether the refusal itself was wrong.
Rationale: Quoted: "The refusal is right (the artifact exists); the wording sends test D's driver looking for an acceptance that did not happen." The fix splits the condition and names the gate to skip instead.
Spec conflict: harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 89 already described the design ("a producer that already ran and waits for its gate is refused: override the gate, which accepts the artifact as it is"), so the conflict was between the code's message and the harness's own documentation rather than with an owner file.
Other context: The alternative, allowing the skip, would have thrown away a finished artifact the owner had already paid for. Fix 4 implemented the split message; review 5 then found that this new message invites a sequence - refusal, then retry - which exposed a different defect, that a refused command left the round's files dirty (JC-375).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-m5; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit c1e7b8c; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M2 and section 4.

## JC-313: Refusing to teach the runner about a plan's scope and non-goals (from JC-R345-33)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, verdict 4 on test C's items.
Context: The automatic postmortem of a sandbox round proposed a new field naming the paths the cleanup step may touch, derived from the plan's scope, plus a check enforcing it (JC-302). The reviewer had to decide whether to endorse a new rule.
Rationale: Quoted at length: "do not build. CLEANUP's paths are wide by design (COMMON-PROJECT invites refactoring within the cap; the owner's CLEANUP-OVERVIEW is one sentence), the runner never parses a plan's scope or non-goals (SPEC.md's architecture list, PROCESS.md:8), and a new SPEC field plus a check plus a prose obligation adds a rule for agents to learn in order to enforce a non-goal that judgment, the living charge and the gate (when on) already hold. Test C shows the judgment holding."
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md states the architectural principle relied on - "The driver interprets owner words and hands the runner verbatim quotes; the runner never parses prose" - while CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt gives the cleanup step a single sentence of instruction, which is exactly the openness the proposal wanted to close.
Other context: This is the clearest case in the review chain of a reviewer declining to move a decision out of an agent's judgment and into the runner. Fix 4 recorded "no change". No later review reopened it, and test F's cleanup agent held the same line unaided (JC-436).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md items table; SPEC.md; harness/locked_prose/CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt.

## JC-314: Refusing to make the archive unit finer than a file (from JC-R345-34)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, "Suggestions from the reports not to build, collected".
Context: The step that decides which tests join the permanent suite must currently move whole files, so a file holding one wanted and one unwanted test has no good answer. A postmortem proposed a per-test unit (JC-296). The reviewer had to decide whether this was a defect or a design change.
Rationale: Quoted: "the file is the unit the runner moves and `suiteCommand` discovers; the owner's prose says 'each test' and the CLARIFICATION is filed; a finer unit is a design change, not a fix".
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "Choose where each test goes: In the permanent suite, or in the round archive?", which speaks of tests, while the runner moves files; the owner's words and the mechanism disagree and no file reconciles them.
Other context: The reviewer's route for the disagreement was the harness's own clarifications file, that is, the owner. Review 5 restated the same position in its brittleness table ("a one-file suite has no choice; CLARIFICATION filed in the sandbox; nothing mechanical ... the owner's to settle"). Fix 4 recorded "no change".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md.

## JC-315: Ruling that whether the owner's quote covers only agent work or everything is the owner's question (from JC-R345-35)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, on the sandbox postmortem's first issue.
Context: The harness divides a round's quoted price into work and gate shares that together account for all of it, and then also charges the same quote for the owner's time, the cost of new text and elapsed time. Either the shares over-allocate or the spend line compares against the wrong figure (JC-300). The reviewer had to decide whether this was a defect to fix or a question to ask.
Rationale: Quoted: "only the owner can say which, and the CLARIFICATION POSTMORTEM filed is the channel. Nothing to build now."
Spec conflict: The owner's harness/project.yaml contains both halves: `gatesFraction: 0.3 # sums to 1 with workFraction`, and separately the prices for owner time, plan words, spec words and living tokens, with `budget: 50000 # project target, not enforced`. Nothing says whether a round's quote is the envelope for agent work alone.
Other context: The reviewer softened the consequence by pointing out that the agent sees both numbers anyway ("COMMON-ROUND prints quote, spent and remaining beside the step budget") and that "Budgets are targets, not gates". Review 5 restated this verdict when a second postmortem raised the same point, and it is one of the four questions the final report puts to the owner (JC-478).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md sections 3 and 6; harness/project.yaml; harness/locked_prose/COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt.

## JC-316: Refusing a step budget that shrinks as the round spends (from JC-R345-36)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, second half of the budget verdict.
Context: One way to reconcile the budget arithmetic is to size each step against what is left rather than against the original quote. The reviewer had to decide whether to propose it.
Rationale: Quoted: "a 'remaining-aware' step budget (it would shrink late steps by the owner's own checkpoint costs and encode a policy nobody stated)".
Spec conflict: harness/docs/PROCESS.md implements the owner's fractions against the quote, and harness/project.yaml contains no key expressing a preference either way; the choice was left open by the absence of any owner sentence about how spending should feed back into later budgets.
Other context: The specific harm named is that the owner's own pauses would silently shrink the budget of the steps that come after them, so the harness would punish steps for the owner's decisions. No later review revisited it.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 115.

## JC-317: Refusing to renormalise the owner's two budget fractions (from JC-R345-37)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, second half of the budget verdict.
Context: The other way to reconcile the arithmetic is to rescale the work and gate fractions so they leave room for the owner, time and text charges. The reviewer had to decide whether to propose it.
Rationale: Quoted: "a renormalisation of the fractions (the owner's comment says they sum to 1 on purpose)".
Spec conflict: harness/project.yaml says exactly that in a comment: `gatesFraction: 0.3 # sums to 1 with workFraction`. The comment is the only statement of intent, and it is in a file the harness treats as the owner's.
Other context: This is a case where a comment in an owner file, not a rule, decided the outcome - which is also why fix 3 and fix 4 worked to keep those comments alive in copied repositories (JC-269, JC-329). No later review revisited it.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; harness/project.yaml.

## JC-318: Ruling that an owner sentence with nothing to apply to is vacuous, not contradictory (from JC-R345-38)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 3, the doc-items table, row "expect one or two rejections per gated step".
Context: In a round with every gate switched off, the owner's instruction to size budgets for one or two gate rejections applies to nothing. Real agents recorded it as an unresolved point rather than acting on it (JC-287). The reviewer had to decide whether the harness owed them an answer.
Rationale: Quoted: "the first is the owner's sentence and, with no gated step, vacuous rather than contradictory: no change, the agents' logged call is the right outcome".
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt's "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them" against harness/project.yaml, where every entry under `gates:` is 0.
Other context: This endorses an agent's decision to record uncertainty instead of inventing an answer, which is what the owner's AGENTS.md asks for. It also declines to add a conditional to the owner's prose, which would have required editing a file the plan forbids touching lightly. Review 3 had reached the compatible verdict that the sentence is "the owner's to settle" for a gates-on round (JC-267).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 3; harness/locked_prose/PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt; harness/project.yaml.

## JC-319: Ruling that undocumented changes to the runner's machine output are not a finding (from JC-R345-39)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 4, on fix 3's report.
Context: Fix 3 had changed several fields of the JSON the runner prints - renaming two, adding three, adding warnings to two payloads - and listed them in its own report rather than in the harness's documentation. The reviewer had to decide whether the omission was a defect.
Rationale: Quoted: "Not a finding: the payloads are read by drivers who see them, and the test reports document them for the next driver."
Spec conflict: None found. harness/docs/PROCESS.md says only "Every command prints one JSON object on stdout"; neither the owner's files nor the plan require every field to be named in a document.
Other context: The risk accepted is that a future driver reads the documentation and not the bootstrap reports. Review 5 repeated the same check on fix 4's report and reached the same conclusion for all but two entries, which it turned into findings; so the tolerance was applied consistently and re-tested.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md "What the docs do not say"; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.

## JC-320: Accepting half of a test report's suggestion and rejecting the other half (from JC-R345-40)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-n4.
Context: Test C reported two problems with one printed command line: a path built with mixed separators, and the absence of a hint about the one-model option. The reviewer had to rule on both.
Rationale: Quoted: "The report's other half, an `--agent` hint on that line: do not build it; DRIVER.md:12 is the place, and the printed command is the common case."
Spec conflict: None found. The printed command belongs to the harness's generated prompt, not to an owner file; what left the choice open is that nothing says how much advice a printed command may carry.
Other context: The reasoning is that guidance for an unusual session belongs in the driver's guide, not in a line every round prints. Fix 4 fixed the path and left the hint out, and also chose to build the path once in a shared place rather than twice (JC-328).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-n4 and section 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit dddbaf2.

## JC-321: Resolving a cost contradiction in the harness's generated prompt rather than in the owner's sentence (from JC-R345-41)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, finding R4-n10.
Context: The owner's standing text tells every step that the text it writes is charged. The plan exempts two carry-forward files from that charge because they are written after the round lands (JC-85). A real planning agent sized a budget on the charge that does not apply. The reviewer had to decide where to state the exemption.
Rationale: The fix is "One sentence in `plumbing/POSTMORTEM.txt`: 'these edits land after LANDING and are not charged as living tokens; be dense anyway'", that is, in the machine-written part of the prompt, leaving the owner's sentence untouched.
Spec conflict: COMMON-PROJECT.txt's "Living tokens are charged when created and refunded on removal, in code and prose alike" against FINAL-PLAN amendment 2 and harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 122 ("edits to the carry-forward files after LANDING are merged by `sync_main` and never charged").
Other context: The pattern across the review chain is consistent - where the owner's prose and the plan disagree, the reviewers add a qualifying sentence to the runner's own generated text rather than edit the owner's file (JC-309, JC-385). Fix 4 wrote the sentence.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-n10; bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit b3a6f96; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendment 2.

## JC-322: Ruling that counting comment lines is not asserting on the owner's prose (from JC-R345-42)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 4, section 6, closing paragraph.
Context: The plan forbids any test that asserts on a sentence of the owner's prose, so that the owner can rewrite those files freely. Fix 3 had added a test that compares the number of comment lines in a copied settings file with the original (JC-270). The reviewer had to decide whether that crosses the line.
Rationale: Quoted: "no test added by fix 3 asserts on a sentence of the locked prose (the real-repository sandbox test counts `#` lines only)".
Spec conflict: The rule is the plan's: FINAL-PLAN amendment 6, "No test asserts on any sentence of the owner's prose." The owner's SPEC.md says the repository "must survive arbitrary edits" to the spec files, which does not settle whether counting is depending.
Other context: The boundary matters because a test that breaks when the owner deletes a comment would make the owner's file effectively read-only. The judgment is defensible but not forced: deleting one comment does break this test. Review 5 repeated the same check for fix 4 and reached the same conclusion.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 6; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendment 6; harness/tests/test_probe.py.

## JC-323: Giving every runner-written "assumed" line one shape, changing the wording of an existing one (from JC-R345-43)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit c1e7b8c, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 4 asked for a small helper used at two new places (JC-166, JC-306). A third place already wrote a similar line with different wording. The fixer had to decide whether to unify them, which changes text that was already correct.
Rationale: Recorded: "`settle_question(who, why)` writes the one undefined line and clears the question; it serves the delegated branch (text unchanged), `skip_gate` and the no-ruling branch as well, so every runner-written 'assumed' line has one shape (the no-ruling line now reads `(runner: GATE attempt N gave no ruling; question: Q)`)."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's AGENTS.md asks for "a small record with context" per judgment call and does not prescribe its shape; the runner writes these lines on the agents' behalf, a case the owner's files do not describe at all.
Other context: The change is visible to anyone reading a round's judgment-call file and to any test matching those lines. Review 5 checked it and found "`settle_question` has one shape for three callers ... sound". Fix 5 then deleted one of the three callers when review 5's major finding removed the delegated branch (JC-373), and recorded that the remaining two keep the shape.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit c1e7b8c; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; harness/src/shackles/round.py.

## JC-324: Adding a driver-facing sentence the review asked to put only in the rules document (from JC-R345-44)

Context: Review 4's fix for the unchecked sweep (JC-305) asked for a sentence in the normative rules file. The fixer had to decide whether the person who would actually meet the behaviour reads that file.
Rationale: Recorded: "DRIVER.md got a sentence too (the reviewer named PROCESS.md only), because test D's driver reads DRIVER.md literally and will override a running step."
Spec conflict: None found. The split between the two documents is the harness's own; nothing says which audience a new rule must reach.

## JC-325: Making the half-given delegation flag work where it can, not merely refusing it (from JC-R345-45)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit 972dce4, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 4 asked for a refusal (JC-311). The fixer noticed a case where the flag is meaningful without its partner: when the plan itself is already delegated.
Rationale: Recorded: "the reviewer's message, plus `--through` is applied when a delegated plan makes it acceptable (it replaces the plan's `through`); refusing-but-ignoring was the defect."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt describes the owner's words, not the command line. What left the choice open is that the command line and the plan can each carry a delegation, and nothing said which wins.
Other context: The fixer's diagnosis - that the defect was accepting an instruction and then ignoring it - is broader than the reviewer's, and produced a rule the documentation now states: the command line replaces the plan's value. Review 5 confirmed both halves and separately found that the resulting record mislabelled where the delegation came from.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit 972dce4; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 22; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m6 and section 4.

## JC-326: Making the simulated agent obey the instruction the same prompt now carries (from JC-R345-46)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit ef824c8, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Two of review 4's findings met here. One told the planning prompt to say that the first step's budget share must be listed (JC-309); the other rewrote the simulated agent's example plan (JC-310). The fixer had to decide whether the simulated agent should follow the new instruction.
Rationale: Recorded: "the stub also lists CHAT-TO-PLAN at the minimum its prompt shows, because R4-m3's instruction now asks every planner to, and a seed that ignores its own prompt is not a clean seed."
Spec conflict: None found. The instruction lives in the harness's generated prompt, not an owner file; what left the choice open is that the simulated agent reads prompts only loosely, so nothing forced it to comply.
Other context: The implementation reads the number out of the rendered prompt rather than hard-coding it, so the example follows the owner's configured minimum if the owner changes it. Review 5 verified the resulting example plan in its own reproduction.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit ef824c8; harness/tests/stub_agent.py; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 4.

## JC-327: Leaving the gate budgets split evenly so the numbers published for the next test stay true (from JC-R345-47)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit ef824c8, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: While making the example plan realistic by weighting the work budget, the fixer could have weighted the gate budgets too. Review 4 had published the exact gate budgets the next test's driver should expect.
Rationale: Recorded: "the work shares are weighted, the gates split stays equal (the reviewer's probe budgets, `$5.0` and `$0.9`, stay true)".
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files says whether gate budgets should be weighted; the constraint here came from a document written for the next human-driven test, not from a rule.
Other context: This is a choice to hold a published number stable rather than to make one more thing realistic. Review 5 confirmed the numbers still hold in twelve live probes and repeated them to test E's driver; fix 5 repeated them again.

## JC-328: Building the draft plan's path once in a shared place instead of at the two sites the review named (from JC-R345-48)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit dddbaf2, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 4 asked for a path key built "in `round_context` and `planning_contexts` (three lines)" (JC-320). The fixer found a common ancestor of both.
Rationale: Recorded: "`draft_plan` is built once in `prompts.empty_round_context` from `harness_root`, which covers the round, the planning and the fixture (`doctor`) contexts, instead of twice."
Spec conflict: None found. This is an implementation choice inside derived code; what left it open is that the review specified the sites, not the structure.
