## JC-329: Flipping individual settings with a pattern match because the general method would destroy the comments the finding was about (from JC-R345-49)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 4, commit dddbaf2, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 4 asked the probe builder to reuse the comment-preserving editor fix 3 had written (JC-269). The fixer found that the editor replaces a whole block, and the settings that needed changing sit inside a block whose comments are exactly the ones the finding wanted preserved.
Rationale: Recorded: "`set_yaml_keys` replacing the whole `gates` block would drop the block's inline comments, the very ones the finding names; scalars (the gate flags, `lostValuePerHour`) are flipped in place by a regex and the other keys go through `set_yaml_keys`."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; the constraint is that harness/project.yaml carries meaning in inline comments, including "# mechanical, run by the runner", which an earlier review had already shown a real driver needed (JC-261).
Other context: The fixer therefore used two mechanisms in one function, which is less tidy but preserves the property the finding asked for. Review 5 confirmed the outcome across twelve generated repositories and that a test pins the comment count.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md; commit dddbaf2; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md R4-n8; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 5.


================================================================================
TEST D: TWO SANDBOX ROUNDS IN ONE SANDBOX, EXERCISING THE APPROVAL SKIPS
================================================================================

## JC-330: Setting each round's money quote, $150 for round 0001 and $120 for round 0002 (from JC-D-1)
Who and where: the driver of test D, while writing each round's draft plan before the two start commands.
Context: Every round in this harness is opened with a quote, a dollar figure the owner is notionally paying. Almost everything downstream is a fraction of it: each step's spending budget, the price a gate is told a rejection would cost, and the point at which the round stops itself. Nothing in the harness computes the quote, so the driver had to name a number for each round.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the driver picked a round figure comfortably above what the earlier tests had spent so the round would not stop on budget, and then trimmed the second round from $150 to $120 because the first had used only 63 per cent of its quote.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files fix the arithmetic that uses the quote - harness/project.yaml sets the work and gate fractions and the hard-stop multiple - but say nothing about how the figure itself is arrived at. Round 0001's own postmortem raised exactly this as one of its seven issues: "nothing reviews the quote, yet every budget, FAIL threshold and stop is a fraction of it".
Other context: The two numbers shaped both rounds. Round 0002 finished at 89.6 per cent of its $120 and its postmortem's seventh issue was that "the $16.80 step budget exceeded the $15.22 left of the quote" - a budget larger than the money remaining, the same arithmetic test C had reported (JC-300). Round 0001's postmortem separately complained that the gates were being told an inflated rejection price derived from these budgets (JC-345). Neither round revisited the number once set. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md sections 2.5 and 2.6; section 10; sections 11.5 and 11.6.

## JC-331: Writing round 0001's plan to leave nothing open, and settling bad-input behaviour inside it (from JC-D-2)
Who and where: the driver of test D, writing the draft plan before round 0001 started.
Context: The driver also played the owner, so it wrote the plan the round would be built from. It had to decide how much the plan would settle in advance. Round 0001 was meant to exercise the skip paths - delegation and overrides - and an open question in the plan would have raised an extra stop for the owner and mixed two things together. The driver chose to close everything, including four assumptions about how the new function behaves, one of which reads "Bad input raises whatever Python raises; no validation is added."
Rationale: The report states it plainly: "The plan is deliberately airtight - round 0001 was to test the skips, not a question." The plan's own summary says "Nothing is left open: the owner's words and the assumptions below settle every behaviour, so no step should need to stop and ask."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; what left the choice open is that harness/docs/DRIVER.md tells the driver only to "Converse with the owner, write `harness/DRAFT-PLAN.json`" and gives no guidance on how much a plan should settle.
Other context: The airtight plan worked as intended - round 0001 raised exactly one stop for the owner. But the fourth assumption was factually wrong: reversing a list does not raise. That error is what forced the spec step to correct the sentence (JC-338) and the gate to decide whether to say so (JC-339), and round 0001's postmortem listed it as its fifth issue, noting that the owner's approval "answer[ed] the diff and not the contract". The driver also chose the function name here because the earlier tests had used the others. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 2.5; section 11.2; section 11.5.

## JC-332: Splitting round 0001's producer money across the eight producing steps (from JC-D-3)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The harness hands this step a pot of money - 70 per cent of the quote, $105 - and asks it to divide it into shares across the round's steps. Any split summing to one is legal, so the agent had to weigh which steps deserve the money.
Rationale: Quoted: "work split of $105 as CHAT-TO-PLAN 0.05 and PLAN-AGENTS 0.03 (both at their floors), PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.24, POSTMORTEM 0.18, SPEC-TO-TESTS 0.16, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.14, TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.11, CLEANUP 0.09; the money follows where judgment is still open (the spec) and where the round's durable value sits (the carry-forward), not the size of the diff."
Spec conflict: None found; the prose deliberately leaves it open. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says only "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision, and anything else you deem reasonable to consider. Optimize the budget, within reason."
Other context: The gate that judged this artifact endorsed the shape of the split - "the money follows where judgment is still open ... and where a one-line round leaves durable value ..., with tests above implementation - all defensible". No step came near its budget: the largest booked run cost $1.45 against budgets of $18 to $25. It is the fourth different answer to the same question (JC-191, JC-220, JC-284). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (first line); section 11.5.

## JC-333: Splitting round 0001's gate money across the six gates that would run (from JC-D-4)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Separately from the producer money, 30 per cent of the quote is reserved for the gates. Round 0001 ran with all seven gate flags on, so six gates had to be sized. This is a different pot with a different consequence from JC-332: these shares decide how much reading each gate can afford.
Rationale: Quoted: "gate split of $45 as PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.28, SPEC-TO-TESTS 0.19, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.18, POSTMORTEM 0.18, TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.14, PLAN-AGENTS 0.03 (floor); each gate is sized by what a miss costs downstream, not by the size of what it reads."
Spec conflict: None found; the same open instruction in PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt leaves it to the agent, and harness/project.yaml supplies only floors.
Other context: Only three of the six gates actually ran, because the driver's owner later overrode three of them (JC-341, JC-343, JC-344). Round 0001's postmortem made that its third issue: "3 of 6 LLM gates ran under a plan promising all six, and no checkpoint message showed gate coverage" (JC-346). The gate judging this plan noted its own share "proved sufficient". Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (second line); section 11.5.

## JC-334: Reserving no budget for refactoring in either of test D's rounds
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent of test D, in round 0001 attempt 1 and again in round 0002 attempt 1.
Context: The harness encourages each round to tidy the code it touches and allows up to 30 per cent of a round's budget for it. The agent had to decide how much of that headroom to reserve in advance, anywhere between nothing and the cap.
Rationale: Quoted, in words repeated almost exactly in the second round: "no refactor budget planned; the plan's non-goals confine the round to src/toy/text.py and tests/toy/test_text.py, so nothing in scope is stale and the 0.3 cap is not approached."
Spec conflict: None found. PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says "Plan to refactor where sensible, but avoid the refactor cap" and COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Refactoring in a round is encouraged" - both invite the choice without settling it.
Other context: Nothing in either round found anything stale, so the reservation was never missed. This is the thinnest kind of entry in the test D slice: the alternative (reserving a contingency) existed but was not attractive. The spec step made the matching declaration separately (JC-228). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in both rounds.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (fourth line) and section 11.3 (third line); sections 11.5 and 11.6.
Merged from: JC-D-6, JC-D-28

## JC-335: Settling on three assertions for round 0001's one test, and which three (from JC-D-9)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The specification carries a test plan. The agent had to decide how many cases the single new test method would check and which inputs they would use - the prose asks it to "Exhaust edge cases, within reason", and every extra assertion costs money when it lands.
Rationale: Quoted: "edge cases drawn at three assertions inside the one test method, the plan's own 'ab c' example, mixed case with punctuation, and the empty string; a fourth plain-word case would assert nothing the first does not."
Spec conflict: None found. PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Exhaust edge cases, within reason" and nothing says where "within reason" stops.
Other context: The three assertions landed unchanged and the suite was green. The same agent in round 0002 drew five instead, for a function with more rules, and priced each one (JC-353). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (seventh line); section 9.7; section 11.5.

## JC-336: Leaving bad input and cross-system testing out of round 0001's test plan (from JC-D-10)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Having fixed the three ordinary cases, the agent had to decide whether to also pin what happens when the function is handed something that is not text, and whether to add a test that crosses more than one part of the project. The prose explicitly asks for the second.
Rationale: Quoted: "no bad-input assertion and no integration test; the plan asks for one small test that asserts the reversal, a non-string argument is Python's behaviour rather than this module's, and one dependency-free module has nothing to cross."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Include integration tests that cross multiple systems, where sensible" - an instruction the agent had to decline. It is silent on whether a function's behaviour on wrong input belongs in the specification at all.
Other context: Kept as one entry rather than two, as the test D auditor had it: the integration-test half had no real alternative in a project with a single dependency-free module, while the bad-input half was live and is the test-coverage side of JC-338. Leaving bad input unpinned is what let round 0001's postmortem list the contract as an open question for the owner, and round 0002 hit the same gap again for a different reason (JC-354). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (eighth line); section 11.2; section 11.5.

## JC-337: Writing the function's body into the specification word for word
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, in round 0001 attempt 1 and again in round 0002 attempt 1.
Context: A specification can describe behaviour and leave the code to the implementing step, or it can dictate the code itself. For a one-line function there are several equivalent ways to write it, and the agent decided to remove that freedom, in both rounds.
Rationale: Quoted from round 0001: "the implementation is pinned to 'return text[::-1]' under a 'def reverse(text):' line, matching shout's shape, so no later step picks between equivalent one-liners." Round 0002 repeats it for its own function, "matching shout and reverse".
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt asks for a spec that is "complete, unambiguous and sensible" without saying whether that extends to dictating code.
Other context: It worked exactly as intended - round 0001's implementation step reported "No behaviour was left open to me - SPEC.md gave the function body verbatim - so I made no judgment call of either kind", and its gate confirmed the diff was the pinned lines. In round 0002 it mattered more: when the test-writing step was later skipped, the pinned body was the only written record of the behaviour until the cleanup step wrote the test, and the cleanup step relied on it - "it matches the one line already in src/toy/text.py" (JC-365). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call in both rounds.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (ninth line) and section 11.3 (eighth line); sections 11.5 and 11.6.
Merged from: JC-D-11, JC-D-34

## JC-338: Quietly correcting the plan's false statement about bad input instead of sending the plan back (from JC-D-13)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1 - its one undefined judgment call.
Context: The plan the driver had written (JC-331) asserted "Bad input raises whatever Python raises". For the pinned implementation that is simply untrue: handed a list, the expression reverses it and raises nothing. The agent noticed the contradiction and had to choose between sending the whole round back to have the plan corrected, stopping to ask the owner, or writing a corrected sentence into the specification and recording that it had done so.
Rationale: Quoted: "the plan assumes 'Bad input raises whatever Python raises', but with no validation a non-string sequence is reversed rather than raising (reverse([1,2]) gives [2,1]); the spec says a non-string argument gets Python's own behaviour, adds no check, and pins none of it in a test."
Spec conflict: The plan's own assumption line conflicts with the behaviour the same plan asks for, and COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt offers a route the agent declined: "UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong: quote the contradiction." Nothing says how small a wrongness has to be before rewriting it in the next artifact is preferable to sending it back.
Other context: This was the only thing either round flagged to the owner before the final review, and the review checkpoint's message showed it to the owner verbatim. The owner - the driver - replied "The diff is right", which the postmortem noted as "answering the diff and not the contract" and carried to the owner's clarification list as an open question. The gate saw both the plan's false assumption and the spec's corrected sentence and raised no finding (JC-339). Round 0002 met the same shape of gap and resolved it the same way (JC-356). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.2 (first line); section 5.1; section 4.1; section 11.5.

## JC-339: Passing the specification without raising the plan-versus-spec divergence as a finding (from JC-D-14)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate saw both the plan's false assumption and the spec's corrected sentence (JC-338). It had to decide whether that divergence was worth writing down as a finding - which would follow every later step of the round - or whether to say nothing and pass.
Rationale: Quoted: "raised no finding where the spec's 'a non-string argument gets Python's own behaviour' departs from the plan's assumption 'Bad input raises whatever Python raises'; the spec's wording is the accurate one, keeps the plan's operative 'no validation is added', pins nothing in a test, and the producer already logged it as undefined, so a finding would carry noise to every later producer for no gain."
Spec conflict: The gate prose sets a cost test it cannot compute exactly. COMMON-GATE.txt's rule prices a FAIL against a retry, and PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE.txt separately says "Unaligned with the owner's intent? Fail." Whether a corrected-but-divergent sentence is misalignment is left to the gate.
Other context: The gate's verdict was recorded as a clean pass with no findings, so the divergence survived to landing. Round 0001's postmortem picked it up as its fifth issue, describing it as "settled by an undefined call at PLAN-TO-SPEC and a defined one at its gate". The two other gates that ran in round 0001 passed cleanly with nothing recorded, so they have no entries. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call, appended by the runner from the gate's own verdict.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (eleventh line); section 11.5; section 9.8; section 7.14.

## JC-340: Running every override only in the safe gap between recording a step and asking for the next one (from JC-D-15)
Who and where: the driver of test D, from round 0001's first override onwards and for all four overrides in the slice.
Context: An override is the owner's instruction to skip a step. The harness accepts one at any time, but review 4 had found that overriding a step whose agent is mid-flight throws away that agent's unrecorded work, and that the harness's own documentation invites exactly that timing (JC-305). The driver had to decide whether to run its overrides at the natural moment - while a step was in flight - or only at the one moment where nothing can be lost.
Rationale: The report states it: "no override named a producer whose gate was pending, none named a step with an attempt pending (I overrode only between `record` and `next`, as review 4 note 1 advises)". Review 4's note reads "Workaround until fixed: override only between `record` and `next`".
Spec conflict: harness/docs/DRIVER.md warns without forbidding - "Overriding the step whose attempt is pending discards that attempt's unrecorded work ... instead of committing it unchecked: `record` first to keep it" - while harness/docs/PROCESS.md says an override "skips overridable steps not yet accepted, at any time".
Other context: The consequence is that three known-risky behaviours of the harness went untested in this slice, and the review that followed had to say so: "test D's driver happened never to be refused". The driver made the run reliable at the cost of coverage. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 7.11; sections 3.1 and 3.2; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md.

## JC-341: Skipping the gate that judges the round's tests, and giving a reason (from JC-D-16)
Who and where: the driver of test D speaking as the owner, round 0001, after the test-writing step had been recorded and before its gate ran.
Context: The test brief required an override of a gate outside a checkpoint, but not which gate, at what moment, or with what words. Skipping a gate has a real effect: the harness treats the override as acceptance of the artifact as it stands, which in this case also froze the tests.
Rationale: The owner's sentence the driver wrote and quoted to the runner: "override SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE - the spec pinned every assertion, so take the tests as they are."
Spec conflict: None found; this is the driver's own territory. harness/docs/DRIVER.md says outright that "Interpreting the owner's words is your judgment call", and harness/docs/PROCESS.md says "The owner's words are interpreted by the driver".
Other context: The runner behaved as documented - the gate was skipped with a findings file marked "override" and the tests were frozen at the override's own commit. It is worth contrasting with round 0002, where the driver's owner overrode the test-writing step itself rather than its gate (JC-361); that froze nothing and caused the round's largest problem. Round 0001's postmortem counted this as one of the three skipped gates behind its third issue. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 4.3; section 3.1; section 1; section 9.8; section 11.5.

## JC-342: Deciding not to log the bad-input problem a second time (from JC-D-17)
Who and where: the SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The harness asks every step to append a line to the round's judgment-call files whenever it meets something the specification leaves open. This agent hit the same non-string gap the spec step had already recorded (JC-338), and had to decide whether to add its own line or rely on the existing one.
Rationale: Quoted from its final message: "No validation was added, so a non-string argument gets Python's own behaviour, consistent with the plan's assumption and with the carried PLAN-TO-SPEC note in UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md about non-string sequences; that note is already recorded and needed no new line from me."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "If you ever feel like you are making an UNDEFINED JUDGMENT CALL ... append a small record with context inside your round's UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md file", and COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "one short line appended per call" - neither says whether a second agent meeting a gap another has already recorded should repeat it.
Other context: The counts the owner sees at a checkpoint come from these files, so the choice changed what the owner was shown: round 0001 ended with two undefined lines rather than three. The agent recorded no judgment calls for the attempt, so this is one of the decisions nobody labelled.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.5; section 11.2.

## JC-343: Skipping the step that sorts the round's tests, and giving a reason (from JC-D-18)
Who and where: the driver of test D speaking as the owner, round 0001, after the implementation gate had passed and before the test-sorting step ran.
Context: The test brief required an override of a producing step outside a checkpoint, but not which step. Of the steps the harness allows to be skipped, the driver picked the one that decides which of the round's tests join the project's permanent suite. Skipping it makes the harness keep every test by default.
Rationale: The owner's sentence the driver wrote: "override TESTS-TO-SUITE - one test file is the whole suite here, so keep every test and skip the sorting."
Spec conflict: None found; the choice of which overridable step to name is the driver's. harness/docs/PROCESS.md lists what may be skipped and what the default then is - "TESTS-TO-SUITE (every test kept)" - without saying when skipping is wise.
Other context: The runner did exactly what the documentation promised: no archive folder was created, every test was kept, and the round's cleanup step noted that the missing artifact was "absent by design, not by omission". The choice was deliberately the benign one; in round 0002 the driver's owner named the far more consequential test-writing step instead (JC-361). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 4.3; section 3.1; section 1; section 11.5.

## JC-344: Answering round 0001's final review with one sentence carrying both an approval and an override (from JC-D-19)
Who and where: the driver of test D speaking as the owner at the review checkpoint raised after the cleanup step of round 0001, and then as the driver executing it.
Context: The test brief required an override to be tried at a review checkpoint. The driver had to compose what the owner would say. It chose a single sentence that both approves the round's diff and orders a gate to be skipped - which is the one shape that exercises the documented "override first, then approve" sequence (JC-167).
Rationale: The owner's sentence: "The diff is right. override POSTMORTEM-GATE - nobody needs to judge a postmortem on a two-line round." The driver's own note on executing it: "one sentence carrying two acts - an approval of the diff and an override of a named gate. Per DRIVER.md line 40 the override goes first and the checkpoint stands, so the same sentence is the `--quote` of both commands."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What settled the execution once the sentence existed was harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 40, which says an override at a checkpoint resumes the round only when the checkpoint's own step is now overridden, "never at a `review`, whose step is already accepted"; the composition of the sentence was not settled by anything.
Other context: The harness did what the documentation said - the override returned the checkpoint again and the approval then resumed the round. It also produced a defect the driver reported and the following review accepted as a nit: the same sentence was written to the owner's log twice, so round 0001's log holds four lines for three owner statements (JC-384). Overriding the postmortem's gate also meant the round's postmortem was accepted with no gate reading it. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 4.1; section 4.4; sections 7.7 and 8.5; section 3.1; section 11.5.

## JC-345: Choosing round 0001's seven issues and ranking them by what a repeat would cost (from JC-D-20)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The prose tells this step to "identify issues, and rank them by importance" without saying what importance means or how many issues to report. The agent had to pick both the list and the yardstick.
Rationale: Quoted: "seven issues reported and ranked by what a repeat of each would cost the project, so the gates' price signal leads and owner-log hygiene trails; the code itself raised nothing, the diff matching SPEC.md exactly."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says "First, just identify issues, and rank them by importance" and is silent on what importance is measured in.
Other context: The ranking put at the top the finding that the gates are told an inflated price for rejecting work - "$26.26 vs $2.34, $15.76 vs $1.70, $4.21 vs $2.08" - which review 5 accepted as a real measurement but declined to act on before test E (JC-386), and which the bootstrap's final report records as not built. The round 0002 agent chose the same yardstick independently (JC-368), as had test B's and test C's (JC-241, JC-300). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (twelfth line); section 11.5; section 7.14.

## JC-346: Sending the skipped-gate problem to the fix list rather than to the owner's questions (from JC-D-21)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The prose gives this step exactly two destinations for every issue: a fix proposed for the next round, or a question put to the owner. The agent had to place the fact that only three of six planned gates actually ran (JC-333).
Rationale: Quoted: "the skipped gates went to TODO rather than CLARIFICATIONS; the owner wrote each override themselves with a reason, so what is missing is the gate-coverage summary at the checkpoint, not a question for them."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt sets the two destinations by whether "the owner is unaware" of a conflict. Whether an owner who wrote three overrides one at a time is "aware" that only half the gates ran is exactly what the prose does not settle.
Other context: This routing has its own recorded reasoning and its own gap in the prose, which is why it is a separate entry from JC-347 and JC-348 rather than one entry for "the postmortem routed its issues". The fix it proposed - show gate coverage in the checkpoint message - was reported to the owner and, per the final report, not built; review 5 refused it in terms (JC-387). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (thirteenth line); section 11.5.

## JC-347: Sending a contradiction between two of the owner's own files to the owner rather than fixing it (from JC-D-22)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The agent found that the harness charges for "living" files under paths that, in the sandbox, exclude the harness's own source - so the round paid $17.50 for nine lines of toy code while a round rewriting the runner would pay nothing. Two of the owner's files describe this differently. The agent had to decide whether that was a bug to fix or a question only the owner can answer.
Rationale: Quoted: "livingSourcePaths versus AGENTS.md's 'src/, docs/, and tests/ are living' went to CLARIFICATIONS rather than TODO; both are the owner's spec.yaml files and either could be the one that is right."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "`src/`, `docs/`, and `tests/` are living: every token you add is charged, every token you remove is refunded", while harness/project.yaml sets `livingSourcePaths: ["src/", "docs/", "tests/"]`, which in this round resolved to the toy project's folders and not the harness's own code.
Other context: This is the routing decision in round 0001 with the clearest justification, and the mirror image of JC-346. Review 5 later ruled that the two owner files agree on the real repository and disagree only in a sandbox whose settings were rewritten to point at a toy project, so the complaint is an artefact of the test environment (JC-389). The same reasoning was applied again in round 0002 to a different issue (JC-369). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (fourteenth line); section 11.5.

## JC-348: Adding round 0001's evidence to questions already open rather than opening new ones (from JC-D-23)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Two of the issues this round found were already sitting on the owner's question list from an earlier test. The agent had to decide whether to file fresh entries or append to the existing ones.
Rationale: Quoted: "no new clarification where test-A's open questions already cover the ground (unverified owner words, specCostPerWord); this round's evidence was appended to them instead, to keep the owner's list one question per question."
Spec conflict: None found. POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says to "add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review" and harness/docs/PROCESS.md says "The carry-forward files mirror POSTMORTEM's TODOs and CLARIFICATIONS; the runner never parses them" - so the file's shape is entirely the agent's to choose.
Other context: Round 0002's postmortem made the same choice for five open questions, using the same closing phrase word for word (JC-371), and test C's and test F's postmortems made it too (JC-303, JC-438). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.1 (fifteenth line); section 11.5.

## JC-349: Tracing issues that predate the round to the owner's files and the project vision (from JC-D-24)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0001, attempt 1 - its one undefined judgment call.
Context: The prose orders this step to trace every issue back to the plan that caused it and to the owner's words behind that plan. Three of round 0001's seven issues are about parts of the harness built before any round existed, so there is no earlier plan to trace them to. The agent had to invent a substitute or drop the tracing for those issues.
Rationale: Quoted: "the prose says to trace each issue to 'the plan(s) that originated them, and the owner words that led to the plan(s)', but three issues touch components built before round 0001 (retry_cost, livingSourcePaths, the unreviewed quote) and archives/rounds holds no earlier plan; traced those to the spec.yaml files that define them (project.yaml, AGENTS.md, the locked prose, SPEC.md) and to the vision, and said so in each trace."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt line 9 reads "For each issue, trace the component(s) touched by the issue to the plan(s) that originated them, and the owner words that led to the plan(s)" - an instruction with no answer for a harness that has no earlier round.
Other context: Round 0002's postmortem hit the identical wall and, knowing of this one, followed it (JC-372). The test report drew the obvious conclusion: "Two independent agents, same hole, same resolution ... Worth one sentence of prose", and review 5 eventually offered that sentence as an optional prompt clause which fix 5 applied (JC-385). A gate in test B had met the same limit from the other side (JC-252). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.2 (second line); section 5.1; section 7.16; section 11.5.

## JC-350: Building round 0002's plan around one decision the owner deliberately reserved (from JC-D-25)
Who and where: the driver of test D, writing round 0002's draft plan before it started.
Context: Having made round 0001 airtight (JC-331), the driver had to decide what round 0002 would look like. The brief required the round to be delegated from the command line, and the driver wanted to reach a question stop - the kind of pause a step raises when only the owner can decide. So it planted one behaviour-affecting choice, wrote owner words forbidding anyone to settle it, and relied on delegation skipping only reviews.
Rationale: The report states the design: "The open detail is modelled on test C's: one behaviour-affecting choice with exactly two sensible answers, one line either way, and the owner's own words telling PLAN-TO-SPEC to end NEEDS-OWNER." The owner's words in the plan read: "One thing I have not decided and do not want decided for me ... The step that has to pin it must stop and ask me - finish with NEEDS-OWNER, do not pick." On the reading the design rested on, the report says plainly that the harness's own rules document "is what the assignment and I both read".
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md line 13 states "Delegation skips only review checkpoints; questions, blocks, limits and spec edits still stop the round", and harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 87 repeats it. Three lines further down the same file says the opposite, at line 90: "NEEDS-OWNER with the gate disabled and the round delegated proceeds on the stated assumption and appends it to `UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md`". The code followed line 90, which came from the merged plan (JC-19).
Other context: This is the decision that produced the whole slice's headline result. No question stop was ever raised; the runner settled the owner's reserved decision on the step's own assumption and the round landed on it. The report calls it "the one failure ... a `question` checkpoint cannot exist in a delegated round", and it cascaded into JC-352, JC-357, JC-358, JC-360 and the first issue of the round's postmortem. Review 5 ruled the owner's sentence right and the code wrong, and fix 5 removed the branch (JC-373); test F then retested it (JC-472). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 2.6; section 1; sections 7.1 and 7.2; section 11.6; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 87 and 90 at the tested commit; SPEC.md line 13.

## JC-351: Reshaping round 0002's work split because no gate would run (from JC-D-26)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: Round 0002 ran with every gate switched off, so no step's artifact would be read by a reviewer before the round moved on. The agent had to decide whether that changed how the money should be divided, and by how much.
Rationale: Quoted: "work split of $84 as CHAT-TO-PLAN 0.05 and PLAN-AGENTS 0.03 (both at their floors), PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.22, POSTMORTEM 0.20, SPEC-TO-TESTS 0.15, CLEANUP 0.15, SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.12, TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.08; with no gate running this round the money follows the steps that fix judgment (the spec), freeze it (the tests), last check it before the owner (cleanup) and carry it forward (the postmortem), not the size of the diff." Its fuller reasoning: "with no gate reading anything, a clean DONE is accepted on the mechanical checks alone, so the care a gate would have bought moves into the producers."
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt leaves the split open and says nothing about a round with no gates at all.
Other context: Raising the cleanup step's share turned out prescient - cleanup was the step that had to rescue the round by writing its missing test (JC-365). The unspent gate money, $36 of the quote, simply went unused. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (first line); section 11.6.

## JC-352: Foreseeing that delegation would swallow the owner's reserved question, and finishing normally anyway (from JC-D-29)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - recorded in its final message, not in either judgment-call file.
Context: While reading the round's state, this agent worked out that the round had been started delegated, so the later spec step's required stop-and-ask would be settled by the runner instead of reaching the owner - directly contradicting the plan it had just been handed, which says the owner "will not have it decided for them". It had to choose between finishing its own artifact normally, sending the round back upstream because the plan could not be honoured, or stopping to ask.
Rationale: Quoted from its final message: "Two state facts bear on it too: the round is delegated through PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE, so PLAN-TO-SPEC's required NEEDS-OWNER settles on its stated assumption rather than pausing, and the owner's first look is the review checkpoint after CLEANUP; and the tests freeze at SPEC-TO-TESTS with M2 reverting later changes, so a late owner disagreement about the ending costs a round re-entry, not an edit." Having said so, it ended DONE.
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt offers "UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong: quote the contradiction" and "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide". Neither fits cleanly: the plan was not wrong, the round's configuration was, and nothing in the owner's files tells a step what to do when the way a round was started makes the plan's own instruction unachievable.
Other context: Round 0002's own postmortem singled this out - "AGENTS-PLAN.json foresaw the collision in writing and ended DONE rather than UPSTREAM" - and made the collision its first issue. Had the agent routed upstream, the round would have been re-planned and the harness's contradiction would have surfaced before the owner's decision was made for them. The agent recorded three defined and no undefined calls for the attempt, so this one is unlabelled and the auditor found it in the history.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.6; section 7.1.

## JC-353: Drawing round 0002's test at five assertions and dropping the empty-string case (from JC-D-32)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: The new function had more rules than round 0001's, and the agent had to decide how many of them to pin in the single test method and which candidate cases were redundant. It also priced the decision.
Rationale: Quoted: "edge cases drawn at five assertions inside the one test method, one per rule the spec states: the cut, exactly at the limit, shorter than the limit, a cut keeping case, punctuation and a space, and a limit of zero; empty text is left out as the shorter-than-limit case again, and each assertion costs about $4 of living charge."
Spec conflict: None found; PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says only "Exhaust edge cases, within reason".
Other context: Those five assertions are exactly what the cleanup step eventually wrote into the test file (JC-365) and what the owner saw in the final diff. The living charge the agent priced turned out to be $27 for the round, against $17.50 in round 0001. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (sixth line); section 9.7; section 11.6.

## JC-354: Leaving bad input, a negative limit and cross-system testing out of round 0002's test plan (from JC-D-33)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: The mirror of JC-336 for a function that takes a number as well as text, so there was an extra candidate case - a negative limit - which behaves oddly and which the agent chose not to pin.
Rationale: Quoted: "no bad-input or negative-limit assertion and no integration test; the plan asks for one small test, bad input is Python's behaviour rather than this module's, and one dependency-free module has nothing to cross."
Spec conflict: As in JC-336, PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt asks for "integration tests that cross multiple systems, where sensible" and is silent about wrong input.
Other context: The untested negative limit is the subject of the same agent's undefined judgment call (JC-356) and became the sixth issue of round 0002's postmortem, appended to round 0001's still-open question about the same gap. Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (seventh line); section 11.4; section 11.6.

## JC-355: Holding round 0002's owner-facing spec to 266 words and pushing the rest into the machine-readable file (from JC-D-36)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: Every word of the human-readable specification is charged to the owner at ten cents, while the machine-readable companion file costs nothing. The agent had to decide how much detail the owner pays to read, in a round where about a fifth of the document was taken up by the question it was raising.
Rationale: Quoted: "SPEC.md held to 266 words ($26.60), of which the open question is about 57; the plan's non-goals are condensed into one sentence there and carried verbatim in SPEC.json, where words are free."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt gives two instructions that pull against each other - "Assume a cost of ${{ project.specCostPerWord }} per word in the spec, and the spec needs to read as plain English" and "Carry the plan's non-goals in the spec" - without saying whether condensing the non-goals in the paid document satisfies the second.
Other context: The resulting $26.60 was the round's second-largest single charge after the plan itself, and the round's owner cost was nearly six times its agent cost. Round 0001's postmortem had already flagged the per-word spec price as an open question for the owner. It is the same optimisation tests B and C made (JC-230, JC-289). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (tenth line); section 10; section 11.6.

## JC-356: Leaving a negative limit to Python's own behaviour and pinning none of it (from JC-D-37)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - the first of its two undefined judgment calls.
Context: The plan again asserted "Bad input raises whatever Python raises", and again the pinned implementation does not raise: with a negative limit the function silently shortens the text from the other end. As in JC-338, the agent had to decide between sending the plan back, asking the owner, or writing a corrected sentence and recording it.
Rationale: Quoted: "the plan assumes 'Bad input raises whatever Python raises', but a negative limit neither raises nor shortens to that length under 'text[:limit]' (truncate('abcdefg', -2) gives 'abcde'); the spec says bad input and a negative limit get Python's own behaviour, adds no check, and pins none of it in a test."
Spec conflict: The plan contradicts the behaviour it asks for, and COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt offers the route the agent declined ("UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong").
Other context: The second occurrence of the same shape of decision in two rounds by two agents; the earlier is JC-338. The round's postmortem made it issue 6 and appended it, rather than filing a new question, to round 0001's still-open question about the same thing. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.4 (first line); section 5.2; section 4.2; section 11.6.

## JC-357: Choosing the plain cut as the assumption the whole round would be built on while the owner was asked (from JC-D-38)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - recorded by the runner as an undefined line when it settled the question on the agent's behalf.
Context: The plan ordered this step to stop and ask which of two endings a shortened text should have, and to pick neither. But a step that stops must still hand over a complete specification, so the agent had to choose which ending everything downstream would be written on meanwhile, and how to frame the question so the owner could answer cheaply.
Rationale: Quoted, as the runner recorded it: "The plain cut: truncate is 'return text[:limit]', so truncate('abcdefg', 5) is 'abcde' ... SPEC.md, SPEC.json and the five assertions are all written on that." Its framing of the question is quoted in the same line: "The plain cut is the single line the spec pins and needs no further rule; the ellipsis needs a second decision from you (what a limit under 3 gives, where '...' no longer fits) and a longer one-liner. Cheapest moment to answer is now: once SPEC-TO-TESTS is accepted the tests freeze, and switching endings after that is a round re-entry against maxRoundAttempts 2."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt tells the step to "build on your best assumption meanwhile and state it; you never contact them yourself, the gate weighs the question" - but the round had every gate switched off, so no gate existed to weigh it, and the harness's own rules then let the runner accept the assumption as the answer (JC-19).
Other context: This assumption became the shipped behaviour. The owner never got to choose; they were shown the settled result at the final review and said so - "I reserved that ending and was never asked" (JC-360). The agent's own framing, that answering later would cost a round re-entry, turned out to be the accurate description of the trap. The five assertions the cleanup step eventually wrote are all on this assumption. Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call, written by the runner but recording the agent's decision.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.4 (second line); section 5.2; section 4.2; section 1; section 11.6.

## JC-358: Running the impossible test item's own command at what the driver judged the equivalent moment (from JC-D-39)
Who and where: the driver of test D, round 0002, immediately after recording the spec step and before asking the runner for the next action.
Context: One item of the test brief was to run an override at a question stop. No such stop arose, because the harness settled the question itself (JC-350, JC-357). The driver had to decide what to do with an item that could not be run as written: abandon it, invent a different command that would reach a different stop, or run the item's own command at the nearest comparable point in the round.
Rationale: Quoted from the report's deviations section: "Rather than improvise a different command, I ran the item's own command, `override --steps SPEC-TO-TESTS --quote '<the owner's words>'`, at the equivalent moment in the round - after PLAN-TO-SPEC's `record` and before the `next` that would have rendered SPEC-TO-TESTS's prompt, so that the override still preceded the step it names and no unrecorded work existed."
Spec conflict: None found. What left the choice open is that the test brief assumed a stop the harness cannot produce; nothing in the owner's files or the driver documentation covers a test item whose premise is false.
Other context: The driver reported honestly which half of the item survived and which did not - "The half that does not ... was not reachable and is reported as such." Because the override was run outside a stop, the harness returned no warnings at all with it, which the driver then reported as a separate defect that review 5 accepted. The choice also preserved the override timing rule of JC-340, and the coordinating session then decided not to re-run the test but to cover the missing half later (JC-472). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 8.1; section 3.2; section 1; section 4.3; section 7.6.

## JC-359: Reading the source rather than running the `answer` command to see it refused (from JC-D-40)
Who and where: the driver of test D, round 0002, after the question was settled without a stop.
Context: The owner had an answer reserved and no stop at which to give it. The driver wanted to know whether the harness's `answer` command would work outside a stop. It could have run the command and observed the refusal, or read the runner's source and report what the code does.
Rationale: Quoted from the report: "`answer` has no meaning outside a checkpoint, so when item (f)'s `question` did not arise there was no way for the owner to give the answer they had reserved: `owner_command` (`round.py:1183`) refuses every command but `override` and `abandon` while `status != 'checkpoint'`. I did not run it - this is read from the source, not observed - but it is why the second half of (f) had to be run as a bare `override`."
Spec conflict: None found. harness/docs/PROCESS.md describes what `answer` does at a stop and says nothing about running it elsewhere.
Other context: The consequence is one claim in the test report that rests on reading code rather than on observation, which the driver flagged itself. Review 5's own major finding separately records that a refusal of this kind leaves the working tree dirty (JC-375), which is a real cost the driver avoided; review 5 also declined to build an out-of-pause answer command, because restoring the pause made it unnecessary (JC-390). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md sections 7.10 and 8.1; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md finding R5-M2.

## JC-360: Accepting, as the owner, the ending the harness had chosen for them (from JC-D-41)
Who and where: the driver of test D speaking as the owner, round 0002, after the spec step's question was settled by the runner.
Context: The owner had just been shown that a decision they had explicitly reserved had been made for them. The driver had to decide how that owner would react: insist on the other ending (which would mean re-entering the round against a limit of two re-entries), abandon the round, or accept the settled answer and carry on.
Rationale: The owner's words, first half: "I reserved that question and the runner settled it for me; leave the plain cut."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; this is the driver's interpretive territory under harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 20. What made the choice sharp is that the harness caps re-entries at two (JC-45), so insisting would have cost the round one of its two lives.
Other context: Accepting meant the round landed with the plain cut, and the same owner said so again at the final review (JC-367). Had the driver chosen to insist, the slice would have exercised the re-entry path instead and the harness's contradiction might have been shown a second way. This is one half of a single owner sentence; the other half is JC-361, entered separately because its consequences were entirely different. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md sections 4.3 and 4.4; section 3.2; section 11.6.

## JC-361: Skipping the test-writing step and telling the implementation step to write the tests (from JC-D-42)
Who and where: the driver of test D speaking as the owner, round 0002, in the same sentence as JC-360.
Context: The second half of the owner's sentence named the step to skip and gave a reason that assigned the skipped work to another step. Nobody had checked whether that other step was able to do it.
Rationale: The owner's words, second half: "override SPEC-TO-TESTS - the spec already pins the assertions, so let the implementation step write them."
Spec conflict: The instruction is defeated by the harness's own arrangement, which no owner file describes. harness/docs/PROCESS.md says only "SPEC-TO-TESTS (nothing frozen)" about the consequences of skipping that step, while the implementing step's writable paths come from the specification's implementation paths alone (JC-194) - so the file the owner was asking it to edit would be silently reverted. The prompt that step is given contradicts itself in this situation: its heading reads "write the code the spec describes, and nothing else, until the frozen tests pass" while it is handed an empty list of frozen tests and told a wrong test is "UPSTREAM to SPEC-TO-TESTS", the step just skipped.
Other context: This single clause caused the rest of round 0002's trouble. Three agents logged undefined judgment calls about it (JC-362, JC-363, JC-365), the test-sorting step raised two flags to the owner, and the round landed green only because the cleanup step happened to be allowed to write test files. The round's postmortem made it issue 2 and review 5 made it a finding that fix 5 built (JC-380). The driver reported it as one of the round's central observations: "The round still landed green only because CLEANUP wrote the test - an accident of `livingSourcePaths`, not a documented route." The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 4.3; sections 7.8 and 7.9; sections 11.4 and 11.6; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md finding R5-m2.

## JC-362: Writing only the function and leaving the round's test to a later step (from JC-D-43)
Who and where: the SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - its one undefined judgment call.
Context: The owner had just told this step to write the tests (JC-361), but the step's declared writable paths covered only the source file. Writing the test anyway would have been reverted by the harness and would have cost a rejected attempt. The agent had to choose between obeying the owner and being undone, or doing its own job and naming who could still do the rest.
Rationale: Quoted: "the plan scopes 'one test method for truncate' and the owner's override says 'let the implementation step write them', but this step's WRITE_PATHS are SPEC.json's implPaths only (['../src/toy/text.py']), so any edit to tests/toy/test_text.py is an M1 stray and is reverted; I added the function alone and left test_truncate for CLEANUP, whose write paths include ../tests/ and where M2 is inert because SPEC-TO-TESTS was overridden and tests_frozen_at is null, rather than spend a retry writing a file the runner would undo."
Spec conflict: The owner's instruction, the plan's scope and the plan's own success criterion ("The toy suite is green with the new test in it") all want the test written; the harness's writable-path map forbids it. No sentence of the owner's files or the process documentation covers the case.
Other context: The agent also worked out and passed on the next problem in the chain - "Note that TESTS-TO-SUITE runs first and will see no changed test file, so a test CLEANUP adds afterwards falls outside SUITE.json's keep/archive verdict" - which became the second of the two owner flags. Its nomination of the cleanup step is what the cleanup step then acted on (JC-365). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.4 (third line); section 5.2; section 7.8; section 11.6.

## JC-363: Naming an unchanged file in the keep list although both lists could have been empty (from JC-D-44)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: Because the test-writing step had been skipped, no test file had changed, so the step's contract was satisfied by two empty lists. The agent had to decide whether to say nothing, or to record a verdict in advance for a test that did not yet exist.
Rationale: Quoted from the undefined file: "the contract asks only that every test file changed this round appear exactly once in keep or archive, and none changed, so both lists could have been left empty; I named the unchanged ../tests/toy/test_text.py in keep instead, which moves nothing (the runner acts on archive only) but states the verdict in advance for the test_truncate that CLEANUP still has to write, CLEANUP reading SUITE.json as an input." Its reasoning for keeping rather than archiving, from the defined file: "the test still owed ... belongs in the permanent suite because that file is the whole toy suite, runs in milliseconds, and an assertion of the cut is the only thing that would catch a later edit to a one-line function, against about $3 testBaseCost plus the method's tokens booked once".
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt frames the choice only as "Choose where each test goes: In the permanent suite, or in the round archive?" and the harness's check only requires that every changed test file appear exactly once. Neither covers a round in which no test file changed and one is still owed.
Other context: The agent recorded this one decision in both judgment-call files, so the two mentions are merged here into one entry. The advance verdict was acted on: when the cleanup step wrote the test, it noted "SUITE.json already keeps that file in the permanent suite, so the test joins it". Recorded as both defined and undefined.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (eleventh line) and section 11.4 (fourth line); section 5.2; section 11.6.

## JC-364: Flagging two problems to the owner rather than stopping the round to ask (from JC-D-45)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: The agent had found two real problems - the round's test did not exist at all, and the harness runs this step before the only step that could still write one. It had to choose between stopping the round with a question for the owner (which costs the owner's time) and passing the items along as flags that appear in the log and reach later steps.
Rationale: Quoted: "the missing test and the ordering that caused it go to raise_with_owner rather than NEEDS-OWNER, the owner having already asked for the test", expanded in its final message: "Neither needs an owner decision - the owner's words already ask for the test and the override assumed the implementation step would write the assertions - so they are flagged, not ask[ed]".
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt says "If there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner, flag it" while COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide and spending their time is sensible given their cost". Which channel a problem the owner has arguably already answered belongs in is left open.
Other context: Entered separately from JC-363 because it is an independent decision from the same logged line with a quite different consequence - it decided whether the round paused. The routing worked: both items reached the log as "flagged for the owner", the cleanup step read them and closed the first by writing the test, and the postmortem carried the second forward. It is the second field confirmation of the route review 3 built (JC-263). Logged as a DEFINED judgment call, part of the same line as JC-363.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.3 (eleventh line); section 4.4; section 11.6; section 7.15.

## JC-365: Writing the round's missing test in the cleanup step rather than landing the new function untested (from JC-D-46)
Who and where: the CLEANUP agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - its one undefined judgment call.
Context: By this point the round's new function existed with nothing asserting its behaviour, no step was documented as responsible for the test, and this was the last step before landing. The agent's own instructions describe it as "standard cleanup, no new behaviour". It had to choose between landing the function untested and writing a test that was arguably outside its job.
Rationale: Quoted: "with SPEC-TO-TESTS overridden and SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION's write paths implPaths only, no prose or artifact says who writes test_truncate now; I wrote it into TextTest exactly as SPEC.md's test plan pins it, five assertions on the assumed plain cut, rather than land truncate asserted nowhere against the plan's validation 'The toy suite is green with the new test in it' - about $24 of living charge and testBaseCost against $50.92 of quote left, and the owner sees the assertions at the review checkpoint this step raises."
Spec conflict: CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt opens "CLEANUP: standard cleanup, no new behaviour" and says only "Clean up the round, so that it can be landed without issue" - writing the round's only test is new work by any reading, but landing without it fails the plan's own success criterion. The owner's files say nothing about who writes a test when the test step is skipped.
Other context: This is why the round landed green at all, and the driver called it "an accident of `livingSourcePaths`, not a documented route" - the cleanup step is allowed to write test files only because the harness's list of chargeable living folders happens to be reused as its writable paths (JC-103). The test the agent wrote is exactly what the owner saw in the final diff, and the five assertions are all built on the assumption of JC-357. Review 5 then gave the case a documented route by widening the implementation step's paths (JC-380). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.4 (fifth line); section 5.2; section 9.7; section 7.8; section 11.6.

## JC-366: Leaving the ordering problem to the postmortem and staying out of the documentation folders (from JC-D-47)
Who and where: the CLEANUP agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1.
Context: Two problems had been flagged to the owner and routed through this step. The agent closed the first by writing the test (JC-365) and had to decide what to do with the second - that the harness runs the test-sorting step before cleanup - and, separately, whether to use the write access it had been given to the project's documentation folders.
Rationale: Quoted from its final message: "The second carried flag, that TESTS-TO-SUITE runs before CLEANUP so a test CLEANUP writes gets no keep/archive verdict, is a harness-ordering question outside my write paths and is left for POSTMORTEM." And on the documentation: "docs/ and INDEX.md are in my write paths but the round's non-goals forbid touching them, and docs/TODO.md's items are POSTMORTEM's carry-forward, not this round's".
Spec conflict: The harness's writable-path list for this step and the round's own non-goals point in opposite directions, and no rule says which wins. harness/docs/PROCESS.md says flagged items are "carried to every later producer and to POSTMORTEM" without saying who should act on one.
Other context: The postmortem did pick the item up and carried it to the owner's fix list, so the routing worked. The restraint over the documentation folders is the reason the round's diff was exactly the two planned files, and it is the same line test C's and test F's cleanup agents held (JC-297, JC-436). ## JC-372: Tracing round 0002's pre-existing issues the way round 0001's had (from JC-D-53)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent of test D, round 0002, attempt 1 - its one undefined judgment call.
Context: The same wall as JC-349: four of this round's seven issues concern parts of the harness that existed before any round, and the prose demands each issue be traced to the plan that caused it. This agent could see round 0001's archived postmortem and had to decide whether to follow it or find its own answer.
Rationale: Quoted: "the prose says to trace each issue to 'the plan(s) that originated them, and the owner words that led to the plan(s)', but issues 1, 3, 5 and 7 touch components built before round 0001 ... and archives/rounds holds no earlier plan; as round 0001's postmortem did, I traced those to the spec.yaml files and locked prose that define them and to the vision, and said so in each trace."
Spec conflict: The same unanswerable instruction in POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt line 9.
Other context: Entered separately from JC-349 because a different agent made it and its reasoning is different - this one is a decision to follow a precedent it could read, where the first had to invent the answer. The test report treats the pair as one gap in the prose worth one sentence of fix, and review 5 supplied that sentence as an optional clause (JC-385). Logged as an UNDEFINED judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md section 11.4 (sixth line); section 5.2; section 7.16; section 11.6.

## JC-373: A question with no gate to weigh it stops the round even when it is delegated
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-M1; built by the fix-5 fixer in `round.record_producer` (the NEEDS-OWNER branch), commit 3f613d7.
Context: When a step decides only the owner can answer a question and its judging gate is switched off, the runner in a delegated round answered for the owner: it recorded an assumption and carried on (JC-19). In test D the owner had said in their own words that the round "must stop and ask me", and afterwards observed "I reserved that ending and was never asked" (JC-350, JC-357, JC-367). The reviewer had to decide which of four written statements governs: the owner's file, the harness's rules file (which contradicts itself), the merged plan, or the code.
Rationale: Quoted: "Verdict: the owner's sentence is right, PROCESS.md:87 and DRIVER.md:20 are right, the runner and PROCESS.md:83/:90 are wrong. Delegation is 'approval without reviews'; a NEEDS-OWNER is the producer's judgment that the owner's time is worth spending (COMMON-OVERVIEW), and with the gate off nobody weighs that judgment: the runner overruling it silently is what test D shows." The reviewer also dated the competing statements: the plan's rule came from "`merged-B.md:32` decision 17 (plan 3), adopted by FINAL-PLAN amendment 10 on 2026-09-13 00:18; the owner's sentence was committed at 07:48 the same day (`2db4ecf`), after the implementation milestones, into a file whose header says the listed files 'say what the harness must do'." The fix commit carries the same reasoning into the code as a comment: "no gate weighs the question, so the owner does, delegated or not: delegation skips only review checkpoints (SPEC.md)".
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md line 13: "Delegation skips only review checkpoints; questions, blocks, limits and spec edits still stop the round." Against it, merged-B decision 17: "NEEDS-OWNER with the gate disabled: checkpoint `question` unless delegated, in which case the round proceeds on the assumption and logs it to `UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md`." CHAT-TO-PLAN-OVERVIEW.txt's "delegate" means "approved; no checkpoints", which read literally covers every checkpoint.
Other context: The two timestamps are exactly as review 5 states; the review-3-to-5 auditor verified them with the repository's log, and so did this merge. The reviewer noted that the runner already honoured the owner's sentence for the other three cases it lists, which made the question the single exception. The fix removes about twelve lines, deletes the producer prompt's delegated variant of the status, and extends the question checkpoint's resume list to offer an override, which the runner already accepted there (JC-114). Test F was run afterwards specifically to check it, and the pause appeared (JC-427, JC-472).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M1, section 3, section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `round.record_producer`; `plumbing/producer.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 83 and 90; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 37; SPEC.md line 13; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md decision 17; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendment 10; commits 3f613d7 and 0851d49.
Merged from: JC-R345-50, JC-CODE-47

## JC-374: Settling the conflict by deleting runner code rather than asking the owner to reword (from JC-R345-51)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, closing sentence of section 6.
Context: Having found the owner's sentence and the harness in conflict (JC-373), the reviewer could have recommended an edit to the owner's file - the harness has a whole mechanism for flagging such edits - or a change to the harness. It had to choose which side moves.
Rationale: Quoted: "No edit to a `spec.yaml` file is recommended by this review: R5-M1 is settled by removing runner code, not by rewording the owner." The fix is described as one that "removes code; about 12 lines in all".
Spec conflict: The governing instruction is in spec.yaml's own header: "They belong to the owner and change often. Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them. Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly."
Other context: All three later reviews end with the same statement, but only here does it decide a major finding. The consequence is that the harness now pauses a delegated round for a question, which costs the owner attention that delegation was meant to save; the reviewer accepted that cost explicitly and priced the owner's cheaper alternatives instead (JC-264).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 5; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 6; spec.yaml.

## JC-375: Undoing a refused command's traces at the point of refusal rather than reordering the command's work
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-M2; built by the fix-5 fixer in `round.owner_command`, commit ecc46b2.
Context: Every owner command first writes the owner's words into the round's log and history, then decides whether the command is allowed. Eight different refusals happened after those writes, leaving the round's own files modified; the next command then either counted an infrastructure error or rejected an agent's work for touching files the agent never touched, costing a paid run. The reviewer had to choose how to make a refusal leave no trace.
Rationale: Quoted: "Fix (four lines in `owner_command`, one test): wrap the dispatch ... in `try/except RunnerError`, and on a refusal revert the two files before re-raising ... Alternative, not preferred: move the appends after the dispatch, which spreads the validation of four commands." The fix commit records the failure it removes: "Every refusal raised after the quote and the RESUME line were appended ... left HISTORY.md and the OWNER.log slice dirty, so the next `next` counted an infrastructure error and reset them, and a pending attempt's record was rejected with a blocking M1 on the runner's own files."
Spec conflict: harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 51 already promised the behaviour - "a refused command writes nothing (its quote line and `RESUME` entry are undone)" - so the owner-facing rule existed and the code did not implement it; no owner file addresses it, and what left it open is that the runner's own files are runner-owned and therefore strays when they are dirty.
Other context: The rejected alternative would have scattered the validation logic of four commands, trading a local repair for a structural change. The code now carries the property as a comment: "a refusal leaves no trace: the quote line and the RESUME entry are undone, nothing is saved or committed". The same commit made the resume payload carry its warnings so an unverified-quote flag reaches a driver that reads only JSON. Review 5's other major depended on this one being in place first (JC-305).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M2 and section 7 note 4; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `round.owner_command`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 51; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_a_refused_owner_command_leaves_no_trace; commit ecc46b2.
Merged from: JC-R345-53, JC-CODE-57

## JC-376: Grading a scoring weakness in a test tool as major because no review will follow the test that uses it (from JC-R345-55)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-M4.
Context: The tool that scores a gate's answer in an experiment checked whether the gate's quotation contains one expected fragment. For five of six planted defects, a gate that correctly failed for the other half of the plant scored as if it had failed for the wrong reason. The problem is in test tooling, not in the harness a round uses, which would ordinarily place it lower on the scale.
Rationale: Quoted: "No review follows test E, so a false `quote_contains_planted` on a correct FAIL would be read as a gate that failed for the wrong reason." The reviewer had already set the rule for itself in its process section: "No review follows test E, so anything on this path is a major at least."
Spec conflict: None found. The scoring tool is derived tooling that no owner file mentions; what left the choice open is that severity here depends on who will be left to catch a mistake, which the assignment, not the specification, determines - and the assignment's own rule for this review came from the coordinating session (JC-477).
Other context: The same reviewer graded a different tool defect in the same file as a minor because it produced a visible crash rather than a wrong answer (JC-381), so the scale was applied on the basis of whether a wrong conclusion could be drawn silently. Fix 5 fixed it (JC-377).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M4, section 1, section 7 note 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit ada60d6.

## JC-377: A planted defect may be quoted by either half
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, the fix half of finding R5-M4; built by the fix-5 fixer in `probe.score` and the expectation fixtures, commit ada60d6.
Context: Each planted defect has two halves - the sentence that is wrong and the place it contradicts - and a gate that correctly fails may quote either. The reviewer could have made each plant single-sided so that only one quotation is possible, changed the gate prose, or accepted several quotations; it also had to choose the second acceptable fragment for each of five fixtures.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "let `quote_contains` be a string or a list and match if any anchor is in the quotes ... list both halves", with the five pairs named. Each choice is justified by what the owner's gate prose points the gate at; for one of them, "the gate prose ... points the gate at the spec's component ... which holds no 'shout'". The fix commit records the same: "quote_contains in expect.json may be one anchor or a list, and a FAIL quoting any of them scores quote_contains_planted; five fixtures now list both halves of their plant", and the code comment states it: "a plant has two halves and a right FAIL may quote either".
Spec conflict: None found; the scoring is the harness's own test instrument. What left it open is that COMMON-GATE.txt tells a gate to "quote the text you judge" without saying which end of a contradiction that is, and each gate's own prose points it at a different end - for instance SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt's "For each component of the spec: * Is it not covered by tests? Fail", which directs the gate at the uncovered component rather than at the planted test.
Other context: This loosens the ground truth the gate-effectiveness measurement rests on, which is a real trade: a gate that quotes an unrelated sentence still fails the score, but the measurement can no longer distinguish which half the gate understood. The reviewer confirmed by reproduction that every alternative fragment is actually visible to the gate, and the existing test that checks the fixtures load stayed green because it only requires the field to be non-empty. Test E then hit exactly the case the change was for, where the planted defect was an absence and the only quotable text was the requirement (JC-410).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-M4 and section 5; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `probe.score`; harness/tests/fixtures/defects/*/expect.json; harness/docs/TESTING.md line 31; harness/tests/test_probe.py::test_probe_check_scores_any_listed_anchor_and_finds_its_folder; commit ada60d6.
Merged from: JC-R345-56, JC-CODE-93

## JC-378: Turning a step's appeal to a skipped predecessor into an ordinary rejection instead of a re-entry
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-m1; built by the fix-5 fixer in `round.upstream`, commit b887f2a.
Context: A step that believes an earlier step's output is wrong can send the round back to it. If the owner has skipped that earlier step, the round sends itself back, skips the step again, and loops until it hits its re-entry limit, wasting two paid runs. The harness's prompt for one step actively encourages this move. The reviewer could refuse the owner's skip, teach the loop to stop, or answer the step directly.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Fix (two lines in `upstream()`): when `target in st['overrides']`, take the existing unknown-target path with the finding text 'UPSTREAM names SPEC-TO-TESTS, which is overridden this round: its paths are yours (R5-m2), fix it here or return BLOCKED'; no re-entry, no orphaned U1." The fix commit records what went wrong without it: "the plumbing still routed a wrong test UPSTREAM to the overridden step, which upstream() re-entered: skipped again, U1 orphaned, the source re-run with no finding, round_retries burnt to round-limit." The code comment states the rule: "an overridden target never runs again: re-entering it would only skip it and burn a round retry."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says "UPSTREAM when an earlier artifact is wrong: quote the contradiction", with no exception for a step the owner removed from the round. harness/docs/PROCESS.md already had a path for an unknown target but not for a skipped one.
Other context: The choice reuses a path the harness already has rather than adding a new one, and it answers the step with instructions instead of bouncing it: the refusal is a finding on the source step with the suggestion "fix it under your own WRITE_PATHS, or return BLOCKED", so the round has somewhere to go rather than looping. It is the runner choosing to bound a loop the prose permits. The reviewer paired it with the write-path change so that "fix it here" is actually possible (JC-380), and the fixer generalised the wording (JC-394).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m1 and section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `round.upstream`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 79; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_upstream_to_each_earlier_producer_and_to_the_plan; commit b887f2a.
Merged from: JC-R345-57, JC-CODE-48

## JC-379: Ruling that the agents who recorded uncertainty about a skipped test step were right and the prose needs no change (from JC-R345-58)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-m2 and the section on the undefined judgment calls about the overridden step.
Context: With the test-writing step skipped, three agents in test D recorded that they could not tell who should write the round's tests, and one recorded that the owner's phrase "until the frozen tests pass" has no meaning when nothing is frozen (JC-362, JC-363, JC-365).
