The reviewer had to say whether those records were correct behaviour, a symptom of a defect, or a sign the owner's prose should change.
Rationale: Quoted: "Verdict on the two undefined calls the producers logged: both correct and the expected outcome. 'Who writes the test' was undefined because the harness gave the override no route ... '"until the frozen tests pass"' is the owner's sentence in SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-OVERVIEW and, with nothing frozen, `FROZEN_PATHS: []` is the truthful plumbing beside it; verify still gates the step. Nothing in the prose needs a word."
Spec conflict: SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-OVERVIEW.txt line 1: "write the code the spec describes, and nothing else, until the frozen tests pass" - a sentence that presumes the earlier step ran, which the owner is free to skip.
Other context: The reviewer separated the two halves cleanly: one was the harness's fault and got a code fix (JC-380), the other is an owner sentence whose emptiness the machine states truthfully. This is the clearest example in the review chain of a reviewer validating agents' recorded uncertainty rather than treating it as noise to be engineered away - the same stance review 4 took on a different sentence (JC-318).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m2, section 3, the dedicated verdicts section, section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md.

## JC-380: Handing the tests to the implementation step instead of reordering the round or refusing the skip
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, the fix half of finding R5-m2; built by the fix-5 fixer in `prompts.step_context` and `plumbing/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION.txt`, commit b887f2a.
Context: When the test-writing step is skipped, no step before the cleanup step may write tests, and test D's round only landed green because the cleanup step's paths happened to cover them (JC-361, JC-365). Three repairs were available: let the implementation step write tests, move the step that sorts tests to later in the round, or refuse to let the owner skip the test step at all.
Rationale: The reviewer wrote the fix as "one line of code, two clauses of text" widening the implementation step's write paths when the test step is skipped, and explicitly closed the other doors: "Not to build: reordering TESTS-TO-SUITE after CLEANUP (POSTMORTEM 0002 issue 4), or refusing the override." The fix commit records the mechanism: "Now SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION's WRITE_PATHS include testPaths when SPEC-TO-TESTS is overridden (its gate's DIFF_FILE and TESTS-TO-SUITE's changed list follow), the plumbing says so beside FROZEN_PATHS", and the code comment reads "an overridden SPEC-TO-TESTS leaves the tests to the implementation".
Spec conflict: None found; no owner file describes what an override of a step implies for the following step's write paths, and each step's prose describes only its own step. harness/docs/PROCESS.md lists the test step as overridable and says "nothing frozen", so the owner's freedom to skip it is established.
Other context: This deviates from the merged plan, which described an override of that step only as a subtraction - "no tests; the frozen set is empty" (JC-8) - by giving it a positive effect; the alternative, leaving the round without tests, would have made the override useless for any real change. The chosen fix also makes the downstream sorting step see the tests, which closed one of the two flags test D had raised. The runner's generated prompt now says so in every implementation prompt.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m2 and section 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `prompts.step_context`; `plumbing/SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION.txt`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 38; harness/tests/test_outcomes.py::test_overridden_spec_to_tests_hands_the_tests_to_the_implementation; commit b887f2a.
Merged from: JC-R345-59, JC-CODE-9

## JC-381: Grading two crashes in the probe tool as minor because a crash cannot be mistaken for a result (from JC-R345-60)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-m8.
Context: The scoring tool crashes with a raw error when run without a folder argument, and crashes again when pointed at a folder it has already used. Both are on the path of the very next test, which the reviewer's own rule says makes anything there "a major at least".
Rationale: The reviewer overrode its own rule and explained why: "Minor rather than major: the documented form (TESTING.md:30, the manual hint) passes `--dir`, and a traceback is not a false conclusion."
Spec conflict: None found. The tool is derived test tooling; the tension was between the reviewer's own severity rule for the upcoming path and the nature of the failure.
Other context: The distinction drawn - a visible crash versus a silently wrong score - is the same one that made the sibling finding a major (JC-376), so the scale was applied consistently even while an explicit rule was set aside. Fix 5 fixed both crashes anyway, in the same commit as the major, and widened the guard (JC-396); test E then deliberately provoked two of the refusals to confirm them (JC-423).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m8 and section 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit ada60d6.

## JC-382: Raising a thin example specification as a probable fixture problem while admitting it is less certain (from JC-R345-61)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, finding R5-m9.
Context: The clean example specification used to test the gate that judges specifications is three sentences with a four-word test plan, while the owner tells that gate to fail an incomplete specification. This is the same class of problem as the two fixture findings before it (JC-307, JC-310), but whether a gate would actually fail it is a matter of degree. The reviewer had to decide whether to raise it and how confidently.
Rationale: Quoted: "a gate told 'Incomplete? Fail.' may fail the fixture, not the prose ... Same class as R4-M3 and R4-m6, less certain."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE.txt's "Incomplete? Fail." against PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt, which demands the specification be "complete, unambiguous and sensible" with "a high-level test plan in prose covering the whole implementation"; the fixture predates that comparison.
Other context: The reviewer both raised it and told the next test's driver how to live without the fix ("read a clean-seed FAIL's findings before concluding anything about the gate (PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE's seed is the thinnest)"), and left the decision to the fixer ("Apply R5-M4 (and R5-m9 if the fixer agrees)"). Fix 5 applied it, and kept the round's cost model unmoved while doing so (JC-398). Test E then ran the thickened seed and the gate passed it with one non-blocking wording finding, which the report treats as the evidence that the repair held (JC-406).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m9, section 6, section 7 note 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit 7e963bb.

## JC-383: Leaving an equally thin example postmortem alone because that gate's rules have nothing to bite on (from JC-R345-62)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, last sentence of finding R5-m9.
Context: Having decided one thin fixture should be thickened (JC-382), the reviewer looked at another, an example postmortem whose whole content is "Issues: none stood out.", and had to decide whether the same reasoning applies.
Rationale: Quoted: "The clean `POSTMORTEM.md` ('Issues: none stood out.') is thin too, but that gate's rules are per issue and hold on none."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt is written per issue ("For each issue found by the postmortem: Is the trace incomplete? Fail. Is the decision not sensible? Fail."), so a postmortem with no issues triggers no rule; the gate prose does not say whether finding no issues is itself suspect.
Other context: This is a deliberate limit on how far the fixture programme goes, and it turns on reading the owner's prose closely rather than on a general principle that fixtures should be rich. Fix 5 left that fixture untouched. Test E then showed the reasoning was incomplete: the gate faced with the empty fixture invented a standard of its own and judged the zero-issue conclusion against the round record, producing two findings (JC-417, JC-418).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m9; harness/locked_prose/POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt; harness/tests/fixtures/canned/POSTMORTEM.md.

## JC-384: Making a repeated owner quotation two labelled acts rather than removing the duplicate
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, nit R5-n1; built by the fix-5 fixer in `round.owner_command` and `owner.append_line`, commit 708b0e8.
Context: The round's copy of the owner's words records one line per command, so an owner whose single sentence is used for two commands appears twice - which is what test D's combined approval and override produced (JC-344). A postmortem proposed removing duplicates. The choice was between deduplicating and labelling.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "Tag the line with the command, `[via driver: override]` ... so two lines are two acts; do not dedupe." The fix commit repeats it: "The driver line in the round's OWNER.log slice is tagged with the command that carried the quote ([via driver: override]), so an override followed by an approve with one sentence reads as two acts, not a duplicate", and the code comment states it: "one line per command: two acts with one sentence are two lines".
Spec conflict: None found. The log and its verification are the harness's own mechanism; SPEC.md describes it only as "A prompt hook logs the owner's chat to a gitignored OWNER.log; the runner verifies quotes against it", which is silent on repetition.
Other context: The reasoning preserves the record of what was actually done with the owner's words, which matters because the harness verifies every command's quotation against that log (JC-120). The tag also records whether the quote was verified, which is how a reader of the archive can tell a checked word from an unchecked one without going to the history.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-n1 and section 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `round.owner_command`; `owner.append_line`; harness/tests/test_owner.py::test_round_slice_and_driver_lines; commit 708b0e8.
Merged from: JC-R345-63, JC-CODE-84

## JC-385: Telling the postmortem what to do when a component predates every archived plan
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, nit R5-n7; built by the fix-5 fixer in `plumbing/POSTMORTEM.txt`, commit 708b0e8.
Context: The owner's prose tells the final step of a round to trace each issue back to the plan that caused it. Parts of the system are older than the first recorded round and have no such plan, and both of test D's postmortems recorded the same uncertainty (JC-349, JC-372). The reviewer had to decide whether to stop the recurrence and, if so, how.
Rationale: Quoted from the review: "The prose is the owner's; an optional clause in `plumbing/POSTMORTEM.txt` ... stops the recurrence without a prose edit. Optional: the calls were correct and cheap." The fix commit records what it says: "The POSTMORTEM plumbing names the earlier round folders' PLAN.json and says a component older than the first archived round has no plan to trace to, only the spec files; the owner's prose is untouched."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt line 9: "For each issue, trace the component(s) touched by the issue to the plan(s) that originated them, and the owner words that led to the plan(s)" - an instruction with no answer for a component that predates every plan, and for the entire harness at round one.
Other context: Marking a fix optional is itself a judgment: it leaves the fixer free, and the reviewer supported it with a cost argument rather than a correctness one. Fix 5 chose to apply it, so the harness now answers a question the owner's prose leaves open, in the machine-written part of the prompt rather than in the owner's sentence - the same pattern as JC-309 and JC-321. The closing clause of the commit message ("the owner's prose is untouched") shows the alternative that was rejected: editing the prose to add the exception.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-n7 and section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; `plumbing/POSTMORTEM.txt`; harness/locked_prose/POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt; commit 708b0e8.
Merged from: JC-R345-64, JC-CODE-98

## JC-386: Refusing to price a gate's retry from what the last run actually cost (from JC-R345-65)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, section 3, "Suggestions from test D and its postmortems not to build".
Context: Gates are told what a rejection would cost so they can weigh whether to reject (JC-37). The figure is derived from the owner's quote and ran two to eleven times higher than what runs actually cost in test D; a postmortem proposed computing it from recorded spending instead (JC-345). This was the eve of the test that measures gate behaviour.
Rationale: Quoted: "the figure is PROCESS.md:116's owner-derived formula ... Pricing it from the last recorded attempt would change the number every gate is weighed against on the eve of test E, and in a probe it would print the stub's fake `$0.01`, so the same gate would see a retry costing `$1.22` in a probe and `$26` in a round. The gap the postmortem measured is between the quote and today's prices, which is the owner's calibration ... Revisit after test E with the measured PASS/FAIL rates."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt builds the gate's whole decision rule on this number: "A FAIL costs a retry of the producing step (${{ step.retry_cost }}) plus this gate again: FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that". The owner's prose names the number but not how it is computed.
Other context: The reviewer gave two independent reasons - changing the instrument immediately before measuring with it, and a simulated agent's fake cost poisoning the figure in experiments - and deferred rather than refused. It is the only "not to build" in the review chain with a stated revisit condition.
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3 and section 6; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md items table; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 116.

## JC-387: Refusing a new line reporting how many gates ran (from JC-R345-66)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, section 3, "Suggestions ... not to build".
Context: A postmortem proposed that every pause and the landing report state which gates had actually run, so the owner can see how much judging happened (JC-346). The reviewer had to decide whether the existing records suffice.
Rationale: Quoted: "the owner wrote each override; HISTORY carries each skip line; `status` with R5-m5 lists the overrides. Nothing else needed."
Spec conflict: None found. FINAL-PLAN amendment 8 requires judgment calls to be surfaced loudly but says nothing about gate coverage; the proposal was new ground.
Other context: The refusal leans on a fix from the same review that adds the list of skipped steps to the status command, so the reviewer is declining the new feature partly because its own smaller fix covers the need. Fix 5 recorded "no change".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3 and R5-m5; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md.

## JC-388: Refusing to attach judgment-call counts to the lines that record a skipped pause (from JC-R345-67)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, section 3, "Suggestions ... not to build".
Context: A postmortem proposed that when a delegated round skips a pause, the line recording the skip should also name the judgment calls the owner is therefore not seeing. The reviewer had to decide whether that adds anything.
Rationale: Quoted: "the driver already relays `undefined_new` at every `record`, delegated or not (DRIVER.md:35, observed in D section 5)".
Spec conflict: None found. The relevant rule is the plan's amendment 8 and the driver instruction created by review 3's finding (JC-256); the owner's files do not describe the skip lines.
Other context: This is the direct descendant of review 3's ruling that delegation does not delegate monitoring - the earlier fix made the proposal unnecessary, and the reviewer said so rather than adding a second channel for the same information. Fix 5 recorded "no change".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3; bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md R3-M3; harness/docs/DRIVER.md line 35.

## JC-389: Ruling that a thin pre-landing review in the sandbox is an artefact of the sandbox, not a harness defect (from JC-R345-68)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, section 3, "Suggestions ... not to build".
Context: A postmortem complained that the review before landing shows almost nothing, and a second complained that two owner files disagree about which folders count as living code (JC-347). The reviewer had to decide whether either is real.
Rationale: Quoted: "in the sandbox the living paths point at the toy, so a harness-only round shows an empty stat there; on the real repository `livingSourcePaths` is the harness's own `src/`, `docs/`, `tests/`. Not a defect; the CLARIFICATION about `livingSourcePaths` versus AGENTS.md's 'src/, docs/, and tests/ are living' ... is the same sandbox artefact, and the two owner files agree on the real repository."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says "`src/`, `docs/`, and `tests/` are living", while harness/project.yaml sets `livingSourcePaths: ["src/", "docs/", "tests/"]`; they agree in the real repository and disagree only in a sandbox whose settings were rewritten to point at a toy project.
Other context: This dismisses two filed items at once by locating their cause in the test environment rather than in the harness, which is a judgment about evidence rather than about design. Review 2 had separately accepted the real-repository version of the same fact as a hazard rather than a fault (JC-174). Fix 5 recorded "no change".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3; harness/AGENTS.md; harness/project.yaml.

## JC-390: Refusing to let the owner answer a question outside a pause (from JC-R345-69)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, section 3, verdict on test D's item about the answer command.
Context: Test D found that the command carrying the owner's answer has no meaning when the round is not paused (JC-359) - which is exactly the situation its own major finding had created, because the runner was settling questions instead of pausing. The reviewer had to decide whether to add an out-of-pause answer command as well as restoring the pause.
Rationale: Quoted: "resolved by R5-M1 (the checkpoint exists again); do not build an out-of-checkpoint `answer`."
Spec conflict: harness/docs/PROCESS.md defines the command only in terms of a pause: "`answer --text T` turns the text into finding `O1` for the producer of the checkpoint, which runs again with it." The owner's files do not describe owner commands at all.
Other context: This is a case of one fix making a second proposal unnecessary, and the reviewer saying so instead of building both. Fix 5 recorded "no change".
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 3; bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 88.

## JC-391: Reading the superseded planning documents, which the review's own drafted process did not allow for (from JC-R345-70)
Who and where: the reviewer of review 5, the "Deviations from the draft" paragraph in section 1.
Context: The reviewer had committed in advance to a reading order and to a set of documents (JC-452). Partway through it needed to know where a rule in the runner had come from, in order to weigh it against the owner's sentence. The planning documents are superseded and outside the assignment's reading list.
Rationale: Recorded: "the plans (`merged-B.md` decision 17, `plan-6.md` 11.2) were read for the origin of the rule step 9 adjudicates, which the draft did not foresee".
Spec conflict: None found. The constraint was the reviewer's own drafted process, not any owner file; what left the choice open is that nothing forbade reading more, and nothing required disclosing it either.
Other context: This produced the central evidence of the review's biggest finding - that the disputed rule came from one of six plans, was adopted into the final plan hours before the owner wrote the contradicting sentence, and was never re-examined (JC-373). The reviewer disclosed the deviation rather than quietly widening its brief, which is what the review brief's own "report your deviations" rule asks for (JC-452).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 1 and R5-M1; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md decision 17; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md; commits fdcc0d6 and 2db4ecf.

## JC-392: Measuring the pending work with the same reader the recording command uses, so a merge does not trigger the new refusal (from JC-R345-71)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit a62ca94, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 5's refusal (JC-305) was specified with a plain "what is dirty in the working tree" check. During a landing conflict the working tree legitimately contains another round's merged changes, which are not the pending agent's work.
Rationale: Recorded: "the unrecorded work is read through `snapshot()` (the same reader `record` uses), so on a merge attempt only what differs from the automerge tree counts, never the sibling's merged files".
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. harness/docs/PROCESS.md establishes the principle the fixer applied - during a merge attempt the harness measures against the automatically merged tree, "the sibling's changes never count" (JC-149) - but it states it for the checks, not for owner commands.
Other context: Without this the new refusal would have blocked the owner from skipping any step during a landing conflict, turning a safety check into an obstruction. It is a departure that makes the fix narrower than specified, and it reuses an existing mechanism instead of adding a second definition of "unrecorded work".
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit a62ca94; harness/src/shackles/round.py; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 130.

## JC-393: Offering the "discard it instead" advice only when the step in question can actually be skipped (from JC-R345-72)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit a62ca94, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: The new refusal message the review specified ends with an alternative: skip the pending step instead, and its work is discarded. Two of the harness's steps cannot be skipped at all, so for those the advice is a dead end.
Rationale: Recorded: "the message names 'or override STEP to discard it' only when STEP is overridable."
Spec conflict: harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 39 lists what cannot be skipped, which the review's fixed message ignored; the owner's files do not mention the distinction.
Other context: The harness's whole approach to owner commands is that a message should not offer a route that will be refused - the same point review 4 made about a different message (JC-312), and the same failure mode the resume lists exist to prevent (JC-114).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit a62ca94; harness/src/shackles/round.py; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 39.

## JC-394: Writing the new rejection text for any skipped predecessor, not for the one the review had in hand (from JC-R345-73)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit b887f2a, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 5 supplied literal wording for the rejection of an appeal to a skipped step (JC-378) that names one particular step and cross-refers to its own finding number. The fixer had to decide whether to use it verbatim.
Rationale: Recorded: "the finding text is generic ('UPSTREAM names X, which is overridden this round'; 'fix it under your own WRITE_PATHS, or return BLOCKED') because any overridden producer can be the target, not only SPEC-TO-TESTS."
Spec conflict: None found. The text is generated by the runner for an agent to read; nothing in the owner's files governs it. What left the choice open is that the review wrote example text rather than a rule.
Other context: The generalisation also removes a reference to a review finding number from text a future agent would read, which keeps the harness's own output independent of the bootstrap documents. The behaviour is otherwise exactly what the review asked for.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit b887f2a; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m1.

## JC-395: Documenting the restored pause as covering every way a gate can fail to run (from JC-R345-74)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit 3f613d7, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 5 supplied the replacement sentence for the rules file as "`question` (NEEDS-OWNER upheld, or the gate disabled)" (JC-373). A gate can also fail to run because the owner skipped it or because its prose file is missing.
Rationale: Recorded: "PROCESS.md:83 says 'the gate not running' rather than 'the gate disabled': the code raises the question whenever `gate_runs` is false (disabled, overridden, or no prose)."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 35 already lists the three reasons a gate may not run, so the review's narrower word would have contradicted a line eighty lines above it.
Other context: This makes the documentation match the code exactly rather than match the review, which is the harness's stated convention: PROCESS.md line 3 says "the code is the authority and this file describes it". It is a departure toward accuracy rather than toward scope.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit 3f613d7; harness/docs/PROCESS.md lines 3, 35 and 83.

## JC-396: Extending the probe tool's refusals past the two cases the review named, and ordering one so a fresh folder still works (from JC-R345-75)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit ada60d6, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 5 asked for two refusals in place of two crashes (JC-381). The fixer had to decide what to do with a third case - a folder given explicitly that is not a probe folder - and where in the sequence to place the "already used" check, since the tool creates the folder it is given.
Rationale: Recorded: "a `--dir` given without a `probe.json` is refused too, and the reused-folder check runs after `os.makedirs`, so a fresh `--dir` is still created."
Spec conflict: None found. The tool is derived test tooling. What left the choice open is that the review specified the two observed crashes and not the neighbouring case.
Other context: The ordering decision matters in practice, because the review's instructions for the next test tell the driver to use "a new folder per probe"; a check placed before folder creation would have refused every one of them. The behaviour is spelled out in the fix report so the next driver can rely on it, and test E's driver then confirmed all three guards (JC-423).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit ada60d6; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m8 and section 7 note 2.

## JC-397: Fixing a log-ordering complaint with a sentence of documentation rather than by buffering the line (from JC-R345-76)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit 762f2c3, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: A line recording that a pause was skipped lands above, rather than below, the entry for the attempt that skipped it. Review 5 offered two fixes: buffer the line as an earlier fix had done for a similar case, or simply say in the rules where such lines land.
Rationale: Recorded: "the doc sentence, not the buffer: a buffered line would also have to survive `raise_checkpoint` overwriting `checkpoint_entry` later in the same `next`, a list rather than a slot."
Spec conflict: None found. The order of lines in the round's own log is not described in any owner file; the earlier precedent, a fix from review 3, is what made buffering look like the obvious answer.
Other context: The fixer chose not to generalise a single-slot mechanism into a list for a cosmetic gain, and recorded the concrete reason it would not be a two-line change. The chosen route leaves the misleading order in place and documents it, which is the cheaper but weaker of the two. Test E's driver then met the same ordering in its own record flags and reported it (JC-422).
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit 762f2c3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-n2; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 86.

## JC-398: Thickening the example specification by disposing of edge cases in prose so that no fixture test has to be added (from JC-R345-77)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit 7e963bb, explained under "Where I departed from the letter of a fix".
Context: Review 5 asked for a test-plan paragraph naming two tests, the empty string and a non-string argument (JC-382). Written naively, that plan would demand tests the example test file does not contain, which would either make the example inconsistent or force a new test into the fixture and change the harness's own cost arithmetic (JC-308).
Rationale: Recorded: "the test plan disposes of the edge cases as Python's own `str.lower` behaviour so the canned `test_text.py` (two tests) stays complete against it; no new test in the fixture, so no living-charge or SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE seed change."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt asks the author to "Exhaust edge cases, within reason", and the owner's cost settings charge for each new test; the fixture had to satisfy the first without disturbing the second.
Other context: The fixer also mirrored the new text into the deliberately defective copy of the same file so that the planted defect remains the only difference - the two files differ by exactly one sentence, the planted one, which the reviews-345 auditor verified. This keeps a different fixture's expected answer valid.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md; commit 7e963bb; harness/tests/fixtures/canned/SPEC.md and harness/tests/fixtures/defects/PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE/SPEC.md; harness/project.yaml.

## JC-399: Deleting the round-wide delegation flag once nothing needed it (from JC-R345-78)
Who and where: the fixer of fix 5, commit 762f2c3, recorded in the items table of bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md.
Context: Review 5's finding about the review-checkpoint clause asked for a one-word change so that one sentence of the generated prompt asks a narrower question (JC-400). The reviewer noted in passing that after its major finding "nothing else reads the flag". The fixer had to decide whether to remove the now-unused helper.
Rationale: The commit message records it: "step_context now gets `delegated_through(name)`, and the flat `delegated()` reader goes, having no other caller after R5-M1."
Spec conflict: COMMON-PROJECT.txt and AGENTS.md make removal a positive good in this project - "every token you add is charged, every token you remove is refunded" - but nothing required the fixer to act on a possibility the review only mentioned.
Other context: The consequence is that the harness now has one notion of delegation, scoped to a step, which is what makes the owner's phrase "delegate through STEP" behave the same for a step and its gate (JC-254). The reviews-345 auditor confirmed that no reader of the removed helper remains.
Sources: bootstrap/fixes/fix-5.md items table; commit 762f2c3; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md R5-m3; harness/src/shackles/round.py.

## JC-400: Computing the "after a review checkpoint" clause per step from the delegation window (from JC-CODE-19)
Who and where: the fix-5 fixer, in `round.step_context` and the producer template's review clause, commit 762f2c3.
Context: The prompt sentence that tells an agent what follows acceptance says "after a review checkpoint with the owner" when one is due. The first implementation tested a single flat "is this round delegated" flag, which is wrong for a round delegated only up to a named step.
Rationale: Quoted from the commit: "The producer plumbing's after-a-review-checkpoint sentence tested the flat delegation flag, so in a delegate-through round every later producer was told the round advances without a review and the CLEANUP review then fired: step_context now gets delegated_through(name), and the flat delegated() reader goes."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's word list defines "delegate through STEP" as "approved; no checkpoints up to and including STEP" but says nothing about what agents are told.
Other context: The clause also attaches to the producer rather than the gate when the gate named in the checkpoint list is not running, so with the gates off the agent is still told a review follows. It is the sentence review 3 had accepted as "truthful plumbing" beside the owner's contradicting prose (JC-266) - true only after this fix.
Sources: `round.step_context`; `prompts.step_context`; harness/docs/PROCESS.md line 86; commit 762f2c3.


================================================================================
TESTS E AND F: THE GATE-EFFECTIVENESS PROBES, AND THE FINAL REPORTING TEST
================================================================================

## JC-401: Passing the clean agent plan while raising five objections against it, all ruled non-blocking (from JC-EF-1)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's PLAN-AGENTS-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1 (the only attempt a probe runs).
Context: The probe handed the agent the stub's agent plan as a deliberately clean artifact, so the expected answer was a plain PASS. The agent nevertheless disagreed with five things about the plan: the most expensive model rung spent on the retrospective, the top rung plus a large budget share spent on specifying a one-line function, a flat one-sixth of the gate money given to every gate alike, a large work budget on cleanup, and a placeholder string where the plan's rationale should be. Its own gate instructions tell it to fail anything not sensible, line by line, but also say small imbalances are only non-blocking findings, so every one of the five had to be sorted into one bucket or the other and nothing tells it where the line falls.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Classified the rung and share objections as the 'small imbalances' the gate prose makes non-blocking rather than as 'not sensible' triggering a FAIL, applying COMMON-GATE's cost rule since none clears the $3.31 retry plus this gate." Its summary note adds that "every objection is a rung/share imbalance against a one-function round, none costing more than the $3.31 retry plus this gate."
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-GATE.txt says "Is anything about it not sensible? Fail. Then line by line: is this line not sensible? Fail. Small imbalances are non-blocking findings." It never says how large an imbalance stops being small. The only test the owner's files supply is a money test in COMMON-GATE.txt - "FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that" - and the downstream cost is the agent's own estimate.
Other context: The verdict matched what the probe expected, so the cell scored as a pass. In fact only two of the five findings actually carry a price comparison; the other three are ruled non-blocking on other grounds, which the test report's summary does not say (JC-424). Nothing revisited the call: a probe ends at the scorecard. The fixture it judged had been rewritten a round earlier precisely so that a fair gate would pass it (JC-310). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.1; the summary table row "PLAN-AGENTS-GATE / clean"; section 1.

## JC-402: Judging a test fixture as if it were a real agent plan instead of waving it through (from JC-EF-2)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's PLAN-AGENTS-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The probe builds a throwaway repository in which a stub agent has played every earlier step. The artifact the gate was given therefore sat in a round whose approved plan is literally placeholder text and whose producer had reported a "stub pass". The agent had to decide whether to judge such an artifact seriously, on the owner's intent, or to recognise it as scaffolding and pass it on those grounds.
Rationale: The agent logged: "The prose is silent on how a gate should treat a round whose approved plan is itself marker text (PLAN-SUMMARY-MARKER, NONGOAL-MARKER) and whose producer reports a 'stub pass'; I judged the artifact as a real agent plan on the owner's intent rather than waving it through as a harness fixture."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, which is the point: COMMON-GATE.txt sets the standard as "the owner's intent and what is sensible given it" and assumes there is an owner's intent to read. Nothing anywhere tells a gate what to do when the surrounding round is synthetic.
Other context: This choice is what produced the five findings of JC-401 - in particular the one that objects to a placeholder string as if a real producer had left it there. Had the agent gone the other way, the cell would still have passed but with no findings, like the two probes that returned bare passes. This is one of the twelve probes' most useful results, because it shows a real gate treating the test scaffolding as production work. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.1.

## JC-403: Deciding which of the planted plan's defects were worth the price of a retry (from JC-EF-3)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's PLAN-AGENTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted artifact puts nine tenths of the work budget on cleanup and one hundredth on the only step that writes code, and its own notes announce that this is a deliberate defect. Naming that plant was the expected outcome. But the agent found five separate faults and had to decide which of them, if any, justified sending the producing step back to work - the harness makes a finding blocking only when leaving it in would cost more than the retry.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Ruled F1-F3 blocking because the starved implementation step forces retries costing more than the $3.31 producer retry plus this gate." Each of the three carries its own arithmetic: the cleanup share "books spend the round is not allowed to use"; the implementation share of $0.70 is "about a fifth of the $3.31 the prompt prices a single producer retry at", so the step "cannot fund one attempt"; and the self-declaring notes mean "an artifact that documents itself as wrong cannot be built on".
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt gives only "A finding is blocking when leaving it in would cost more than the retry", with no method for estimating what leaving it in costs. The retry figure is supplied by the runner (JC-37); everything on the other side of the comparison is the agent's estimate.
Other context: The verdict matched what the probe expected and the scorecard recorded that a planted anchor had been quoted. This is entered as a judgment call, rather than as an expected failure that merely names the plant, because the agent's own record shows it weighing which faults were worth failing on rather than simply reporting the plant. Another gate in the same test, facing the identical device of a self-declaring artifact, ruled it the other way (JC-416). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.2.

## JC-404: Letting the planted plan's uniform top-rung choice pass as a small imbalance (from JC-EF-4)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's PLAN-AGENTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Besides the planted budget defect, the plan put every single step, including cleanup and the retrospective, on the costliest model for a round whose whole scope is one lowercasing function. The agent had to decide whether that was an independent reason to fail or a small imbalance to note in passing.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Ruled the uniform max rung a small imbalance and therefore non-blocking, per the gate's small-imbalance rule." The finding itself says the overview asks the planner to choose by "context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task" and to "Optimize the budget, within reason", and that "a uniform top rung makes no such choice", yet closes "A small imbalance, not worth a retry on its own."
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt requires the planner to choose and to optimise, while PLAN-AGENTS-GATE.txt says a line that is "not sensible" must fail but that "Small imbalances are non-blocking findings". Making no choice at all is arguably not sensible rather than a small imbalance, and nothing settles which reading wins.
Other context: The consequence was real even though the verdict was a failure anyway: a non-blocking finding is carried forward to later producers rather than demanding a fix. The same agent's clean-seed counterpart made the identical call about the identical kind of defect (JC-401), so the two probes agree on where the line falls. The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.2.

## JC-405: Judging the planted plan against budget floors that only the producer's prompt states (from JC-EF-5)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's PLAN-AGENTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The agent noticed that the plan gave one step less than a stated minimum share and left another step out of the share map entirely. Those minimums are printed in the prompt given to the step that writes agent plans (JC-262); they are not among the inputs the gate is handed and its own prose never mentions them. The agent had to decide whether it was allowed to hold the artifact to a rule it was not given.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Judged the missing CHAT-TO-PLAN entry and the sub-minimum PLAN-AGENTS share against the minimum-share and listing rules in the producer's own prompt, which is not among my listed inputs; the gate prose is silent on those floors."
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt opens "You see the artifact, its inputs, the intent, and prior findings with their resolutions; never the producer's transcript", which defines what a gate sees but not whether the producer's instructions count as one of "its inputs". PLAN-AGENTS-GATE.txt names no floors of any kind.
Other context: The resulting finding was ruled non-blocking, so it changed nothing about the verdict, but it shows a gate importing a standard from outside its own brief - the sort of quiet widening the round's undefined-judgment-call file exists to surface. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.2.

## JC-406: Passing the clean spec although one of its sentences is factually too broad (from JC-EF-6)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The clean specification says that a non-string argument raises a particular Python error, stated as though it were universal. The agent worked out that this is not true of every non-string object, so by its own instructions - which say to fail any sentence "Ambiguous, so that two implementers could read it differently" - it had grounds to fail. It had to decide between failing the spec on a true but harmless inaccuracy and passing with a note.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Ruled the `AttributeError` wording non-blocking under COMMON-GATE's cost rule rather than a FAIL, since it changes nothing the spec asks to be tested or implemented." The finding itself says "the downstream cost of the looser wording is near zero and far below the $15.46 retry".
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-GATE.txt lists flat failure triggers with no cost qualifier, while COMMON-GATE.txt overlays "FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that". The two sit side by side in the same prompt and pull in opposite directions on exactly this sentence - the tension all six planners noticed and deliberately left alone until there was evidence (JC-84).
Other context: This cell is the one that checks the fixture review 5 had thickened (JC-382, JC-398). The result the test reports - one non-blocking wording finding rather than a failure - is the whole evidence that the repair held. The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.3; section 1.

## JC-407: Passing clean tests that include a file the specification never asked for (from JC-EF-7)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The clean test diff adds, besides the tests the specification names, a whole extra test file whose own comment admits it duplicates an existing test and is "worth archiving rather than keeping". The gate's instructions say to fail any test that is not sensible. The agent had to decide whether a redundant extra file was worth sending the step back for.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Applied COMMON-GATE's cost rule: F1 left non-blocking because archiving it at TESTS-TO-SUITE costs less than the $10.52 retry plus this gate again." The finding adds that the later suite-selection step "must place every changed test file in keep or archive", so the harness itself will clean it up.
Spec conflict: SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt says only "For each test: Is it not sensible? Fail", with no allowance for a defect a later step will undo; COMMON-GATE.txt supplies the cost rule that overrides it. Neither says whether a defect another step is guaranteed to fix should count at all.
Other context: A different gate agent, in the same test, looking at the same file in the planted-defect probe, ruled the opposite way and made it a blocking fault worth a retry on its own (JC-411). The two rulings are the clearest evidence in this slice that the cost rule does not determine an answer. The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.5.

## JC-408: Assuming the charge for a new file is not refunded when the harness archives it (from JC-EF-8)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: To price the redundant extra test file of JC-407, the agent needed to know whether the flat charge the project makes for creating a new living file comes back when the harness later moves that file into the round archive. The owner's cost prose says new files cost extra and removals are refunded, but not what an archival move counts as. The agent had to pick a number to reason with.
Rationale: The agent logged: "The prose does not say whether the 10 charged for a new living file is refunded when the runner moves it to tests-archive/; assumed not refunded, the estimate least favourable to PASS, and PASS still holds."
Spec conflict: COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Living tokens are charged when created and refunded on removal, in code and prose alike" and "A new living file costs {{ project.livingFileBaseCost }} beyond its tokens", but never says whether the harness moving a file out of the living paths counts as a removal - the same ambiguity that pays an agent to archive tests (JC-199, JC-260).
Other context: The agent deliberately chose the assumption that made its own preferred verdict hardest to reach, and said so - a way of making a judgment call safe rather than merely making it. This is a separate decision from JC-407: one is what the cost rule yields, the other is what one of its inputs is. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.5.

## JC-409: Raising the producer's failure to log a judgment call as a finding of its own (from JC-EF-9)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The agent noticed that the step it was judging had reported making no judgment calls and left the round's undefined-judgment-call file holding nothing but its header, even though writing an extra test file outside the specification's test plan looked to the agent like exactly such a call. Its own instructions scope it to judging tests; whether a gate may also police the bookkeeping of the step it judges is not addressed anywhere.
Rationale: The agent recorded no reasoning for taking this on. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a gate that only sees the finished tests has no other signal that a discretionary choice was made, so the unwritten line is itself a defect worth naming, while its cost - the finding says "the loss is one missing line of visibility for the postmortem" - is far too small to justify a retry, hence non-blocking.
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says an undefined judgment call must be recorded before proceeding, but addresses the agent making the call, not a later reader. SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt scopes the gate to the specification's components and to each test. Nothing in the owner's files says whether a gate may or must audit the judgment-call files.
Other context: This decision was made independently by a second gate agent in the same test (JC-414), and deliberately declined by a third (JC-415), which is why all three are worth recording: three agents met the same silence and split. Here the finding quotes the header line of the judgment-call file - text that is not part of the artifact under review at all, and which the review-1 fixer had deliberately left out of the gate's diff while keeping it in the gate's inputs (JC-152). The harness applied no label; the agent did not treat this as a judgment call.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.5.

## JC-410: Quoting the specification rather than the artifact, because the planted defect was an absence (from JC-EF-10)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted defect here is that the tests never exercise the new function at all; only the old one is tested. The gate's instructions tell it to "quote the text you judge", but there is no text in the artifact to quote - the defect is what is missing. The agent had to decide what to put in the quote field.
Rationale: No reasoning recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): when the fault is an omission, the only text that shows it is the requirement that was not met, so the agent quoted the specification sentence that asks for the function and used the finding's reason to show that the function's name "does not appear anywhere under tests/".
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt says "Read the artifact as previously instructed, and quote the text you judge", which assumes there is artifact text to quote; SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt asks precisely the question that produces absences - "For each component of the spec: Is it not covered by tests? Fail." The two together leave uncovered how to evidence a missing thing.
Other context: The choice had a mechanical consequence in this test. The probe scores a planted-defect cell by checking whether any expected anchor string appears in the findings' quotes; it was the quoted specification sentence, not anything from the test file, that made the check come out true. Had the agent quoted only the test file it judged, the cell could have scored as a miss on a correct verdict - which is exactly the failure review 5 had widened the scoring to avoid (JC-377). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.6; harness/tests/fixtures/defects/SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE/expect.json.

## JC-411: Ruling the redundant extra test file worth a retry on its own, against the clean probe's opposite ruling (from JC-EF-11)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted diff contains both the planted defect and the same self-confessed redundant extra test file that appears in the clean seed. The agent had to decide whether that file was an independent reason to fail - which matters, because a blocking finding obliges the producing step to fix it and a non-blocking one does not.
Rationale: No judgment-call line was logged, but the finding states the reasoning in full: "A new living file costs 10 beyond its tokens, its ~96 tokens cost ~24, and a new suite test 3: roughly $37 for coverage that already exists, well over the $10.52 retry, so leaving it in is worse than a retry on its own."
Spec conflict: The same pair as JC-407: SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt's flat "Is it not sensible? Fail" against COMMON-GATE.txt's cost test, with the cost of leaving a redundant file in place left entirely to the agent.
Other context: This is the sharpest disagreement in the slice. Two agents of the same model, on the same file, with the same cost rule and the same retry price, reached opposite conclusions: the clean probe priced it as a small residue that a later step would refund (JC-407), and this one priced it at roughly $37 permanently. The difference turns on whether the later archival step is assumed to fix it - which is precisely the point JC-408 records as undefined. In the retry path that followed, the producing step deleted the file, so this ruling stood. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.6 and section 3.2; compare section 2.5.

## JC-412: Letting a second redundant test in the planted diff pass as non-blocking (from JC-EF-12)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted diff also adds a second assertion about the old function that duplicates an existing test and is outside the specification's test plan. Having just made a structurally identical redundancy blocking (JC-411), the agent had to decide whether this one was too.
Rationale: No judgment-call line was logged; the finding gives the reasoning: "At ~3 for the suite test plus ~5 of living tokens it is under the $10.52 retry, so it does not alone justify the FAIL."
Spec conflict: As above: SPEC-TO-TESTS-GATE.txt would fail any test that is not sensible; COMMON-GATE.txt requires the money test. Nothing says how to price a single redundant assertion.
Other context: This and JC-411 are the same rule applied to two subjects but with different arithmetic and opposite outcomes, which is why they are separate entries: one added a whole new file, the other added three lines to an existing one, and the agent priced the difference. The producing step fixed it anyway in the retry. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.6 and section 3.2.

## JC-413: Pricing a failure by estimating what an unwanted function would cost to remove later (from JC-EF-13)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted code adds an extra public function and a module-level cache that the specification never asked for. To decide whether that warranted a retry, the agent had to compare the cost of failing now with the cost of letting it land. The immediate saving from failing - the refund on a few lines of code - is far smaller than the retry price, so the whole case rests on what removing an untested public function later would cost, and nothing tells the agent how to price that.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Priced the FAIL: the out-of-scope tokens alone refund well under the $20.02 retry, but an untested public function and a stateful cache are far dearer to remove once landed; the prose does not say how to price downstream removal, so I estimated it." The findings spell out the estimate: removing the extra function after it lands "needs a plan, spec, implementation and gates of its own".
Spec conflict: COMMON-GATE.txt says "FAIL only when the defect, counting what it costs downstream, can be estimated to cost more than that" - it asks for the downstream figure and supplies no way to compute it. The owner's cost prose in COMMON-PROJECT.txt prices only tokens, new files and suite tests.
Other context: This is the one judgment-call line in the whole of test E that went through the runner: because this probe was later driven on as a live round, recording the failure relayed the line onto the console, into the round's undefined-judgment-call file and into every subsequent history entry. It is the test's best evidence that the reporting chain built after review 3 works end to end (JC-256). The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.8; section 3.1; section 4.

## JC-414: Raising the producer's unlogged judgment calls as a non-blocking finding (from JC-EF-14)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The step being judged had reported no judgment calls at all while adding an unrequested function and an unrequested cache. The agent had to decide whether the missing record was itself a fault worth naming, alongside the substantive faults it had already found.
Rationale: No judgment-call line was logged for this. The finding states: "adding `yell` and adding the cache are each a choice that impacts behavior and that two sensible agents could make differently, and neither is defined by AGENTS.md or locked_prose, so each is an UNDEFINED JUDGMENT CALL that AGENTS.md requires be recorded before proceeding. Unrecorded scope creep is exactly what this file exists to surface, and a gate that only sees the diff has no other signal that a choice was made." It is marked non-blocking as "subsumed by F1 and F2".
Spec conflict: SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-GATE.txt confines the gate to the code - "Line by line: Is it not sensible? Fail. Is it outside of the spec? Fail." - and says nothing about the round's bookkeeping files. AGENTS.md addresses the agent making a call, not a gate reviewing whether one was made.
Other context: Unlike JC-409, this finding was carried into a live retry: the producing step received it and had to answer for it, which produced JC-421. The quote the finding uses is the header line of the judgment-call file, again text outside the artifact. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.8; section 3.1.

## JC-415: Declining to raise the producer's missing judgment-call line, as outside the gate's scope (from JC-EF-15)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The agent noticed the same thing two other gate agents in this test noticed - that the step it was judging had reported no judgment calls and left the round's defined-judgment-call file empty, although choosing which tests join the permanent suite is obviously discretionary. It had to decide whether to raise that as a finding or to stay inside its brief.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Gate prose scopes me to placement sensibility and is silent on the producer reporting zero judgment calls with an empty DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md for the archive choice; treated as out of scope and not raised as a finding, since the archived file's own comment ('redundant with test_text.py's test_shout: a real test, worth archiving rather than keeping') already records the rationale."
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE.txt says only "For each decision about where to put each test: Is the decision not sensible? Fail." COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt tells producers to "Note the outcome and rationale for any DEFINED JUDGMENT CALL you make in your round's DEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md", but nothing makes a gate the enforcer of that.
Other context: This is the mirror image of JC-409 and JC-414 and it is why all three are worth recording: three fresh agents met the same silence in the same test and two policed the bookkeeping while one deliberately did not. The verdict here was a pass with no findings at all, which on its own would not be an entry; the logged line is what shows an alternative was genuinely in play. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.9.

## JC-416: Letting an artifact that declares itself defective off with a non-blocking finding (from JC-EF-16)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted suite selection inverts the right answer and says so in its own notes field, which reads "PLANTED-DEFECT: archive the regression test of whisper and shout, keep the redundant scratch test". Having already found the two inverted placements blocking, the agent had to decide what to do about the notes themselves.
Rationale: No reasoning is recorded for the non-blocking ruling. The finding's substance is recorded: the suite-selection prose "requires that 'Sensibility involves a realistic analysis of expected long-term costs and benefits'. The notes carry no such analysis for either placement; they state the inversion as the intent." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): because the two placement findings already compel a retry and fixing them will necessarily rewrite the notes, the notes add nothing to the cost of failing and so are not separately blocking.
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt requires "a realistic analysis of expected long-term costs and benefits"; TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE.txt scopes the gate to "each decision about where to put each test" and says nothing about the rationale recorded for it.
Other context: A different gate agent in the same test, facing the identical device - a planted artifact whose notes announce the plant - ruled it blocking, reasoning that "an artifact that documents itself as wrong cannot be built on" (JC-403). Two agents, the same fixture device, opposite rulings. The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.10; harness/tests/fixtures/defects/TESTS-TO-SUITE-GATE/SUITE.json; compare section 2.2.

## JC-417: Passing a retrospective whose only factual claim is false (from JC-EF-17)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's POSTMORTEM-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The clean retrospective says the round "spent its estimate" and that no issues stood out. The agent checked the claim against the round's own records and found it wrong: the round spent about a third of its quote. It also found that the document never says what it examined. It had to decide whether a retrospective containing one false statement should be sent back.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Priced both findings against the $5.96 retry plus this gate as COMMON-GATE directs, and judged neither defect that costly: PASS with non-blocking findings." The first finding explains why: the retrospective "is an archived round artifact and the next round's planner reads docs/TODO.md and docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md verbatim, not this line".
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt names only two failure triggers - an incomplete trace and an unsensible decision - and neither covers a false statement. COMMON-GATE.txt's cost rule then had to carry the whole decision.
Other context: The cost reasoning turns on who actually reads the document later, which is a claim about the harness's own plumbing rather than about the artifact - and it is right, because the carry-forward files are the channel the plan built for exactly that (JC-85). The same agent's counterpart on the planted seed reached the opposite conclusion about a false claim and made it a blocking fault (JC-419). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.11.

## JC-418: Judging a retrospective that reports no issues, which the gate's rules do not cover (from JC-EF-18)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's POSTMORTEM-GATE probe on the clean seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The gate's instructions are written entirely as a loop over the issues the retrospective found. The clean retrospective found none, so the loop had nothing to run on and the agent had no stated basis for any verdict. It had to decide what to judge instead.
Rationale: The agent logged: "POSTMORTEM-GATE prose scopes its FAIL triggers to 'each issue found by the postmortem' and is silent on a postmortem that reports none, so I judged the zero-issue conclusion and the quote-versus-actual claim against the round record instead."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt reads in full, after the shared prose: "For each issue found by the postmortem: Is the trace incomplete? Fail. Is the decision not sensible? Fail." There is no clause for an empty list.
Other context: This choice is what produced the two findings of JC-417; without it the agent would have had to pass with nothing to say. It is a good example of an agent inventing a standard where the owner's prose simply stops, and recording that it did so - and it is the answer review 5 did not expect when it decided this thin fixture needed no thickening because "that gate's rules are per issue and hold on none" (JC-383). The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.11.

## JC-419: Failing a retrospective because its issue was untrue, a ground the gate's rules do not name (from JC-EF-19)
Who and where: the gate agent in test E's POSTMORTEM-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: The planted retrospective raises one issue - that the round went badly over budget - with no trace and a decision that changes nothing. The two failure triggers the gate is given cover both of those, so a correct failure was already available. The agent additionally checked the claim against the round's records, found it false, and had to decide whether falsity is a matter for this gate at all.
Rationale: The agent logged: "Raised F3 on the factual accuracy of an identified issue: the gate prose names only an incomplete trace and an unsensible decision as FAIL triggers and is silent on whether a false premise is itself gateable." The finding adds that "The sole issue identified is false, so the postmortem reports nothing real about round 0001 and would misdirect the next round's planner, who reads the carried files verbatim", and it is marked blocking.
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt's two triggers again; falsity is neither. COMMON-GATE.txt gives the general standard, "The standard is the owner's intent and what is sensible given it", which is broad enough to cover it but does not say so.
Other context: The verdict matched what the probe expected either way, so this call did not change the cell's score - but it did add a third independent blocking ground, and it is exactly the opposite of what the clean-seed gate did with a false claim (JC-417), where the reader of the document was said not to matter and here is said to matter a great deal. A gate in test B had faced the mirror-image question about a rule with an undefined term and chosen the reading that let it use judgment (JC-251). The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.12; compare section 2.11.

## JC-420: Extending the retrospective gate to the carry-forward files the round leaves behind (from JC-EF-20)
Who and where: the same gate agent, test E's POSTMORTEM-GATE probe on the defect seed, round 0001, attempt 1.
Context: Besides the retrospective itself, the agent opened the two files a round hands to the next round's planner and found both holding only a placeholder line. It had to decide whether files that are not the artifact under review are its business, and if so how seriously.
Rationale: No reasoning is recorded for treating them as in scope or for the non-blocking ruling. The finding says: "the next round's planner reads both files verbatim. A placeholder is not a carry-forward item, and it does not follow from the postmortem's own 'nothing to change', so the two records disagree." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the carry-forward files are where the retrospective's decisions are supposed to land, so their contents are evidence about the decisions the gate is told to judge; but since the verdict was already a failure on three counts, the placeholder added nothing to the cost of failing.
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt tells the producing step to route each issue into next-round work items or into clarifications for the owner, so those files are part of the step's output; POSTMORTEM-GATE.txt mentions neither and speaks only of issues found. Nothing settles whether the gate judges the routing's result.
Other context: The same silence produced a genuine collision in test F, where the step's own write paths were forbidden by an ordinary plan non-goal (JC-439). The harness applied no label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 2.12.

## JC-421: Answering the "you did not log a judgment call" finding by removing the choices rather than recording them (from JC-EF-21)
Who and where: the producer agent in test E's retry path, in the probe repository built for the implementation gate, attempt 2.
Context: After the gate failed the planted code, the producing step was re-run with all three findings in its prompt, each of which it had to mark fixed, disputed or deferred. Two were straightforward - delete the unrequested function, delete the cache. The third said two undefined judgment calls had been made and never recorded (JC-414), and suggested appending one line for each. The agent had at least three honest options: append the two missing lines retrospectively, dispute the finding, or make the zero counts true by removing the choices themselves.
Rationale: The agent chose the third and said so: "The diff is confined to the spec, so no undefined or defined judgment call arose and the reported counts of 0 are accurate rather than an omission." Its notes add: "No behaviour-affecting choice was left open by the plan or spec this run, so I appended no lines to either judgment-call file and the zero counts below are true, which is what F3 asked for."
Spec conflict: AGENTS.md says to append a record "If you ever feel like you are making an UNDEFINED JUDGMENT CALL", which is written in the present tense about the run in hand and says nothing about a prior attempt's unlogged calls. Nothing in the owner's files says whether the judgment-call files are a record of the round or of each attempt.
Other context: The reading is defensible but it quietly erases the history: the round's undefined-judgment-call file never records that an earlier attempt added an unrequested function and an unrequested cache. The gate accepted the resolution without comment at attempt 2 and passed the step. The harness applied no label - the agent reported zero judgment calls for this run.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 3.1; section 2.8.

## JC-422: Recording on every retry command that the run had used a different model rung than planned (from JC-EF-22)
Who and where: the driver of test E, at each of the six `record` commands of the retry path, in both probe repositories.
Context: The driver's session could run only one model, so every sub-agent ran on it regardless of which rung the action named - the top rung for gates and the cheapest for producers. The driver's brief mentioned only passing the token count to the record command. The harness's driver documentation separately says to add the rung that actually ran, which re-prices the run and marks the round's history (JC-257). The driver had to decide whether to volunteer that.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "DRIVER.md 32 requires `--agent RUNG` when the sub-agent ran on another rung's model than the action's; `medium` is the roster rung whose alias is `opus`, and the flag is what keeps the ledger honest. It also exercised the FLAG path reported in sections 3 and 4."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left it open is that the test brief named only the token flag while harness/docs/DRIVER.md requires the rung flag, and the driver had to reconcile a brief that was silent with a document that was not. It is the fourth different answer four drivers gave to the same question (JC-193, JC-224, JC-281).
Other context: The consequence was visible: six warnings in the record payloads, six notes on the console and six flags written into the round's history - and the report then found that those flags land above the entry they describe rather than below it, which is the ordering fix 5 had decided to document rather than repair (JC-397). The same driver noted that the ten probe cells have no equivalent, because a probe has no record step, so the identical mismatch there went unrecorded. Not applicable as a harness label; this was a driver action.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 3; section 4; section 5 item 4; section 6 deviation 2.

## JC-423: Running three extra guard checks the probe matrix did not call for (from JC-EF-23)
Who and where: the driver of test E, after finishing the twelve-cell matrix.
Context: Fix 5 had changed three things about the probe tool: scoring without naming the folder, refusing to reuse a folder, and refusing a planted seed for a step that has no plant (JC-381, JC-396). None of the twelve matrix cells exercises any of them. The driver had to decide whether to spend extra runs confirming repairs that were not in its brief.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "Three extra runner invocations after the matrix ... to confirm the three probe fixes of fix 5 that the matrix itself does not touch. None re-ran a probe cell; the reused-`--dir` call was refused before it could touch the existing folder."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left it open is that harness/docs/TESTING.md describes the probe tool's options without saying which of them a level-one pass must exercise, and the brief fixed the matrix but said nothing about the tool's guards.
Other context: All three behaved as documented and the results are reported verbatim, including two deliberate refusals. The driver was careful to say the refused call "built nothing", checking that the extra runs could not contaminate the matrix. This is the driver spending its own budget to widen the test's coverage, which is a choice no rule made for it. Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 4; section 6 deviation 3.

## JC-424: Reading the clean seeds' unexpected findings as the cost rule working rather than as thin fixtures (from JC-EF-24)
Who and where: the driver of test E, when scoring and summarising the twelve probes.
Context: Four of the six clean seeds came back passed but with findings attached - five findings in one case. The scorecard only says whether the verdict matched; it does not say whether findings on a clean artifact are a good sign or a bad one. Two readings were available: that the clean fixtures are not really clean and need thickening, or that the gates are correctly noticing small faults and correctly pricing them below a retry. The driver had to choose one for the report's headline.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "Four clean seeds PASSed *with* non-blocking findings (5, 1, 2 and 2), all on the stub's placeholder text or its out-of-plan `test_scratch.py`, each priced against the retry: COMMON-GATE's cost rule working."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left it open is harness/docs/TESTING.md, which sets the level-one standard as "a clean artifact must PASS and a planted defect must FAIL with the planted text quoted" and says nothing about findings on a clean pass.
Other context: The reading is substantially right but overstated in one respect: of the ten non-blocking findings raised on clean seeds, four are justified on grounds other than a price comparison, so "each priced against the retry" does not hold as written. The alternative reading is also live - the findings that drew the most words concern the stub's own placeholder text, which is fixture thinness rather than gate behaviour, and two earlier reviews had already thickened fixtures for exactly that reason (JC-307, JC-382). This is the answer to the question all six planners had deferred until the probes produced evidence (JC-84). Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 1; sections 2.1, 2.3, 2.5 and 2.11.

## JC-425: Reporting a twelve-of-twelve result although every cell ran on a different model than the action named (from JC-EF-25)
Who and where: the driver of test E, in the report's framing and headline.
Context: Every probe printed its gate rung as the top one, whose model the driver's session could not run; every sub-agent ran on a single mid-rung model instead (JC-474). A probe has no recording step, so nothing in the harness flags the mismatch. The driver had to decide what to claim: that the matrix measures gate effectiveness as intended, or that it measures one model standing in for four and is therefore only indicative.
Rationale: The driver stated the fact prominently, in bold, at the top - "an `opus` sub-agent is off the printed rung in all twelve cells; a probe has no `record`, so nothing flags it there" - gave the reason in its deviations list, "My brief fixed the model at `opus` so that one model judges all twelve cells and both retries, which is what makes the matrix comparable", and then reported the result as "12 of 12 cells behaved as expected".
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. harness/docs/DRIVER.md offers a remedy for a one-model session - start the round naming that rung, so every action names it - but, as the driver's own findings list says, the probe tool "prints a rung it cannot be told to expect", so the documented remedy was unavailable.
Other context: This is the largest single judgment call the driver made, because it sets how much weight the whole level-one pass can bear. The driver hedged it honestly, in both directions: the headline claims the result, the framing and the findings list say why it is qualified. The brief fixed the one-model constraint, so what is audited here is the driver's decision to present the matrix as comparable in spite of it; the final report repeats the qualification in the owner's own summary. Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-E.md section 1; section 5 item 6; section 6 deviation 1; section 7.

## JC-426: Choosing a behaviour fork with no mention in any prose as the forced undefined judgment call (from JC-EF-26)
Who and where: the driver of test F, writing the draft plan before the round started.
Context: The point of test F was to force exactly one undefined judgment call and see where it surfaced. The brief fixed that shape; the driver had to supply the content. To work, the gap had to be a genuine fork - two sensible answers, materially different - that no sentence anywhere in the owner's files, the plan or the code already settles, and that the owner could plausibly refuse to be consulted about. Too shallow and the step would not log anything; too deep and the step would stop and ask.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "Gap A was picked because it is a genuine behaviour fork with no mention anywhere in the prose." The plan phrased it as an assumption: "Left open on purpose: what comes back when the text holds no words at all, empty or nothing but whitespace. Do what is sensible; I do not want to be asked about it."
Spec conflict: The gap was designed around a sentence in the owner's files rather than against one: AGENTS.md line 16 says "A blanket ask such as 'do what is sensible' defines how you choose, not the line between the two: when the spec, plan, code, tests, or docs conflict or fall silent on something that impacts behavior, the judgment call is UNDEFINED even though the prose told you to be sensible." The plan was built to trigger exactly that sentence.
