Other context: It worked: the specification step met the fork, chose one answer and logged it in its own words (JC-429), and that line then travelled through the recording command, the console, the checkpoint message, the round history, the finish payload and the round's file - the seven surfaces the coordinating session had asked for (JC-476). The landed test asserts the chosen behaviour. The choice also produced a side effect the driver later reported as a problem: the checkpoint printed this very line back to the owner who had said they did not want to be asked about it (JC-441). Not applicable as a harness label; this is plan design.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 2.3; section 5; section 11.

## JC-427: Choosing the function's headline behaviour as the question reserved for the owner (from JC-EF-27)
Who and where: the driver of test F, writing the draft plan before the round started.
Context: The second thing test F had to force was a question that stops a round the owner had otherwise delegated - the behaviour fix 5 had just restored (JC-373). For that, the driver needed a decision the owner would plausibly reserve, with exactly two sensible answers, cheap enough that either answer is one line of code, and central enough that the specification cannot be written around it.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "gap B [was picked] because it is the function's headline behaviour with exactly two sensible answers, one line of code either way." The plan reserved it in the owner's voice: "One thing I do want to decide myself: whether the word comes back exactly as it stood in the text or lower-cased. Ask me before writing the spec; do not choose. The step that has to pin it must finish with NEEDS-OWNER."
Spec conflict: The choice was designed to collide with COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt, which says a step that needs the owner should "build on your best assumption meanwhile and state it". Instructing a step not to choose while the prose tells it to choose provisionally is a conflict the driver created on purpose and which the step then had to resolve (JC-430).
Other context: The collision was the test's most valuable finding: the report lists "'Do not choose, ask me' cannot be obeyed as written" as a real hole in the owner's prose, not a defect in the harness. Making the question centre on the headline behaviour is also what let the driver set up the contrast where the owner's answer cuts against the step's assumption (JC-292). It is the same design test C used, extended (JC-279). Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 2.3; section 4; section 7 item 7; section 9.

## JC-428: Splitting the work budget by expected effort rather than evenly, and double-funding the step built to run twice (from JC-EF-28)
Who and where: the PLAN-AGENTS agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: This step allocates each later step's share of the round's money. The plan it was given announced that one step would run twice by design, because the owner had reserved a decision for themselves. The agent had to decide whether to spread the budget evenly or to weight it, and by how much.
Rationale: The agent logged: "work shares split by expected effort and mandated reruns, not evenly - PLAN-TO-SPEC 0.30 (it runs twice by design, NEEDS-OWNER then the owner's answer), SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION 0.18, POSTMORTEM 0.15, SPEC-TO-TESTS 0.12, TESTS-TO-SUITE 0.10, CLEANUP 0.07, PLAN-AGENTS 0.03 and CHAT-TO-PLAN 0.05 at their minimums; shares.gates {} because no gate runs."
Spec conflict: PLAN-AGENTS-OVERVIEW.txt says to "Choose what is sensible given context windows, cost, agent strengths, the task, the vision, and anything else you deem reasonable to consider. Optimize the budget, within reason." and "Expect one or two rejections per gated step and size for them." That last sentence assumes rejections come from gates; here no gate ran at all and the extra run came from the owner, so the sizing rule had to be extended by analogy - a fifth answer to the sentence three earlier rounds had also struggled with (JC-221, JC-287).
Other context: The allocation held: the step that ran twice used $1.21 and $0.72 of a $27.30 budget, so nothing came close to its cap, and the round finished at $114.43 against a $130 quote. The empty gate-share map follows mechanically from no gates running and is not itself a call. The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11; section 10; section 3.

## JC-429: Returning the empty string, not nothing, when the text holds no words (from JC-EF-30)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The plan told this step to settle, sensibly, what the new function returns when there is no word to return, and said the owner did not want to be asked (JC-426). The two obvious answers are the empty string and Python's null value. They differ for every caller: one keeps the return type constant, the other forces callers to check.
Rationale: The agent logged, in its own words: "text with no words at all, empty or whitespace only, gives back the empty string rather than None; the plan left this open with 'do what is sensible', so the line between the two is undefined; '' keeps the answer a string for every input, so callers never have to guard the type."
Spec conflict: The plan itself is the silence: "Left open on purpose: what comes back when the text holds no words at all ... Do what is sensible; I do not want to be asked about it." AGENTS.md line 16 covers exactly this case - "A blanket ask such as 'do what is sensible' defines how you choose, not the line between the two" - and tells the agent to record it as undefined and proceed, which is what it did.
Other context: This is the centrepiece of test F and the line the whole reporting chain was built to carry. It appeared in the recording command's payload and on the console, in the checkpoint message shown to the owner, in the round history at every subsequent step, in the finish payload, in the status output and in the round's undefined-judgment-call file - seven surfaces, all matching. The decision is visible in the landed test, which asserts the empty string for both an empty input and a whitespace-only one. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 1; section 4; section 5; section 9; section 11.

## JC-430: Writing the whole specification on a stated assumption although the plan forbade choosing (from JC-EF-31)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The plan ordered this step not to decide the casing question and to finish by asking the owner (JC-427). Three other things pulled the other way: the shared producer prose says a step that needs the owner should build on its best assumption meanwhile and state it; the step's own prose demands a complete, unambiguous specification; and the harness's mechanical check refuses an attempt whose specification file is missing or invalid. The agent therefore had to write a specification that took a position on the very thing it was told not to decide, in order to be allowed to ask about it.
Rationale: The agent logged: "the plan says PLAN-TO-SPEC must not choose the casing, while the overview says a NEEDS-OWNER producer builds on its best assumption and states it, and S1 needs a valid SPEC.md either way; the spec is therefore written in full on the stated assumption (the word keeps the case it had), with that paragraph marked as the owner's and replaced by their answer before any test is written."
Spec conflict: COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt: "NEEDS-OWNER when only the owner can decide and spending their time is sensible given their cost: build on your best assumption meanwhile and state it". PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt: "Break the plan into implementation steps until the spec is complete, unambiguous and sensible." Against the plan's "Ask me before writing the spec; do not choose." Nothing in the owner's files says what a step does when the plan forbids the assumption the contract requires.
Other context: The agent's own mitigation - marking the paragraph as the owner's and pledging to replace it before any test was written - worked: attempt 2 replaced it and the landed specification says in as many words that the word does not keep the case it had. The test report elevates this to a real hole in the prose: "'Do not choose, ask me' cannot be obeyed as written", and the step's own retrospective filed it as the round's first clarification for the owner (JC-437). Test C's spec agent had faced the softer version of the same bind and simply chose an assumption (JC-288). The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 4; section 5; section 7 item 7; section 9; section 11.

## JC-431: Choosing "the word keeps its case" as the assumption to build the specification on (from JC-EF-32)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: Having decided to write the specification on an assumption (JC-430), the agent still had to pick which of the two answers to assume. This is a separate decision with a separate consequence: if it guesses the owner's answer, the second attempt is a formality; if it guesses wrong, the second attempt rewrites the specification, and if the owner approves rather than answers, the guess ships.
Rationale: Recorded in the question the agent posed, which the runner printed verbatim in the checkpoint: "The word keeps the case it had: first_word returns it exactly as it stood, not lower-cased, since shout is already the transforming function and a caller who wants lowercase can call .lower(). SPEC.md is written in full on that and flags the paragraph as the owner's."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files - the plan deliberately left the casing to the owner and nothing else in the prose bears on it. What left the choice open is the instruction to state a best assumption without any guidance on how to form one; the agent fell back on symmetry with the module's existing function.
Other context: The agent guessed wrong, which is what made the test work: the owner chose the other answer, so the answer reached attempt 2 as a real, binding correction rather than a confirmation, and the specification's withdrawal of the assumption is explicit. The report notes the risk directly: had the driver resumed with an approval instead of an answer, the harness would have proceeded on this assumption and shipped the opposite of what the owner wanted (JC-293). The harness applied no label to this as a call of its own; the surrounding decision was logged as undefined.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 4; section 1; section 7 item 4; section 9.

## JC-432: Writing no integration tests and taking no refactoring, in a specification the prose invites both into (from JC-EF-33)
Who and where: the PLAN-TO-SPEC agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The step's prose invites integration tests that cross systems and refactoring, in both cases "where sensible", and a separate budget line allows refactoring up to a share of the round. The round added one pure function to a two-function module. The agent had to decide whether either invitation applied.
Rationale: The agent logged: "the overview invites integration tests and refactoring 'where sensible', and this spec adds neither - one pure function in a two-function module has no second system to cross and the toy suite is itself the end-to-end check, and the plan's non-goals leave nothing to refactor - so refactor is [] and all edge cases sit in the one new test method the plan allows."
Spec conflict: PLAN-TO-SPEC-OVERVIEW.txt says "Include integration tests that cross multiple systems, where sensible. Exhaust edge cases, within reason" and "Refactor where sensible", while COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Refactoring in a round is encouraged, capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget". "Where sensible" and "within reason" are the whole of the guidance.
Other context: The decision propagated: the eight edge cases the specification did name became the eight assertions the test step had to shape (JC-433), and the cleanup step independently reached the same conclusion about refactoring later in the round (JC-436). It is the same pair of refusals earlier rounds' spec agents made separately (JC-227, JC-228). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11; section 5; section 9.

## JC-433: Writing eight specified assertions as one loop rather than eight separate statements (from JC-EF-36)
Who and where: the SPEC-TO-TESTS agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The specification named eight cases the new test must cover but said nothing about the shape of the test method. Eight separate assertion statements and a single loop over pairs behave the same way and cover the same cases; they differ in how many living tokens they cost, which the project charges for, and in what a failure message says.
Rationale: The agent logged: "the spec names the eight assertions but not the shape of the method, so they are written as one loop over (given, want) pairs with repr(given) as the failure message rather than eight separate assertEqual lines; same cases, same stop-at-first-mismatch behaviour and the failing input is still named, at about a third fewer living tokens."
Spec conflict: SPEC-TO-TESTS-OVERVIEW.txt says only "Cover the spec's test plan and the plan's validation steps. Do this sensibly", and COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt says "Living tokens are charged, and refunded on removal: be dense, not clever". Those two pull against each other on exactly this question - the loop is denser, and whether it is also cleverer is a matter of taste - and nothing settles it.
Other context: The report notes this is the third round in a row where "shape of the code the spec does not pin" produced an undefined line (JC-294), and suggests either a sentence in the specification contract about shape or an acceptance that such lines are noise the owner will learn to skim. The chosen shape is in the landed test. The harness's own label: UNDEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 5; section 7 item 9; section 9; section 11.

## JC-434: Packing the empty-input behaviour into a single-expression idiom, and logging nothing (from JC-EF-37)
Who and where: the SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The specification pinned the behaviour - the first word, lower-cased, and the empty string when there is no word - but not how to write it. The agent chose a single expression that folds the no-word case into a default argument, in preference to the more common shapes that test the split result explicitly. It reported making no judgment calls.
Rationale: None recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the plan and specification both call for one line beside the existing function, and this spelling is the shortest one that covers both the ordinary and the empty case without a conditional, so it satisfies the round's own "one line" scope and the project's charge on living tokens; and because the behaviour is identical to every alternative, the agent did not see the spelling as a choice that needed recording.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION-OVERVIEW.txt says "Implement the spec. Do what is sensible" and nothing else; COMMON-OVERVIEW.txt's "be dense, not clever" again cuts both ways, since this idiom is dense and arguably clever.
Other context: This is the same class of decision the neighbouring test-writing step logged as undefined the step before (JC-433), and the cleanup step later weighed a variant of it - it recorded that "a denser spelling of the one-line body would save under a living token and read worse" - so the round contains three agents treating the same kind of question three different ways: one logged it, one weighed it and left it, and this one did not mark it at all. The harness applied no label; this step reported no judgment calls of either kind.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 9; section 11; section 5.

## JC-435: Leaving the owner unflagged about the middle-ground test placement (from JC-EF-38)
Who and where: the TESTS-TO-SUITE agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: This step decides which of the round's tests join the permanent suite, and its prose adds a separate instruction to flag anything worth raising to the owner. The round had a single test file, so the placement itself was effectively forced - archiving it would have emptied the suite. The live question was whether anything about it deserved the owner's attention, in particular the empty-input behaviour the owner had refused to be consulted on.
Rationale: The agent logged, as part of its placement line: "raise_with_owner is left empty: the choice is per file, both methods belong in the suite, and the owner said they did not want to be asked about the no-words case."
Spec conflict: TESTS-TO-SUITE-OVERVIEW.txt: "If there is a middle ground worth raising to the owner, flag it", with no definition of a middle ground. Against the plan's "Do what is sensible; I do not want to be asked about it", which speaks to a different step and a different decision. The agent had to decide how far the owner's refusal reached.
Other context: The recording command confirmed the field was empty, so the harness recorded that nothing was flagged. The reading is defensible but the owner's refusal was about being asked before the choice was made, not about being told afterwards which behaviour was shipped - and the checkpoint had already shown them the decision anyway, which is the tension JC-441 records. It is the narrow reading test A's agent took and test B's and test C's rejected (JC-200, JC-239, JC-296). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11; section 3.

## JC-436: Spending the cleanup step on nothing, and declining the refactoring allowance (from JC-EF-39)
Who and where: the CLEANUP agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The round's budget allowed refactoring up to a share of the round, and the cleanup step is where that would normally be spent. The agent had a working, landable round in front of it and had to decide whether any edit would improve it or merely churn it, knowing that the step's artifact would then be a single line of explanation and nothing else.
Rationale: The agent logged: "nothing is changed and no refactor is taken of the 0.3-of-budget the prose allows, because the round is already landable and every candidate edit is churn - shout is untouched, first_word is the one line SPEC.md pins, the frozen test asserts all eight spec cases, verify and the permanent suite are the same command and green (2 tests OK), the diff from the base commit touches only src/toy/text.py, tests/toy/test_text.py and archives/rounds/0001 ... a denser spelling of the one-line body would save under a living token and read worse, and the test file is frozen, so the artifact of this step is this line alone."
Spec conflict: COMMON-PROJECT.txt says "Refactoring in a round is encouraged, capped at {{ project.maxRefactorOverhead }} of its budget; the cap limits scope, and gains do not change it" - encouragement plus a ceiling, with no floor and no test for when refactoring is worth doing. CLEANUP-OVERVIEW.txt's one sentence says nothing about doing nothing.
Other context: The step still cost $1.04 and consumed 115,126 tokens to conclude that nothing should change, which is a real cost the round paid for the assurance. The agent's list is a genuine audit of the round, not an excuse. Notably it considered and rejected the very spelling question that the implementation step never recorded (JC-434). It is the third cleanup agent to reach the same conclusion, and the only one to log it (JC-202, JC-297). The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11; section 10; section 3.

## JC-437: Routing seven findings between next-round work and questions for the owner, and splitting one across both (from JC-EF-40)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The retrospective's prose gives a two-way fork for each issue: either everything was in alignment and the round simply missed something, in which case propose a fix for next round, or something is in conflict or ambiguous and the owner does not know, in which case write it up for the owner with the plans and their exact words. The agent identified seven issues, ranked them and had to place each one on one side of the fork - and found that one issue did not fit on either side whole.
Rationale: The agent logged: "routing the seven issues across the fork the step's prose defines - issues 4 (no gate ran), 6 (shares without bite) and the SPEC.md-length half of 3 go to TODO because plan, owner words and vision agree there and the round simply missed them; issues 1 (the plan forbids choosing while the prose demands a complete spec and a stated assumption), 2 (unverified owner words), 5 (a non-goal that forbids harness doc changes versus POSTMORTEM's own carry-forward files), 7 (a delegated decision printed at the checkpoint) and the pricing half of 3 go to CLARIFICATIONS as real conflicts".
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt presents the two branches as exclusive - "for each issue, decide: 1) ... or 2) ..." - and offers no third option and no way to split one issue between them, which is precisely what one issue required. Test A's and test D's postmortems had met the same wall and split an issue too (JC-204, JC-369).
Other context: The routing determines what the next round's planner reads and what the owner is asked, so it is the step's whole product. Three of the issues it routed to the owner - the "do not choose" conflict, the collision over its own write paths, and the checkpoint printing a waived decision - are the same ones the driver independently listed as documentation problems, which is two agents reaching the same conclusions separately. The report's claim that the retrospective also made the approve-versus-answer near miss its first clarification is not borne out by this routing line, which assigns the first issue to the "do not choose" conflict and lists nothing matching the near miss. The harness's own label: DEFINED.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11; section 7 items 4, 5, 7 and 8.

## JC-438: Filing two repeat issues as priced recurrences of open questions rather than as new ones (from JC-EF-41)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: Two of the seven issues had already been put to the owner in an earlier round and were still sitting unanswered in the clarifications file. The agent had to decide whether to raise them again as fresh questions, which would put them in front of the owner twice, or to attach them to the existing entries as evidence that the question keeps costing money.
Rationale: The agent logged, as the last clause of its routing line: "the two that repeat docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md's test-A questions are appended as priced recurrences of those open questions rather than as new ones, so the owner is not asked twice."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt says to "add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review with the plan(s) and exact owner words that led to the conflict" with no provision for an issue already there; COMMON-PROJECT.txt prices the owner's time only through the round budget, so "not asking twice" is the agent's own economy rather than a stated rule.
Other context: This is separable from JC-437 because it has its own consequence: the owner sees two entries grow rather than four entries appear, and the recurrence carries a price attached, which is information the original entries did not have. It also depends on the retrospective reading the carry-forward files from an earlier round, which is the same access that produced the conflict of JC-439. Test C's and test D's postmortems made the same call (JC-303, JC-348, JC-371). The harness's own label: DEFINED, as part of the same logged line.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 11.

## JC-439: Writing the carry-forward files although a routine plan non-goal forbade it (from JC-EF-42)
Who and where: the POSTMORTEM agent in test F's round, attempt 1.
Context: The step's own declared write paths are the two files a round hands forward, which live under the harness's documentation folder (JC-85). The plan carried an ordinary boilerplate non-goal, "No change to the harness, its docs, its prose or its config", and the owner's words in the plan ended "Nothing else." Writing the files disobeys the plan; not writing them disobeys the step's own prose and leaves the round's findings nowhere. The agent had to pick which instruction bound it.
Rationale: The agent logged: "the plan's non-goal 'No change to the harness, its docs, its prose or its config' and the owner's 'Nothing else.' forbid what this step's own prose and WRITE_PATHS require, edits to docs/TODO.md and docs/CLARIFICATIONS.md; I wrote both, reading the non-goal as binding the round's product and not the harness's own process records, which the next round's planner must read to act on anything found here."
Spec conflict: POSTMORTEM-OVERVIEW.txt requires the files to be written - "propose a sensible fix and add it to TODOs for next round" and "add it to CLARIFICATIONS for owner review" - while COMMON-ROUND.txt and the round's plan together make the plan's non-goals binding on the round. Nothing in the owner's files distinguishes the round's product from the harness's process records.
Other context: The report calls this "one sentence of plumbing would settle it" and notes that every round carrying that boilerplate non-goal will pay for the same line again. The agent also raised the collision as one of its own issues and routed it to the owner (JC-437), so the round both made the call and reported it. A gate in test E had met the neighbouring silence, about whether those files are the gate's business at all (JC-420). The harness's own label: UNDEFINED, and it was the round's last undefined line.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 5; section 7 item 8; section 11; section 2.3.

## JC-440: Running the state command twice instead of after every attempt, and still counting the surface tested (from JC-EF-43)
Who and where: the driver of test F, during the round and when writing the report.
Context: The test brief asked for a status reading after each attempt, which would have been ten readings, to check that the judgment-call counts and the undefined-line tail show correctly throughout. The driver ran two: one at the checkpoint in the middle and one after the round finished. It then had to decide what to claim about that surface in the report.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "Every `record` payload already carries the same three keys ... and HISTORY preserves the per-attempt figures, so surface 3 is evidenced at both ends and by eight `record` payloads in between - but the coverage is two of a possible ten calls, and a `status` between, say, SPEC-TO-TESTS and SPEC-TO-IMPLEMENTATION was not taken. I count surface 3 a match on what was observed and flag the gap here rather than claim more."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. What left it open is that the brief asked for one thing and the driver did another, then had to decide how to score its own shortfall; nothing in harness/docs/DRIVER.md requires a status reading at all, since it lists status among the read-only commands that "may be run at any time".
Other context: The reasoning is sound - the recording payloads carry the same three fields and the history preserves the per-attempt figures - but the driver's own summary still prints "7 of 7 surfaces matched" with the caveat only in the deviations section and a parenthetical in the surface table. The honest thing here is that the driver both took the shortcut and reported it plainly, which is the same pattern as the coordinating session's own budget for the test (JC-475). Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 8 deviation 2; section 1; section 3.

## JC-441: Ruling that showing the owner a decision they refused to be asked about is right behaviour (from JC-EF-44)
Who and where: the driver of test F, when writing up the checkpoint.
Context: The owner's plan had said, of the no-word case, "I do not want to be asked about it" (JC-426). The harness's rules require every checkpoint message to print the last undefined judgment calls (JC-51), so when the round stopped to ask its other question, it printed that very decision back to the owner. The driver had to decide whether this was the harness violating the owner's instruction or the harness working as designed with thin documentation.
Rationale: The driver wrote: "PROCESS.md line 84 requires the last undefined lines in every checkpoint message, so gap A's line ... was printed to the owner who had just written 'I do not want to be asked about it'. DRIVER.md line 35 gives the reason ('the owner delegated the reviews, not the monitoring') and I think the behaviour is right, but nothing connects the two sentences ... Worth one sentence in PROCESS.md line 84 saying the tail is a record, not a question."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The two sentences in tension are both in the harness's own documentation - the rule requiring the tail in every checkpoint message, and the driver instruction created by review 3's finding that delegation covers reviews but not monitoring (JC-256) - and neither refers to the other.
Other context: The round's own retrospective raised the same thing independently and routed it to the owner as a clarification (JC-437), so two agents met it and both judged it worth the owner's attention while neither called it a defect. The alternative ruling - that the harness should suppress the tail when the owner has waived a decision - would have been a substantial change to the checkpoint contract, which is why the call matters. Not applicable as a harness label.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-F.md section 1; section 4; section 7 item 5; section 11.


================================================================================
THE TWO COORDINATING SESSIONS
================================================================================

## JC-442: Summarising the sibling project for the planners instead of letting them read it (from JC-COORD-1)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit 31fe13b and the file it adds, bootstrap/vision-harness-round-concurrency.md.
Context: Another repository on the same machine already contained a working system for running concurrent rounds. The session had to decide how the six planners it was about to spawn would learn from it: by reading that repository themselves, or by reading a summary.
Rationale: The commit body says the summary was "Written by an Opus 5 sub-agent from vision_harness read-only, so that planners can build something like it without reading that repo."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files say nothing about the sibling project or about what a planner may read; the choice was left open by silence.
Other context: The summary became a first-class input to the whole plan chain; both merges cite it, and merged-B's decision log repeatedly measures the six plans against "the summary's" failures. A summary that was wrong would have been wrong in all six plans at once - and one of its claims was wrong, which both merges caught (JC-87). The whole concurrency design came across with it (JC-54). It was never revisited.
Sources: commit 31fe13b; bootstrap/vision-harness-round-concurrency.md; bootstrap/merged/merged-A.md; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md.

## JC-443: Every phase writes a standalone report, committed on its own, into the bootstrap folder (from JC-COORD-2)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the shape of the whole commit history and the bootstrap folder as it stands.
Context: The session had to decide whether the work of each phase existed only in its own chat, or was written down as a document and committed before the next phase began.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a coordinating session cannot hold six plans, five reviews and six test rounds in its own context, and no later session or auditor could see any of it otherwise; writing each phase to a file and committing it alone makes the next agent's input a path rather than a paste, and makes the trail survive the session.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. It is worth saying plainly that bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md lists "Do not touch `bootstrap/`" under the heading "Constraints from the owner, restated for the implementer", so the existence of the folder may well have been the owner's idea; what each session chose to put in it, and that every phase produced a document at all, is the session's own.
Other context: This is why this audit is possible at all. The second session continued the habit and added a folder of its own (JC-469). The cost is visible: the reports are long, and the final report's own accounting totals about 3.58 million sub-agent tokens over about nine hours.
Sources: bootstrap/ as a whole; commits 8769287, 42d7622, fdcc0d6, 8ed4870, 6bbf15d, 5b8276b, fd95394, 57cacbe, 2ca755c; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md "Constraints from the owner".

## JC-444: Six planners, given the same prompt and kept blind to one another (from JC-COORD-3)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit 8769287 and bootstrap/plans/plan-1.md to plan-6.md.
Context: The harness had to be designed from the owner's prose before anything could be built. The session chose how many independent designs to buy and whether the designers could see each other's work.
Rationale: The commit body records the design but not the number: "Each planner saw the same prompt and none of the others' work." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for the count: six independent attempts at the same brief make agreement meaningful evidence, and the session later used exactly that ("All six planners independently proposed simulated agents for the test suite" is reported to the owner as a fact worth knowing).
Spec conflict: None found. Nothing in the owner's files says how the harness should be planned; the owner's files describe what the harness must do, not how to design it.
Other context: The six plans are what the two merges (JC-445) and then the final plan (JC-92) were built from, and the final plan names them as the third reference when the merges are silent. The agreement of all six was reported to the owner in the final report, which is only meaningful because they were kept blind to each other - and the disagreements are what most of the planning entries above record.
Sources: commit 8769287; bootstrap/plans/plan-1.md to plan-6.md; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 7.

## JC-445: Two independent merges of the six plans rather than one (from JC-COORD-4)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit 42d7622, bootstrap/merged/merged-A.md and merged-B.md.
Context: Six plans had to become one buildable document. The obvious route is one merge agent; the session bought two, each merging all six plans without seeing the other's merge.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a single merge agent's blind spots would silently become the plan, whereas two merges can be compared, and disagreement between them marks exactly the places where a human-level choice is needed. The final plan's structure - a base plus an amendment list (JC-92) - only works because there is a second merge to amend from.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files say nothing about how plans are consolidated.
Other context: The two merges did disagree on real matters, and the disagreements were resolved one by one in the final plan's ten amendments, two of which - the gate verdict rule (JC-14) and the spec baseline's location (JC-65) - reached the owner's own SPEC.md wording later. Both merges independently found the same flaw in all six plans (JC-87), which is the clearest return on the second purchase. The cost was one extra full-plan merge.
Sources: commit 42d7622; bootstrap/merged/merged-A.md; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md amendments 1 to 10.

## JC-446: Probing whether a sub-agent can spawn sub-agents, and making driver mode the primary test path (from JC-COORD-6)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, the closing section "Verified after writing the above".
Context: The harness can be run two ways: a chat session drives it and spawns one sub-agent per step, or the runner calls the installed command-line tool itself. Every later real-agent test would have to use one of them, and the session did not know whether a sub-agent is allowed to spawn sub-agents on this machine. It chose to find out by experiment before committing.
Rationale: Quoted: "A sub-agent spawned with the Agent tool does have the Agent tool itself and can spawn its own sub-agents (probed: a nested spawn returned 'pong'). Driver mode is therefore the primary path for every later real-agent test: a driver session runs `next`, spawns one sub-agent per action, saves the final message, runs `record`."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files, which are silent on how the harness is driven. The nearest owner sentence is AGENTS.md, which describes what a driver does ("each `next` gives you a prompt file; give it verbatim to a fresh sub-agent") without saying that a driver must be a chat session rather than the runner itself.
Other context: This single decision shaped all six real-agent tests: five were driven by chat sessions spawning one sub-agent per action, and test E by a chat session spawning one sub-agent per probe. It also created the exception in JC-461, and it removed the stated reason for the headless requirement while leaving that requirement standing (JC-96).
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md "Verified after writing the above"; bootstrap/tests/test-A.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 3.

## JC-447: The implementer builds alone, in one session, spawning nobody (from JC-COORD-8)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner, restated for the implementer": "Do not spawn sub-agents. Do not start servers or browsers. Do not touch the screen."
Context: The harness is a large piece of software - it ended at 184 tests. The session had to decide whether to parallelise the build across several agents or give the whole thing to one.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): one agent holding the whole design in one context produces a coherent runner, and parallel builders would have had to agree on interfaces that only the plan describes; the machine is also shared with other agent sessions, which is why the same sentence bans servers, browsers and the screen.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The heading attributes these constraints to the owner, so parts of this sentence may be the owner's standing rules for this machine rather than the session's choice; the no-sub-agents half is the part with a visible consequence for how the work was organised.
Other context: It worked in the sense that the build finished, but it is also why the run was so long-lived that a second coordinating session was needed (JC-457). The same restriction was then relaxed for the bootstrap's own agents, where drivers were allowed to spawn (JC-461).
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner"; commits 7c4c6ea, ef1296d, 349461d, 05509b7, 14c1f8f.

## JC-448: The agents building the harness are not bound by the harness's own rules (from JC-COORD-9)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner, restated for the implementer".
Context: The repository being built contains a document, harness/AGENTS.md, that begins "You are inside the development harness" and lays down invariants for agents. An agent building that harness, inside that repository, has to be told whether those invariants bind it.
Rationale: Quoted: "You are not subject to the harness's runtime rules while building it (harness/AGENTS.md governs agents inside rounds). Commit freely on your branch, one commit per milestone at least, repo-local identity already configured."
Spec conflict: harness/AGENTS.md is one of the owner's files and says, in its invariant list, "Only the runner (`src/run.py`) commits, pushes, and contacts the owner. Producers edit only inside their worktree and only within their step's declared paths plus the round folder." Read literally, an agent committing by hand inside this repository breaks that invariant. The file does not say who it applies to, and the session read it as applying to agents inside a harness round only. This decision predates SPEC.md, whose header now says the listed files "say what the harness must do", which supports the session's reading.
Other context: Every commit in this history is a hand commit by an agent, so the reading was relied on constantly. The second session kept it; no reviewer ever challenged it.
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner"; harness/AGENTS.md; the whole commit history.

## JC-449: Pre-authorising a mid-build handoff if the implementer ran low on context (from JC-COORD-10)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, last constraint bullet.
Context: One agent was being asked to build the whole harness in one session (JC-447). The session had to decide in advance what should happen if that agent ran out of room before finishing, rather than discovering it at the time.
Rationale: Quoted: "If you run low on context before finishing: commit everything, write `bootstrap/HANDOFF.md` describing exactly what is done, what is not, and what is failing, and stop. A second session continues from it."
Spec conflict: None found; nothing in the owner's files concerns how agent work is resumed.
Other context: The implementer did not need it, but the coordinating session used the same device on itself a day later, writing the same file and stopping (JC-457). The pattern, and the file name, were therefore already settled when the pause came.
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner"; commit 2ca755c; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md.

## JC-450: The procedure invented for touching one of the owner's files (from JC-COORD-11)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md ("Exactly the two one-token edits of merged-B section 4, in their own commit, with the diff in the commit body"), commit 9a13bb2, and the rules section of the handoff it left.
Context: Two of the owner's locked prose files referred to a configuration key that the owner's settings file does not define, so a prompt could not render (JC-83). The owner's own instruction is that agents may edit these files minimally and must flag it; the session had to invent what "flag it" means mechanically.
Rationale: Quoted from the handoff: "If you must, make the smallest edit in its own commit with the diff in the commit body, re-accept the spec baseline in the next commit (the convention used so far: `9a13bb2` then its baseline; `2db4ecf` then `e148c10`), and flag it loudly in the final report."
Spec conflict: spec.yaml's own header is the instruction and stops short of a procedure: "They belong to the owner and change often. Everything else in the repository must stay robust to arbitrary changes in them. Agents edit them minimally, only when genuinely necessary, and flag every edit loudly." harness/project.yaml's header adds "This file contains (and only contains) user settings, and is not to be edited without owner approval." Neither says where the flag goes, what the commit should look like, or that the baseline must be re-accepted in a following commit.
Other context: The convention held for the whole bootstrap. Exactly one agent-made edit to an owner file exists in the history, its commit subject is "SPEC EDIT (owner review required)", its body carries the diff, the next commit re-accepts the baseline, and the final report reproduces the diff as its first section; the coordination auditor verified with the log that no other agent-made edit exists. Review 1 separately judged the edit itself necessary and minimal (JC-145).
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md, "Constraints from the owner"; commit 9a13bb2; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 1; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current).

## JC-451: Two full reviews before any real agent was paid to run (from JC-COORD-12)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commits 8ed4870 (review 1) and 6bbf15d (review 2), with fix passes between and after them.
Context: Once the harness was implemented, the session could have gone straight to a real-agent round, which is the only way to learn how real agents read the prompts. Instead it ran two complete read-only reviews with fixes first, and only then spent money on agents.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a real round costs "tens of dollars per pass" (the harness's own testing guide) and takes about forty minutes, and a defect found by reading is far cheaper than one found by a round that has to be re-run; review 2 describes itself as "the last review before real-agent testing", which is the plan stated from the reviewer's side.
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files say nothing about reviewing the implementation; the whole review chain is the sessions' own invention.
Other context: The two pre-test reviews produced 9 majors, 19 minors and 12 nits between them, all fixed before test A but one (JC-159). Review 1's own title line records that it reviewed the last implementation milestone, so nothing was tested before it had been read twice. From review 3 onward the order flipped: test first, then review (JC-456).
Sources: commits 8ed4870, 6086792, 6e98e69, 6bbf15d, dc035b7, fd6d47a, 94a6095; bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-2.md; harness/docs/TESTING.md.

## JC-452: What a reviewer is told to do, and told not to do (from JC-COORD-13)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, visible through the process sections the reviewers wrote in each of the five reviews and restated in the handoff.
Context: The session had to decide what a review agent is, mechanically: what it may touch, how it plans its own work, what it must produce, and what happens to its output.
Rationale: Recorded in the briefs as the reviewers describe them. Every review is "read-only, in a linked worktree", every one drafts its process "before reading the implementation" and then reports its own deviations from that draft, every one runs the suite once and reproduces suspicions on throwaway repositories rather than in the repository, and every one ends, in review 4's words, "Confirm `git status --short` shows only this file; commit it alone with the given subject and trailer; do not push."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files contain nothing about reviewing this repository; harness/AGENTS.md's rules about read-only gates apply to agents inside a harness round, not to a reviewer of the harness itself.
Other context: This brief was reused unchanged five times, which is what makes the five reviews comparable; reviews 4 and 5 both say the severity scale was "transcribed from review 3 and applied unchanged" (JC-130). The "drafts its own process" requirement also produced the useful side effect that each review's deviations from its own plan are on the record - which is how review 5's reading of the superseded plans became visible (JC-391).
Sources: bootstrap/reviews/review-1.md to review-5.md, section 1 of each; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md step 3.

## JC-453: The first two real-agent rounds designed as a pair, gates off then gates on (from JC-COORD-16)
Who and where: the first coordinating session; bootstrap/tests/test-A.md and test-B.md, commits 5b8276b and fd95394.
Context: The first spend on real agents had to be shaped: how many rounds, with which of the seven quality gates switched on, with which owner word, and on what task. Test A ran one sandbox round with all seven gates off and the owner's word "delegate"; test B repeated the same tiny toy task with all seven gates on and the same word.
Rationale: Partly recorded. Test B's heading calls itself "Level 2 of `harness/docs/TESTING.md`, second pass: the same toy task as test-A, but with all seven `gates:` flags at 1 and the round delegated", and the harness's own testing ladder prescribes that order. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference) for keeping the task identical: holding the task fixed makes the second round a clean measurement of what the gates add, and the gates are the expensive half - test B cost $86 against test A's $70, and six extra agent runs.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. harness/docs/TESTING.md prescribes the order but is an agent-written document produced by this same bootstrap, so it does not settle the question from outside; the owner's harness/project.yaml simply ships every gate at 0 with the comment "1 = enabled".
Other context: Both rounds reached the end, which is what the first session had to establish before handing over. Test A exposed a real defect - a delegated round still stopped twice for review (JC-189) - which was fixed before test B ran (JC-455).
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md; bootstrap/tests/test-B.md; harness/docs/TESTING.md; harness/project.yaml; commits 5b8276b, fd95394.

## JC-454: Every test spawn forced to one generic agent type and one model (from JC-COORD-17)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the brief test A's driver reports and as a standing rule in the handoff ("The driver reads `harness/docs/DRIVER.md` and follows it literally, spawning `general-purpose` sub-agents with model `opus`").
Context: The harness prices each step at a rung from the owner's roster and generates a matching agent definition per rung (JC-89). A driver session can only use those definitions if it was started in the repository that holds them, which a spawned driver cannot be. The session had to decide what the driver should spawn instead, and whether to let the rungs vary.
Rationale: Test A's report records the instruction and its effect: "DRIVER.md line 18 prescribes `subagent_type` = the action's `agent_type` and `model` = `model_alias`. As instructed for this test, every spawn used `subagent_type: 'general-purpose'` with `model: 'opus'` and no worktree isolation. The documented fallback (line 21) covers the type; the model is a deliberate deviation."
Spec conflict: The owner's harness/subAgents.yaml defines four rungs with different models and prices, and the owner's SPEC.md, written later, records the machine fact behind the problem: "Agent definitions are generated per rung: the Agent tool takes effort only from one loaded at session start." Nothing in the owner's files says what a test driver should do when it cannot load them.
Other context: The cost is stated plainly in test A: "the dollar figures in STATE/HISTORY are therefore priced at the wrong rung for four of the seven runs" (JC-193). The same single-model constraint then applied, forced rather than chosen, to the later tests, and was chosen again for test E (JC-474). The final report discloses the residue: "the printed probe rung is `max`, which the drivers could not run."
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-A.md section 4; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; harness/subAgents.yaml; SPEC.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 3.

## JC-455: Test A's findings fixed at once; test B's held back for the next review (from JC-COORD-18)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commits 619b0a4, 6b82c29, 0bcc8fa, 9b282a9, 2d1387b, cd2be94 and 7c2486f - all titled after test-A items - against the absence of any such commits after test B.
Context: Each real round produced a list of problems. The session had to decide, twice, whether to fix them immediately or carry them into the next review. After test A it fixed seven commits' worth before running test B; after test B it ran review 3 first and handed the open test-B items to the fixer that followed.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): test A's headline finding was that a delegated round still stopped for owner review, which would have corrupted test B's measurement of the gates if left in place, so it had to be fixed before the second round; after test B there was no third round waiting, so the cheaper course was to let a reviewer judge the items with both rounds in hand rather than fix them unreviewed.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files say nothing about when defects are fixed.
Other context: The handoff makes the second half explicit as an instruction to the next session: step 1 is "Fixer (Fable max): fix the sandbox builder above, then review-3's findings plus the open test-B items, following the reviewer's verdicts". Review 3 checked the first half of the decision by re-reading those seven commits against test A's list.
Sources: commits 619b0a4 to 7c2486f; bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 1; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md step 1; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md.

## JC-456: Review 3 run after the rounds, with the test reports treated as evidence (from JC-COORD-19)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit 57cacbe and bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 1.
Context: After two real rounds the session could have gone on testing. It chose to stop and review again, and to give the reviewer something the first two reviews never had: the transcripts of real agents using the system.
Rationale: Recorded in the review's own process: "The owner's files and the two test reports first, in full... The test reports were read as evidence of how real agents parse the prompts and docs: every place an agent logged an undefined judgment call, worked around the runner, or misread a document was written down before any code was opened."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not govern the review chain.
Other context: This became the shape of the rest of the bootstrap (JC-458): a test, then a review that rules on the test's items, then a fix. Reviews 4 and 5 repeat the same step, each reading the preceding test report in full and building a table of items needing a verdict - which is what turned a driver's complaint into either a fix or a recorded refusal.
Sources: commit 57cacbe; bootstrap/reviews/review-3.md section 1; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 1; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 1.

## JC-457: Pausing and handing the rest to a second session (from JC-COORD-20)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit 2ca755c and the file it wrote.
Context: After review 3 the session had eight substantial steps left, including four real-agent tests, two reviews and three fix passes. It had to decide whether to continue in the same context or stop and write everything down.
Rationale: The handoff opens "Continue the bootstrap of `C:\Users\twolf\Claude\shackles_harness`. Everything so far is committed on branch `claude/bootstrap` and pushed; local and remote `main` point at the same commit." It gives a reading order, the state, the known defect, the standing rules and the ordered steps, but records no reason for pausing. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the session had already spent its context on six plans, two merges, five review and fix cycles and two forty-minute rounds, and the device for exactly this had been pre-authorised in the final plan (JC-449).
Spec conflict: None found; nothing in the owner's files concerns session boundaries.
Other context: The pause is also where the push rule was exercised: the handoff records that everything to that point was pushed, which is the "or at a pause" half of JC-464. The second session picked the file up and ran all eight steps.
Sources: commit 2ca755c; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md.

## JC-458: The rest of the bootstrap fixed in advance as eight ordered steps (from JC-COORD-21)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff's "Remaining steps, in order".
Context: Handing over meant deciding how much of the remaining work to specify. The session specified all of it: eight numbered steps, each naming the kind of agent, the configuration of each remaining test, and where its report goes.
Rationale: Quoted in part: "2. Test C (Opus driver): a tiny throwaway task with the owner's word 'approve' instead of 'delegate', so the round pauses at review checkpoints and resumes through owner commands; include one question checkpoint answered with `answer`... 4. Test D (Opus driver): approval skips: `override` of a step and of a gate, and 'delegate through STEP'... 6. Test E (Opus): gate effectiveness: planted defects that gates must FAIL, then the retry path... 7. Final test (Opus): fabricate a situation that forces an undefined judgment call... and check that the harness records it in UNDEFINED_JUDGMENT_CALLS.md and surfaces it at `record`, at checkpoints, in `status` and at `done`."
Spec conflict: None found. The owner's files say nothing about the test programme. The harness's own testing ladder covers levels, not this sequence of four specific rounds, and it is an agent-written document besides.
Other context: The second session followed every step and added the concrete designs (JC-279, JC-471, JC-473, JC-476). The alternation "test, then review, then fix" comes from here, as does the decision that the reviews sit between tests rather than at the end. Each remaining test's purpose was therefore chosen by a session that would not run it.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md, "Remaining steps, in order"; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current); bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 4.

## JC-459: No review after test E (from JC-COORD-22)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff: "Fix anything that breaks; no further review after this one."
Context: The handoff's plan alternated tests and reviews. The session had to decide where to stop reviewing, given that two tests - the gate-effectiveness matrix and the final test - still lay ahead of the last planned review.
Rationale: Recorded only as the instruction quoted above. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a sixth review after test E would have cost another full read of a system that had by then been read five times, and tests E and F were designed to confirm behaviour rather than to explore it; the judgment was that review 5 could cover both remaining paths in advance.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not govern the review chain.
Other context: The second session compensated by raising the bar in review 5's brief (JC-477): the reviewer's own process records "No review follows test E, so anything on this path is a major at least", which is the rule it then applied and once deliberately set aside (JC-376, JC-381). In the event nothing broke in test E, and test F found no defect either, so no fix pass ran after review 5's.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md step 6; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 1; bootstrap/tests/test-E.md; bootstrap/tests/test-F.md.

## JC-460: One sub-agent at a time, in its own worktree, removed afterwards (from JC-COORD-23)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff's "Rules that still apply".
Context: The coordinating session had to decide how to run its own helpers: how many at once, whether they share the checkout, and what happens to the working copies afterwards.
Rationale: Quoted: "Keep your own context small: spawn one sub-agent at a time, with worktree isolation for anything that reads or writes the repo; collect its files from `.claude/worktrees/agent-*`, then remove the worktree and delete its branch."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The worktree-isolation half restates a standing machine rule, which the drivers cite, so the judgment here is the serialisation and the clean-up, not the isolation.
Other context: Serialising the helpers is why the bootstrap took about nine hours of wall clock for about 3.58 million coordination tokens, and it is also why every phase could be measured separately in the final report. The clean-up half was honoured: none of the bootstrap's own agent branches still exists in the repository.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current); bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 8; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 8.

## JC-461: Sub-agents work alone, with the test drivers as the single exception (from JC-COORD-24)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff: "Sub-agents work alone (no nested spawning), except the test drivers, which spawn one sub-agent per harness action."
Context: The final plan had forbidden the implementer to spawn anyone (JC-447), but driver mode had been made the primary test path (JC-446), and a driver cannot work without spawning one agent per step. The session had to decide whether to carve out an exception or find another way to run the tests.
Rationale: Recorded as the rule itself; the reason is in the final plan's verified note, which establishes that nested spawning works on this machine and that driver mode is therefore available.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files; harness/AGENTS.md describes a driver spawning a fresh sub-agent per step but says nothing about who may be a driver or how deep spawning may go.
Other context: This exception is what every real-agent test rests on: test A spawned seven step agents, test B thirteen, test E sixteen, test F eight. The final report counts their tokens separately from the coordination tokens for exactly this reason.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md "Verified after writing the above"; bootstrap/tests/test-A.md; bootstrap/tests/test-E.md; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md sections 3 and 8.

## JC-462: Fable at maximum effort for planning, review and fixes; Opus for the drivers and step agents (from JC-COORD-25)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff's "Rules that still apply".
Context: Two model families were available. The session had to decide which kind of agent got which model, and how to obtain a maximum-effort agent of the first kind when no such named agent type might exist in a given session.
Rationale: Quoted: "Fable 5.1 max agents: use subagent_type `fable-max` if it is listed, otherwise `general-purpose` with model `fable` (verified to give claude-fable-5-1 at max effort; check once by asking the agent to quote its model line and effort tag). Test drivers and their step agents: Opus, any effort."
Spec conflict: None found for the bootstrap's own agents. The owner's harness/subAgents.yaml does rank the two families for agents inside a round, which is consistent with the choice but does not govern who reviews or fixes the harness itself.
Other context: It held to the end. Every review and fix report opens by quoting its own system prompt to prove its model and effort, and every test report opens by naming its driver. The final report records that three generic agent definitions were added to the user's home folder to make this possible, which is a change outside the repository and is disclosed as such.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md, fix-4.md, fix-5.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md, review-5.md; bootstrap/tests/test-A.md; harness/subAgents.yaml; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 7.

## JC-463: Merging fast-forward only, and moving local main with every merge (from JC-COORD-26)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff: "Fixers commit on their branch; fast-forward merge into `claude/bootstrap`, then `git branch -f main claude/bootstrap`."
Context: Each helper worked on its own branch. The session had to decide how that work rejoined the line of development and when the branch called main would follow.
Rationale: Recorded as the rule itself. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): a fast-forward merge keeps one readable line of commits for the reviewers who read the history commit by commit, and moving main at the same moment keeps the two branches identical so that a sandbox built from either is the same system.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. It is worth noting that the harness the sessions were building deliberately rejected this mechanism for its own rounds: merged-B decision 10 rejects moving a local branch first because "it needs `main` parked, an unenforced precondition that failed late in the sibling", and the harness lands rounds by pushing instead (JC-56). The bootstrap's own procedure and the product's procedure differ on purpose.
Other context: The whole history is linear, with no merge commits at all, and both branches and their remote counterparts end at the audited commit. The second session restated the rule in its own handoff.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; git log and git branch on the audited commit; bootstrap/merged/merged-B.md decision 10.

## JC-464: Pushing only two branches, and only after a passing review or at a pause (from JC-COORD-27)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff: "The GitHub remote now exists with `main` and `claude/bootstrap`. Push only those two branches, and only after a passing review or at a pause."
Context: A remote repository existed. The session had to decide how often work left the machine, and which branches were allowed off it at all.
Rationale: Recorded as the rule itself. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): publishing only reviewed states keeps the public history free of half-fixed intermediate work, while the "or at a pause" clause guarantees that nothing is lost if a session ends; restricting it to two branches keeps the dozens of throwaway agent branches local.
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files do not mention the remote. The workspace's own instruction, which is the owner's, asks agents to flag local unmerged changes, which is the tension this rule creates.
Other context: The rule was visibly obeyed and visibly noticed. When test C ran, fix 3 had been merged locally but not pushed, and the driver reported it as a flag for the owner and fast-forwarded its own worktree to reach the right system under test (JC-278). Each review's brief also ends "do not push", leaving the push to the coordinator. At the end everything was pushed, and both remote branches sit at the audited commit.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md section 8; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md section 1; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current).

## JC-465: Real-agent rounds confined to a sandbox with a local, offline origin (from JC-COORD-28)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff: "Real-agent tests run only in a sandbox made by `run.py sandbox --dir <temp dir>` (a local bare origin, so a round there can never reach GitHub); set `git config core.longpaths true` there."
Context: A harness round commits, pushes and lands code. Running one against the real repository would have tested the real thing; running it in a throwaway copy with a fake remote tests the same code with no way to damage the project.
Rationale: Quoted above; the parenthesis is the reason, and it is the session's own. Review 1 had recommended the same thing for its own reasons (JC-146).
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The harness's own testing guide reserves the real repository for "Level 3: the owner's tiny task on the real repository", but that document was written by this bootstrap, so the containment rule is the sessions' judgment rather than an external instruction.
Other context: Held without exception for all six tests; every test report states that the real repository received nothing but its own report file. It is also why the bootstrap ends by handing the last level to the owner: "What follows is the owner's: Level 3 of `harness/docs/TESTING.md`, a tiny task on the real repository from a session started in this checkout". One cost is recorded: a defect review 2 found was "Not reachable in the sandbox" and so went unexercised by every test (JC-164).
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; bootstrap/tests/test-A.md to test-F.md; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current); harness/docs/TESTING.md.

## JC-466: The SPEC.md sandbox defect diagnosed in the handoff instead of fixed (from JC-COORD-29)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, commit ca24820 and the "Known defect to fix first" paragraph of the handoff it wrote.
Context: The owner added SPEC.md at the repository root and listed it in spec.yaml after the handoff had been written. That broke the sandbox and probe builders, which copied a fixed list of root files, so every temporary repository was missing a spec file and two tests failed. The session had already decided to stop; it had to choose between fixing this before handing over and writing the diagnosis down.
Rationale: It wrote the diagnosis down in unusual detail: "In `harness/src/shackles/probe.py`, the `sandbox` function (around line 168) and the probe repo builder (around line 69) must copy `spec.yaml` and every path `spec.yaml` lists instead of a fixed list... Add a test that a fresh sandbox passes `spec status` clean and `doctor` without errors." Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the session had reached its stopping point, and a precise diagnosis costs a paragraph while a fix costs a full fixer pass and a suite run.
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md, which had just been added, is the reason the defect exists: "The files spec.yaml lists are the implied shackles spec... Everything else in the repository is derived from them and must survive arbitrary edits to them." Nothing said that adding a file to spec.yaml had to keep working without a code change, and nothing said whose job it was to notice.
Other context: The next session's fixer did exactly what the paragraph said, in its first commit, and added the test that was asked for (JC-268). The two failing tests passed afterwards, and the suite went from 164 of 166 to 176 of 176.
Sources: commit ca24820; git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md; commit 7936c64; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md.

## JC-467: The final report's contents fixed in advance (from JC-COORD-30)
Who and where: the first coordinating session, in the handoff's step 8.
Context: The report to the owner would be written by a session that had not done most of the earlier work. The first session decided what that report must contain, rather than leaving it to the writer.
Rationale: Quoted in part: "Final report to the owner, containing: the spec-edit diff (commit `9a13bb2`, plus any later agent-made one) flagged loudly; that all six planners independently proposed simulated agents for the test suite; that the plans' probe ladder... was adopted as the documented testing loop...; that the standalone Claude CLI on this machine is not logged in, so headless mode is stub-verified only...; that `.claude/settings.json` in the repo adds a prompt hook that logs the owner's chat lines to the gitignored `harness/OWNER.log`; that three generic agent definitions were added under `C:\Users\twolf\.claude\agents\`; and every finding the fixers skipped, with the reason."
Spec conflict: None found; the owner's files contain no reporting requirement. The nearest owner instruction is spec.yaml's "flag every edit loudly", which this list turns into a concrete obligation.
Other context: All seven required items appear in the delivered report, and the second session added more (JC-479). Three of the seven are disclosures about things done outside the repository or left imperfect, which is the part of the list that a session writing its own report might have been tempted to omit.
Sources: git show ca24820:bootstrap/HANDOFF.md step 8; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md sections 1, 5 and 7.

## JC-468: Every sub-agent fast-forwards its worktree to the local branch before reading anything (from JC-COORD-31)
Who and where: the second coordinating session; the briefs as the agents report them and the rule as restated in the current handoff.
Context: The tool that creates an isolated copy of the repository for a sub-agent cuts it from the remote branch, not the local one. Because pushes were deliberately rare (JC-464), a fresh agent's copy could be many commits behind the work it was supposed to review, fix or test. The session had to decide how to handle that.
Rationale: Recorded in the final report: "Every agent worktree cut by the Agent tool was based on the remote branch, not the local one; each sub-agent was told to fast-forward first and every report records the commit it tested."
Spec conflict: None found; nothing in the owner's files concerns agent worktrees.
Other context: The effect is visible everywhere and it mattered at least once: test C's driver found itself ten commits behind and wrote "Without the fast-forward the sandbox would have been built from the pre-fix-3 runner - the wrong system under test" (JC-278). Every later report opens by naming the exact commit it examined, which is what makes the chain auditable - and what let this audit begin by checking one hash. The rule was important enough that the second session put it in its own handoff for later sessions.
Sources: bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 7; bootstrap/HANDOFF.md (current); bootstrap/fixes/fix-4.md, fix-5.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-4.md, review-5.md; bootstrap/tests/test-C.md to test-F.md.

## JC-469: Fixers write a report into a new folder of their own (from JC-COORD-32)
Who and where: the second coordinating session; commits 255461d, 4708527 and 511d5f0, which each add one file to a folder that did not exist before.
Context: The first session's two fix passes left only code commits; a reader had to reconstruct what was fixed from commit subjects. The handoff asked for fixes, not for a report. The second session decided its fixers would also write one, into a folder of their own.
Rationale: Not recorded. Expected rationale (the auditor's inference): the reviewer that follows a fix has to check each claimed fix against the code, and the test driver that follows has to know what changed underneath it; a report with an item table and the fixer's own notes makes both cheap. The reports' own shape shows the intent: each ends with a section called "What the docs do not say", whose last bullets are addressed to the next runs by name.
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. It does cut across the first session's instruction "Do not touch `bootstrap/`", which the second session evidently relaxed to exactly one file per fixer: both later fix reports record "`bootstrap/` untouched but for this file".
Other context: Reviews 4 and 5 both read the fix report first and then check it against the commits ("check each claim against the code, not against the fix note"), and the final report points the owner at these files for the detail behind the skipped findings. Fixes 1 and 2 still have no report, so the trail is uneven across the two sessions - which is why several entries in this audit had to read the review-1 and review-2 fixes out of their commit bodies alone.
Sources: commits 255461d, 4708527, 511d5f0; bootstrap/fixes/fix-3.md, fix-4.md, fix-5.md; bootstrap/FINAL-PLAN.md; bootstrap/reviews/review-5.md section 1; bootstrap/FINAL-REPORT.md section 5.

## JC-470: The driver plays the owner, with the owner's words written in advance (from JC-COORD-33)
Who and where: the second coordinating session; the "Roles" paragraphs of tests C, D and F and the plans they contain.
Context: Tests C, D and F needed an owner: someone to write the plan's owner messages, to answer a question at a checkpoint and to approve or override at a review. The real owner was not in the loop. The session had to decide who spoke for them and when those words were fixed.
Rationale: Recorded in each report as a role declaration, for example test C: "Roles: I played both the **driver** and the **owner**. Everything the owner said is marked **OWNER** and appears only in section 4 and section 2's plan." Test A, under the first session, had a narrower version: "The owner was absent. Owner messages 1 and 2 were used verbatim as given. At both checkpoints the standing instruction 'their word for any review is `approve`' was used."
Spec conflict: None found in the owner's files. The design does sit oddly with the harness's own premise that the driver must never decide for the owner: harness/AGENTS.md reserves owner contact to the runner, and the harness verifies quoted owner words against a log that, in a sandbox, does not exist (JC-121).
Other context: The sessions handled the risk by scripting the owner's words into the plan before the round started and by having the owner choose, at the question checkpoint, the option the producer had not assumed, so that the answer's effect could be traced into the landed code (JC-292). Every such round also carries the unverified-quote flag, which the reports record.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-C.md; test-D.md; test-F.md; test-A.md section 4; harness/AGENTS.md.

## JC-471: Test D designed as two rounds in one sandbox with the gates flipped between them (from JC-COORD-35)
Who and where: the second coordinating session, bootstrap/tests/test-D.md and commit e150b52.
Context: The handoff asked for one test covering both kinds of approval skip. The session had to decide whether one round could carry all of it and, if not, how to split it; and it had to decide which way each round's delegation was expressed, since the harness accepts it either from the plan or from the command line, naming either a producer step or its gate.
Rationale: Recorded in the report: round 0001 runs with all seven gates on and the delegation coming from the plan naming the producer, round 0002 with all seven off and the delegation coming from the command line naming the gate. The first round's plan is described as "deliberately airtight - round 0001 was to test the skips, not a question" (JC-331), and the second as "The plan says `approved`/`approve`, as the assignment prescribes; the delegation comes from the command line and names the **gate**."
Spec conflict: The owner's SPEC.md contains the sentence the second round ended up testing: "Delegation skips only review checkpoints; questions, blocks, limits and spec edits still stop the round." The owner's file says what must happen but does not say how the two ways of expressing a delegation should be exercised.
Other context: The two rounds cost about $200 of ledger dollars and around 1.9 million step-agent tokens together, and the second one is what exposed the defect later fixed as review 5's first major (JC-350, JC-373). Flipping the gates between rounds also required a spec edit, an acceptance, a commit and a push inside the sandbox, which the report records in full.
Sources: bootstrap/tests/test-D.md; commit e150b52; SPEC.md.

## JC-472: Test D's unreachable item left to a review and to a later test rather than re-run (from JC-COORD-36)
Who and where: the second coordinating session; test D's deviations, review 5's first major finding, and test F's stated purpose.
Context: One of test D's six planned items, an override issued at a question stop, could not be run, because no question stop ever arose: the runner settled the question itself (JC-350). The session could have re-run test D after a fix, or covered the missing half elsewhere.
